d) Secretariat’s assessment;

assessment; and 
g) Gender documentation.

NOTE TO ACCREDITED ENTITIES ON THE USE OF THE FUNDING PROPOSAL TEMPLATE 
• 
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference 
to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
• 
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• 
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 
60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service 
standard time. 
• 
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• 
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be 
disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the 
redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities 
are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages) 
1. Historically, Jordan’s climate has been arid, with a high degree of rainfall variability and temperatures in 
summer (June–August) that often exceed 40°C. Approximately 94% of Jordan’s annual rainfall is lost 
through evaporation, limiting the amount of freshwater available in the country. These dry conditions have 
resulted in Jordan’s current ranking as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world, with only 125 
cubic metres per year (CM/yr) of water available per capita4.  
 
2. This baseline water stress is exacerbated by climate change, with climate projections under RCP4.5 and 
RCP8.5 showing increasingly dry conditions in the country. This drying effect is a result of an increase in 
maximum temperatures and a reduction in annual average rainfall, which is causing extended dry periods 
and a shorter rainfall season, with an increased frequency of high-intensity rainfall events. The increasing 
deviation from natural cycles of wet and dry periods in the country associated with climate change is also 
resulting in an observed increase in the frequency of severe droughts over the past 50 years. These 
extreme drought periods are expected to continue becoming increasingly frequent under future climate 
change scenarios. Such shifts in rainfall, combined with the effects of an increase in the average 
temperature by 2–4°C and the resulting increase in evaporation rates will cause a reduction in safe yield 
water supplies beyond Jordan’s existing deficit of 416 MCM/yr. Aquifer recharge rates are also projected 
to decline, resulting in a reduction in the available amount of groundwater. This will cause reduced baseflow 
in rivers and consequently also a decrease in surface-water availability. This problem will be further 
compounded by the increasing demand for freshwater for irrigation and domestic consumption linked to 
rising temperatures which will result in increases in groundwater abstraction rates.  
 
3. Notably, the impacts of climate change will result in a considerable reduction in Jordan’s agricultural 
production. For example, barley production is expected to decline by up to 50% under future climate 
conditions, which will severely affect local food supplies and consequently also food security in the country. 
The potential severity of this climate impact was demonstrated during a severe drought between 1998 and 
2012 — worst drought recorded in Jordan over the past 900 years. During this period, rainfall decreased 
by up to 70% compared with long-term averages, resulting in severe agricultural, socio-economic and 
environmental impacts5. The drought caused a reduction in cereal harvests to only 0.6% of the country’s 
total needs, diminished non-cereal crop production by 23% and reduced meat and dairy production by 
 
4 WRI. 2019. Aqueduct baseline water stress rankings. Available at: https://www.wri.org/aqueduct/. 
5 Drought Conditions and Management Strategies in Jordan. Prepared for the Initiative on ‘Capacity Development to Support 
National Drought Management’ Policies Workshop. 2014.

=== PAGE 7 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 3 of 144 
 
40%6. Such impacts will also affect livestock production through disruptions in the supply of fodder. Already, 
shortages of up to 77% in the amount of fodder available for livestock have been observed, and are partly 
attributable to climate change over the past decades. Therefore, there is a need to not only strengthen the 
supply of water resources in Jordan, but also transition food systems from those reliant on climate-
vulnerable crops like barely to those which are more diverse and resilient to climate change, such as quinoa 
and pearl millet, which contribute to food security under climate change conditions. 
 
4. Climate change is also strongly affecting the functioning of ecosystems in Jordan and consequently the 
services they provide — particularly maintenance of the hydrological cycle. Ecosystems in the country are 
already being degraded by numerous non-climatic drivers, including: i) land loss or degradation from 
urbanisation and agriculture; ii) overgrazing; iii) unregulated fuelwood harvesting; iv) water over-extraction; 
v) species over-exploitation; vi) the introduction of invasive plant and animal species; vii) mining; and viii) 
pollution. Climate change is accelerating this degradation — with numerous adverse impacts, including 
increased soil erosion and sedimentation of dams and waterways, decreased quality and quantity of water 
resources, increased forest dieback, changes in species composition, desertification, habitat degradation 
and species loss — and threatening the climate-resilience of communities reliant on these ecosystems’ 
services.  
 
5. In the absence of urgent climate change adaptation interventions at the landscape level, the projected 
drying trend across Jordan will have increasingly severe impacts on Jordan’s water supplies, extending the 
water supply deficit to 687 MCM/yr by 20407. Combined with the compounding impacts on ecosystems, 
this water shortfall will have significant impacts on the livelihoods and well-being of local communities 
across Jordan. 
 
6. The Government of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan (GoJ) is requesting GCF grant finance to respond 
to the above-mentioned climate threats and impacts. GCF finance will be combined with in-kind government 
(MoENV) and donor (Consortium of NGOs) co-financing to initiate a paradigm shift in the approach to land 
and water resources management in Jordan that will result in climate-resilient sustainable development 
nationwide. 
 
7. The objective of the proposed project is to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on water 
resources in Jordan through improved aquifer recharge in a landscape-level approach. The proposed 
approach includes: i) improved ecosystem management through land restoration, improved agricultural 
practices and managed aquifer recharge; ii) rainwater harvesting; iii) evaporation reduction from the King 
Talal Dam; and vi) strengthened governance. A Water Fund will be established to scale up good landscape 
management practices and to promote water conservation in the agriculture sector which currently takes 
the largest share of water demand: agriculture.  The Water Fund will leverage financing from private 
(industry and commercial agriculture) and public sector stakeholders (municipalities) — downstream water 
users — for aquifer replenishment services from the restoration and management in upstream watersheds. 
The project will be implemented at the Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Kafrain watersheds in the Jordan 
Valley. 
 
8. Under the proposed project, on-the-ground, climate-resilient ILWRM interventions will be implemented 
across three sites within the Jordan Valley. These interventions will include 8,000 ha of restored 
ecosystems, 12 rockfill dams for managed aquifer recharge, 3,000 rooftop rainwater harvesting tanks and 
1 MW of floating solar panels for evaporation reduction — leading to an ultimate increase in the country’s 
annual water supply of ~8.9 MCM after eight years (project duration) and a potential cumulative increase 
of ~154.5 MCM after and 25 years. Upscaling of all four on-the-ground interventions by others is expected 
to cumulatively yield an increase in water resources of 34 MCM under a conservative scaling-up scenario 
and 344 MCM for a best-case scenario, both after 25 years. Furthermore, the project will demonstrate to 
decision-makers that the implementation of climate-resilient ILWRM as a national response to climate 
change has the potential to enable Jordan to balance its water demand with water supply in the long term 
despite climate change impacts.  
 
9. Project interventions will directly benefit a total of 246,942 people (~2.2% of the Jordanian population) — 
135,736 men (55%) and 111,206 women (45%) — in the Jordan Valley by reducing their climate 
vulnerability. Of this total: i) 62,446 (30,607 women and 31,839 men) are from the Yarmouk site — Khaled 
 
6 FAO. 1999. Worst drought in decades decimates cereal crops in Jordan. Available at: 
http://www.fao.org/NEWS/GLOBAL/GW9907-e.htm. 
7 Government of Jordan. 2014. Jordan’s Third National Communication on Climate Change.

=== PAGE 8 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 4 of 144 
 
Bin Al Waleed and Al Seru municipalities; ii) 105,841 (45,179 women and 60,662 men) are from the King 
Talal Dam site — New Ardah, Deir Alla and M’addi municipalities; and iii) 78,655 (35,420 women and 
43,235 men) are from the Kafrain site — Shouna Alwasta and Mahes municipalities. Direct beneficiaries 
include all people who will benefit directly from a project activity or the result of an activity. For example, 
under Output 2.1, 1,200 smallholder farming households (~6,000 people — an average of 5 people per 
smallholder household) across the three sites will benefit from on-farm interventions, while 2,800 
households across the sites (~14,000 people) will benefit directly from rainwater harvesting infrastructure 
implemented on houses. Direct beneficiaries also include all vulnerable peoples in the project’s three target 
areas, as they are all expected to benefit from the augmentation of water resources under Outputs 2 and 
3, either via improvements in water supplies for domestic and/or agricultural usage. Under Output 3, all 
172,179 people living in the municipalities of the three target sites (and related municipalities) listed above 
will benefit from the improved protection, supply and use of water for domestic purposes under climate 
change conditions. Specifically, they will directly benefit from enhancements in the quality and quantity of 
ground and surface water through Activity 3.1. This will be achieved through the construction of rockfill 
dams to support managed aquifer recharge and the rehabilitation of active freshwater springs.  
 
10. As integrated land and water resources management interventions (ILWRM) are scaled up via the Water 
Fund (Activity 2.4), it is expected that an additional 4,000 ha of land will be placed under improved 
management for water resources benefits, with at least one additional (adjacent) municipality per watershed 
site benefitting directly. The 4,000 ha is related to total water attenuation potential for the three watersheds, 
in addition to the 8,000 ha to be restored directly by the project (see Table 27 and Figures 116-118 in Annex 
2).  The rainfall attenuation potential represents the slowing of overland water flows which reduces the 
amount of water running off the land and increases the amount of time available for water infiltration, and 
hence groundwater recharge.  The Water Fund will scale the set of restoration and land management 
interventions in Component 2 to target the entire 12,000 ha with potential for restoration to support water 
attenuation. Consequently, an additional 74,763 people (41,604 men and 33,159 women)8 are expected to 
benefit directly from the project.  
 
11. The indirect beneficiaries of improved watershed management across the northern Jordan Valley will 
include all the region’s remaining municipalities, amounting to 756,000 people (355,320 women and 
400,680 men). Indirect benefits will include: i) the enhancement of the quality and quantity of water 
resources across the northern Jordan Valley area; ii) strengthened local governance to implement climate-
resilient ILWRM; iii) reduced rate at which water is lost from the King Abdullah Canal through evaporation; 
and iv) improved awareness on the impacts of climate changes and the adaptation options to address them.  
Interventions at the regulatory and institutional level will indirectly benefit the Jordanian population as a 
whole, amounting to ~10 million people. 
 
12. Co-benefits will accrue through the proposed project: GHG emission reductions and women’s 
empowerment.  Other sustainable development potential includes reduced losses and damages from flood 
attenuation potential associated with re-vegetated landscapes; improved health outcomes from cleaner 
water and enhanced food security; and enhanced biodiversity. In terms of gender-responsive development 
impacts, the proposed project will support a transformation in the water sector to one in which women are 
included in the decision-making for and management of water resources at the community level, including 
being involved in monitoring of water resource us and maintenance of community-level water infrastructure. 
This will allow for their specific water-related climate-change adaptation needs to be addressed. The 
project’s community-level gender-responsive approach also includes promoting gender-inclusive climate-
resilient livelihoods that will benefit female-headed households (can be implemented at home and allow for 
the strengthening of adaptive capacity). Tailored awareness-raising and outreach programmes will also 
allow women to become leaders in the promotion of climate change adaptation in their communities. 
 
13. Ultimately, the proposed project will confer climate change adaptation benefits to the country’s entire 
population. These benefits will be achieved by reducing the adverse impacts of climate change on Jordan’s 
water supplies and by providing the country’s main economic sectors and ecosystems with mechanisms 
and interventions to build long-term climate resilience. Moreover, the proposed GCF project will enable 
Jordan to serve as an example of how a transformative approach to climate change adaptation can combat 
the increasingly arid conditions resulting from climate change in the Middle East and Mediterranean 
landscapes. The proposed adaptation solution contributes directly to the GCF’s Updated Strategic Plan 
(2024-2027). This includes references to programming priorities: i) Mitigation and Adaptation: Supporting 
 
8 https://dosweb.dos.gov.jo/DataBank/Population/Population_Estimares/Municipalities.pdf

paradigm shifts across sectors, including to climate-resilient ecosystems and food systems; ii) Adaptation: 
Addressing urgent and immediate adaptation and resilience needs, for particularly vulnerable countries. 
Relevant GCF Targeted Results for 2024-2027 include: 4. Food: Support for developing countries that 
results in 190 to 280 million beneficiaries adopting low-emission climate-resilient agricultural and fisheries 
practices, securing livelihoods while reconfiguring food systems; 5. Ecosystems: Support for developing 
countries that results in 120 million to 190 million hectares of terrestrial and marine areas conserved, 
restored or brought under sustainable management; and 9. 40-70 approved proposals for adaptation 
projects, including for locally-led adaptation action.

B.  PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION 
B.1. Climate rationale and context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Context  
14. The Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan (hereafter referred to as Jordan) is a Middle Eastern country situated on the 
eastern bank of the Jordan River. The land area is divided into three main topographic regions, namely the: i) north-
western central highlands with Mediterranean forest and the Irano-Turanian steppe; ii) western low-lying subtropical 
Jordan Valley; and iii) eastern arid desert (Badia), which covers 80% of the country9. Most of Jordan’s population 
of 10.5 million people (51% male and 49% female)10 are concentrated in the north-western region of the country 
(predominantly in the Jordan Rift Valley; 1,194 people/km2), which is attributable to the area’s fertile soils, higher 
rainfall and consequently greater agricultural productivity and prospects, relative to other parts of the country.  Over 
the past six decades, the population of Jordan has increased 12-fold, driven by inter alia a historically high rate of 
endogenous population growth and an influx of refugees and immigrants as a result of crises in the region, such 
as the Syrian conflict. This rapid increase in Jordan’s population has contributed to several developmental 
challenges for the country, including: i) shortages in food, water and other natural resources; ii) reduced 
groundwater recharge and increased erosion resulting from overexploitation and degradation of ecosystems linked 
to agricultural and urban expansion; iii) limited housing and employment opportunities; and iv) overburdening of 
the education system, health services, and urban infrastructure11,12. Of these challenges, water insecurity is a 
primary challenge. Jordan is currently ranked as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world13, with only 125 
CM/yr of water available per person14. This water scarcity is a function of: i) naturally low rainfall levels, as well as 
high temperatures and evaporation rates; ii) limited availability of shared transboundary surface water resources; 
iii) an increasing population and influx of refugees from neighbouring countries exacerbating water demand; and 
iv) an increase in unsustainable agricultural and industrial development. The current water usage in Jordan already 
exceeds safe yields15 by 416 MCM/yr16, leading to the over-extraction of renewable and non-renewable 
groundwater reserves.  
 
15. The country’s economy faces considerable challenges to accommodate the increasing demand for resources from 
its growing population. Although Jordan is an upper-middle-income country and is classified as an emerging 
economy, it is among the smallest in the Middle East and North Africa (MENA) region, with a Gross Domestic 
Product (GDP) of ~US$40 billion. The largest contributors to Jordan’s GDP are the: i) services (~67%); ii) industry 
(~29%); and iii) agriculture (~4%) sectors. Because of a slowing economy and influx of refugees, Jordan has high 
unemployment levels. Labour force participation — characterised by large gender differences — is 64% for men 
and 14% for women17, while unemployment rates are 17% for men and 27% for women.  
 
Climate profile 
16. Jordan is characterised by an arid climate with hot and dry summers contrasted with short, cool winters. Average 
temperatures range from 9–22°C in the winter months (from December to March) to 26–39°C in the summer months 
(from April until November)18. The dry Badia in the east receives less than 200 mm of rainfall annually, while the 
central highlands in the northwest receive the highest levels of rainfall at 350–500 mm, mostly through intense 
winter storm events19. Increasing rainfall variability in Jordan has resulted in more frequent drought and flash flood 
events, as well as greater levels of erosion in degraded landscapes. 
 
Climate change in Jordan20

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 7 of 144 
 
17. Detailed primary and secondary climate analyses for the proposed project are presented in Annex 2: Feasibility 
Study, in Section 2 and Appendix 6, respectively. The information presented below is a summary of what is 
presented in the more detailed analyses, which should be consulted as necessary. 
 
18. Observations of historical climate trends (1950–2015) in Jordan show that the country has already experienced 
significant changes in both temperature and rainfall. Since 1950, minimum and maximum temperatures have 
increased by 0.4–2.8°C and 0.3–1.8°C, respectively21 (Figure 1). In terms of rainfall, average annual values have 
decreased by 5–20% since 1950, with the average number of rainy days decreasing by 3–10% during the same 
period22 (Figure 2). This decrease is marked by extended drought periods, as well as periods of increased rainfall 
intensity as a result of a shorter rainfall season. This rainfall decline, combined with extreme temperatures, has 
resulted in prolonged drought periods in the country. Droughts are projected to increase in frequency and severity, 
with the number of years affected by meteorological drought in a 30-year period expected to increase from seven 
(1981–2010) to 28 (2071–2100)23. 
 
 
Figure 1. Observed maximum temperature trends in Jordan.

reducing transboundary flow. Science Advances 2017; 3:e1700581.

Figure 2. Observed rainfall trends in Jordan. 
19. Changes in the standard precipitation evapotranspiration index (SPEI) were compared over time to determine if 
climate change has already impacted water availability in Jordan24. The SPEI is a good measure of drought 
severity, where a value of +3 indicates exceptionally wet months and -3 indicates exceptionally dry months. Figure 
3shows changes in SPEI in Jordan from 1954–2019. A 48-month running average is used to determine long-term 
trends instead of short-term meteorological anomalies. Although there are natural oscillations over time, from the 
late 1990s there is a strong shift towards the prevalence of drier months, with the gradual decrease in SPEI values 
indicating more extreme drought conditions in Jordan. The current SPEI trends differ from natural cyclic phases 
and are likely a consequence of climate change. This means that Jordan is currently experiencing the impacts of

=== PAGE 13 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 9 of 144 
 
climate change in water scarcity both from hydrological and agricultural droughts that result from these 
meteorological droughts. 
 
Figure 3. Observed drought trends in Jordan. 
20. As Jordan is already impacted by extreme baseline climate conditions — resulting in ecosystems and communities 
existing on the threshold of survivability — even small changes in temperature or rainfall are expected to have 
considerable impacts. By the end of the 21st century, projections indicate that Jordan will experience: i) a warmer 
climate — where, between 2070–2100, the average temperature is expected to increase by between 2.1°C 
(RCP4.5) and 4°C (RCP8.5); ii)  more heatwaves — where average maximum summer temperatures for the whole 
country are expected to exceed 42°C (RCP4.5) and 44°C (RCP8.5) (Figure 4); iii) a drier climate — where, between 
2070–2100, total annual rainfall is expected to decrease by between 15% (RCP4.5) and 21% (RCP8.5); iv) more 
droughts — where from 2040 to 2070 the average number of consecutive dry days is likely to increase by a 
maximum of 30% under both RCP4.5 and RCP8.525; and v) an increase in extreme rainfall events within a

shortened rainy season. The above temperature and rainfall projections for 2071–2100 agree with those from the

21. Current annual rainfall has decreased in recent decades and this trend is projected to increase into the future with 
some estimates showing an overall decrease of between 5–60% between now and the end of the century (Figure 
5). This is expected to lead to enhanced drought frequency and severity, as well as intensified aridity. The current 
trends and continuation of decreases in the projected rainfall are expected to only exacerbate water scarcity in the 
future. There are projected increases in the number of dry days but also in the rainfall intensity in some months. 
The mid-season rainfall is projected to be lower each week relative to the historical period (1980-2010).

22. Jordan will experience an average increase in ETp of about 70–100 mm by the year 2050, and 150 mm by the year 
210026. An increase in ETp, along with a decreasing trend in Standard Precipitation Indices (SPI) — above zero 
during the baseline period 1981–2010 (wet years); to below zero by 2085 (dry years) — combined with the projected

=== PAGE 17 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 13 of 144 
 
changes in rainfall, is likely to increase the frequency, duration and severity of drought events27 (Figure 6). The 
long-term (1990-2020) SPEI average of Jordan is between Near Normal Dry to Moderately/Severely Dry (-0.29 to 
-1.21). Drought conditions in Jordan are also expected to be intensified by reductions in soil moisture and 
streamflow. These variables are likely to decrease by 28% and 58% respectively, by 2100 under RCP 8.528. Under 
RCP8.5, the probability of years having simultaneous drought as well as average annual temperature increases of 
greater than 1 SD above the baseline is projected to increase from less than 10% to more than 90% by 210029.

Climate change impacts and vulnerabilities

=== PAGE 18 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 14 of 144 
 
 
23. Current and predicted climate change are presently and will continue to have multiple negative impacts on Jordan, 
particularly with regards to water resources, ecosystems and the agriculture sector. These impacts are summarised 
below and provided in more detail in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. 
 
Water resources 
24. Jordan’s worsening climatic conditions and the above-mentioned compounding factors have resulted in increased 
water stress in the country over the past decade, with water availability decreasing by 145 CM/yr per person 
between 2007–201730. Extreme temperatures and the ongoing drying trend in Jordan are the primary causes of 
severe water scarcity in the country, with the resultant evaporation, for example, accounting for the loss of 7,636 
MCM of water in 201731, which is 94% of the annual rainfall Jordan received that year. Between 2007 and 2017, 
the impacts of climate change contributed to water availability decreasing by 145 CM/yr per capita. Jordan 
consequently ranks as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world with only 125 CM/yr currently available 
per capita — well below the international water poverty line of 500 CM/yr32.  
 
25. Most of Jordan’s water supply (59%) is extracted from 10 renewable and two non-renewable groundwater basins, 
which are currently being over-extracted by ~169% and ~124% of safe yield, respectively33. The current rate of 
abstraction is ~190 MCM/yr higher than the estimated safe yield of ~275 MCM/yr for all renewable groundwater 
combined. For non-renewable groundwater, the rate of abstraction is ~314 MCM/yr higher than the estimated safe 
yield of ~143 MCM/yr34. Currently, water-use quotas and water-saving policies are implemented by the Ministry of 
Water and Irrigation (MWI) and its subsidiaries (such as the Jordan Valley Authority) to limit over-extraction. 
Groundwater is accessed either through springs that reach the surface or through wells. Of the country’s 861 
springs, 130 have run dry as of 201335. Concurrently, the current number of wells in Jordan exceeds 3,200, a 
number that has increased by 10% over the last decade36. The safe yield of all renewable basins combined equates 
to 275 MCM/yr — which is 190 MCM/yr less than the current abstraction — which in turn is only a fraction of 
Jordan’s current water needs (1,077 MCM/yr). On average, the water deficit from all non-renewable groundwater 
basins is 124% of the safe yield, while renewable groundwater basins show a deficit of 169%37. 
 
26. Reduced rainfall and increased evaporation from higher temperatures related to climate change have already 
resulted in observed impacts on Jordan’s surface water sources. For example, the direct input of rainfall through 
runoff into the Upper Jordan River — the primary surface water source for most of the population — has decreased 
by 10% over 50 years, from 2,138 MCM/yr in 1950 to 1,930 MCM/yr in 200038. This reduction in runoff in the Upper 
Jordan River watershed is projected to decrease by a further 23%, from an average of 375 mm/yr-1 in the 1961–
1990 period to 289 mm/yr-1 in the 2070–2099 period39. Overall, a 51% reduction in streamflow as a result of the 
combined effect of reduced runoff and increased evaporation under climate change conditions is projected to occur 
in Jordan40. 
 
27. As surface water resources become less reliable under conditions of climate change, and wet seasons become 
shorter than before, there will be a greater dependence of communities on groundwater. With ~60% of Jordan’s 
total water supply — and 73% of the municipal supply41 — already coming from groundwater, any reduction in 
groundwater recharge will have severe implications for water security in the country42. A climate change-induced

decline in annual rainfall by 12% is predicted to reduce groundwater recharge by more than 25% by 209943,44. This 
is likely to result in a reduction of renewable water supplies by 416 MCM/yr (996 to 580 MCM/yr)45.  
 
28. Given the combination of impacts from climate change, by 2022 the country’s water resources46 (both renewable 
and non-renewable) are expected to be limited to ~1,132 MCM/yr. By this time, demand is predicted to increase to 
~1,635 MCM/yr, resulting in a deficit of ~503 MCM/yr from a current baseline of 416 MCM/yr (increase of 87 
MCM)47. Without urgent climate change adaptation interventions, Jordan’s water deficit will increase by an 
additional 184 MCM to 687 MCM/yr by 204048.  
 
Ecosystems and ecosystem services 
29. Climate change is strongly affecting the functioning of ecosystems and their services in Jordan, which are already 
being impacted by numerous non-climatic threats, including: i) land loss or degradation from urbanisation and 
agriculture; ii) overgrazing; iii) unregulated fuelwood harvesting; iv) water over-extraction; v) species over-
exploitation; vi) the introduction of invasive plant and animal species; vii) mining; and viii) pollution49. These non-
climatic drivers are expected to be exacerbated by climate change, which is predicted to impact ecosystems 
through increased forest dieback, changes in community composition, desertification, habitat degradation and 
species loss50.  
 
30. Most notable in the context of Jordan is the effect climate change-induced vegetation loss is expected to have on 
water balances. While intact rangelands in Jordan experience runoff losses of 9–21 mm/yr, degraded vegetation 
increase these losses to ~20–28 mm/yr by decreasing infiltration and promoting runoff 51. Forest loss and landscape 
degradation also decrease surface roughness, resulting in increased albedo and temperatures, as well as lower 
evapotranspiration rates, altering moisture and heat fluxes between terrestrial surfaces and the atmosphere52. 
Indeed, by changing these processes, large-scale forest dieback or desertification linked to climate change is 
expected to reduce rainfall in some regions by as much as 30%53, further limiting water availability. The loss of 
vegetation cover will also reduce groundwater infiltration and soil stability. Desertification and ecosystem 
deterioration related to climate change and land-use changes result in soil degradation, leading to reduced soil 
organic carbon and impoverished soil structure54. As a result, infiltration and water retention capacity of the soil will 
be reduced, which will, in turn, lead to a decline in aquifer recharge.  In addition, bare soils resulting from ecosystem 
degradation increase the risk of flooding, particularly during extreme rainfall events, which are expected to increase 
in frequency and intensity under future climate conditions. This is because the loss of vegetation results in soil 
hardening or compaction, thereby reducing infiltration and increasing runoff55. 
 
Agriculture 
31. Agriculture accounts for 65–75% of Jordan’s total water consumption56. As a result, water scarcity poses a risk to 
current food systems and food security in the country as the diminishing water supply results in lower agricultural 
yields and an increase in the need to import food. Rainfed agriculture — including locally valuable crops such as 
barley and wheat — is the most climate-vulnerable form of cultivation in Jordan57. Under future climate change, a

reduction in precipitation of 20% (by 2055 under RCP4.5) will result in a decline in expected yield for rainfed barley 
by 35%, and for wheat by 21%58. The total yield for irrigated agriculture is expected to decrease by 5% by 205059. 
In addition to the impacts on crop agriculture, climate change will have a considerable impact on livestock 
production because of reduced cultivated fodder and rangeland vegetation. Declines in barley yields and the fertility 
of pasturelands as a result of climate change have already led to a 77% shortage in livestock feed60. During the 
severe drought of 1998–2012, the 70% reduction in rainfall resulted in a decline in cereal yields to only 0.6% of the 
country’s needs and non-cereal crop production decreased by 23%, while meat and dairy production declined by 
40%61.  Economic losses from inadequate water supply are estimated to cost Jordan ~0.34% of its GDP each 
year62. Elevated temperatures and reduced rainfall will also increase the demand for water for irrigation of crops 
by 14% and 28% for 2030 and 2050 respectively, placing additional stress on Jordan’s water supply63. 
 
32. Under future climate change conditions, increasingly variable and decreasing rainfall, as well as more severe 
droughts, will not only limit the optimal time available for planting crops, but also the types of crops that can be 
grown. For example, a reduction in precipitation of 20% (by 2055 under RCP4.5) will result in a decline in expected 
yield for rainfed barley by 35%, and for wheat by 21%64. By 2050, the combined effect of a 20% reduction in rainfall 
and an increase in temperature by 4°C is predicted to decrease barley production by 50%65. An increase in 
temperature is also predicted to lower the yields of irrigated crops. At 1°C above current values, irrigated vegetable 
yields in Jordan are expected to decrease by 5%. This decrease is predicted to double to 10% by 2050 with an 
increase in temperature of 2°C. Elevated temperatures and reduced rainfall will also increase the demand for water 
for irrigation of crops by 14% and 28% for 2030 and 2050 respectively, placing additional stress on Jordan’s water 
supply66. This stress will be compounded by reductions in overall water availability. Therefore, there is a need to 
not only strengthen the supply of water resources in Jordan, but also transition food systems from those reliant on 
climate-vulnerable crops like barely to those which are more diverse and resilient to climate change, such as quinoa 
and pearl millet, which contribute to food security under climate change conditions. 
 
Vulnerability of local populations 
33. Vulnerability in Jordan is largely driven by income levels and poverty rates. Unemployment levels67 in Jordan are 
high, ranking 17th highest in the world in 2019 with ~14.9% of the total labour force unemployed68. High 
unemployment is attributable to a slowing economy and the refugee influx, particularly into urban centres69. 
Approximately 90% of registered Syrian refugees in Jordan that live outside of refugee camps have settled in the 
governorates of Amman, Mafraq, Irbid, and Zarqa70. Since the beginning of the Syrian conflicts in 2011, these 
governorates — as well as the small northern governorate of Jerash — have experienced the highest growth in the 
unemployment rate nationally71. The rate in these areas has increased by ~3% since 2011, compared with a ~1% 
decrease elsewhere in Jordan, indicating how Syrian refugees compete with Jordanians in the labour market72.  
 
34. Women and youths have the highest respective unemployment rates, with unemployed women particularly 
reflecting the greatest proportion of unemployed people in Jordan73. Employment amongst women is extremely low

— only ~13% of women over 15 years of age are included in the paid labour force74. In the Global Gender Gap 
Index of 2015, which provides information on labour market participation, Jordan ranked 140th out of 145 analysed 
countries75. Youth unemployment is also high in Jordan, particularly among those holding university degrees76. The 
male-female divide in labour market participation is further evident in women’s low levels of employment, despite 
elevated levels of education77. While more than 75% of unemployed men in Jordan hold a high school diploma at 
most, the equivalent proportion of unemployed women hold at least a high school diploma, and many also hold 
tertiary qualifications78. Unemployment and insufficient dependable income intersect and contribute to poverty in 
Jordan, where the proportion of the population below the poverty line79 was ~14% in 2010, half of whom were 
‘working poor’80,81,82. Like unemployment, poverty in Jordan is concentrated in the governorates that have 
historically hosted the largest numbers of refugees — with the highest number of poor households occurring in the 
urban areas of the governorates of Amman, Irbid, Zarqa and Mafraq83. Across the project’s sites of Yarmouk, King 
Talal Dam and Kafrain, poverty rates are 15%, 18% and 13%, respectively. Approximately 13% of the total 
population lives in rural areas, where poverty is more prevalent than in urban areas and living conditions are difficult. 
As an arid country with less than 5% of land available for farming and little to no rainfall, prospects of self-
sustainability through farming are limited. The most vulnerable groups include large rural households with low 
literacy and education levels, women-headed households, households 12% with sick or elderly people, and 
households that do not own land or have very little land. 
 
35. The project target area is located within the Jordan Valley — the primary agricultural region of the country. The 
majority of local communities in the region depend on agriculture for their livelihoods, 25% of whom live below the 
poverty line84. This dependence on agriculture combined with local poverty rates and low adaptative capacity, make 
these communities and the food systems (barley- and wheat-based) they rely on for food security highly vulnerable 
to the impacts of climate change, particularly reductions in water resources. As agricultural activities are vulnerable 
to temperature fluctuations, increased exposure to droughts and greater precipitation variability, crop productivity 
in the Jordan Valley is directly affected by climate change. Under future climate conditions, decreased agricultural 
yields will continue to reduce food security and the income potential for local communities who already face high 
poverty rates85 without a transition to climate-resilient food systems.  
 
36. Future climate change will additionally present multiple health risks to Jordan’s most vulnerable inhabitants, 
including children, women and refugees. Climate change impacts on health include an increase in the incidence of 
heat stress-related illnesses and exposure to diseases from reduced water quality86. Also, the health of the 
population is adversely impacted by reduced food security related to lower agricultural yields87. As yields decline, 
the total quantity of food available in the country decreases, causing food prices to fluctuate. Consequently, higher 
prices lead to a reduction in the diversity of food that households can afford, causing malnutrition88. 
 
Institutional context 
37. Within Jordan, institutions of relevance to integrated landscape and water resources management include inter alia 
the: i) Ministry of Environment (MoEnv) which is responsible for the country’s legal and policy framework relating 
to environmental issues; and ii) Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI), which is responsible for overseeing the 
water sector, water supply and wastewater system. The MWI’s mandate includes the formulation of national water

functions of all Jordan’s water and sewage systems for both municipal and industrial users; and ii) Jordan Valley

Organisational challenges 
38. For the water sector and MWI, there is a growing need for a more consolidated, harmonized and coordinated water 
management organization, backed by a strong legal/regulatory framework especially when the IWRM approach, 
supported by key management instruments, is being adopted and reinforced to optimize water resource use. A 
harmonized national structure needs to be empowered to implement operational plans for water resourcing, 
distribution and delivery systems; mobilize public/private capital for expansion and improvement of infrastructure; 
sustainably recover operating and maintenance costs; protect the quality of water resources and water-dependent 
ecosystems; and protect the rights of consumers. The national water sector would require a reworked governance 
strategy and institutional framework that rationalizes, consolidates and reorganizes the core governance functions 
for: (i) national policy and planning; (ii) management of operations and quality assurance; (iii) delivery of sustainable 
sector services; (iv) regulatory and normative functions; and (v) sector coordination and client interface. This in turn 
requires a revised organizational structure and regulatory framework that factors in the new realities facing the 
sector91. 
 
Related projects/interventions 
39. Considerable baseline investments are being made through public expenditure and donor-funded initiatives to 
increase the resilience of Jordan’s vulnerable communities to the impacts of climate change. Lessons learned and 
best practices from these investments have been incorporated into the design of the proposed project to replicate 
successful techniques and ensure that there is complementarity between the project and existing actions.  
 
40. During the development of JILMI’s Funding Proposal package, the Funding Proposal for another project in the 
Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan was approved for funding by the GCF board. The project, titled “Building resilience 
to cope with climate change through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector (BRCCJ)” or FP15592, 
will be executed jointly by the Food and Agriculture Organisation (FAO), United Nations Development Programme 
(UNDP), Ministry of Environment (MoEnv) and Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI). BRCCJ’s goal is to increase 
the climate resilience of water management systems and the farming community in four governates located in the 
Dead Sea Basin; Karak, Madaba, Talifah and Ma’an. To achieve this the project has been designed to address the 
lack of infrastructure, limited capacity of rural farming households and weak institutional capacity through three 
components. These include: Component 1 – Climate resilient water systems for enhanced water security; 
Component 2 – Climate change resilience for enhanced livelihoods and food security; and Component 3 – Scaling 
up climate adaptation into policy and across actors (institutions, private sector and civil society). While the BRCCJ 
project focusses on addressing climate change impacts in the agricultural sector through improved water use 
efficiency at the smallholder level, the proposed project will use a novel broader, integrated approach at the 
landscape level to address the climate change impacts of rising temperatures, droughts and floods not only on 
agriculture but on communities as a whole. Table 1 provides a summary of the key similarities and differences, 
while Table 2 provides a summary of complementarities between the two projects. A more detailed comparison is 
provided in Annex 29: Analysis of similarities, differences and complementarities between JILMI and BRCCJ 
(FP155). One approved, JILMI’s Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entities (EEs) will coordinate with their 
BRCCJ counterparts to ensure that the two projects work closely together during implementation to foster alignment 
and a collective contribution towards increasing climate-resilient sustainable development in Jordan.

technologies and awareness of climate adaptive 
technologies and practices and Government capacity in 
planning, information dissemination and policy development 
THEN vulnerable communities will be able to increase their 
resilience and enhance their livelihoods and improve their 
health, well-being and food security BECAUSE of their 
adoption of new practices and government 
Approach 
The objective is to reduce the negative impacts of climate 
change on water resources in Jordan through a landscape-
level approach. The proposed approach includes: i) improved 
ecosystem management; ii) managed aquifer recharge; iii) 
rainwater harvesting; iv) evaporation reduction; v) improved 
agricultural practices; and vi) strengthened governance. The 
approach will be implemented through four components: 
Component 1: Climate change adaptation governance in 
Jordan 
Component 2: ILWRM under conditions of climate change 
Component 3: Improved protection, supply and use of water 
resources under conditions of climate change 
Component 4: Knowledge management and awareness on 
climate change adaptation 
The objective is to increase the resilience to climate change 
of water management systems and the farming community. 
The project is designed to deal with a lack of infrastructure, 
limited capacity of rural farming households and weak 
institutional capacity through three components:  
Component 1: Climate resilient water systems for enhanced 
water security 
Component 2: Climate change resilience for enhanced 
livelihoods and food security 
Component 3: Scaling-up climate adaptation into policy and 
across actors 
Outcomes / 
results 
Outcome 1: Increased public budgets for adaptation 
Outcome 2: Climate-resilient ILWRM practiced in three Jordan 
Valley watersheds 
Outcome 3:  Enhanced water security for households in target 
areas 
Outcome 4: Household behavioural change achieved through 
improved information and outreach 
Expected result 1: Enhanced water availability to address 
climate change risks 
Expected result 2: Enhanced capacity of people to deal with 
climate change risks 
Expected result 3: Gender resilience tools and practices to 
adapt to climate change are mainstreamed into national 
policy, educational, administrative and social frameworks. 
Results 
areas/Fund-
level impacts 
A1.0 Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the 
most vulnerable people, communities and regions 
A2.0 Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food 
and water security 
A4.0 Improved resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem 
services

and water security

Increasing the resilience of both 
displaced persons and host communities 
to climate change-related water 
challenges in Jordan and Lebanon 
(2021–2025; AF; US$13,968,139) 
This project aims to increase the response to water-related climate change vulnerability in Jordan and 
Lebanon within the context of the ongoing Syrian crisis. The project specifically focusses on addressing 
the needs of both displaced peoples (DPs) from Syria and host communities in the target countries, 
especially women and the youth. For Jordan, the project is targeted within the governorates of Irbid and 
Mafraq; the former which also includes the target area for the JILMI project. The AF project is 
complementary to the GCF JILMI project in several ways. Urban master plans to increase institutional 
capacity to manage climate change and water scarcity under the AF project aligns with Outcome 1 of the 
JILMI project that aims to mainstream ILWRM at the national and sub-national level. Unlike the JILMI 
project, this capacity building is focussed on urban settings and are at a municipal level only. Awareness 
raising for DPs and host communities on climate change adaptation measures will complement the 
proposed project’s adaptation outreach programme (Activity 4.1). Awareness raising in the AF project will

education on climate change and adaptation. Activities 2.3 and 3.3 of the JILMI project will be

the AF projects, including rooftop rainwater harvesting, water efficient irrigation and permaculture. Although

Healthy Ecosystems for Rangeland 
Development (HERD): Sustainable 
Rangeland Management Strategies and 
Practices (2017–2021; GEF, 
US$15,879,968)94 
The HERD project was developed with the objective of strengthening the restoration and sustainable 
management of pastoral rangelands in Egypt and Jordan to enable the continued provision of ecosystem 
services and protection of biodiversity. The proposed project will complement HERD activities within 
Jordan through: i) strengthened governance and capacity for integrated landscape and management 
(ILWRM) under Activity 1.1; and ii) outreach programmes to raise awareness within local communities of 
ILWRM under Output 4. The proposed project will additionally adopt best practices for rangeland 
restoration and community-based management implemented by HERD. As HERD is implemented by 
UNEP (GCF AE) and executed by IUCN (GCF EE), potential for complementarity and avoidance of 
duplication has been maximised during design and will be a key focus during implementation. 
Jordan Water Management Initiative 
(WMI; 2016–2021; USAID, 
US$33,247,544)95 
The objective of the WMI project is to address Jordan’s growing water scarcity by increasing the 
accountability and sustainability of water sector management and governance. This will be accomplished 
by supporting and strengthening the Jordanian government’s policy development and implementation, as 
well as its capacity-building efforts. The proposed project’s strengthened governance and capacity for 
climate-resilient ILWRM (Outcome 1) will complement the WMI project’s focus on water sector 
management and governance. 
Increasing the resilience of poor and 
vulnerable communities to climate 
change (2016–2020; Adaptation Fund 
(AF), ~US$9,226,000)96 
Implemented by Jordan’s Ministry of Planning and International Cooperation (MOPIC), the overall objective 
of this project is to increase the adaptive capacity of Jordan’s agricultural sector to climate change-induced 
water shortages and food security stresses. The proposed project will build on existing activities aimed at 
reinforcing the concept of a participatory water and agricultural development and management approach 
that involves users, planners and policymakers at all levels (Output 1.1). Lessons learned regarding the 
use of non-conventional water sources have been used to inform the design of the proposed project, with 
some technologies being replicated. For example, Output 2.1 of the JILMI project will build on the AF 
project’s use of water harvesting technologies through improved watershed management. The proposed 
project will additionally replicate and assist in expanding the use of permaculture techniques which address 
challenges relating to soil and irrigation water quality (Activity 2.3). An important gap in the AF project is 
that ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) interventions were not implemented for the improvement of water 
resources and increasing climate resilience. The proposed project’s climate-resilient ILWRM approach will 
fill this gap, therefore providing an alternative to conventional adaptation solutions. 
Jordan Watershed Restoration Project 
(2015–2020; USDA, ~US$180,000)97 
Implemented by the International Centre for Agricultural Research in the Dry Areas (ICARDA), the project 
objective is to create vegetation islands within the Badia by planting seedlings in Vallerani98 structures.  
This will reduce the amount of rainwater lost as overland runoff and enable a 50% increase in groundwater 
recharge. Moreover, the vegetation islands are expected to enhance natural revegetation, improve 
ecosystem viability and increase the productivity of rangelands for livestock fodder. The proposed project 
will replicate the successful interventions of this ongoing project in its target watersheds (Output 2.1). 
Additionally, the proposed project will build on the work of the Jordan Watershed Restoration Project 
through the use of watershed restoration and management as an adaptation approach. 
Sustainable use of Ecosystem Services 
in Jordan — Energy and Climate Fund 
(EKF -ESS; 2014–2019; GIZ, 
~US$5,420,000)99 
The project’s objective is to promote national policy-making processes that account for biodiversity and 
ecosystem services as well as the importance of climate change adaptation and ensure they become a 
permanent focus within Jordanian policies. The proposed project will complement and build on the work of 
the EKF-ESS by promoting climate-resilient ILWRM mainstreaming into relevant national and local policies 
(Activity 1.2), including a focus on climate change. Specifically, the ‘Ecosystem Service Opportunities’ 
Framework will be used and expanded upon to find appropriate instruments for ecosystem service 
protection based on location-specific opportunities. In addition, the proposed project will highlight the link 
between ecosystem services and adaptation through its climate-resilient ILWRM approach, implementing 
on-the-ground activities that will complement policy changes made by EKF-ESS (Activity 2.2). 
Managing Water Resources in Jordan 
(2006–2018; GIZ)100 
The project’s objective was to enable national water institutions to better respond to the mounting pressure 
of the country’s limited water resources by providing strategic, technical and expert support. The project 
has led to an improvement in the management capabilities of the MWI, the establishment of decentralised, 
semi-autonomous water utilities with improved capability to provide a reliable source of water, Water User 
Associations within the agricultural sector and a water resource modelling unit. The proposed project will 
build on this project by promoting integrated watershed management and establishing Watershed 
Management Committees (WMCs) that will contribute to providing alternative sources of water to local 
communities.

MENARID: Mainstreaming Sustainable 
Land and Water Management Practices 
(2009–2015; GEF, ~US$29,740,000)101 
This project formed part of the Middle East and North Africa Regional Program for Promoting Integrated 
Sustainable Land Development (MENARID). The main objective of the project was to reduce the causes 
and negative impacts of land degradation and promote the incorporation of an ecosystem-based approach 
into public-supported productive and poverty-reduction activities. The proposed project will draw on this 
project’s information management systems, which were developed to monitor land resources and link land 
changes to natural processes, human interventions and project interventions when developing restoration 
protocols. Specifically, these information management systems will assist the JILMI project by providing a 
decision-support tool on SLM for governmental stakeholders (Activity 1.1). Best practices from Project 
Demonstration Agro-Ecosystems will be used to inform the promotion of climate-resilient livelihood options 
under Activity 2.3 of the JILMI project. the implementation of the proposed project’s participatory integrated 
watershed management plans will complement and draw on this project’s participatory action plans that 
focus on sustainable land and water management. In addition, the proposed project will build on the 
institutional structures and capacities enhanced through this project to promote the awareness of 
sustainable land and water management practices within local communities. With MoEnv (GCF EE) being 
one of the executing agencies of MENARID, potential for complementarity and avoidance of duplication 
has been maximised during design and will be a key focus during implementation. 
Securing Rights and Restoring Lands for 
Improved Livelihoods (2011–2013; 
EU)102 
The overall objective of this global project was to reduce poverty and promote the sustainable management 
and restoration of ecosystems in drylands and rangelands. Specifically, the objective of the project was 
the conservation, restoration and sustainable management of ecosystem services as the basis for 
improved livelihoods. The three-year Jordan component of the project built the capacity of local 
communities to manage and restore their ecosystems and improve livelihoods. The proposed project will 
draw on best practices and lessons learned from this project’s ecosystem services valuation and rural 
community income generation options to inform participatory climate-resilient ILM plans as well as climate-
resilient livelihood and agricultural practices (Activity 2.3). 
 
Land-management rights, including women’s land rights, were negotiated by participating communities 
over state-held lands. The project also promoted the revival of customary institutions such as Hima 
practices for rangeland governance and management. Moreover, the Jordanian National Rangeland 
Strategy was revised to emphasise community-based approaches. The proposed project will build on these 
activities by drafting further amendments to Jordan’s land-management policy, planning and legal 
frameworks (Activity 1.2). 
Integrated Ecosystem and Natural 
Resource Management in the Jordan 
Rift Valley (2007–2013; GEF, ~US$12.7 
million)103 
The objective of this project was to secure the ecological integrity of the Jordan Rift Valley as a globally 
important ecological corridor and migratory pathway for birds. Interventions included: i) implementing 
generally accepted principles of integrated ecosystem management relating to land use in the Jordan 
Valley; and ii) establishing a network of well-managed protected areas (PAs) and special conservation 
areas (SCAs) that meet ecological, social and economic needs. The proposed project will draw from best 
practices and lessons learned about integrated ecosystem management and the related socio-economic 
benefits during implementation. In addition, the proposed project will complement the efforts of the GEF 
project by using an ILWRM approach to enhance the conservation of ecosystems and their services to 
local communities to support adaptation to climate change. Specific complementarities include: i) increase 
the mandate for landscape management of major development agencies, including the JVA; ii) increasing 
awareness amongst beneficiary communities on sustainable ecosystem management and resilient 
livelihood practices; and iii) expanding options for alternative and resilient livelihoods for vulnerable 
communities. Although there is some potential for overlap between the two projects, the JILMI project will 
differ in that it has a more specific focus on climate change and adaptation. Socio-economic activities and 
livelihoods introduced by the JILMI project will also be different but complementary to this GEF project. 
Additionally, shared implementing and technical partners — including RSCN and JVA — will ensure there 
is minimal duplication and maximum complementarity between the projects.   
Community-based Optimisation of the 
Management of Scarce Water 
Resources in Agriculture in Central-West 
Asia and North Africa Project (CWANA; 
2003–2013; IFAD, AFESD, OPEC Fund 
for International Development and

This regional project consisted of two phases, with the first phase taking place from 2003–2008 and the

management options to increase water productivity and optimise water use within three main agro-systems 
in the target regions, namely irrigated, rainfed and Badia systems. Outputs 2 and 3 of the proposed project 
will build on best practices regarding rainwater harvesting and rangeland rehabilitation developed by 
CWANA’s two-phased on-the-ground interventions.

B.2. (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram) 
Problem statement 
42. Jordan is currently experiencing extreme climatic conditions, which contributes to its ranking as the fifth most water-
stressed country in the world. Climate change is causing an increase in temperatures and a decrease in rainfall, 
compounding the ongoing drying trend across the country. These changes are already having and will continue to 
have a considerable impact on local communities, particularly through several pronounced effects on water 
resources. First, the drying trend resulting from climate change is reducing the country’s naturally limited and over-
exploited groundwater and surface water sources. The reduction in water resources under future climate conditions 
will exacerbate existing challenges related to water quantity and quality. Second, by increasing the severity of 
already extreme climatic conditions, climate change will exacerbate the degradation and desertification of Jordan’s 
ecosystems. Many of the country’s ecosystems are already threatened and fragmented by inter alia: i) the 
unregulated collection of fuelwood and non-timber forest products (NTFPs); ii) overgrazing; iii) pollution; iv) invasive 
animal and plant species; iv) illegal hunting; and v) urban and agricultural expansion. Under future climate 
conditions, these pressures will increase and continue to inhibit the supply of numerous ecosystem services (e.g., 
regulation of water flow and quantity) on which many communities rely for livelihoods, as well as for buffering the 
impacts of climate change. Third, a reduction in water resources will decrease the productivity of agriculture and 
other water-dependent livelihoods, which are already pressured by extreme baseline temperatures and water 
scarcity. This includes pastoralism as the extent of rangelands shrink and become less able to supply fodder for 
the rearing of livestock. Despite several past and ongoing interventions being implemented in Jordan to address 
the impacts of climate change on water resources, these have lacked an integrated approach, limiting their 
effectiveness at the landscape level. Furthermore, limited focus has been placed on the potential contribution of 
EbA to improve water resources under climate change conditions, and how, as part of an integrated approach, it 
can result in multiple co-benefits, which will further strengthen the climate-resilience of vulnerable communities.  
Problem tree and impact pathway analysis diagrams for the proposed project are presented in Section 5 of Annex 
2: Feasibility Study.  These diagrams map out the relationships between the causes (climatic and non-climatic) and 
impacts relevant to the project’s preferred solution. 
 
Target communities 
43. The proposed project will target local communities and small-scale farmers, including women and female-headed 
households, who are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change on water resources, which continue 
to reduce water, food and livelihood security in these communities. This increased vulnerability will be considerable 
in the Jordan Valley, where agriculture is the primary source of employment. Additionally, their resilience to these 
impacts is limited because of their inflexibility in cropping and irrigation systems. Specifically, mixed farm systems 
are common in the project area and comprise the least financially secure category of smallholder tenure as well as 
being under rental or sharecropping agreements. These systems are allocated a water quota of 550 CM/ha/yr, with 
drip-irrigation being the most commonly used method of water use for crops. The average: i) revenue is 
US$1,050/ha/yr; ii) farm size is 2 ha; and iii) number of family workers is 7 per farm. 
 
Preferred adaptation solution  
44. For Jordan to adapt to the impacts of climate change and strengthen the climate resilience of vulnerable 
communities, it is necessary to secure the provision of water resources. This is achieved by shifting from a 
compartmentalised, sector-specific approach to landscape and water resource management to one that is 
integrated, participatory and adaptation-focused, and that contributes to gender-responsive climate-resilient 
sustainable development in the country. An integrated landscape and water resources management (ILWRM) 
approach in Jordan should include a strong focus on a combination of: i) improved ecosystem management; ii) 
interventions to improve groundwater recharge; iii) rainwater harvesting; iv) evaporation reduction; v) strengthened 
governance on water resources in the Jordan Valley; and vi) considers gender-related water needs and the unique 
challenges faced by women in terms of water use and access105. The ILWRM approach to be implemented under 
the proposed project will incorporate these practices to enhance the climate resilience of water resources. 
Consequently, the productivity of agricultural and natural resource-based livelihoods (including those implemented 
predominantly by women) under climate change conditions — as well as domestic water supplies and ultimately 
the resilience of vulnerable communities — will be enhanced. The adoption of an ILWRM approach is most effective 
when supported by developing an enabling environment for climate-resilient ILWRM at both the governmental and 
non-governmental levels. The proposed adaptation solution contributes directly to the GCF’s Updated Strategic 
Plan (2024-2027). This includes references to programming priorities: i) Mitigation and Adaptation: Supporting 
paradigm shifts across sectors, including to climate-resilient ecosystems and food systems; ii) Adaptation: 
Addressing urgent and immediate adaptation and resilience needs, for particularly vulnerable countries. Relevant 
GCF Targeted Results for 2024-2027 include: 4. Food: Support for developing countries that results in 190 to 280 
 
105 As identified and confirmed through consultations with key actors and communities (see Annex 6).

=== PAGE 28 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 24 of 144 
 
million beneficiaries adopting low-emission climate-resilient agricultural and fisheries practices, securing livelihoods 
while reconfiguring food systems; 5. Ecosystems: Support for developing countries that results in 120 million to 190 
million hectares of terrestrial and marine areas conserved, restored or brought under sustainable management; 
and 9. 40-70 approved proposals for adaptation projects, including for locally-led adaptation action. The preferred 
adaptation solution is expanded on in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study, in which further details on the causal 
pathways and links to the project’s objectives are provided. 
 
Barriers to the adaptation solution  
45. Barrier 1: Insufficient institutional and technical coordination capacity, as well as limited mandates of state and non-
state actors for climate change adaptation, including climate-resilient ILWRM106. This is particularly relevant for the 
MoEnv and its subsidiaries — including the JVA — as well as the MWI and Ministry of Local Administration (MLA). 
Technical and institutional capacity shortfalls are the result of inter alia: i) limited coordination between and within 
ministries and subsidiaries, leading to conflicting goals or duplication of efforts; ii) the existence of a culture of 
competition for financial resources — particularly a limited national budget — between ministries; and iii) insufficient 
clarification of responsibility for project completion. Limitations in state and non-state actors’ mandates are the 
result of insufficient integration of, and gaps in, relevant policy and legislature focusing on climate change 
adaptation. While the need for climate change adaptation has been acknowledged by the Jordanian government, 
institutional and technical capacity within government institutions for adaptation planning, implementation and 
enforcement remains insufficient. 
 
46. In the water sector, legislation surrounding the management of water resources and associated regulations provide 
little support in facilitating adaptation action related to groundwater management107. For example, the mandate of 
the JVA108 to effectively implement watershed conservation is currently limited, as most of the factors influencing 
water supply in the valley — including surface runoff and soil erosion — occur in areas outside of the jurisdiction of 
the JVA109. The JVA also faces a wide range of other challenges, including disregard for the water-saving policies 
of the Authority’s water supply system by farmers. For example, water use quotas to prevent the over-exploitation 
of limited water supplies are assigned but largely ignored110. Other examples of misuse and abuse of the JVA’s 
water supply system include inter alia: i) deliberate damage to water meters; ii) unauthorised adjustment of valves, 
flow-limiting devices and pressure regulators; and iii) improper or unlawful conduct such as providing unmerited 
benefits to relatives and associates, or acceptance of monetary donations for personal or political gain111. As a 
result of these practices, several adverse socioeconomic impacts occur, including increased maintenance costs, 
loss of public funds, low agricultural production, as well as additional social costs as a result of social instability in 
farming communities112. The loss of income from and expenditure required to address these impacts makes it 
difficult for the JVA to effectively implement adaptation interventions in the Jordan Valley, further increasing the 
vulnerability of local communities.  
 
47. Cross-sectoral coordination and dialogue related to adaptation and watershed management are also insufficient, 
with limited emphasis on the interdependence between sectors such as water, energy and food. The lack of unified 
guiding principles at policy-level is resulting in fragmented implementation of projects relating to climate change, 
reducing their potential impact. Cross-sectoral coordination and dialogue are also inhibited by informal activities 
which are outside of the control of state actors, such as the drilling of illegal wells and the unsustainable use of 
water for agricultural purposes. The economic benefits, rewards and privileges related to these activities have 
contributed to differing opinions across sectors in terms of the most appropriate policy decisions and the allocation 
of resources, in addition to interference with national reforms related to adaptation. As a result, improved 
management and technical solutions related to the adaptation of water and other sectors are alone not enough for 
a coordinated response to adaptation in Jordan — with reforms towards an inclusive and coordinated approach to 
adaptation in Jordan therefore also required113.

=== PAGE 29 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 25 of 144 
 
48. Barrier 2: Limited availability of and access to climate change adaptation information and data. The constrained 
access of decision-makers to relevant tools, knowledge and advice to make particularly tough decisions related to 
climate change is one of the most considerable barriers to adaptation in Jordan. While there have been 
advancements in recent years, such as the establishment of a Drought Management Unit by MWI and NARC to 
provide information on drought management, including adaptation interventions, availability of and access to 
climate change adaptation information and data remains constrained. Increasing the resilience of communities in 
the country is currently inhibited by the insufficient availability of technological information on adaptation and 
sustainable development in general114. The current gaps in information impede the ability of decision-makers to 
effectively integrate climate change adaptation into development strategies and planning, particularly in the water 
and agriculture sectors.  
 
49. Barrier 3: Insufficient awareness by politicians, community leaders, decision-makers and the public of the impacts 
of climate change and appropriate adaptation responses. This relates to the limited awareness of policymakers, 
main institutions and the public of the biophysical, humanitarian, economic and social costs of climate change, as 
well as the benefits of adaptation actions. While awareness of climate change of politicians within Jordan’s 
government is increasing, the perception that poverty and unemployment are the primary challenges to meeting 
the country’s Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) remains115. Although recent steps, such as the 
establishment of a national body in response to recent floods, have been taken, these remain reactive and political 
leadership towards the improved management of water resources under climate change conditions is lacking. At 
the public level, awareness on water scarcity, drought and climate change adaptation is moderate and limited 
particularly in the water sector116. 
 
50. Barrier 4: Insufficient financial capacity for climate change adaptation. No formal mechanism for the integration of 
financial requirements for climate change adaptation into national budgets is current available. A considerable 
funding gap exists between the country’s adaptation goals and its public finances. Effective climate change 
adaptation in Jordan is challenging and remains highly conditional on the availability of financing117. While the 
country has acknowledged the need to commit funds to climate change adaptation and mitigation, there is no formal 
mechanism for the integration of these requirements into the national budget, which along with the current deficit 
in domestic funding impede the implementation of necessary activities118,119. This is compounded by a lack of 
access to finance for rural communities and local groups to support climate change adaptation at the local and 
household levels120. 
 
51. Barrier 5: Limited awareness amongst decision-makers (including MWI and MoA) and farmers of the financial and 
economic effectiveness of dryland-adapted water management, including the management of green water, in 
Jordan as opposed to unsustainable abstraction of groundwater. While considerable work has been done towards 
the improvement, adaptation and integration of dryland-adapted water management (such as the management of 
green water, rainwater harvesting and other agro-ecological approaches for conserving soil moisture) into 
landscape management practices — particularly within the agricultural sector — its implementation in Jordan has 
been limited121. This is compounded by a lack of knowledge on the relationship between land degradation and 
dryland-adapted water management. Currently, local actors (such as WUAs in the agriculture sector, Development 
Advisory Committees and agricultural cooperatives) are only experimenting with dryland-adapted water 
management approaches such as rainwater harvesting through grants provided by international organisations and 
non-government organisations (NGOs)122. As a result, access to dryland-adapted water management technologies 
is restricted, limiting their uptake123. Systemic challenges are also preventing the expansion of the dryland-adapted
