water management sector in Jordan. For example, subsidies for groundwater abstraction are causing 
overexploitation of the resource because of the perception that short-term benefits of this practice are greater than 
the long-term costs124.  
 
52. Barrier 6:  Gender inequality. The socio-economic situation for women in Jordan is categorized by high 
unemployment rates, economic inequality, political marginalization of youth and women, and a mismatch between 
educational skills and skills sought by the labor market. Inequalities remain in participation in economic and political 
spheres, as well as in legal rights related to marriage, inheritance, divorce and employment. There are indications 
that, within society, boys are preferred to girls and decision-making within the home is largely in the hands of 
husbands and male family members. Structural and systemic gender inequality is a major obstacle impeding 
women’s empowerment in Jordan, preventing women from exercising their right to lead a full life in both public and 
private spheres.  Men control access to resources in areas where they own the key productive assets such as land, 
livestock and medium to large businesses. The capacity of women to participate in and implement climate change 
adaptation activities is also reduced by a lack of access to finance, along with their high illiteracy, unemployment 
and poverty rates in Jordan.  
 
Project approach to overcoming barriers to adaptation  
53. The proposed project will overcome the abovementioned barriers and consequently enhance the climate resilience 
of Jordan’s water sector through four interconnected outputs, described in detail in Section B.3. Error! Reference 
source not found. articulates which project activities and sub-activities have been designed specifically to address 
the described barriers. The project’s theory of change diagram is presented in Figure 7 below.  
 
54. Barrier 1 will be addressed by increasing the institutional and technical capacity of relevant actors (for example, 
MoEnv, MoA, JVA, and MLA, and other major institutions or bodies) in a  gender-responsive manner to plan and 
implement climate-resilient ILWRM. Capacity-building interventions will include developing and implementing a 
gender-inclusive training programme for staff members and assisting them to develop gender-responsive 
participatory climate-resilient watershed management plans for the target watersheds in the Jordan Valley. This 
will be complemented by building institutional capacity at the local level. The mandate of these institutions for 
climate-resilient ILWRM in the Jordan Valley will be strengthened via the integration of ILWRM into Jordan’s land 
policy, planning and legal frameworks. This will include defining roles, responsibilities and jurisdictions at the 
institutional level, as well as mainstreaming adaptation into national budget lines, which additionally contributes 
addressing Barrier 4.  
 
55. Barrier 1 will additionally be overcome through the establishment of gender-inclusive WMCs within the Jordan 
Valley to ensure collaboration and dialogue between all stakeholders (including local communities) involved in 
watershed management. Members of these WMCs will receive gender-responsive technical training, enabling them 
to collaborate directly with the local governmental institutions and municipalities. This will be supported by the 
development and implementation of participatory climate-resilient integrated watershed management (IWM) plans. 
The plans will define the roles and responsibilities of stakeholders and provide a coordinated direction on all future 
interventions within the watersheds. These plans will be complemented by the establishment of a Water Fund to 
strengthen and maintain the restoration of upstream ecosystems to ensure the sustained supply of services under 
climate change conditions — most notably the supply of water to downstream users (more information is presented 
under the description of Activity 2.1.5 in Section B.3. and Section 8.5 of the Feasibility Study). The Water Fund will 
serve as an additional platform through which the collaborative management of watersheds and water resources 
takes place, ensuring that all stakeholders play a role and benefit. Moreover, the Water Fund will be a mechanism 
through which finances are channelled for the maintenance and upscaling of upstream ecosystem management 
interventions to attenuate water resource supplies, as part of an EbA approach, which will contribute to addressing 
Barrier 4. 
 
56. Capacity-building activities will be supported by the development of knowledge-management tools and the 
establishment of a national climate change adaptation knowledge repository. As a result, access to best practices 
and lessons learned on adaptation will be increased, thereby addressing Barrier 2. This will facilitate both the 
planning and implementation of adaptation solutions, including EbA and ILWRM.  
 
57. To improve the awareness of decision-makers and the public of the impacts of climate change and appropriate 
adaptation responses (Barrier 3), a gender-inclusive adaptation outreach programme will be implemented under 
the proposed project. This will include both national and site-specific awareness-raising activities and will be 
 
124 Sixt GN, Klerkx L & Griffin TS. 2018. Transitions in water harvesting practices in Jordan’s rainfed agricultural systems: 
Systemic problems and blocking mechanisms in an emerging technological innovation system. Environmental Science & 
Policy, 84: 235–249.

=== PAGE 31 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 27 of 144 
 
championed by a Jordanian public figure to stimulate interest and support from women, men and youth. At the 
policymaking and planning level, the project will generate and share evidence of the economic benefits of ILWRM 
to project beneficiaries. Within the Jordan Valley, community centres — including mosques and churches — will 
be equipped in a gender-responsive manner to serve as climate change vulnerability and adaptation awareness-
raising hubs, while capacity-building activities targeting decision-makers will also be used to enhance their 
awareness. In addition, local media platforms within project target communities — such as television and radio — 
will be used to further promote awareness among local people.  
 
58. While traditional methods of the harvesting of rainwater have historically been widely practised in Jordan, the use 
of effective, modern techniques is currently limited. To increase the uptake of rainwater harvesting technologies in 
Jordan (Barrier 5), local communities at project sites, including smallholder farmers, will be trained on the 
installation, use and maintenance of systems for the harvesting of rainwater. This training, along with the installation 
of appropriate technologies at community centres and local institutions, will build awareness for and provide a 
practical demonstration of the benefits of rainwater harvesting for local communities. Complementing this will be 
activities aimed at generating economic evidence on the adaptation benefits of climate-resilient ILWRM for use by 
policy- and decision-makers. 
 
59. Gender inequality (Barrier 6) will be addressed by raising awareness on the different vulnerabilities to and impacts 
of climate change on men and women. This will also be addressed through the livelihoods- and water supply and 
management-based activities, where the specific needs of female beneficiaries related to vulnerabilities and climate 
change adaptation are emphasised during implementation. The project will support the establishment of Watershed 
Management Committees (WMCs), local decision-making bodies focused on the climate-resilient management of 
watersheds and their resources. This is an opportunity to improve the representation and participation of women 
in the management of their local watersheds and associated resources to support climate change adaptation in 
their communities. In terms of access to finance, the on-granting facility and the Water Fund’s grant mechanism 
will provide an opportunity for women to access grant finance to the support the implementation of gender-
responsive ILWRM initiatives, empowering them to contribute to climate change adaptation in their own 
communities that directly benefits local women. All proposals submitted for consideration for funding by the on-
granting facility and the Water Fund grant mechanism will need to be gender responsive, promoting the inclusion 
of women, considering their roles in society and have activities specifically tailored for women and align with the 
project’s gender action plan (GAP). This will be complemented by the introduction of gender-responsive climate-
resilient livelihoods that will specifically target female-headed households. In addition, local communities, including 
women, will be empowered to monitor and manage local water supplies more effectively. This will include 
developing gender-responsive community-based water use monitoring and management plans for project 
beneficiary communities; implementing a gender-inclusive outreach campaign to train community members to 
monitor and manage the use of clean water (100% female representation); and promoting additional livelihoods 
related to the monitoring and management of clean water (including at least 50% female beneficiaries). Please in 
additional information in Annex 8: Gender Assessment and Action Plan. 
 
60. Together, the abovementioned interventions will overcome the barriers to climate change adaptation in Jordan and 
thereby catalyse a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient sustainable development through the adopting of an 
integrated approach to land and water resources management. This shift will ultimately reduce the impact of climate 
change on local communities by improving the provision and management of ecosystem services, with a focus on 
water resources, and strengthening the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages) 
61. The objective of the proposed project is to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on water resources in Jordan 
through a landscape-level approach — specifically Integrated Landscape and Water Resources Management 
(ILWRM) — including improved ecosystem management, enhanced protection, supply and use of water, behavioural 
change and strengthened governance. This objective is strongly aligned with the GCF’s paradigm shift objective of 
increased climate-resilient sustainable development. Through the project, the management of water resources at a 
landscape level in Jordan will shift away from a business-as-usual model that is uncoordinated and compartmentalised 
to one that is integrated across economic sectors, government ministries and departments, municipalities, non-
government organisations and civil society, as well as into water resources and land-management policy. This 
proactive and transformative approach will include direct investments into the restoration of ecosystems in watersheds 
of the northern Jordan Valley to strengthen their resilience to climate change, as well as increase their capacity to 
supply ecosystem services such as increased water resources and flood attenuation. These services will buffer the 
impacts of climate change on the lives and livelihoods of local people, while also responding to gender-specific needs. 
Increased water supply under climate change conditions through watershed restoration in the Jordan Valley will be 
supplemented by implementing: i) rockfill dams to promote managed aquifer recharge; ii) floating solar panels over the 
King Abdullah Canal to reduce evaporation and supplement local electricity supplies; iii) small-scale rooftop water 
harvesting interventions in vulnerable communities; iv) water harvesting and conservation practices on smallholder 
farms; and v) women-led community-based water monitoring and management. This project’s focus on land, 
agriculture and water is aligned with — and seeks to build on and complement — major GoJ development and 
adaptation planning priorities. 
 
62. The maintenance and upscaling of ecosystem management and restoration activities will be fostered by a Water Fund, 
which will leverage GCF resources to crowd-in private and public investment into ecosystems-based adaptation (EbA),

=== PAGE 39 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 35 of 144 
 
thereby overcoming the key barrier of insufficient finance for the long-term sustainability of adaptation interventions. 
This will be done via the Fund’s long-term financing strategy, which will include securing agreements with major water 
users (private sector) who are willing to pay for ongoing ecosystem management in return for the water resources 
generated — a PES model. Payers for ongoing ecosystem management would include major water users in 
downstream areas of the Jordan Valley who would benefit from and rely on increased water resources generated via 
the Fund’s interventions in upstream ecosystems. In addition to catalysing financing for adaptation, participatory 
climate-resilient ILWRM plans will be implemented in the target watersheds, namely the Jordan Valley, Yarmouk, 
Amman Zarqa and Jordan Rift Valley Side Wadis Basins. This will be complemented by facilitating the mainstreaming 
of climate-resilient ILWRM into Jordan’s policy, planning and legal frameworks in agriculture, land, water and natural 
resource management to promote the replication and upscaling of project interventions across the country. Institutional 
and technical capacity for this mainstreaming will be built within government — for example, MoEnv and JVA — and 
non-governmental institutions responsible for land management in Jordan. Finally, the improvement of climate change 
adaptation knowledge management under the project will contribute to capacity building activities and promote 
informed decision-making by state and non-state actors involved in ILWRM.  
 
63. An overview of the design of the proposed project’s four components is provided below. Specific detail on the technical 
design of the activities under each component is presented in Section 10 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study. 
 
Component 1: Climate change adaptation governance in Jordan 
Outcome 1: Increased public budgets for adaptation 
Output 1.1 Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient integrated landscape and water resources 
management (ILWRM) and Output 1.2: Economic assessments developed quantifying net benefits of ILWRM as an 
adaptation approach  
 
64. Outputs 1.1 and 1.2 will contribute to GCF Outcome A5.0 — Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for 
climate-responsive planning and development — and A8.0 — Strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk 
reduction processes — by enhancing governance and capacity for climate-resilient ILWRM in Jordan. To achieve this, 
the mandates and capacities of governmental (such as MoEnv, JVA, MoA, WAJ and MWI125) and non-governmental 
(such as NGOs) stakeholders for landscape management across the Jordan Valley will be built through several 
interventions, including the development of land-use guidelines, standards and protocols for climate-resilient ILWRM 
in the Jordan Valley. This will include clarifying the roles and responsibilities of all relevant actors. 
 
65. The mandates of the institutions involved in climate-resilient ILWRM will be strengthened through its integration into 
relevant policies, plans and legislature, including, inter alia, Jordan’s Decentralisation Programme and National 
Development Plan. To ensure that this is done effectively, several capacity-building actions targeting national- and 
local-level decision-makers and planners will be implemented. These include training: i) policy and decision-makers 
on the mainstreaming of climate-resilient ILWRM; ii) planners on climate-resilient ILWRM planning; and iii) staff from 
relevant national and sub-national institutions to use climate data, the results of climate analyses and risk assessments 
in decision-making processes, as well as planning and disaster risk management related to ILWRM. The capacity built 
through these trainings126 will facilitate the preparation of policy briefs with recommendations for the integration of 
climate-resilient ILWRM practices into relevant policies, plans, strategies and legislature. 
 
66. Currently, an unsustainable model of sectoralised water resource management exists in Jordan that does not prioritise 
landscape-level ecosystem resilience. However, adaptation of the country’s population through conservation and 
adaptive measures in land and water resources management can be achieved through an integrated approach to 
conserving Jordan’s water resources under climate change conditions. Baseline gaps identified in the National Water 
Strategy (2016–2025) include that it does not reflect the initiated decline of available freshwater, presuming that 
alternative water supplies would increase in availability from 2025 (which is at odds with preceding and proceeding 
National Communications to the UNFCCC). Addressing this is part of the Jordan Water Management Initiative’s (2016–
2021) scope, by increasing the accountability and sustainability of water sector management and governance. In 
addition, the National Water Strategy highlights gaps in how the GoJ manages water demand in the agricultural sector, 
as well as specific interventions proposed to address these gaps. These interventions and broader themes, along with 
specific options have been detailed under Section 1.3.2 ‘Relevant national policies, strategies and frameworks’ of 
Annex 2: Feasibility Study. The development of a legislative framework on climate change was identified as a gap 
through Vision 2025 and the EDP — which emphasised the need for a legal framework to regulate national climate 
adaptation activities, in addition to enhancing the capacities of national institutions and experts to address climate 
change impacts. Addressing these gaps has been supported by the AF-funded project entitled ‘Increasing the 
 
125 Please see Section 1.3.1 Legal, institutional and policy context of the Feasibility Study for further information on the relevant 
governmental stakeholders. 
126 Delivered with guidance from the policy and institutional expert to be housed within the Project Management Unit (PMU).

=== PAGE 40 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 36 of 144 
 
resilience of poor and vulnerable communities to climate change’, which makes specific reference to Vision 2025 as a 
priority policy to be reviewed and amended. In addition, the Climate Change Policy for a Resilient Water Sector aims 
to bridge the gap between priority areas to support policy coherence by building upon other policies and plans in the 
water sector. For the proposed project to catalyse a paradigm shift in land and water resource management in Jordan, 
policy changes are necessary to facilitate a move away from the current business-as-usual approach.  
 
67. To ensure a participatory and cross-sectoral approach to landscape management, WMCs will be established at each 
of the project’s three target areas. Staff of WMC member institutions, will be trained on climate-resilient watershed 
management to enhance technical capacity at both the local and national level. Training will differ according to the role 
of the stakeholder as defined in the land-use guidelines, standards and protocols, which will be used to develop a 
training programme for climate-resilient watershed management.  
 
68. Outputs 1.1 and 1.2 will see the establishment of an enabling environment for the implementation of climate-resilient 
ILWRM at scale across Jordan through a combination of activities. These include strengthening the mandate and 
capacity of main government institutions and non-state actors for ILWRM; mainstreaming ILWRM as an adaptation 
approach into regulatory frameworks for land, water, agriculture and natural resources; and generating evidence of 
economic benefits of ILWRM (as an adaptation approach) for use by policy makers and planners. Through its policy-
related activities, the project will support budget reforms, increasing the allocation of public budgets to adaptation 
(ILWRM in particular), which, along with the Water Fund (Activity 2.5), would support scaling up of the proposed 
project’s interventions in the medium to long term (see Component 2 and Section D.2.). This will support a long-term 
balance in the country’s water resources in the face of climate change. 
 
69. Under the project’s delegated execution structure, MoEnv will act as Executing Entity for Component 1. 
 
Activity 1.1.1. Strengthen the mandate and capacity of main government institutions — including MoEnv, MWI 
and their subsidiaries127 — for climate-resilient ILWRM in Jordan. 
70. Activity 1.1.1 includes two sub-activities aimed at strengthening the mandate and capacity of Jordan’s government 
institutions involved in water resources management to incorporate climate-resilient ILWRM into national and sub-
national policy- and decision-making processes, supporting the scaling up and replication of the project’s interventions. 
These sub-activities include developing land use guidelines, standards and protocols for climate-resilient ILWRM and 
training national and sub-national policy- and decision-makers on the use of climate-related data.  Institutional 
strengthening under Activity 1.1 includes extending the mandate of the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA) — whose current 
mandate also includes oversight of water and irrigation policy implementation — to facilitate the uptake of ILWRM 
principles in the decision-making process for sustainable socioeconomic development. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.1.1 Develop land use guidelines, standards and protocols for climate-resilient ILWRM in the 
Jordan Valley. 
71. Guidelines, standards and protocols will be developed in year 1 to facilitate a standardised and coordinated approach 
to climate-resilient ILWRM across the Jordan Valley. These guidelines, standards and protocols are necessary 
because knowledge, technical and institutional capacity to implement activities related to climate change adaptation 
remains limited. The materials will be developed with, and for, the state and non-state actors responsible for landscape 
and water resources management including the MoEnV, NGOs, MLI, MLA, DLS, AGM, ASEZA, JVA, MoA, WAJ, MWI 
and WMCs. This participatory approach will ensure vertical and horizontal coordination in ILWRM across the Jordan 
Valley, working within existing coordination and planning structures. It will also target both supply- and demand-side 
factors of vulnerability and prevent maladaptation.  
 
72. The protocols, standards and guidelines will: i) define roles and responsibilities for the relevant actors; ii) promote 
gender-inclusive community engagement and participation in landscape management; iii) prioritise supply- and 
demand-side landscape-management interventions across the watersheds of the Jordan Valley in consideration of 
and building on MWI’s water-use quotas and water-saving policies, with a focus on enhancing the climate resilience 
of local communities; iv) describe how fiscal and/or market incentives (e.g., via tax reductions) can be used to 
encourage poor rural communities to invest in rainwater harvesting infrastructure (such as rooftop water tanks) or other 
water-saving, -efficiency and -storage options; and v) ensure that all development activities planned for and 
implemented in the relevant watersheds are part of a coordinated and standardised ILWRM approach to climate 
change adaptation, by linking them to the watershed management plans developed under Sub-activity 1.4.5. These 
protocols, guidelines and standards will seek to build upon existing planning and development processes, to coordinate 
and integrate activities, bridge gaps and address priorities previously identified (see Section 10 of Annex 2: Feasibility 
 
127 Institutions through which the ministries operate at the local level, such as the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA).

=== PAGE 41 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 37 of 144 
 
Study). Their development will consider the results of the latest climate analyses (including long-term climate 
projections), risk assessments and hydrological modelling128 (under future climate scenarios).  
 
Sub-activity 1.1.1.2. Train staff from relevant national and subnational institutions to use climate data, and the 
results of climate analyses and risk assessments in policy- and decision-making processes, as well as planning 
and disaster risk management. 
73. Staff from relevant national-level institutions will be trained to interpret and apply climate data, as well as the results 
of climate analyses and risk assessments to improve the mainstreaming of climate-resilient ILWRM into Jordan’s 
policy-making and planning processes. The gender-inclusive training for national-level staff, which will include an 
annual three-day workshop for 30 people (at least 50% female representation), will also focus on the use of this data 
for disaster risk management related to ILWRM.  National-level staff to be trained will include representatives from 
MoEnv (including the Climate Change Directorate), MWI, MoA, and members of the National Committee on Climate 
Change129. In total, 24 training days are allocated across the project’s 8-year implementation period.  
 
74. To enhance the technical capacity of sub-national-level stakeholders for climate-resilient ILWRM, training for staff 
will be conducted on the use of climate data, the results of climate analyses and risk assessments in gender-responsive 
policy- and decision-making processes, as well as planning and disaster risk management related to ILWRM. The 
gender-inclusive training will target 34 local-level-staff members (at least 50% female representation) during an annual 
three-day workshop in the northern Jordan Valley. Local-level staff to be trained will comprise: i) 8 staff from the Irbid 
Governate, and 3 staff each from its relevant districts, including At-Taibah, Al-Wastiyah, Qasabah Irbid and Al-Kurah 
(20 staff in total); and ii) 8 staff from the Balqa Governate, and 3 staff each from its relevant districts, including Dir Alla 
and Ash-Shunah al-Janubiyah (14 staff in total). In total, 24 training days are allocated across the project’s 8-year 
implementation period.  
 
Activity 1.1.2. Draft amendments to Jordan’s policy, planning and legal frameworks for agriculture, land, water and 
natural resources, to mainstream climate-resilient ILWRM as a climate change adaptation approach. 
75. Activity 1.1.2 will follow the development of ILWRM guidelines, standards and protocols (Sub-activity 1.1.1.1), as well 
as the training undertaken in Sub-activity 1.1.1.2 to mainstream climate-resilient ILWRM into policy, planning and legal 
frameworks relating to agriculture, land, water and natural resources. Sub-activities will involve: i) training of national 
and sub-national planning as well as policy- and decision-makers; ii) reviewing of policies, plans and legislation; iii) 
preparing of policy briefs; and iv) convening workshops to validate the policy briefs. The mainstreaming of ILWRM into 
agriculture, land, water and natural resources regulatory frameworks, contributes to the establishment of an enabling 
environment required to support the scaling up of the project’s on-the-ground interventions aimed at improving the 
water resource balance in Jordan (through improved budget allocations, for example).  
 
Sub-activity 1.1.2.1. Train policy- and decision-makers on the integration of climate-resilient ILWRM practices into policies, 
plans and strategies. 
76. Sub-activity 1.2.1 will enhance the capacity of policy- and decision-makers from institutions including but not limited to 
MoEnv, JVA, MoA, WAJ and MWI to integrate climate-resilient ILWRM practices into policies, plans and strategies 
through annual (from year 2 to 8) two-day workshops presented by a local policy expert for at least 25 people, including 
a minimum of 50% women.  
 
Sub-activity 1.1.2.2. Train relevant national and sub-national planners on climate-resilient ILWRM planning. 
77. Training under Sub-activity 1.1.2.1 will be complemented by the training of national and sub-national development and 
land-use planners on gender-responsive climate-resilient ILWRM planning through one two-day national-level 
workshop for at least 20 people (year 2), and two two-day sub-national workshops for at least 20 people (year 2), each 
including at least 50% women. The 40 individuals trained will comprise: i) 10 staff from the Irbid Governate, and 4 staff 
each from its relevant districts, including At-Taibah, Al-Wastiyah, Qasabah Irbid and Al-Kurah (22 staff in total); and ii) 
10 staff from the Balqa Governate, and 4 staff each from its relevant districts, including Dir Alla and Ash-Shunah al-
Janubiyah (18 staff in total). 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.2.3. Review Jordan’s national land-management policies, plans and legislation to identify gaps and 
opportunities for the consideration of climate-resilient ILWRM (including EbA, climate analyses and risk assessments) as 
an adaptation approach. 
 
128 An international consultant will work with a local hydrological modelling expert to undertake long-term hydrological modelling 
for the Jordan Valley under long-term climate scenarios and develop a risk assessment to inform climate-resilient ILWRM 
planning. 
129 Including the Ministry of Planning and International Cooperation, Ministry of Social Development and the Jordan

78. Once the training under Sub-activities 1.1.2.1 and 1.1.2.2 has been completed, trained individuals will, in year 3, review 
Jordan’s national and local policies, plans and legislation related to water resources and landscape management to 
identify gaps and opportunities for the consideration of gender-responsive climate-resilient ILWRM as an adaptation 
approach — such as the Agriculture Law No. 44 of 2002, Jordan’s Rangeland Strategy, the Climate Change Policy for 
a Resilient Water Sector, as well as MWI’s water-use quota’s and water-saving policies. The review process will be 
supported by a policy and planning expert (procured by the project) and project Technical Partners. Also involved in 
the reviews will be staff trained through Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2, who will identify gaps for the integration of climate-related 
data into policies, plans and disaster risk management for ILWRM, consider the need for revisions to be made to water-
use quotas and water saving policies, as well as identify fiscal and/or market incentives (e.g., via tax reductions) for 
poor rural communities (including female-headed households) to invest in rainwater harvesting infrastructure (such as 
rooftop water tanks) or other water-saving, -efficiency and -storage options. These tasks will be informed by climate 
data, as well as results from the latest long-term climate analyses and risk assessments (including hydrological 
modelling of water resources in the Jordan Valley under future climate scenarios to inform ILWRM). 
 
79. Sub-activity 1.1.2.3 dependencies: 
• 
Training under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2 — ongoing throughout project implementation, starting from year 1. 
• 
Training under sub-activities 1.1.2.1 and 1.1.2.2 — completed in year 2. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.2.4. Prepare policy briefs with recommendations for the integration of climate-resilient ILWRM practices, 
and the results of climate analyses and risk assessments into relevant national policies, plans and strategies. 
80. Based on the reviews undertaken in Sub-activity 1.1.2.3, policy briefs — to inform ongoing and anticipated policy-
development processes — will be developed in the fourth year of project implementation. These will include 
recommendations for the mainstreaming of gender-responsive climate-resilient ILWRM and the results of long-term 
climate analyses and risk assessments (including assessments based on hydrological modelling of the Jordan Valley’s 
water resources under future climate conditions) into the relevant policies, legislation and plans, as well as 
recommendations for the use of fiscal and/or market incentives (e.g., via tax reductions) for poor rural communities 
and vulnerable groups (such as female-headed households) to invest in rainwater harvesting infrastructure (such as 
rooftop water tanks) or other water-saving, -efficiency and -storage options — at both the national and local levels 
(such as the Agriculture Law No. 44 of 2002, Jordan’s Rangeland Strategy and the Climate Change Policy for a 
Resilient Water Sector). To ensure sustainability, recommendations presented in the policy briefs will also support the 
commitment of policy and budget allocations to support the ILWRM elements introduced by the project, as well as to 
the improved management of water resources and water infrastructure.  
 
81. Sub-activity 1.1.2.4 dependencies: 
• 
Policy reviews under Sub-activity 1.1.2.3 — completed in year 3. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.2.5. Convene a workshop to present the policy briefs prepared under 1.1.2.4 to representatives 
from relevant ministries and departments (with at least 50% female participation) for validation. 
82. Once policy briefs have been developed under Sub-activity 1.1.2.4, a multi-sectoral workshop (with at least 50% female 
participation) will be hosted by the NCCC in the second half of year 4 to validate the policy briefs, including ensuring 
that they are gender responsive. Moreover, the workshop will enhance sectoral coordination in terms of ILWRM in 
Jordan. Once validated, the policy briefs will be submitted to the GoJ via the NCCC. 
 
83. 
Sub-activity 1.1.2.5 dependencies: 
• 
Policy briefs developed under Sub-activity 1.1.2.4 — completed in the first half of year 4. 
 
Activity 1.1.3. Generate evidence of the economic benefits of ILWRM to project beneficiaries for use by policymakers and 
planners. 
84. To prioritise an ILWRM approach and ensure that it is implemented at scale, decision-makers are required to 
understand and appreciate the relevant interlinked ecological processes and how they directly produce economic 
benefits. The relevant information and evidence will be generated by the monitoring and analysis of ILWRM 
interventions implemented under the project. This analysis will consider: i) avoided costs — impacts or losses that 
would otherwise occur in the absence of ecosystem services, such as reduced water resource quantity and quality; 
and ii) replacement costs — the implication of implementing ‘grey’ artificial systems (as opposed to ‘green’ nature-
based solutions) that would be required to deliver the same services as ecosystems. Where feasible, monitoring of the 
following sustainable development co-benefits will be included: flood attenuation, enhanced biodiversity, decreased 
pollution of water resources, improved access to clean water, strengthened food security, increased climate resilience 
of pastoral livelihoods and energy generation from floating solar.

=== PAGE 43 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 39 of 144 
 
85. The monitoring of the climate-resilient ILWRM investments will be guided by scientists (including encouraging the 
involvement of female scientists), but conducted wherever feasible by community members via WMCs. The long-term 
data sets created will be used for adaptive management of project interventions throughout the duration of the project 
and to inform future climate-resilient ILWRM investments across Jordan, and a full impact study of the project will be 
conducted in the project’s final two years. The full socioeconomic and environmental benefits of restoration investments 
are often maximised a decade or more after implementation. For this reason, the long-term data sets from the project 
will be archived within Jordanian research institutions (including relevant NGOs and project Technical Partners) and 
the long-term monitoring of the GCF project’s investments will also be institutionalised. Similarly, detailed, long-term 
Operations and Maintenance (O&M) plans will be developed for all ecological and physical infrastructure constructed 
by the project. Government co-financing for O&M will include: i) financing of ongoing restoration, conservation and 
management of those ecosystems restored by the project; and ii) maintaining the mechanisms and tools established 
by the project. 
 
86. The GoJ will include the monitoring and analysis in their ongoing operations to contribute to the capacity building at 
the policy- and decision-making level and policy reform process under Activity 1.1.2 to integrate climate-resilient 
ILWRM into Jordan’s planning, policy-making and budgeting process. This will facilitate the embedding of coordination 
and operation of these mechanisms and structures in budgets and mandates beyond the life of the project. These 
plans will also include long-term funding commitments for enhancing the ecological infrastructure and maintaining the 
physical infrastructure associated with livelihood interventions. By recognising ILWRM’s positive environmental, 
economic and social co-benefits to communities, cross-sectoral cooperation and integrated planning to conserve and 
augment water resources will be fostered.  
 
Sub-activity 1.1.3.1. Gather primary and secondary data from the project’s ILWRM interventions. 
87. To establish the necessary evidence base for ILWRM in Jordan, primary and secondary data will be generated during 
the monitoring and analysis of ILWRM interventions implemented under the project. This will include collecting data 
through a 1-day gender-inclusive stakeholder engagement workshop at each of the project’s three sites during years 
6 and 7. The data gathered will be focussed on inter alia flood levels, biodiversity, the levels of pollution of water 
resources, the availability of and access to clean water (including by women), food security, and energy generation 
from floating solar. 
 
88. Sub-activity 1.1.3.1 dependencies: 
• 
Component 2’s ILWRM ecosystem restoration activities — ongoing throughout project implementation. 
• 
Component 3’s activities to improve the protection, supply and use of water for domestic purposes — ongoing 
throughout project implementation. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.3.2. Analyse monitoring data from ILWRM interventions implemented under the project to identify benefit 
streams for valuing ILWRM benefits. 
89. Monitoring data from ILWRM interventions collected under Sub-activity 1.1.3.1 will be analysed in year 7 to identify 
benefit streams for valuing ILWRM benefits under different climate scenarios. This analysis will include a focus on 
ecosystem services generated by EbA-related project interventions — particularly the regulation of the hydrological 
system and production of water.  The process of collecting these data as part of the monitoring process will be guided 
by scientists, but wherever feasible will be undertaken by community members via WMCs. 
 
90. Sub-activity 1.1.3.2 dependencies: 
 
• 
Collection of data under Sub-activity 1.1.3.1 — completed between years 6 and 7. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.3.3. Identify data streams and modelling methodology for valuing ILWRM benefits. 
91. Based on the analysis of monitoring data from ILWRM interventions under Sub-activity 1.1.3.2, data streams and a 
modelling methodology for valuing ILWRM benefits will be identified by the specialists (scientists) procured by the 
project. Data streams will include inter alia long-term data sets from the project — archived within Jordanian research 
institutions — as well as the results of climate analyses and risk assessments which will be stored in a publicly 
accessible online database (i.e., a national climate change adaptation knowledge repository developed under Activity 
4.2). 
 
92. Sub-activity 1.1.3.3 dependencies:  
• 
Analysis of ILWRM monitoring data under Sub-activity 1.1.3.2 — completed in year 7. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.3.4. Carry out valuation modelling exercises of benefit streams identified under 1.1.3.1.

=== PAGE 44 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 40 of 144 
 
93. Projections of the values generated by the benefit streams for climate-resilient ILWRM will be modelled by the 
specialists (scientists) procured by the project in year 8. 
 
94. Sub-activity 1.1.3.4 dependencies: 
• 
Identification of data streams and modelling methodology under Sub-activity 1.1.3.4 — completed in year 7. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.3.5. Prepare full and summary reports of the analysis and results, including briefing notes for decision-
makers highlighting evidence of ILWRM as an adaptation approach and specific policy recommendations. 
95. To enable policy- and decision-makers to effectively use the data generated under Activity 1.1.3, full and summary 
reports of the analysis and results will be prepared in year 8 of the project. This will include briefing notes highlighting 
the evidence for and benefits of ILWRM as an adaptation approach, as well as specific policy recommendations to 
address the particular needs highlighted in the reports.  
 
96. Sub-activity 1.1.3.5 dependencies: 
• 
Results of valuation exercises carried out under Sub-activity 1.1.3.4 — completed in year 7. 
 
Activity 1.1.4.  Strengthen the mandate and capacity of non-state actors for climate-resilient ILWRM in the Jordan Valley. 
97. Activity 1.1.4 complements the state actor capacity building under Activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.2 by strengthening the 
mandate and capacity of non-state actors for climate-resilient ILWRM in a gender-inclusive manner. This will be done 
through the establishment and training of Watershed Management Committees (WMCs) that promote the inclusion of 
women in decision-making, training staff in relevant sub-national institutions, developing integrated watershed 
management plans and implementing ILWRM capacity building for NGOs. Inclusion of at least 50% women in WMCs 
will be prioritised. By improving local-level mandates and capacities for ILWRM, scaling up and replication of the 
project’s interventions will be supported, contributing to an improvement in water resource supplies both locally and 
nationally under conditions of climate change. 
 
Sub-activity 1.1.4.1. Establish WMCs, under the leadership of the JVA and local governors, at each of the three target 
watersheds to facilitate dialogue and participatory watershed management between upstream and downstream 
stakeholders. This will include working with MoEnv for the formal recognition of WMCs in relevant legislature — including 
defining their responsibility towards working with communities. 
98. 
To promote the representation and participation of all relevant stakeholders — including government, NGOs, 
CSOs, local communities, farmers and the private sector — in gender-inclusive watershed management in the Jordan 
Valley, WMCs (with at least 50% female and 20% youth representation) will be established within each of the project’s 
three target sites (Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Kafrain)130. JVA and local governors131 will co-chair the WMCs, 
convening quarterly meetings to discuss ongoing activities, as well as any plans for future activities related to 
watershed management. Members will include representatives from all stakeholders involved in landscape 
management, including governates (Irbid and Balqa) and districts132, local municipalities, local communities (including 
women’s groups), as well as project Technical Partners. Once established, the project will work closely with the MoEnv 
to formally recognise and include WMCs in relevant legislation. This will not only reinforce their contribution to the 
project and strengthen the participation of local stakeholders (communities in particular) but contribute to a paradigm 
shift in community participation in development projects.  Fostering the formal recognition of WMCs in the relevant 
environmental legislature will ensure their recognition and authority, contributing to the representation of communities 
(including women and youth) and building their trust in the project. Moreover, the local experience and footprint of the 
consortium of NGOs involved in project execution will contribute to successful engagement with local-level 
stakeholders, helping to mitigate the potential risk of the WMCs' course being redefined post project.  Furthermore, by 
recognising them in legislation and formally defining their mandate, the medium- to long-term sustainability of the 
WMCs will be enhanced, particular from the perspective of community involvement. As a result, the communities will 
no longer be marginalised (including vulnerable groups such as women and youth), but have key roles to play in the 
implementation and decision-making processes.  
 
99. The presence of WMCs will ensure that target communities’ needs and concerns are considered in the development 
and implementation of water monitoring and management plans. This will include specifically focusing on the needs of 
vulnerable groups such as women and youth. Representatives of target communities will be members of the WMCs, 
providing them with direct inputs into decision-making processes and ensuring that relevant information is always made

Component 2: Integrated landscape and water resources management (ILWRM) under conditions of climate 
change 
Outcome 2. Climate-resilient ILWRM practiced in three Jordan Valley watersheds 
Output 2.1 Climate-resilient ILWRM practices and ecosystem restoration implemented in three watersheds in the Jordan 
Valley 
109. 
Without the implementation of adaptation measures such as active and passive restoration of forest ecosystems, 
the supply of ecosystem services will be compromised under climate change conditions through continued degradation 
aggravating the vulnerability of local communities, particularly groups such as women and youth. Vegetation loss from 
desertification and ecosystem deterioration related to climate change has the potential to disrupt other important water-
related ecosystem services in Jordan such as the reduction of runoff and prevention of soil erosion during heavy rainfall 
events. This is because the loss of vegetation results in soil hardening or compaction, thereby reducing infiltration, 
increasing runoff and leading to a higher likelihood of flooding134. A positive feedback loop results whereby the 
deterioration in soil quality caused by increased erosion prevents vegetation from establishing on bare or degraded 
areas, further increasing the impact of flooding events linked to climate change. A loss of 11% of rangeland in northern 
Jordan, for example, could result in an increase in soil loss from erosion of ~1 tonne/ha over 17 years135. Current 
watershed restoration activities in Jordan include both passive and active approaches that provide best practices for 
the proposed project, which will build on these activities. The removal or exclusion of sources of ecosystem degradation 
through passive restoration, for example, has been successfully implemented in areas such as the Zarqa and Yarmouk 
River basins and Swaimeh located north of the Dead Sea through the Hima system, with considerable economic 
benefits (Section D.6. of this Funding Proposal and Section 8.2.3 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study). Projects that 
specifically incorporate restoration and ILWRM in Jordan include inter alia the Sustainable use of Ecosystem Services 
in Jordan — Energy and Climate Fund, Building Resilience to Cope with Climate Change in Jordan Through Improving 
Water Use Efficiency in the Agriculture Sector (BRCCJ), Healthy Ecosystems for Rangeland Development (HERD), 
National Programme for Rangeland Rehabilitation and Development, and the Jordan Watershed Restoration Project. 
Further detail on these projects, as well as additional projects are provided in Section B.1 of this Funding Proposal and 
Section 7 of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).  
 
110. 
The project will also introduce gender-responsive climate-resilient livelihood options, including beekeeping and 
vegetable gardening (via hydroponics and permaculture). Numerous initiatives have been developed to improve the 
beekeeping sector in Jordan. The National Center for Agricultural Research and Extension (NCARE) established the 
Bee Research Department, which is mandated to conserve the indigenous honeybees, Apis syriaca, for research 
purposes136. Support to Jordan’s beekeeping industry has also been provided by the USAID’s Jordan Local Enterprise 
 
133 i) Royal Botanical Garden (RBG); ii) Future Pioneers Empowering Communities (FPEC); iii) Horizons for Green 
Development (HGD); iv) International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources (IUCN), Regional Office for 
West Asia (ROWA); v) Jordan Hashemite Fund for Human Development (JOHUD); vi) Princess Alia Foundation (PAF); vii) 
Jordanian Royal Society for the Conservation of Nature (RSCN); and viii) The Royal Marine Conservation Society of Jordan 
(JREDS).

world. Global Environmental Change, 43: 51–61.

hazard in northern Jordan using remote sensing and GIS. Procedia Environmental Sciences 19:912–921.  
136 The Fifth National Report on the Implementation of the Convention on Biological Diversity. 2015. Available at: 
https://www.cbd.int/doc/world/jo/jo-nr-05-en.pdf

=== PAGE 47 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 43 of 144 
 
Support Project (LENS), an ongoing project that aims to encourage sustainable economic growth and develop the 
potential of vulnerable communities by combining local economic development efforts with private sector initiatives137. 
Vegetable gardening has previously been promoted by projects such as the Greening the Desert Project138, which 
ongoing work includes: i) supplying permaculture gardens for local schools; ii) empowering women through 
permaculture sustainable livelihoods; and iii) donating wicking beds139 to vulnerable families (including female-headed 
households) for vegetable gardening.  
 
111. 
Output 2.1 will contribute to GCF Outcome A7.0 — strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to 
climate risks — by implementing an ILWRM approach and restoring ecosystems in a climate-resilient manner in the 
Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Kafrain areas of the Jordan Valley (Figure 8Error! Reference source not found. This 
will benefit all community groups in these areas, including the most vulnerable (women and youth). The three 
biogeographical zones and associated ecosystems, including unique species, plant assemblages and non-climatic 
threats, will be taken into account when developing site- and ecosystem-specific restoration protocols. Included in the 
protocols will be recommendations for climate-resilient plant species, water harvesting and conservation measures140 
as well as maintenance guidelines. Restoration protocols will be used to inform the restoration of ecosystems within 
watershed areas of the intervention sites and will subsequently allow for upscaling after project completion. Restoration 
activities will include: i) passive restoration through the traditional Hima system; and ii) active restoration through re-
establishing vegetative cover in degraded areas and afforestation. The restoration of the target ecosystems will 
strengthen the supply of ecosystem services, such as water, fodder and NTFPs — such as fuelwood and edible and 
medicinal plants, to buffer the impacts of climate change on the lives and livelihoods of local communities. Current 
baseline demand for water in the project target areas is ~292 MCM/yr. Both contracted labourers and local communities 
(men and women) surrounding the restoration sites, will be involved in the implementation of restoration activities 
under Output 2.1.  
 
112. 
Active restoration activities will be led by the project’s Technical Partners who will oversee local labourers 
(predominantly underprivileged community members including local women). Restoration labourers will be trained on 
the relevant site- and ecosystem-specific protocols to ensure effective implementation in a gender-responsive manner. 
Selection of restoration labourers will occur via WMCs with oversight from relevant Technical Partners, with a focus 
on gender equity. The benefits of the project’s restoration activities will be enhanced by the implementation of 
participatory gender-responsive climate-resilient integrated watershed management plans in the three target areas. 
This will include the implementation of forest, rangeland, wadi and riparian management interventions to enhance the 
climate resilience and conservation of these ecosystems in the target watersheds. The plans will include options for 
additional natural resource-based livelihoods that are climate resilient, gender responsive and appropriate for the local 
context, in addition to being complementary to the watershed restoration and management activities. Furthermore, 
climate-resilient agricultural techniques141, including crop diversification, drought-tolerant crop varieties and 
permaculture, will be introduced on smallholder farms (headed by male and female farmers) at project sites — 
supported by National Agricultural Research Centre (NARC).  
 
113. 
Gender-responsive climate-resilient agricultural techniques will be implemented on smallholder farms to 
increase productivity under climate change conditions, providing immediate benefits for local communities, while at the 
same time reducing the pressure on degraded ecosystems undergoing restoration. Such techniques will include the 
installation of in-field rainwater harvesting systems. These interventions have been designed to build upon and scale 
up existing practices and technologies that have proven to be effective in Jordan, including those which directly focus 
on the development of female farmers. For example, rainwater harvesting being successfully used in Jordan, in-field 
rainwater harvesting structures have been used to improve soil structure and decrease soil erosion rates by increasing 
soil organic matter and intercepting runoff water at relatively short-distance intervals142,143. These structures have also 
successfully supported the re-establishment of drought-tolerant forage shrubs in degraded rangelands. An economic 
analysis undertaken for the Badia Benchmark Project, for example, demonstrated that planting of shrubs with 
harvested rainwater produced an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) estimated at ~17%, with the addition of 
numerous environmental benefits.

ecosystems, incentivising the enforcement of land-management laws, resulting in reduced human-driven ecosystem 
degradation and increased support for restoration activities. To facilitate this activity, best practices and lessons 
learned including those related to gender equity, will be drawn from previous and ongoing projects with a focus on 
community-based landscape management and restoration. These projects include: i) HERD; and ii) the Community-
based Optimisation of the Management of Scarce Water Resources in Agriculture in the CWANA Project (Section 7.2 
of Annex 2: Feasibility Study, provides further detail). The implementation of the plans will be divided across Sub-

will be established. This will include establishing community fora specifically for women and youth to ensure that their 
feedback is captured via a setting where they do not feel intimidated by the presence of men. These mechanisms will 
inform and draw on the project’s knowledge-management interventions implemented under Activity 4.1.2 and will be

Activity 2.1.2. Restore ecosystems in the watersheds of the Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Kafrain areas of the 
Jordan Valley. 
120. 
Activity 2.1.2 consists of four sub-activities aimed at supporting and implementing climate-resilient ecosystem 
restoration, including the development of protocols, establishment of nurseries and the implementation of small-scale 
rainwater harvesting interventions at the restoration sites. The activity will result in the climate-resilient restoration of 
forest, rangeland, riparian and wadi ecosystems across the project’s three intervention areas (Figure 3). The extent of 
restoration measures will include: i) 3,178 ha within the Yarmouk site; ii) 2,309 ha within the King Talal Dam site; and 
iii) 2,512 ha within the Kafrain site. The ecosystem restoration interventions will prioritise increasing water resource 
supplies across an arid landscape under climate change conditions. Co-benefits will include the increased supply of 
other ecosystem services such as flood attenuation of the surrounding areas, as well as increased water and fodder 
supplies, to support the predominantly agricultural livelihoods of local communities. 
 
Sub-activity 2.1.2.1. Develop protocols for the climate-resilient restoration approaches identified for each project 
site, based on baselines assessments.

=== PAGE 50 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 46 of 144 
 
121. 
Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 includes the development of detailed protocols for climate-resilient ecosystem restoration 
interventions at each site, led by the Royal Botanical Garden (RBG). Protocols will be developed for activities including: 
i) passive restoration, via the traditional Hima system for example; and ii) active restoration through re-establishing 
vegetative cover in degraded areas and afforestation. Included in the protocols will be inter alia lists of recommended 
plant species, restoration techniques, planting schedules, maintenance guidelines and recommendations for small-
scale in-field rainwater harvesting interventions (to be implemented under Sub-activity 2.1.2.4). The protocols — based 
on short- and medium-term climate change projections — will build on lessons learned and best practices from other 
arid region restoration initiatives in Jordan and globally (Sections 7, 8.2 and 8.3 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study, provide 
additional details). In addition, the protocols will be informed by baseline assessments of the target sites’ ecosystems, 
which will be overseen by the project’s Technical Partners, as well as the results of long-term climate analyses and 
risk assessments, and suitability mapping using appropriate software such as the Open Foris platform (open 
source)144. The baseline assessments will include consultations with local resource users to ensure a participatory 
approach.  The development of restoration protocols will be led by the project’s TPs in collaboration with relevant 
stakeholders including government, academics, practitioners and technicians of ongoing initiatives. Local resource 
users will also be consulted during the development of restoration protocols to ensure that traditional knowledge is 
considered.  
 
Sub-activity 2.1.2.2. Implement climate-resilient restoration of degraded watersheds at project sites, 3,178 ha 
within the Yarmouk site, 2,309 ha within the King Talal Site, and 2,512 ha within the Kafrain site. 
122. 
Under Sub-activity 2.1.2.2, the restoration protocols developed via Sub-activity 2.2.1 will be implemented starting 
in year 2 of project implementation. Multi-use and climate-resilient plant species that can tolerate drought conditions 
and extreme temperatures, will be used for the restoration interventions, as detailed in the protocols. Depending on 
the site, species that are adapted to inter alia increased temperatures, more extreme rainfall events and more severe 
droughts will be selected. The seedlings to be used for the restoration interventions will be supplied by a central nursery 
located at the RBG and community-managed nurseries (primarily managed by women — at least 75% representation) 
at each intervention site (Sub-Activity 2.1.2.3). 
 
123. 
The restoration will use as many indigenous species as possible because species diversity will improve 
resilience/stability of the ecosystem during extreme climatic conditions. While most of the restoration will occur by 
planting indigenous species, natural regeneration will be used where appropriate. However, many of the sites are likely 
to be too dry and/or too degraded for natural regeneration to be successful. The exact mix of planting versus natural 
regeneration will be determined and detailed in the restoration protocols to be developed by RBG. Using climate-
resilient and multi-use species that supply a diverse range of ecosystem goods and services, including NTFPs, will 
enhance the livelihoods and food systems of local communities despite climate change conditions. Some of the 
benefits will accrue in the short-term, using fast-growing plant species, while other benefits will accrue over decades 
(for example, timber from slow-growing, hardwood species). Climate change will take place over the ensuing decades, 
and the planning for long-term benefits (which contribute to resilience) is part of the adaptation strategy. Planting 
schedules for each selected plant species will also be included in the protocols developed under Sub-activity 2.1.2.1. 
Incorporating climate change projections in the design of restoration protocols will climate-proof business-as-usual 
restoration interventions.  
 
124. 
Implementation of restoration activities will be led by the project TPs, drawing from their experience and 
knowledge from previous restoration initiatives across Jordan. TPs will be supported by local WMCs (who will ensure 
the input and buy-in from local institutions), CSOs and CBOs. Local community members, including women (at least 
30%), will be hired through a process overseen the project’s TPs and WMCs on a temporary basis to assist with 
restoration activities, with roles assigned based on gender. This will follow and build on a similar model to the GIZ’s 
Cash for Work project (more information available in Section 7.2 of Annex 2), which temporarily employs 
disadvantaged individuals to carry out work to reduce erosion in dam catchment areas145. 
 
125. 
Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 dependencies:  
• Establishment of WMCs under Sub-activity 1.1.4.1 — completed in year 1. 
• Development of restoration protocols under Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 — completed in year 1. 
• Establishment of nurseries via Sub-activity 2.1.2.3 — completed in year 2. 
 
Sub-activity 2.1.2.3. Establish a central nursery at the RGB and community-managed nurseries (including the 
provision of seed and seedlings of species identified under 2.1.2.1) at each intervention site to grow the seedlings 
required for climate-resilient restoration.

Sub-activity 2.1.2.4. Implement small-scale rainwater harvesting interventions at restoration sites to enhance the 
success of restoration interventions. 
128. 
Active restoration will be complemented by the implementation of small-scale, in-field rainwater harvesting 
interventions (e.g., bunds, planting pits, Negarim and Vallerani basins, and runoff strips) and irrigation (e.g., porous 
hose and deep pipe) within the restoration sites, improving soil moisture and accordingly plant establishment (see 
Section 8.3 of Annex 2). The selection of in-field rainwater harvesting interventions for the restoration sites will be 
detailed in the restoration protocols (Sub-activity 2.1.2.1).

Activity 2.1.3. Introduce gender-responsive climate-resilient additional livelihoods and agricultural practices in the 
project’s three intervention areas. 
130. 
Under Activity 2.1.3, climate-resilient gender-responsive additional livelihoods and agricultural practices will be 
introduced through five sub-activities to reduce the dependency of beneficiary communities on climate-vulnerable 
livelihoods that are heavily reliant on water resources and responsible for ecosystem degradation. This approach will 
complement ecosystem restoration under Activity 2.1.2 by both reducing unsustainable degradative practices and 
promoting livelihoods that are reliant on healthy ecosystems. As a result, the conservation as well as ongoing 
restoration of the ecosystems in the target watersheds will be promoted. This activity will provide immediate tangible 
benefits to the target communities who will only benefit from restored ecosystems in the long term. Additionally, strong 
support for the introduction of climate-resilient additional livelihoods was received from target communities during 
consultations, including those targeting women, as current livelihoods are being negatively impacted by current climate 
changes (see Annex 7). Consequently, it will foster their continued support for the restoration activities, which is 
essential for their success. In addition, the introduced livelihoods will prevent the augmentation of water resources 
achieved via the project from being nullified by local communities who may interpret this as an opportunity to continue 
with practices that result in an over abstraction of water. The additional climate-resilient livelihoods will include those 
that can be implemented by either men or women, such as vegetable gardening and beekeeping, as well as a transition 
to climate-resilient agricultural practices and post-harvest practices (that will reduce food loss and waste) will be 
implemented under the guidance of project Technical Partners — with technical support provided by NARC. This will 
support a transition to climate-resilient food systems, which support food security under climate change conditions — 
in alignment with the GCF’s Updated Strategic Plan’s (2024-2027) fourth target results area147. 
 
Sub-activity 2.1.3.1. Technical partner site visits to promote and train local communities on beekeeping as a 
climate-resilient additional livelihood to incentivize ecosystem restoration. 
131. 
Beekeeping will be introduced at 150 households in total. Broken down across sites according to percentage of 
total direct beneficiaries180, this includes 29 households from Yarmouk, 73 households from King Talal Dam and 48 
households from Kafrain. Each household will receive 10–20 modern beehives and equipment. Each beehive is 
expected to produce ~10 kg of honey per year from year three of project implementation, which can be sold at ~US$14 
per kg. Major adaptation strategies to increase climate resilience of beekeeping will include the provision of gender-
inclusive training on good beekeeping practices that include knowledge of climate change and their impacts, enabling 
beneficiaries to select the right location, the appropriate maintenance and harvesting methods. Training will include 
one annual two-day training workshop (years 2 to 8) at each of the three project intervention areas for the beekeeping

to further scale up this livelihood.

Sub-activity 2.1.3.1 dependencies:

Sub-activity 2.1.3.2. Technical partner site visits to promote and train local communities (at least 75% women) 
on vegetable gardening through hydroponics and permaculture (wicking beds) as an additional livelihood. 
133. 
Vegetable gardening in the form of hydroponics and/or permaculture will be introduced at 600 households in 
total (at least 75% female headed). Broken down across sites according to percentage of total direct beneficiaries180, 
this includes 117 households from Yarmouk, 293 households from King Talal Dam and 190 households from Kafrain. 
Depending on the situation and needs at each of the beneficiary households, either hydroponics, permaculture, or a 
combination of these will be introduced148. Hydroponic production has a low-water/high-energy input requirement, 
making it best suited to dry regions with abundant renewable energy149. Sub-activity 2.1.3.2 will therefore also include 
the introduction of photovoltaic (PV) systems at the 600 households to power the hydroponics systems, combined with 
the training of community beneficiaries on these use and maintenance from year 4 to 8 of the project. The introduction 
of and training on the use of renewable energy and energy-efficient technologies will be overseen by project TPs, 
including identifying and selecting beneficiaries through consultations with WMCs. To avoid conflict related to the 
selection of beneficiaries to be supported, an important selection criterion will include willingness to pay part of the 
cost of the PV systems as well as to cover maintenance costs over time.  
 
134.  Once established, yields from hydroponic production systems can be up to 11 times greater than with traditional 
agriculture150 — additional details on the benefits of hydroponics are presented in Sections 7 and 8.9 of Annex 2. 
Under the proposed project, tomatoes, cucumbers, peppers and eggplants will be grown under the hydroponic 
systems. The primary input required to set up a hydroponic system for vegetable growing is a drip irrigation system, 
which is easy to install and manage, and is relatively low cost. Other inputs will include tunnels, hydroponic drainage 
channels, planting pots or bags, and planting media151.  
 
135. 
Permaculture will be introduced at beneficiary households in the form of wicking beds. Predominantly used for 
the growing of vegetables at the household level, wicking beds are self-irrigating, providing water to plants for several 
weeks, depending on the climate, season and location. Wicking beds consume up to 80% less water than conventional 
vegetable gardening methods. The beds require limited maintenance and the proliferation of weeds amongst vegetable 
plants is reduced because of the dry soil surface. Wicking beds of 1.5 m x 1.5 m will be constructed with beneficiary 
households and are expected to produce up to 100 kg of vegetables — as selected by the beneficiary household152 — 
per year. Each beneficiary household will be equipped with 10 wicking beds. Inputs for the establishment of a wicking 
bed include: i) plastic, wood or brick to construct the basin; ii) plastic lining; iii) PVC piping; iv) compost; v) soil; and vi) 
seedlings.  
 
136. 
A hydroponics and permaculture expert will be procured to oversee the technical design and delivery of the 
vegetable gardening interventions with support provided by the TPs and WMCs. Additional details on permaculture 
are presented in Section 8.10 of Annex 2. Training of communities will include four 2-day training workshops at each 
of the project sites in years 2 and 5 (24 in total).

Sub-activity 2.1.3.3. Technical partner site visits to promote the uptake of climate-resilient agricultural practices 
on 1,200 smallholder farms across the three intervention areas to improve productivity under climate change 
conditions. 
138. 
Climate-resilient agricultural techniques promoted on 1,200 smallholder farms through the project will include 
permaculture, agroforestry, conservation agriculture, organic farming, the planting of appropriate drought-resilient crop 
varieties and post-harvest processing to reduce food loss and waste from year 2 to 8. Broken down across sites 
according to percentage of total direct beneficiaries180, this includes 234 smallholder farms from Yarmouk, 586 
smallholder farms from King Talal Dam and 380 smallholder farms from Kafrain. The farmers will be selected through 
a series of consultations between project TPs, WMCs and community representatives, with at least 30% of selected 
farmers being women. Selection criteria will include: i) representativeness of farmers from across each of the three 
intervention areas to ensure climate-resilient agricultural practices are demonstrated to as many people as possible, 
promoting autonomous upscaling; ii) willingness of farmers to adopt innovative agricultural practices; and iii) 
households whose main livelihood is farming. 
 
139. 
Sub-activity 2.1.3.3 dependencies: 
• Establishment of WMCs under Sub-activity 1.1.4.1 — completed in year 1. 
• Establishment of mechanisms required for the implementation of climate-resilient additional livelihoods under 
Sub-activity 2.1.1.2 — completed in year 2. 
 
Sub-activity 2.1.3.4. Train selected small-scale farmers within the three project sites on the use and maintenance 
of climate-resilient agriculture techniques, including in-field water conservation and harvesting measures. 
140. 
With support from the MWI, MoA, NARC and project Technical Partners, the smallholder farmers selected under 
Sub-activity 2.1.3.3 will be trained to implement and maintain climate-resilient agricultural techniques in year 2. The 
gender-inclusive training will take place via demonstrations on selected farms at each of the three intervention sites. 
This will include 24 2-day climate-resilient agriculture — including in-field water conservation and harvesting measures 
— demonstration workshops for 1,200 smallholder farmers during year 2. The demonstrations will be held on selected 
farms across each of the three demonstration sites. Each workshop will host 50 farmers.  Once trained, the selected 
beneficiary farmers will be supported by the project to implement and manage measures on their farms. 
 
141. 
An important aspect of the climate-resilient agricultural practices will be the introduction of in-field water 
conservation and harvesting measures. These measures will include (as informed by consultations with MWI): i) 
contour ridges for crops; ii) contour bunds for trees; iii) semi-circular bunds; iv) triangular/v-shaped bunds; v) planting 
pits; vi) Meskat basins; vii) grass, shrub and tree runoff strips; viii) use of AI extension and pest and disease 
management technologies; and ix) cultivated runoff strips — see Section 8.6.2 of Annex 2 for more detail. The farmers 
will also receive agricultural inputs to facilitate the implementation of climate-resilient agriculture approaches, including: 
i) climate-resilient crop and seed varieties (including maize, barley, pearl millet and quinoa amongst others); ii) farming 
implements such as watering cans, hoes and wheelbarrows; and iii) equipment to implement in-field water harvesting 
and conservation techniques. 
 
142. 
Sub-activity 2.1.3.4 dependencies: 
 
• Establishment of mechanisms required for the implementation of climate-resilient additional livelihoods under 
Sub-activity 2.1.1.2 — completed in year 2. 
• Selection of smallholder farmers under Sub-activity 2.1.3.3 — completed in year 2. 
 
Sub-activity 2.1.3.5. Identify private sector champions in the agricultural sector to mentor the beneficiaries of 
climate-resilient additional livelihoods and agricultural practices to strengthen their capacity for post-harvest 
processing and marketing. 
143. 
To ensure that beneficiary households (Sub-activities 2.1.3.1 and 2.1.3.2) and smallholder farmers (Sub-
activities 2.1.3.3 and 2.1.3.4) are capacitated to market their produce, private sector champions in the agricultural 
sector (including women) will be identified in year 2 by TPs, with support from WMCs, to mentor the beneficiaries of 
climate-resilient additional livelihoods and agricultural practices to strengthen their capacity for post-harvest processing 
to reduce food loss and waste, and entrepreneurship. This will also include standardising product quality, facilitating 
access to relevant markets and supporting the development of microenterprises where feasible, promoting the 
sustainability of income-generating livelihoods.
