Output 2.2: Water Fund established in the Jordan Valley to maintain landscape investment for enhanced water 
resources 
 
Activity 2.1.4.  Establish a Water Fund in the Jordan Valley to maintain landscape investment for enhanced water resources. 
145. 
Under the proposed project, a Water Fund will be established as a model for the payment for ecosystem services 
(PES), in this case, water resources, serving as a mechanism for leveraging financial resources from private (industry 
and commercial agriculture) and public sector stakeholders (municipalities) for restoration (EbA) interventions in 
watersheds. This will ensure the ongoing availability of funding for the maintenance and restoration of ecosystems in 
the three target watersheds and as such promote a continued supply of ecosystem services — particularly those 
related to water resources. Specifically, the rationale behind this is to provide financial incentives to upstream 
communities to reduce practices that result in compromised water quality and quantity downstream, and thereby serve 
as custodians of the ecosystems in these areas. The general model of a Water Fund is presented in the figure below153. 
 
  
 
146. 
Several studies undertaken in Jordan have demonstrated a substantial willingness to pay (WTP) for the 
preservation and provisioning of environmental resources. For example, local communities in the Dead Sea region 
showed a considerable willingness to pay for the conservation of the Dead Sea Basin — particularly for the provisioning 
of water for domestic and agricultural consumption154. This study highlighted that the long-term benefits of improving 
the environment outweigh the costs, because of the increase in benefits from restoration interventions over time 
compared with the short-term gains from business-as-usual activities in the region155. In 2014, an economic valuation 
of the benefits of ecosystem restoration in Jordan determined the willingness of rangeland field workers and herders 
to pay for water for their livestock, delivered by truck to their communities156. Depending on the aridity of the area and 
the distance to natural sources of water, livestock owners were willing to pay 2 JOD/CM (US$2.80) on average. The 
economic valuation shows that this value adequately reflects the societal marginal WTP for water because of a direct 
correlation with the costs associated with the construction of additional dams in the country157. In another study, the 
WTP of pastoralist communities for the restoration of rangeland ecosystems was determined under the implementation 
 
153 Source: https://iwlearn.net/resolveuid/9249364e-8d03-4e05-98c7-aafd223cfe0d  
154 Becker N & Katz DL. 2009. An economic assessment of Dead Sea preservation and restoration. In The Jordan River and 
Dead Sea Basin. Springer, Dordrecht. 
155 Ibid. 
156 Myint MM & Westerberg V. 2015. An economic valuation of a large-scale rangeland restoration project through the Hima 
system in Jordan. Report for the ELD Initiative by International Union for Conservation of Nature, Nairobi, Kenya. 
157 Ibid. 
Figure 9. Water Fund model.

=== PAGE 55 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 51 of 144 
 
of the Hima System. WTP was estimated at 120 JOD/month (US$170) with all fodder sourced from improved 
landscapes. Because restoration of the landscape results in the replenishment of streams that have dried during the 
previous 20 years, average WTP of herders increased by 56 JOD (US$79) per month158.  
 
147. 
Anchored in the JVA, the Water Fund will be governed by a multi-stakeholder public-private steering committee 
that includes representatives of WMCs (established under Sub-activity 1.1.4.1) from the target watersheds, project 
Technical Partners, governmental partners (such as MWI, MoEnv and MoA), water utilities (such as the Yarmouk 
Water Company159), as well as prominent private sector partners in the Jordan Valley. This will provide both political 
influence, accountability, transparency and societal trust. Direct governance and administrative support will be provided 
by project Technical Partners. The three primary organisational components of the fund will include: i) a funding 
mechanism housed within the JVA for the collection and provision of resources for watershed conservation with a 
focus on climate-resilient ILWRM; ii) a governance mechanism linked to the WMCs and abovementioned implementing 
partners for planning and decision-making; and iii) a mechanism for watershed management also linked to the WMCs 
and abovementioned implementing partners to undertake funded activities for conservation and management — this 
will include an on-granting facility and the Water Fund’s grant mechanism. The design and establishment of the Water 
Fund will take place during project implementation. A climate finance expert will be procured as part of the PMU to 
oversee the financial aspects of the design and operationalisation of the Water Fund during its first two years, while 
an experienced fund manager will be engaged by the project to manage the fund. 
 
148. 
During the establishment, operation and maturity phases of the Water Fund, capital provided by stakeholders, 
including through PES, can be invested in financial markets through a trust fund, with returns on investment used to 
provide a continual flow of funds for ongoing restoration and maintenance of watershed ecosystems, and as a result, 
the continued conservation and attenuation of water resources in the project’s intervention areas160. The use of a trust 
fund as the foundation for the Water Fund has several benefits, including mitigating cash-flow risks, ensuring that water 
producers can be certain that the multi-annual commitments of the fund will be fulfilled, and ensuring the credibility of 
the Water Fund. Another benefit of a trust fund is that it facilitates investment by a variety of investors. Financing 
strategies to capitalise the Water Fund may include: i) voluntary private donations — including funding from individuals, 
philanthropic organisations or private corporations with a strategic interest in watershed protection; ii) direct domestic 
government resources — including financial resources obtained from the country’s national budget for natural 
resources management; iii) multilateral organisations — particularly those with an interest in natural resources 
management and protection; iv) government fiscal mechanism — including taxes or levies (in the case of Jordan, a 
portion of existing water levies collected by the MWI/JVA could potentially be diverted to the Water Fund); and v) other 
sources such as water facilities, hydroelectric companies, or interested productive sector organisations.  
 
149. 
Medium to long-term investment in the Water Fund will be stimulated via the Fund’s long-term financing strategy, 
which will include securing agreements with major water users who are willing to pay for ongoing ecosystem 
management in return for the water resources generated — a PES model (including payments for pollinations services 
to beekeepers such as those supported under Activity 2.1.3). Payers for ongoing ecosystem management would 
include major water users in downstream areas of the Jordan Valley who would benefit from and rely on increased 
water resources generated via the Fund’s interventions in upstream ecosystems. These include large-scale agricultural 
producers such as commercial fruit and vegetable producers (the agriculture sector accounts for more than half of all 
water used in Jordan)161, and the industrial sector (e.g., fertiliser producers, potash and phosphate mining, thermal 
power plants and cement factories)162, which although only currently contributes ~3% to the country’s overall water 
demand, continues to grow163. Potential payers may include agricultural producers such as the Alfaqih Group164 and 
Haddadin Farms165, large industry including the Manaseer Group166, Arab Potash Company167 and Jordan Phosphate 
Mines Company168, as well as water utilities such as the Yarmouk Water Company169. 
 
150. 
While the Water Fund will only be designed during the first two years of implementation of the proposed project, 
its broad scope is presented in the diagram below. The presented structure remains flexible and will be refined during

the design phase. Finance from the proposed GCF project will be used to design, operationalise and establish the 
Fund, while long-term funding will be secured through the implementation of the long-term financing strategy that will 
be developed (see list of potential financing strategies above for capitalising and maintaining the Fund). The Water 
Fund will not directly finance ILWRM measures implemented by the proposed project (such as Component 2’s 
ecosystem restoration — see below). However, it will contribute to the maintenance and scaling up of ecosystem 
management measures in the medium to long term. 
 
151. 
In terms of Water Fund viability, cost-benefit analysis of a reference Water Fund based on a 30-year time 
horizon, with an investment of US$ 10 million being disbursed at a rate of US$ 1 million per year for 10 years, shows 
that the Fund reaches viability around year 20 after implementation (Error! Reference source not found.). While this 
is a relatively long period, it reflects the spread of investment over a 10-year period170. In terms of size and 
replenishment potential of the Water Fund, three potential scenarios are presented below. 
 
• 
Low replenishment scenario — Following an initial GCF investment of US$ 7,500,000 via the Fund’s 
grant mechanism during project implementation (disbursed at a rate of up to US$ 1,500,000/year), this 
is matched by voluntary private donations, donor capitalisation (multilateral fund) and/or public budgets. 
Essentially, US$ 1,500,000 per year will continue to be available through the Fund following project 
closure. Such investment will be supported by the enabling environment created under Component 1, 
including strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient ILWRM in Jordan (Output 1.1) and 
evidence of ILWRM as an adaptation approach and specific policy recommendations (Output 1.2). 
• 
Medium replenishment scenario — This includes the low replenishment scenario described about plus 
the contribution of one major downstream water user (water utility or private sector). An example of 
such a water user is the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA). Assuming annual revenue of at least US$ 
15,000,000,171 JVA could conservatively invest US$ 225,000 (1.5%) into the Water Fund annually, 
resulting in a total annual investment of US$ 7,725,000 annually. 
• 
High replenishment scenario — This includes the medium replenishment scenario described above, 
plus a stronger enabling environment supporting policy and subsidy reform in the water sector that 
enhances investment in landscape management for water resources. Assuming that evidence of 
ILWRM as an adaptation approach generated under Output 2.1 fosters interest in the Water Fund from 
multiple major downstream water users, including the private sector, there is potential for an annual 
investment of at least US$ 10,000,000 in the Water Fund annually.

Sub-activity 2.1.4.1. Design the Water Fund in a participatory manner, including workshops with local communities, 
institutions, private sector and NGOs — Technical Assistance (TA) 
152. During the design phase (first 2 years of project implementation), development of an actionable strategic plan detailing 
how the Water Fund will: i) build a reputation as a credible, collective action platform; ii) increase its ability to influence 
public policy by establishing itself as a credible facilitator of multi-stakeholder dialogue and by evolving from simple 
agreements towards sophisticated legal structures; and iii) create considerable positive impacts on water security through 
its reputation. A water resources expert procured by the project will undertake a feasibility and situational analysis to inform 
the design of the Fund. This will, amongst other things, include design studies and baseline surveys. The baseline surveys 
will be designed to support a future impact assessment, using the ‘before-after, control impact (BACI)’ approach. This will 
provide useful recommendations for Water Fund implementation and monitoring. For example, baseline surveys under the 
Upper Tana-Nairobi Water Fund identified areas with steep and very steep farmlands that lack terraces and areas that lack 
buffer zones of permanent vegetation along local rivers, but where locals were eager to implement soil erosion control 
measures. Such detail can assist with the micro-targeting of Water Fund activities173. The design studies will also include 
a willingness to pay (WTP) study to in the Jordan Valley to assess WTP of downstream users for water resources as an 
ecosystem service generated via Water Fund investments. The study will primarily target major private sector water users 
(including agricultural producers such as the Alfaqih Group and Haddadin Farms, large industry including the Manaseer 
Group, Arab Potash Company and Jordan Phosphate Mines Company, as well as water utilities such as the Yarmouk Water 
Company). A core focus of Water Fund design will be how it can address gender- and youth-related issues, including 
enhancing their access to finance for climate change adaptation. The design phase will also include the development of 
eligibility criteria for accessing the Water Fund. Preliminary eligibility criteria are presented in Table 5. ToR for the design 
of the Water Fund are presented in Annex 32. 
 
153. The design phase will be initiated by project Technical Partners, with technical support from the Nature Conservancy 
(TNC) and WMCs. During this phase lessons and best practices from other Water Funds around the world will be taken 
into consideration, such as the Upper Tana-Nairobi Water Fund174 (see Section 8.5 of Annex 2). Governing partners of the 
Fund will then seek to leverage financial support from stakeholders and investors (including the private sector) for long-
term sustainability. This will include designing the PES model for the Water Fund (including payments for pollinations 
services to beekeepers such as those supported under Activity 2.1.3), as well as identifying and engaging with downstream 
private sector stakeholders (such as commercial agriculture and industry) who rely on and will benefit from the water 
resources as an ecosystem service supplied by the target watersheds. Private sector engagement will also take place as a 
means to building the financial sustainability and scalability of the fund during its implementation. In addition, consultations

Sub-activity 2.1.4.2. Design an on-granting facility (under UNEP’s on-granting accreditation) for the implementation of 
ILWRM sub-projects. This will include finalizing selection criteria for the evaluation of proposals submitted in response to 
RFPs — TA 
155. 
An on-granting facility will be designed and established during year 2 of the project. The facility will allow WMCs 
and their members (including local communities and farmers) to implement sub-projects that are aligned with the 
proposed project’s climate-resilient ILWRM approach and directly or indirectly benefit water resources — additional 
detail is presented in Table 6 below. The on-granting facility will target sub-projects at the community level which fall 
into the ILWRM thematic area and contribute to increasing and protecting the Jordan Valley’s water resources under 
climate change conditions. Sub-projects will focus on ecosystem management and stewardship activities that augment 
water resource supplies for downstream users, who will pay for the ecosystem service provided under the PES model 
of the Water Fund. A core focus of on-granting facility design will be how it can address gender- and youth-related 
issues, including enhancing their access to finance for climate change adaptation. The final design of the on-granting 
facility will be prepared during the Water Fund’s design phase in Sub-activity 2.1.4.1. The on-granting facility will 
function alongside the Water Fund’s grant mechanism to support the implementation of ILWRM sub-projects by 
community-based organisations and non-government organisations.  This will help to build up the profile of Water Fund 
and establish and embed the on-granting practice that will be upscaled by the Water Fund. The Water Fund will be 
open to other contributions and is expected to take over the on-granting model as the exit mechanism for the GCF-
funded on-granting mechanism.   IUCN as Executing Entity will administer the on-granting facility with oversight 
provided by UNEP in its capacity as AE. Funds will be transferred from GCF to UNEP to IUCN for on-granting (see 
Figure 9). All grants disbursed under the on-granting facility will be non-repayable / non-reimbursable in nature.

160. 
The on-granting facility will be accessed through requests for proposals (RFP) for sub-projects for the 
implementation of ILWRM measures across the project’s three target watersheds. IUCN will launch demand-based 
RFPs for WMCs and their community-level members from year 3 of the project — including community-based 
organisations such as women’s cooperatives and non-government organisations (Table 6). The RFPs will focus on 
sub-projects at the community level which fall into the ILWRM thematic area and contribute to increasing and protecting 
the Jordan Valley’s water resources under climate change conditions. IUCN and project TPs will consult with 
communities via WMCs to identify the types of ILWRM sub-projects that are in demand at the community-level and 
prepare the RFPs accordingly. This will include a series of design/technical studies to help optimise which set of 
ILWRM interventions should be implemented via sub-projects and where implementation should take place. 
Implementing sub-projects will help build community and local contractor capacity in the design and implementation of 
ILWRM interventions. This approach will foster the buy-in of WMCs and their community-level members. The selected 
sub-projects will demonstrate tangible results of climate-resilient ILWRM measures at a small-scale, building a case 
for investment at a larger scale. The RFPs will include clear details on the scope of projects that will be considered for 
grants and the selection criteria. Additionally, the structure of the proposals will be defined, and a template provided. 
The project’s TPs will provide support to the WMCs and their members (specifically women and youth) for the 
development of proposals.

Sub-activity 2.1.4.9. Develop and implement long-term financing and scaling up strategies for the Water Fund to leverage 
long-term financing and facilitate upscaling and replication in other areas of the Jordan Valley — TA 
168. 
The final phase (maturation phase) will focus on the long-term viability of the Water Fund through the 
development and implementation of long-term financing and scaling-up strategies. These strategies will focus on: i) 
securing long-term financing through fostering private sector buy-in for PES (improved water resources) as identified 
and engaged during the design phase (aspects of the long-term financing strategy are described in more detail below); 
ii) integrating the Water Fund into the Jordan Valley’s legal framework (institutionalisation) and ensuring long-term 
ownership; iii) monitoring and evaluating progress systematically and applying adaptive management where required; 
and iv) securing support from the public to maximise the positive impacts of the Water Fund.  
 
169. 
The long-term financing strategy for the Water Fund will be distinct from a business case analysis, which only 
focusses on whether there will be financial benefit for investors. At the heart of the long-term financing strategy will be 
the Water Fund’s PES model, which will be used to foster medium to long-term investment in the Fund from major 
downstream water users. This will include securing agreements with major water users who are willing to pay for 
ongoing ecosystem management in return for the water resources generated. Payers for ongoing ecosystem 
management in upstream watersheds would include major water users in downstream areas of the Jordan Valley who 
would benefit from and rely on increased water resources generated via the Fund’s interventions in upstream 
ecosystems (see above for additional details). These include large-scale agricultural producers such as commercial 
fruit and vegetable producers (the agriculture sector accounts for more than half of all water used in Jordan)176, and 
the industrial sector (e.g., fertiliser producers, potash and phosphate mining, thermal power plants and cement 
factories)177, which although only currently contributes ~3% to the country’s overall water demand, continues to 
grow178. Potential payers may include agricultural producers such as the Alfaqih Group179 and Haddadin Farms180,

Also included in the long-term financing strategy will be a detailed model describing how the Water Fund’s PES 
scheme will continue to channel finance into the Fund’s  grant Mechanism (see below) once GCF finance is no longer 
available post project. This will comprise details on lessons learned and experiences generated during the project 
implementation period can be carried over and continue to support the operation of the Water Fund post project. In 
addition, the development of the long-term financing strategy will consider how the Water Fund, once established and 
matured, could link to other financial instruments, such as EbA funds184, and development banks (e.g., the Islamic 
Development Bank), as well as leverage and unlock additional domestic finance and development banks which would 
contribute to ensuring long-term financial support for the fund.  
 
171. 
The long-term financing strategy will also consider how investments in water resources in the country can be 
linked to and integrated into the Water Fund, as well as potential future water funds in Jordan, supporting its scaling 
up and replication, and increasing its impact. To strengthen the inclusivity of the long-term financing strategy from a 
community perspective, WMCs will, through Component 2, identify investment opportunities and develop plans for 
submission to the Water Fund for accessing finance and stimulating private sector investment. Once submitted to the 
Water Fund, the plans will be considered for inclusion in the long-term financing strategy. Component 2 activities 
through which the WMCs can identify investment opportunities and plans are presented below. 
• 
Activity 2.1.1. Support the implementation of participatory climate-resilient integrated watershed management 
plans. Adaptation measures identified in and implemented through the plans (Sub-activity 2.1.1.1) can be 
prioritised for investment for scaling up and replication. Data and from community-based participatory 
mechanisms for monitoring and evaluating change in climate resilience and behavioural change (Sub-activity 
2.1.3) can be used to identify successful measures and best practices for investment, as well as priority 
communities. 
• 
Activity 2.1.2. Restore ecosystems in the watersheds of the Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Kafrain areas of the 
Jordan Valley. Protocols for restoration processes developed (Sub-activity 2.1.2.1) can be used to prioritise 
additional sites and species for planting, supporting targeted investment for effective scaling up and replication. 
Nurseries established to supply restoration sites (Sub-activities 2.1.2.3 and 2.1.2.4) can be prioritised for 
additional investment based on seedling demand and production capacity, as well as lessons learned during 
project implementation — further nursery sites can also be identified for establishment should the need be 
identified. 
• 
Activity 2.1.3. Introduce climate-resilient additional livelihoods and agricultural practices in the project’s three 
intervention areas. Based on the success of this activity in supporting the success of restoration activities, 
investment plans can be developed to scale up and replicate this activity in sites identified for restoration in the 
future. 
 
172. 
Development of a long-term financing strategy will be undertaken by a financial expert (under the oversight of 
the Water Fund manager — recruited under Sub-activity 2.1.4.4), who should have prior experience in the design of 
such a strategy, and should preferably also be involved in long-term financial studies relating to the Water Fund. The 
long-term financing strategy of the Water Fund will be designed in line with the key principles presented below. 
• 
Securing payers to the fund under its PES model (major water users including commercial agriculture, 
industry and water utilities). 
• 
The sources and intended use of the funds will be clearly defined. 
• 
A mechanism for accountability will be established. 
• 
Transparency and scrutiny by fund members will be encouraged. 
• 
The Water Fund will be constituted a legal and authorized financial entity and will obtain all necessary 
permits in line with regulations of the country. 
• 
Mechanisms for minimizing administrative costs will be developed. 
• 
A clear mandate for using funds only for the purpose of the Water Fund will be established. 
• 
A clear and effective management structure will be developed and approved by all Water Fund 
members.

Options for scaling up and replicating the Water Fund to other locations of Jordan will also be assessed through 
the development of a scaling up strategy, aligning closely with the long-term financing strategy. Amongst other things, 
the scaling up strategy will consider how the Water Fund can contribute to the sustainability of other GCF investments 
in Jordan’s water sector, such as BRCCJ (FP155), including the identification of linkages and complementarities 
through consultations with its AE (FAO), EEs and project management unit PMU. During the development of the 
scaling-up strategy, the proposed project will scope the feasibility of replicating the Water Fund approach in other 
locations of Jordan where downstream water users would benefit from and pay for increased water resource supplies 
resulting from improved and maintained management of upstream ecosystems. This will include modelling to prioritise 
where to invest for ecosystem benefits. 
 
Component 3: Improved protection, supply and use of water resources under conditions of climate 
change 
Outcome 3: Enhanced water security for households in target areas 
Output 3.1: Improved protection, supply and use of water under climate change conditions. 
174. With a baseline of 125 CM/yr of water available per capita — 375 CM/yr below the international water poverty 
line — water scarcity is at a critical level in Jordan. The current demand for water of 1,412 MCM/yr exceeds the safe 
yield of renewable water supply by 416 MCM/yr, resulting in abstraction of non-renewable groundwater sources. 
Moreover, water stress in Jordan has increased over the last decade, with water availability decreasing by 145 CM/yr 
per person between 2007 and 2017. Water loss through evaporation is a considerable challenge in Jordan, accounting 
for ~7,636 MCM of rainfall per year185. This is 94% of the annual surface water budget, higher than the average land 
evaporation loss, which accounts for 60% of global surface water186. Jordan’s high evaporation rate has ramifications 
for water levels of the country’s rivers — of which 11,376 km are non-perennial and 11,155 km are perennial — and 
its irrigation canals. Most notably these include the Jordan, Yarmouk and Zarqa Rivers which are 105 km, 50 km and 
65 km long, respectively, and the 110 km long King Abdullah Canal. Given the high temperatures and evaporation 
rates in the country, the use of rainwater harvesting is a particularly effective approach to overcoming challenges 
related to water scarcity and has historically been extensively used in the country. Indeed, until the development of 
well drilling and piping in the 1940s, the entire population of Jordan was dependent on rainwater harvesting for 
domestic uses187. As central treatment facilities began to supply treated drinking water, the popularity of rainwater 
harvesting declined188. However, rainwater harvesting remains the only source of potable water for many rural 
communities that do not have access to public water supply networks189. In 2013, there were a total of 250 rainwater 
harvesting structures in Jordan, including: i) 56 dams; ii) 129 ponds; and iii) 65 pools. During the same year, a total of 
75 future water-harvesting projects were being planned, including 29 dams and 46 ponds190. Further detail on the most 
common rainwater harvesting techniques in Jordan are provided in Section 8.6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study. 
 
175. While rainwater harvesting for agricultural purposes in Jordan also has a long history, the practice has only 
recently received formal attention from the Jordanian government and ordinary farmers — particularly regarding its 
potential to promote the production of dryland crops in low rainfall regions191,192.  Rainwater harvesting techniques 
have also been used to support the regeneration of degraded rangelands193,194. Indeed, the GoJ has recognised the 
need for upscaling of rainwater harvesting in the country in its Water Strategy for 2008 to 2022 (described in detail in 
Section 1.3 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study), with one of the specific goals of the strategy is to promote water-harvesting 
methods for irrigation195.

=== PAGE 65 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 61 of 144 
 
176. 
Under Output 3.1, the quality and quantity of surface and groundwater will be enhanced through the rehabilitation 
of active freshwater springs, restoration of vegetation and building of natural check dams, as well as the establishment 
of rockfill dams to better capture rainwater runoff and contribute to aquifer recharge. This will be complemented by 
introducing rooftop harvesting interventions to communities (female-headed households in particular) designed to 
reduce their vulnerability to water scarcity. Rooftop rainwater harvesting has been identified as a preferred and viable 
option for meeting the country’s increasing water demand, particularly in urban areas196,197. The potential harvested 
water from a 100 m2 roof ranges from 4–29 CM in areas receiving an annual rainfall of 50–800 mm, which could equate 
to an average saving of 8% of the total domestic demand if rooftop rainwater harvesting if implemented across 
Jordan198. The ability of rooftop rainwater harvesting to save water is attributed to water storage in closed containers 
that are not subject to the high levels of evaporation that surface water sources are exposed to, thereby limiting water 
loss and reducing the demand for water within the wider landscape. Potential for rainwater harvesting in the Balqa and 
Irbid governates within the project area is estimated to be 1.7 and 3.3 MCM/year, respectively199,200. Under the 
proposed project, 3,000 rainwater harvesting tanks with a capacity of 32 CM each will be installed (at least 50% female-
headed households), resulting in a minimum annual yield of 0.28 MCM/year. Rainwater harvesting interventions will 
therefore reduce the reliance of community members on groundwater (including women) and limit financial costs 
resulting from digging deeper wells to access lowering water tables.  
 
177. 
Added to the challenge of water scarcity, Jordan currently imports 96% of its energy from neighbouring countries 
and is therefore one of the most energy-insecure countries in the world201. Renewable energy in Jordan — which 
primarily consists of solar and wind energy — accounts for ~10% of energy production in the country202. With its high 
number of sunny days per year, Jordan has considerable potential for the expansion of solar energy projects. By the 
end of 2020, the country aims to generate ~20% of its energy through renewable sources and increase its solar energy 
target by more than 65%203. Multiple solar power projects are planned, however there are numerous challenges to 
further expansion of solar energy204. The first challenge is the current instability of the transmissions grid, which 
resulted in the Ministry of Energy and Mineral Resources placing a halt on further renewable energy auctions for 
projects over 1 MW in 2019. The second challenge is identifying land suitable for solar projects, which depends on the 
availability of sunlight and the proximity of the site to the transmissions grid.  
 
178. 
The installation of floating solar panels over the King Abdullah Canal will reduce water lost to evaporation by 
minimising the exposure of the water body to direct sunlight and wind. This reduction in evaporation increases the 
efficiency of the surface water body and increases its ability to store water, thereby improving water availability for 
communities and reducing water demand pressure on the landscape overall. Floating solar, for example, has been 
found to reduce evaporation from surface water bodies by almost 100% and has the potential to save 8.6 MCM/year 
from an area of 2.7 million m2 205. 
 
179. 
Outcome 3 will contribute to GCF Outcome A7.0 (strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to 
climate risks) and Outcome A8.0 (strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk reduction processes) by 
improving local communities’ access to and use of water for domestic and agricultural purposes, with a focus on 
women. At the domestic level, small-scale rainwater harvesting interventions will be installed on community buildings 
— including mosques, churches and community centres — as well as homes (at least 50% female-headed) and 
schools in project intervention areas206. This will supplement the increased supply of water from the restoration of 
upstream watershed areas, increasing the resilience of communities to the increased duration, frequency and intensity 
of droughts under climate change conditions.

180. 
To improve the community-level use and management of water under climate change conditions, gender-
responsive community-based water monitoring and management plans will be developed for the target areas. These 
plans will ensure social accountability for water usage within beneficiary communities. The development and 
implementation of the plans will be overseen by the WMCs (established under Output 1.1), ensuring complementarity 
with the climate-resilient integrated watershed management plans developed for the three project target areas (Activity 
2.1). WMCs will ensure that target communities’ needs and concerns are considered in the development and 
implementation of water monitoring and management plans (including vulnerable groups such as women and youth). 
Representatives of target communities (such as women’s groups) will be members of the WMCs, providing them with 
direct inputs into decision-making processes and ensuring that relevant information is made available to them207. To 
promote the effective implementation of these plans, community members will be informed of their purpose and 
benefits and will be trained to implement them (including at least 50% women). Gender-responsive additional 
livelihoods related to the monitoring and management of water in target communities will also be promoted — including 
plumbing, rainwater harvesting infrastructure installation and maintenance, as well as the installation of solar pumps.  
 
Under the project’s delegated execution structure, IUCN will act as Executing Entity for Component 3. 
 
Activity 3.1.1 Implement measures to improve the quantity and quality of ground and surface water supplies within 
the project sites. 
181. 
At each of the project’s three sites, interventions will be implemented to enhance the quality and quantity of 
ground and surface water through Activity 3.1.1. This will be done through three sub-activities and will include the 
construction of rockfill dams, vegetation restoration and the rehabilitation of active freshwater springs.  
 
Sub-activity 3.1.1.1 Establish rockfill dams at 12 sites in target area wadis (four per target area) to capture runoff 
and water from flash floods for managed aquifer recharge (MAR). 
182. 
Managed aquifer recharge (MAR) will be implemented via the construction of rockfill dams to collect and store 
water from runoff and flash floods. The rockfill dams will be constructed in wadi areas up to 20 m wide with potential 
catchments of up to 0.9 MCM/yr. With potential infiltration rates of approximately 0,02 mm/s, up to ~0.63 MCM of this 
amount will contribute to aquifer recharge annually208,209. Four rockfill dams will be constructed in each of the project’s 
three target areas, resulting in a combined annual potential aquifer recharge of ~7.6 MCM/year (additional information 
on the potential benefits of managed aquifer recharge is presented in Section 11 of Annex 2). This will also include the 
purchase and installation of piezometers to measure the impacts of the rockfill dams on groundwater levels. Monitoring 
of groundwater levels associated with each rockfill dam will be undertaken via community-based monitoring as detailed 
in Sub-activity 2.1.1.3. 
 
183. 
Site feasibility assessments and design studies for the 12 rockfill dams will be undertaken during year 2 of project 
implementation by a hydrological engineer, with construction taking place between years 3 and 4. The feasibility 
process will include the selection of sites using multiple criteria, such as hydrogeology/soil types, climate, land use, 
land cover, flow availability and rate in streams, water source, water quality, need for permits, licenses or rights 
pertaining to land use and site suitability for technical design (these criteria are elaborated on in Section 8.12 of Annex 
2).  
 
184. 
Environmental and social risk assessments will also be undertaken as part of feasibility assessments to ensure 
that negative impacts are avoided. Potential environmental risks associated with the establishment of rockfill dams are 
inter alia: i) noise and air pollution during construction; ii) landscape degradation; iii) reduced downstream water flows 
affecting local communities and ecosystems; iv) siltation; v) groundwater contamination; and vi) aquifer damage. 
Potential social risks are inter alia: i) disturbance of archaeological sites; ii) land tenure and exclusion of women of 
water sources; iii) public acceptance; iv) vandalism; v) conflict of interest with local communities regarding water use; 
vi) safety of infrastructure; and vii) health risks related to water quality. While potential risks have been mentioned 
above, the risks associated with the rockfill dams to be established by the project are expected to be few, limited in 
their impact, site-specific, largely reversible and readily addressed by mitigation measures, thereby aligning with the 
ESS categorization of the project (Category B). Additional details on potential risks associated with the construction of 
rockfill dams are presented in Section 8.12 of Annex 2 and reflected in Annex 6 along with mitigation measures. 
Furthermore, an operations and maintenance budget has been allocated to the rockfill dams during the project’s 
implementation period (see Annexes 4 and 21). While not meant to directly provide drinking water but rather facilitate

Sub-activity 3.1.1.2. Implement small-scale measures, including restoration of vegetation and establishment of 
natural check dams, to capture silt upstream of rockfill dams.  
185. 
To protect the rockfill dams implemented in Sub-activity 3.1.1.1 from sedimentation, small-scale measures will 
be implemented via project Technical Partners (TPs) in strategic areas upstream from the dams to reduce erosion and 
capture silt. These measures will include the planting of indigenous vegetation — as per the restoration protocols 
developed under Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 — and constructing natural check dams from locally available materials. The 
erosion and silt control measures will be implemented in year 5 of the project and maintained as necessary thereafter 
by TPs. Potential risks related to these small-scale measures will be assessed via the environmental and social risk 
measures for rockfill dams under Sub-activity 3.1.1.1. 
 
186. 
Sub-activity 3.1.1.2 dependencies: 
• Development of restoration protocols under Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 — completed in year 1. 
 
Sub-activity 3.1.1.3. Rehabilitate active freshwater springs across the project sites (Yarmouk and Kafrain). 
187. 
Improved water supply through Sub-activities 3.1.1.1 and 3.1.1.2 will be complemented by the rehabilitation of 
active freshwater springs in the Yarmouk and Kafrain sites under the project (with support from project Technical 
Partners), improving the access of local farmers, livestock owners and communities to water resources. This will 
include the rehabilitation of approximately five springs at each of the sites during years 6 and 7 of the project. This 
sub-activity also includes the identification of the springs, construction of small-scale canals and maintenance. 
 
Activity 3.1.2. Install 1 MW of floating solar panels over the King Abdullah Canal to reduce water loss from 
evaporation. 
188. 
Two sub-activities under Activity 3.1.2 will be aimed at reducing evaporation of surface water resources and 
increasing electricity supplies through floating solar panel arrays. Using suspended or floating covers has the potential 
to reduce evaporation from water bodies by ~77–91%210. In the lower-lying areas of the project intervention area 
(Jordan Valley floor), evaporation reduction measures will be implemented on the King Abdullah Canal — located on 
public land — to reduce surface water evaporation. The floating solar installation will be connected to the local grid 
(year 3 of the project) to supply additional, renewable power to the MWI’s pumping stations located on the King 
Abdullah Canal. Floating or suspended solar panels have not been used in Jordan before the proposed project but 
has been used successfully in other countries, making this an innovative aspect of the project. In India, for example, 
33,816 solar panels were installed over 7.5 ha of the Narmada irrigation canal, supplying 16.2 million units of power 
and saving 0.1 MCM/yr of water by reducing evaporation211. A detailed analysis of alternative evaporation reduction 
methods for surface water sources is presented in Section 8.7 of Annex 2. This analysis includes a comparison of the 
costs and benefits, environmental impacts, etc., of floating solar with alternatives such as tree windbreaks, suspended 
covers, floating covers, destratification and chemical monolayers. The results of the analysis indicate that floating solar 
has the lowest potential environmental impact and provides the best cost-benefit ratio. 
 
Sub-activity 3.1.2.1. Install 1 MW of floating solar panels over the King Abdullah Canal. 
189. 
Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 will include the establishment of at least 1 MW of floating solar panels over the King Abdullah 
Canal, which is used to supply water to Amman, as well as local communities and farmers in the northern Jordan 
Valley212. A local electrical engineering firm, with specialisation in large-scale solar installations, will oversee the design 
and installation during year’s 2 and 3 of the project. This intervention will prevent the evaporation of ~0.1 MCM of water 
annually from the canal and provide 1 MW of power per year to the MWI’s pumping stations on the canal213. A co-
benefit of the approach is reducing MWI’s reliance on fossil-fuel generated energy to supply it with pumping stations, 
contributing to a transformative change in energy supply across the water sector in Jordan and contributing to a 
reduction in the sector’s GHG emissions.  
 
190. 
The MWI, through the JVA, will be responsible for operating and maintaining the floating solar installation once 
in place, and will ensure that the relevant permits for the infrastructure are acquired. According MoEnv’s environmental 
 
210 Helfer F, Zhang H & Lemckert C. 2009. Enhancing reservoir management through the use of mechanical evaporation

211 Ibid.

impact (EIA) regulations, an EIA is only required for solar installations of 5 MW or above214. Consequently, an EIA will 
not be required for this installation. As the floating solar installation will be placed on an artificial water body (King 
Abdullah Canal), environmental risk is limited, while water quality is expected to benefit through algal growth 
suppression215. The shade provided by floating solar installations assists in reducing the presence of algae blooms in 
of freshwater bodies. Algae blooms can be dangerous for human health if they develop in a source of drinking water. 
Algae blooms may also lead to the death of plants and animals living in the body of water216. Section 8.7.4 of Annex 2 
provides a detailed review of floating solar. 
 
Sub-activity 3.1.2.2. Connect the floating solar installation to the local grid to supply power the MWI’s pumping 
stations on the King Abdullah Canal. 
191. 
Under Sub-activity 3.1.2.2, floating solar panels installed under Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 will be connected to the local 
grid (year 3 of the project) to supply additional, renewable power to the MWI’s pumping stations located on the King 
Abdullah Canal. Usage authorisations and permits for energy generated from the floating solar panels, as well as for 
the operation thereof by MWI, will be acquired through the Ministry of Energy and Mineral Resources, who will ensure 
that the installation and operations meet all local requirements and standards.  
 
192. 
Sub-activity 3.1.2.2 dependencies: 
• Installation of the floating solar panels under Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 — completed between years 2 and 3. 
 
Activity 3.1.3. Introduce small-scale rainwater harvesting interventions in the project’s three target areas to 
improve access to clean water under climate change conditions in a gender-responsive manner. 
193. 
Under Activity 3.1.3 small-scale rooftop rainwater harvesting interventions will be installed at public and private 
buildings (including homes, at least 50% of which will be female-headed) across the project’s three intervention sites. 
Rooftop rainwater harvesting is a simple measure, standardized in the country under the Uniform Plumbing Code of 
Jordan217. This rainwater harvesting measure ensures that rainwater falling on rooftops is collected in a dedicated 
cistern/tank, instead of being lost (Figure 12). The activity aligns with Jordan’s NDC (2021) and NAP (2020), in which 
GoJ has included rooftop rainwater harvesting as a priority intervention for climate change adaptation, as well as with 
Jordan’s Water Substitution and Water Reuse Policy (2016).  
 
194. 
An assessment of rooftop rainwater harvesting in northern Jordan indicated that while acknowledging rooftop 
rainwater harvesting as a key measure to adapt to water shortages, 46% of the population have not invested in the 
measure because of the high costs involved (particularly of the storage tank/cistern)218. Another study, which surveyed 
360 households in the Aljoun governate (situated between the project’s two target governates of Irbid and Balqa), 
revealed that only 14% of households own a rooftop rainwater harvesting system. However, the majority, 81%, of those 
who do not own a rooftop rainwater harvesting system showed interest in installing one. Approximately 97% of the 
surveyed households believe that government should provide incentives to subsidize the construction of rooftop 
rainwater harvesting systems, primarily because of their high initial cost219. The need to improve short-term water 
supplies via rainwater harvesting was also identified during community consultations across the project’s target sites 
(see Annex 7). As a result, Activity 3.1.3 has been included as a grant-funded intervention. However, the project will, 
through Activity 1.1.2, also recommend policy reform for the use of fiscal incentives for poor households to equip 
themselves with rooftop rainwater harvesting installations. 
 
195. 
A 32 CM cistern/tank will be installed at each of the selected 3,000 buildings to supplement and reduce the 
demand on domestic supplies during dry periods (i.e., serving as a complementary source) at the household level, as 
well as at community and institutional buildings across the project’s three target areas, thereby increasing climate 
resilience through improved water access220. The water will be accessible from the tank as needed, via an outlet tap 
— i.e., it will not be piped into the building. At the household level, assuming that an average household (5 people) 
would require 500 litres of supplementary water per day for domestic purposes during the dry season, a full 32 CM 
cistern/tank, would provide water, without any recharge, for a minimum of 65 days. Overall, the activity will result in a

Sub-activity 3.1.3.1. Install small-scale water harvesting interventions at community centres, local institutions and 
schools at target sites in the Jordan Valley. 
196. 
Under Sub-activity 3.1.3.1, 50 community/religious centres, 50 institutional buildings and 100 schools in the 
target intervention sites will receive small-scale rooftop rainwater harvesting tanks. The criteria for the selection of 
public buildings will include their water use, willingness to pay for maintenance costs of the rooftop rainwater harvesting 
systems, willingness to monitor their water usage and limited access to supplementary domestic water supplies. The 
tanks will be installed between years 2 and 4 of project implementation.   
 
Sub-activity 3.1.3.2. Install small-scale water harvesting interventions at homes at target sites in the Jordan Valley 
(100% of women trained in their operation and maintenance). 
197. 
Sub-activity 3.1.3.1 will include the installation of 32 CM rooftop rainwater harvesting tanks on 2,800 private 
houses (~14,000 people — 8% of the target population; at least 50% female-headed households) across the project’s 
three target sites. Beneficiary household selection criteria will include: i) the appropriateness of buildings for 
implementation; ii) willingness of owners to pay for maintenance costs; iii) female-headed households; iv) poor 
households, i.e., those unable to afford the installation of rooftop rainwater harvesting infrastructure; v) households 
with a person with a disability or more than 6 dependents; and households with limited or no access to supplementary 
domestic water supplies — i.e., those most vulnerable to water scarcity; and vi) at lest 30% female-headed. Installation 
will take place between years 2 and 4 of project implementation. Local individuals — of which all will be women — will 
be trained to support the operation and maintenance of these community-shared 32 CM tanks. 
 
Activity 3.1.4. Empower local communities, including women, to monitor and manage local water supplies more 
effectively. 
198. 
Through three sub-activities under Activity 3.1.4.1, community-level management of water supply will be 
strengthened across the project’s three target sites. This will include female-headed community-based water use 
monitoring plans, outreach campaigns and the promotion of additional livelihoods that are related to the monitoring 
and management of clean water. Community engagements under this activity will be facilitated by project TPs and the 
relevant WMCs, which will include local community members as representatives (at least 50% women). During the 
project’s inception period, the target communities (consulted during the preparatory phase) will be engaged to sensitise 
them to the project’s scope, objectives, expected results, as well as their roles — such as under Activity 3.1.4. These 
early community engagements (Activity 4.1.1) will also be used to manage the expectations of communities by 
providing clear project timelines and ensuring a common understanding that the project will not be addressing baseline 
development challenges, but impacts related to climate change. 
 
Sub-activity 3.1.4.1. Develop gender-responsive community-based water use monitoring and managing plans for 
project beneficiary communities. 
199. 
Under Sub-activity 3.1.4.1, gender-responsive community-based water use monitoring and management plans 
will be developed for each target site with the support of project Technical Partners, MWI, WMCs and LDCs — one

each for the three target watersheds during year 5 of project implementation. Sub-plans will also be developed for

wastage. This will include collecting information on water resource management interventions that have been 
demonstrated to be effective in areas with similar water scarcity problems and climatic conditions, which will be 
presented to local communities during the awareness raising activities. This activity will be implemented between years 
5 and 8.

Training material (such as manuals and videos) will be stored in the content repository, shared via the communication

diagrams, animations, etc. This will ensure that the training material continues to be available once the training

Sub-activity 3.1.4.3. Promote additional livelihoods related to the monitoring and management of clean water in 
target communities (including at least 50% women) through a training course. 
204.

harvesting infrastructure installation and maintenance, as well as installation and maintenance of solar pumps. This 
additional training (year 6) will enable the selected community members to take on additional livelihoods related to 
water management. Trainees will also receive tools and equipment necessary to practice the livelihoods. The 
livelihoods introduced under this activity will assist in reducing water wastage, which will limit the over abstraction of 
water resources that are being augmented by restoration and water harvesting interventions implemented under 
Output 2.1 and Output 3.1.

Output 4.1 Improved knowledge and awareness of climate change adaptation for landscape and water resources 
management. 
206. Strengthened mandates and capacity for climate-resilient ILWRM in Jordan (Outputs 1.1 and 1.2) will be 
complemented by the improvement of the country’s climate change knowledge-management system. This will include 
the development of knowledge-management tools to facilitate sectoral and cross-sectoral learning and inform the 
related mainstreaming activities mentioned above. Lessons learned and best practices (including those which are 
gender-responsive) on adaptation will be made possible through the establishment of a national climate change 
adaptation knowledge repository. Consequently, Output 4 will contribute to GCF Outcome A8.0 — strengthened 
awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes.  
 
207. 
The activities conducted under Output 4.1 will build upon baselines established by both government institutions 
and previous projects concerned with awareness raising and knowledge management. The MoEnv, in seeking to 
achieve its mission to “maintain and improve the quality of Jordan’s environment, conserve natural resources, and 
contribute to sustainable development”222, conducts activities concerned with, inter alia, public awareness and 
outreach and the support of decision-making through knowledge management. It leads the governance system — 
including other relevant governmental organisations — in initiating and driving policies, legislation, strategies and 
monitoring, as well as by mainstreaming environmental policies into all national development plans223. To achieve this 
the MoEnv has set five objectives for the environment sector, notably the development of capacity and 
institutionalisation of a culture of excellence and raising public awareness and promoting behavioural change for 
environmental protection224.  
 
208. 
To conduct gender-responsive awareness-raising activities, project Technical Partners will be supported by 
MoEnv’s specialist Outreach and Environmental Awareness unit. In addition to building on and scaling up 
governmental activities, the proposed project will incorporate undertakings from the HERD project. HERD’s 
components directly align with the proposed GCF project and in particular Output 4 as they include: i) providing 
evidence-based technical assistance; ii) strengthening institutions for rangeland governance; iii) upscaling good 
practices in Sustainable Rangeland Management (SRM); and iv) promoting SRM knowledge-management at global 
and regional levels. Gender-responsive outreach programmes and improved knowledge-management systems 
implemented by the proposed project to raise awareness within local communities and support decision-making will 
complement HERD’s inclusive dialogue with local organisations for climate-resilient ILWRM. Several other initiatives 
also provide awareness-raising and education activities best practices that will be incorporated by the proposed project. 
For example, the Sustainable use of Ecosystem Services in Jordan project disseminated appropriate knowledge to the 
public using a national information system, highlighted the economic importance of biodiversity of ecosystem services 
and established Jordan as a regional hub for ecosystem services research and awareness. Through the National 
Programme for Rangeland Rehabilitation, a Pastoral Resource Information and Monitoring and Evaluation Unit was 
created that developed a number of knowledge tools, including databases, study site maps, land capability plans and 
vegetation maps for use by rangeland planners and policymakers. Increased awareness at the local, national and 
regional levels on the importance of biodiversity as implemented by the Integrated Ecosystem Management in the 
Jordan Rift Valley, while the project Mainstreaming Sustainable Land and Water Management Practices enhanced 
institutional structures and capacities to promote awareness of sustainable landscape management, including by 
training over 5,000 farmers and study tours.  
 
209. 
To prioritise an ILWRM approach, decision-makers need to understand and appreciate the relevant interlinked 
ecological processes and how they directly produce economic benefits. The relevant information and evidence will be 
generated by the monitoring and analysis of ILWRM interventions implemented under the project. 
 
Under the project’s delegated execution structure, IUCN will act as Executing Entity for Component 4. 
 
Activity 4.1.1 Implement a gender-responsive climate change adaptation outreach programme across the Jordan 
Valley.  
210. 
Through six sub-activities under Activity 4.1.1, awareness of climate change and adaptation responses — 
including climate-resilient ILWRM — among local communities, including local women, in the Jordan Valley will be 
strengthened. This will improve the understanding of and foster support of those who will be directly affected by the 
mainstreaming of climate-resilient ILWRM into land-use and development-planning processes. Awareness will be

raised through an outreach programme implemented by local NGOs and project TPs, working closely with community 
representatives in WMCs. The knowledge management tools developed under Sub-activity 4.1.4.1 (such as a content 
repository, a knowledge search tool, a communication tool, social software to facilitate the socialisation of knowledge 
and a knowledge visualisation tool) will be used to support the implementation of the outreach programme and ensure 
that the information is available post project for continued awareness raising and learning. 
 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.1 Community engagements carried out during the project inception period in the project's three 
target areas to target communities to the project’s scope, objectives and expected results. 
211. 
Community engagements during the project’s inception phase (year 1) will focus on introducing and sensitising 
target communities to the project’s scope, objectives and expected results. Specific engagements will be tailored to 
the needs and availability of different community groups, including women and youth. The focus of these early 
engagements will be to: i) foster buy-in; ii) ensure that there is a common understanding that the focus of the project 
is on climate change adaptation, avoiding any misconceptions that it is a poverty-alleviation or environmental protection 
initiative; iii) manage community expectations; and iv) ensure that the gender-responsiveness of the project is 
promoted. This will include making all relevant project documentation (such as the Funding Proposal, ESIA and ESMP, 
Gender Analysis and GAP) available — including translation into Arabic. Stakeholders targeted will include inter alia 
women, youth, farmers, as well as local decision-makers associated with the agricultural and water sectors in both the 
upstream and downstream areas of the three project target areas. This sub-activity will be implemented by project 
TPs, with support provided by MoEnv’s specialist Outreach and Environmental Awareness Unit. 
 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.2. Upgrade community centres and local institutions (including NGO centres) in the Jordan 
Valley, and train local staff so that the centres can serve as climate vulnerability and adaptation information hubs 
and learning centres. 
212. 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.2 will include equipping community centres and institutions — including mosques, churches, 
centres, as well as schools — across the Jordan Valley to function as climate change adaptation learning centres and 
information hubs as permanent sites accessible to local communities (during years 2 to 8 of project implementation). 
Selected community members from across the Jordan Valley will be trained (at least 50% women), and stationed at 
adaptation learning centres and hubs, to collect data on the extent of awareness — via a scorecard — on climate 
change and appropriate related adaptation responses during the implementation of the project’s outreach programme 
(Sub-activity 4.1.1.4). The information will then be consolidated by the relevant project Technical Partners and 
integrated into the project’s M&E system. To equip religious centres, the project will build on the lessons learned and 
best practices of GIZ’s “Religious Leaders Support Water Conservation Project”, involving religious leaders in the 
outreach programme to make use of the local value systems and beliefs.  
 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.3. Public figures identified and trained to champion the case of climate change adaptation in 
Jordan, including promoting awareness and stimulating local ownership of the project’s climate-resilient ILWRM 
approach. 
213. 
To stimulate interest from the local population in the outreach programme, public figures will be identified and 
trained to champion the case of climate change adaptation in the country. These public figures, including women, will 
promote and contribute to the outreach programme through the locally available media, implemented under Sub-
activity 4.1.1.5.  Training will take place through a 3-day workshop in Amman in year 1 of project implementation. The 
training will focus on increasing the champions’: i) baseline climate change impact and adaptation knowledge; ii) 
understanding of practical methods and approaches to awareness-raising (including gender-responsive approaches); 
iii) knowledge on ongoing development challenges which are exacerbated by climate change; and iv) understanding 
of the proposed project, including objectives and activities. The training will be facilitated by the project’s Technical 
Partners.  
 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.4. Site- and ecosystem-specific gender-responsive climate change adaptation outreach 
programmes developed for the Jordan Valley, including the usage of locally available media, and the inclusion of 
schools via the EcoSchools programme. 
214. 
To ensure that the information shared via the outreach programme is contextually relevant to the different areas 
across the Jordan Valley, it will be tailored to be site- and ecosystem-specific and gender-responsive, through 
engagements with representatives of local communities and institutions who are members of WMCs.  This will include 
developing an in-depth understanding of target groups (such as women and youth) to ensure that preferred 
engagement approaches and environments, timings, durations, tools and materials are considered. The programme 
will include a focus on the benefits and implementation of locally appropriate adaptation approaches (especially EbA 
as part of an ILWRM approach). At a school-level, the design will build-on ongoing work by Technical Partners. The 
design of the programme will identify how best locally available media, such as radio, television and social media can 
be best used to disseminate information. Also included in the design will be a mobile eco-theatre to present climate 
change issues and adaptation approaches via theatrical performances to relevant stakeholders, including schools.  At

=== PAGE 73 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 69 of 144 
 
a school-level, the design will build-on ongoing work by Technical Partners, such as the EcoSchools Programme. This 
sub-activity will be implemented by the project’s Technical Partners with support provided by MoEnv’s specialist 
Outreach and Environmental Awareness Unit during year 1 of project implementation. 
 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.5. Gender-responsive and youth-inclusive outreach programme implemented to promote 
awareness of the benefits and implementation of climate change adaptation approaches (especially EbA) among 
targeted rural communities and relevant stakeholders in the Jordan Valley through locally available media. 
215. 
From years 2 to 8 of the project, based on the development of the outreach programmes in Sub-activity 4.1.1.4, 
engagement approaches under Sub-activity 4.1.1.5 will include: i) household visits (with a focus on women) to 
introduce community members to real time climate change concerns related to their daily lives; and ii) capacitating 
local CBOs to deliver hands-on awareness raising through direct engagements with community members in their 
households, schools, worship houses and workplace. In addition, locally available media, such as radio, television and 
social media, will be used to disseminate information. The knowledge management tools developed through the project 
(Sub-activity 4.1.2.1) will also be used to disseminate the information. 
 
216. 
Information shared through the outreach programme will include details on the benefits and implementation of 
locally appropriate adaptation approaches (particularly EbA as part of an ILWRM approach), including those suitable 
for implementation by women. Moreover, visits to the sites where on-the-ground adaptation interventions are being 
implemented for local community members and relevant stakeholders will be organised as part of the outreach 
programme in the project’s three target areas. This will promote the use of and support for adaptation interventions 
under a climate-resilient ILWRM approach and further explain the subsequent benefits. Engagements with farmers will 
include raising their awareness on the true cost of water to discourage expanding farming of fruits and to consider 
planting drought-resilient crops with a higher economic value. Moreover, awareness-raising activities will include the 
presentation of information of best practices in water resource management from other water-scarce regions globally 
with similar climatic conditions. This will stimulate support from local communities for the interventions implemented 
through the proposed project. 
 
217. 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.5 dependencies: 
• Development of the outreach programme under Sub-activity 4.1.1.4 — completed during year 1. 
Development of knowledge management tools under Sub-activity 4.1.2.1 — completed during year 1.  
 
Sub-activity 4.1.1.6. Train relevant educational practitioners from the Ministry of Education and tertiary institutions 
on the integration of climate change adaptation into syllabi and curricula of Jordanian universities. 
218. 
The outreach programme will also include facilitating the integration of climate change adaptation into school 
syllabi. With oversight from project Technical Partners, educational practitioners from the Ministry of Education will 
receive training on approaches to include climate change adaptation in the curricula of Jordan’s universities. Training 
will take place via a 5-day training workshop in Amman (in years 2) facilitated by an environmental education expert 
(with support from Technical Partners) to train relevant educational practitioners (30 people; 50% women) from the 
Ministry of Education and tertiary institutions on the integration of climate change adaptation into syllabi.  Technical 
Partners will then work with educational practitioners from the Ministry of Education and tertiary institutions to integrate 
climate change adaptation into the syllabi and curricula of at least 5 universities (University of Jordan, and the 
University of Science and Technology, Jarash University, Mutah University and Al Balqa’ Applied University). This will 
include the development of new courses and related materials. The integration will take place in year two, with reviews, 
revisions and updates being made in year 5. This sub-activity will contribute to the long-term sustainability of the 
outreach programme and promote the autonomous spread of climate change adaptation awareness across the 
country.  
 
Activity 4.1.2. Improve knowledge management for climate change adaptation in Jordan to support national and 
sub-national decision-making and build communities of practice. 
219. 
Activity 4.1.2 will target the strengthening of climate change adaptation knowledge management in Jordan. This 
will be done through five sub-activities aimed at developing knowledge management tools, collecting lessons learned 
and best practices, long-term monitoring, the establishment of partnerships with academic institutions and the training 
of users of the knowledge management tools.  
 
Sub-activity 4.1.2.1. Knowledge-management tools developed to facilitate sectoral, cross-sectoral and 
community learning and mainstreaming of climate change adaptation approaches (including EbA) by 
policymakers and planners. 
220. 
To facilitate sectoral, cross-sectoral and community learning (including amongst vulnerable groups such as 
women and youth), capacity building and the mainstreaming of climate-resilient ILWRM (and other adaptation 
approaches) into Jordan policy-making and planning processes, knowledge-management tools (software, including

=== PAGE 74 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 70 of 144 
 
AI-based tools) will be developed under Sub-activity 4.1.2.1. in year 1 of project implementation. Investment in 
knowledge management will enable sharing of information on global best practices, facilitating the gathering and 
sharing of lessons learned and evidence gathered during the project, and underpinning the integration of climate 
change adaptation into water resources and land-management policy- and decision-making. Knowledge management 
tools to be developed include a content repository to manage and share knowledge content, a knowledge search tool, 
a communication tool to capture and share knowledge, social software to facilitate the socialisation of knowledge (such 
as a smartphone application) and a knowledge visualisation tool to visually communicate knowledge. As possible, AI 
will be integrated into the tools. The information and data collected through these tools will be stored in a national 
climate change adaptation knowledge repository, a publicly accessible online database, which will be housed and 
maintained by one of the project’s Technical Partners — the respective partner will be selected during project 
implementation. Lessons learned from the restoration of watersheds, improved gender-responsive livelihoods and 
strengthened local governance practices will also be shared through this national climate change adaptation 
knowledge repository, ensuring coverage of women and other vulnerable groups’ experiences. Access to and use of 
the knowledge repository by user groups — such as planners, policy- and decision-makers, practitioners and the public 
(including women) — will be promoted by project Technical Partner websites and social media pages. Within the 
project, the knowledge management tools will be used to support: i) awareness raising on gender-responsive 
community-based water use and monitoring plans and training of community members (100% women) to monitor and 
manage the use of water — Sub-activity 3.1.4.2; and ii) the implementation of the gender-responsive outreach 
campaign to promote awareness of the benefits of climate change adaptation among rural communities and 
stakeholder in the Jordan Valley — Sub-activity 4.1.1.5. 
 
Sub-activity 4.1.2.2. Collect lessons and best practices across Jordan (including on the project's ILWRM 
investments) and between arid states in the Mediterranean region and Middle East, as well as those generated 
via the project on an annual basis using the knowledge management tools. 
221. 
Knowledge tools developed under Sub-activity 4.1.2.1, including the national climate change adaptation 
knowledge repository, will be used to inter alia capture and disseminate best practices (including those that promote 
gender equity), lessons learned, literature and data on climate change adaptation relevant to the Jordanian context, 
including those related to the project’s climate-resilient ILWRM investments. This will include information generated by 
the proposed project, across Jordan as well as from other arid states in the Mediterranean and Middle East. The 
collection of lessons learned and best practices will take place from year 2 of project implementation. 
 
222. 
Sub-activity 4.1.2.2 dependencies: 
• Development of knowledge management tools under Sub-activity 4.1.2.1 — completed in year 1. 
• Generation of lessons learned via the long-term monitoring plan developed under Sub-activity 4.1.2.3 — 
completed in year 2. 
 
Sub-activity 4.1.2.3. Design and implement a long-term monitoring plan for the generation of lessons learned on 
the project's climate-resilient ILWRM investments. 
223. 
Under Sub-activity 4.1.2.3, lessons learned from the project’s climate-resilient ILWRM investments and the 
operationalisation of the Water Fund will be generated through a rigorous, frequent and long-term monitoring plan 
developed under the project in year 2. These lessons learned will inform Sub-activity 4.1.2.2 and will be captured and 
disseminated by using the knowledge management tools developed under Sub-activity 4.1.2.1. 
 
Sub-activity 4.1.2.4. Establish partnerships (including the signing of MoUs) with Jordanian academic institutions 
and research agencies to participate in the implementation of the long-term monitoring plan (Sub-activity 4.1.2.3) 
and generate relevant research findings and publications to inform innovative landscape-centric approaches to 
climate change adaptation. 
224. 
Under Sub-activity 4.1.2.4, partnerships with academic institutions will support the generation of new research 
findings and publications, and new curricula on climate-resilient ILWRM will be developed for relevant learning 
institutions via the long-term monitoring plan developed under Sub-activity 4.1.2.3. Currently, NCARE is the only 
government agency involved in land-management research and while some Jordanian universities (University of 
Jordan, University of Science and Technology, Jarash University, Mutah University and Al Balqa’ Applied University) 
conduct research on land management, they play a minor role in technology transfer or extension services.  
 
225.
