B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram) 
 
228. The proposed project will be implemented over an eight-year period. UNEP will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for this 
project and the Executing Entities (EEs) will be Jordan’s Ministry of Environment (MoEnv; Component 1) and the 
International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN; Components 2 to 4). Further details on the suitability of the 
AE and EEs are presented below.  
 
Accredited Entity (AE) 
229. UNEP will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project, as designated by the Government of Jordan. The AE will be 
responsible for overseeing the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and closure of the 
project, in coordination with the national-level Project Steering Committee (PSC) and a national-level Project 
Management Unit (PMU). As the AE, UNEP will: i) sign two Project Cooperation Agreements (PCAs; subsidiary 
agreements), one each with MoEnv and IUCN, to establish clear roles and responsibilities for project execution; ii) 
ensure that the project is executed in accordance with GCF and UNEP policies and standards; iii) through its on-
granting accreditation, oversee the implementation of the on-granting facility and the Water Fund’s grant mechanism 
that will be established (Activity 2.1.5), including ensuring that IUCN signs sub-project agreements with legal entities 
(as agreed on in the PCA between UNEP and IUCN):  NGOs and CBOs; iv) supervise and oversee the 
implementation of project interventions; v) report on project progress; vi) co-chair the PSC; and vii) ensure that project 
activities are well coordinated and aligned with national priorities. Through the two PCAs, UNEP will ensure that all 
the relevant requirements under its Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and the Funded Activity Agreement 
(FAA) are passed down to MoEnv and IUCN and the project is implemented in accordance with such requirements. 
The PCAs will enable UNEP to directly monitor and supervise project implementation, request MoEnv or IUCN to 
take remedial actions and ensure that those actions are enforced, as well as to allow GCF to step into the PCAs in 
accordance with the relevant AMA/FAA (i.e., assume the contractual position of the AE in order to continue operation 
of the funded activity or to exercise any rights under the PCAs, if deemed necessary by GCF). A Task Manager (TM) 
will be responsible for project supervision to ensure consistency with GCF and UNEP policies and procedures. This 
TM will participate in: i) biannual PSC meetings; ii) facilitating interim and final evaluations; iii) preparing Annual 
Performance Reports and relevant documentation; and iv) technical reviews of project outputs. 
 
Experience and track record of the AE 
230. As the AE, UNEP will provide the necessary scientific expertise and technical support for managing multiple 
socioeconomic and environmental factors affecting ecosystems. UNEP has a broad global portfolio on climate 
change projects funded through, inter alia, the GCF, Least Developed Countries Fund (LDCF), Special Climate 
Change Fund (SCCF), Adaptation Fund (AF) and bilateral funds such as the European Commission. Through these 
projects, UNEP has supported national governments and local communities to adapt to climate change. UNEP’s 
knowledge-base is derived from completed and ongoing projects supporting: i) methods and tools for decision-
making; ii) prioritising, designing and implementing adaptation interventions; iii) enhancing climate resilience by 
restoring vulnerable ecosystems that underpin community livelihoods; and iv) monitoring the socio-economic and 
environmental benefits of adaptation interventions. It will draw upon previous experiences and lessons learned during 
the implementation of the proposed project.  
 
231. As the GCF AE for the proposed project, UNEP will therefore oversee the efficient and effective delivery of the 
project’s objectives, in alignment with its Programme Manual. UNEP’s in-house policies and expertise on gender 
mainstreaming as well as social and environmental safeguards will contribute towards gender- and environmentally-
sensitive project implementation. A UNEP Programme Officer (PO) located in UNEP’s West Asia Officer (Lebanon) 
will be responsible for project supervision to ensure consistency with GCF and UNEP policies and procedures. The 
functions of the PO will include but will not be limited to the following: a) participating in the Annual PSC meetings; 
b) the clearance of periodic Progress Reports and Project Implementation Reviews; and c) the technical review of 
project deliverables, d) providing input to periodic readiness portfolio reporting to GCF; and e) preparing requests for 
disbursements. The project will be operated in accordance with UN regulations, rules and policies and fiduciary 
management of the project will be managed by UNEP in its role as AE. UNEP will also closely monitor the financial 
management of the Project using the established Monitoring and Evaluation procedure and financial reporting

=== PAGE 76 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 72 of 144 
 
mechanism, including an annual audit; and establish internal controls for the Project and project fund management.   
Within this context, funding allocation mechanisms are managed as per UN rules and procedures, including eligibility 
criteria, proposal evaluation processes, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is 
audited annually by the UN Board of Auditors and has established dedicated trust funds for GCF resources. 
 
Executing Entities (EEs) 
232. Project execution will be with MoEnv (National EE), and IUCN (Lead EE), for Components 1225 and 2–4226, 
respectively (Table 8). UNEP will sign subsidiary PCAs/grant agreements with each EE (one with MoEnv and one 
with IUCN) to document their roles and responsibilities with respect to execution of project activities — including the 
role of IUCN in establishing the Water Fund (Activity 2.1.4).  The PCAs between UNEP and EEs will include 
warranties and caveats by the EEs to inter alia ensure compliance with the Anti-Fraud and Anti-Corruption 
Framework of the United Nations Secretariat, as well as the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices. An MoU will be 
signed between the EEs, detailing roles and responsibilities in implementation. 
 
233. Both EEs have the legal capacity and authority to act and enter into contracts independently as required for the 
proposed project. The responsibility of the EEs will include being accountable to the AE for the effective use of 
resources during project implementation. All operating policies and procedures will follow the UNEP Programme 
Manual, which includes provisions for financial management and procurement227. The EEs, both have the financial 
management capacity to, and will be responsible for the disbursement of financial resources for the implementation 
of all project activities related to their relevant components.

Experience and track record of the EEs 
234. As the head ministry for the National Designated Authority (NDA), MoEnv has been identified as the appropriate 
government entity to fulfil the role of EE for Component 1 of the proposed project, possessing the necessary technical 
expertise for the efficient and effective management of financial resources for the implementation of project activities. 
The MoEnv lead the development of the country’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), National 
Communications to the UNFCCC and NAP process, and has been involved in a number of related projects in recent 
years. This involvement includes managing and delivering national and regional projects focussing on, inter alia: i) 
climate change adaptation; ii) landscape and water sector management; and iii) sustainable development. Moreover, 
MoEnv’s Climate Change Directorate (CCD) oversees all climate change-related activities in Jordan. Some of the 
initiatives supported by MoEnv over recent years are listed below (more details on these initiatives are provided in 
Section 7 of Annex 2, Feasibility Study). 
• 
GIZ — Sustainable use of Ecosystem Services in Jordan; Energy and Climate Fund, 2014–2019 
• 
GEF — dRHS Irrigation Technology Pilot Project to face Climate Change impact in Jordan, 2009—2016 
(US$7.6 million) 
• 
IFAD, AFESD, OPEC Fund for International Development and IDRC — Community-based Optimisation of the 
Management of Scarce Water Resources in Agriculture in Central-West Asia and North Africa (CWANA) 
Project, 2003–2013 
• 
GEF — Mainstreaming Sustainable Land and Water Management Practices, 2009–2015 (~US$29.7 million) 
• 
GIZ — Managing Water Resources in Jordan, 2006–2018 
• 
GEF — Integrated Ecosystem Management in the Jordan Rift Valley, 2007–2013 (~US$12.7 million) 
• 
GEF — Badia Ecosystem and Livelihood Project, 2013–2017 (US$33.3 million) 
 
235. The MoEnv’s mission is to “maintain and improve the quality of Jordan’s environment, conserve natural resources 
and contribute to sustainable development”228. This is achieved by leading the governance system — including other 
relevant governmental organisations — in initiating and driving policies, legislation, strategies and monitoring, as well 
as by mainstreaming environmental policies into all national development plans229. The MoEnv comprises several 
specialist units, including the directorates of: i) Climate Change; ii) Nature Protection; iii) Environmental Inspection; 
iv) Waste Management and Hazardous Substances; v) Licensing and Pollution Prevention; and vi) Outreach and 
Environmental Awareness. These are supported by the functional directorates of Human Resources and 
Administrative Affairs, Financial Affairs, and Policies and Institutional Development230. In 2015, following the 
recommendations of the National Climate Change Policy, the Climate Change Directorate (CCD) was established in 
2014 to serve as the institutional focal point for climate change activities in Jordan, including the country’s 
commitments as a party to the UNFCCC. Additionally, the CCD is the coordinating focal point to global and regional 
environmental governance systems231. The CCD coordinates high-level policy formulation and implementation, 
facilitating the participation of relevant stakeholders at all levels. It also oversees development of the internal capacity 
of MoEnv — as well as the government in general — in addressing the challenges and barriers of climate change232. 
CCD is made up of two departments, namely the Emissions Reduction Section and the Adaptation to Climate Change 
Section. Further detail of the organisational structure of MoEnv can be found in Section 1.3.1 Institutional Framework

Entity. UNEP will undertake a full assessment of the financial management capacity of MoEnv to identify risk

and establish internal controls for the project and project fund management. 
 
236. IUCN’s expertise and extensive network provide a solid foundation for a large and diverse portfolio of conservation 
projects around the world. Combining the latest science with the traditional knowledge of local communities, these 
projects work to reverse habitat loss, restore ecosystems and improve people’s well-being. They also produce a 
wealth of data and information which feeds into IUCN’s analytical capacity. Through its member organisations, IUCN 
supports and participates in scientific research, promotes and helps to implement national conservation legislation, 
policies and practices; and funds, operates or manages thousands of field projects worldwide. An example of an 
initiative supported by IUCN over recent years is (more details on these initiatives are provided in Section 7 of Annex 
2, Feasibility Study) the EU-funded Securing Rights and Restoring Lands for Improved Livelihoods project, 2011–
2013. The value addition of selecting IUCN (a GCF AE) as EE includes the organisation’s presence and footprint in 
Jordan via its regional office (IUCN-ROWA) which is based in Amman. This means that the project will benefit from 
the support of the organisation’s locally-based Jordanian staff who are familiar with the local context and have 
experience working with the stakeholders and in the ecosystems included in the project intervention areas. This 
includes a strong working relationship with the consortium of NGOs that supported the development of this proposal 
and will function as Technical Partners during project implementation (see below). As a result, the selection of IUCN 
as EE, as opposed to UNEP for example, is strategic in that it provides a strong operational foundation for the 
implementation of the project. Additional details on the IUCN’s comparative advantage and capacity to function as 
EE of Components 2 to 4 are presented in Annex 23. 
 
Technical Partners 
237. The required expertise to implement project technical activities (Components 2 to 4) will be sourced primarily from 
members of the Consortium of NGOs, with due care and consideration for the best value for money and best interests 
of the project. The process of selection and managing technical partners is detailed below.  
 
238. IUCN will develop the Terms of Reference for the specific technical assistance to be provided under relevant project 
components, which will include specific information on the technical assistance to be delivered, including activities, 
outputs, expected outcomes and impacts, timeline, deliverables and requirements such as track record including 
experience with carrying out a project of similar size and scope.  The contracting of qualified national and international 
technical partners will be determined using a competitive selection process, based on track record in delivering the 
services and best value of money considering entity co-financing to the project.  The NGOs in Annex 23 are expected 
to the main technical partners.  IUCN will be responsible for the competitive selection of technical partners in 
alignment with UNEP partnership policy and procedures.   The selected Technical Partners (TP) will be contractually 
bound through legal agreements signed with IUCN (IUCN-TP agreements). TPs will produce regular progress and 
financial reports and will submit deliverables to IUCN. Funds for the implementation of activities will be released by 
IUCN to the TPs in the form of cash advances (bank transfers) dependent on cleared expenditure reports and 
progress reports.  
 
Project Steering Committee (PSC) 
239. The PSC will comprise representatives from, inter alia: i) IUCN and alternating co-chairs, along with UNEP as 
permanent co-chair; ii) MoEnv’s subsidiaries; CCD, WAJ and JVA; iii) MWI; iv) TPs; v) the National Technical 
Working Group (TWG); and vi) WMCs. The committee will primarily be responsible for providing technical oversight 
and advisory support. This includes: i) overseeing project implementation; ii) reviewing annual workplans and project 
reports; and iii) approving any changes to the project’s targets, activities or timelines. Biannual PSC meetings will be 
held233 to take management-related and technical decisions, discuss the project's main performance indicators and 
provide strategic guidance. Any changes made by the PSC to the project’s Results Framework or timeline will be 
communicated to the Project Management Unit (PMU). In addition, the PSC will be responsible for: i) overseeing the 
PMU; ii) providing technical and administrative functions; and iii) serving as the focal point for engagements and 
interactions between project stakeholders and partner organisations. ToRs for the PSC will be adopted during the 
first PSC meeting. 
 
National Technical Working Group 
 
233 with ad hoc meetings held as required

240. A National TWG will be established to provide technical advice to National EE, MoEnv, to support the implementation 
of Component 1’s cross-cutting ‘soft’ interventions that are not site-specific — i.e., the capacity building and 
mainstreaming activities which are being implemented at a national level. The membership of the National TWG will 
include entities and agencies involved in the planning of landscape management and water resources, as well as 
climate change adaptation, such as JVA, WAJ, MoA, MOPIC, MWI and the Jordanian National Commission for 
Women (JNCW)234. MoEnv, will work closely with the TWG members (MWI in particular) to ensure efficient 
implementation. In addition, the National TWG will include local specialists and academics with expertise relevant to 
the project, such as in climate change adaptation (including EbA) and integrated landscape and water resources 
management (ILWRM). The National TWG will work closely with the Technical Partners and WMCs to ensure that 
‘soft’ interventions across the Jordan Valley complement the ‘hard’ interventions that will be implemented at the 
project’s three target sites. Additionally, the TWG, together with TPs and the CTA, will evaluate proposals submitted 
in response to the RFPs advertised via on-granting facility and the Water Fund’s grant mechanism. 
 
Watershed Management Committees (WMCs) 
241. To promote the representation and participation of all relevant stakeholders — including government, NGOs, CSOs, 
local communities, farmers and the private sector — in watershed management in the Jordan Valley, WMCs will be 
established within each of the project’s three target sites (Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Kafrain), with at least 50% 
female representation. ToR for the role and function of WMCs will be developed by project Technical Partners 
(framework ToR are presented in Annex 28). The presence of WMCs will provide a platform for dialogue between 
stakeholders in upstream and downstream areas of watersheds, ensuring the coordination of management, planning 
and implementation. WMCs will ensure that target communities’ needs and concerns are considered in the 
development and implementation of water monitoring and management plans. Representatives of target 
communities will be members of the WMCs, providing them with direct inputs into decision-making processes and 
ensuring that relevant information is always made available to them. These community representatives will be 
members of community-based committees already present on the ground in the three project areas, such as 
Development Advisory Committees, Stakeholders Advisory Groups, agricultural cooperatives, women’s cooperatives 
and Water User Associations. Once established, the project will work closely with the MoEnv (project Executing 
Entity) to formally recognize and include WMCs in relevant legislation — including defining their mandate related to 
working with communities. This will not only reinforce their contribution to the project and strengthen the participation 
of local stakeholders (communities in particular) but contribute to their sustainability and a paradigm shift in 
community participation in development projects. 
 
242. The responsibilities of the WMCs will include: i) acting as focal points to facilitate interactions between the PSC, 
PMU, National TWG and stakeholders (specifically local communities) at a local level; ii) providing guidance to the 
PSC and PMU; iii) having representation on the PSC; and iv) coordinating the implementation of the proposed 
project’s interventions in each of the three target sites. Such local-level structures, like WMCs, are important to build 
capacity and structures in support of ILWRM (including EbA), as well as to directly manage the watershed-level 
interventions under the project. The WMCs will serve as important cross-sectoral coordination bodies at the site-
level, which currently do not exist. In addition, WMCs will serve as channels through which climate information, 
including forecasts and early warnings will be disseminated to local communities. JVA and local governors will co-
chair the WMCs (with direct support being provided by project Technical Partners), convening quarterly meetings to 
discuss ongoing activities, as well as any plans for future activities related to watershed management.  
 
243. Several activities and sub-activities are dependent on the formal establishment of the WMCs. These include 1.1.4.2, 
1.1.4.4, 1.1.4.5, 2.1.1.3, 2.1.2.2, 2.1.3.2, 2.1.4.1, 2.1.3.3, 2.1.3.5, 2.1.4.1, 2.1.4.3, 2.1.4.4, 2.1.4.5, 2.1.4.6, and 
3.1.4.1 — dependencies have been mapped across the implementation timeline in further detail in Annex 5. Focus 
will therefore be placed on ensuring that their inclusion in legislature, constitution and operationalisation is prioritised 
and finalised in year 1 of project implementation. 
 
Project Management Unit (PMU) 
244. A gender-balanced PMU will be established by IUCN and MoEnv in year one of project implementation to coordinate

hosted within MoEnv and will consist of: i) a Project Coordinator (PC); ii) a Financial Officer; iii) a Procurement Officer; 
iv) an Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer (ESO); v) a Communications Officer (CO); vi) a Monitoring

Technical Advisor (CTA). The recruitment process will ensure that the PMU is gender balanced. The PC will be a 
full-time staff member, recruited competitively, who will be responsible for the daily on-the-ground implementation 
and management of the project. The PC will be primarily responsible for ensuring that the project achieves the targets

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
 
247. Jordan is ranked as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world235, with only 125 CM/yr of water available per 
person236. Climate change will further exacerbate water stress in Jordan through decreased annual rainfall, higher 
annual temperatures and more frequent, extreme and prolonged droughts. As a result of the country’s existing 
vulnerability to critical water stress, even a minor decrease in water supply resulting from climate change impacts is 
expected to have severe implications for the country’s water security.  
 
248. As water is already scarce in Jordan, the additional impact of a change in climatic conditions at any scale could have 
severe consequences on climate-resilient development and the health and wellbeing of the country’s most vulnerable 
people. Furthermore, many low cost, accessible measures to increase water availability have already been deployed. 
Consequently, it is not expected that there are many easily implementable, low-cost options to increase water 
availability remaining. However, the benefits of enhancing water availability are likely to be large, especially in the 
context of climate change. As a result, there is a strong economic argument for exploring interventions that increase 
water availability which cannot easily be provided by markets and the current enabling environment. This is especially 
the case where expected climatic conditions make the probability of upscaling existing measures seem particularly 
high and where success is very likely. 
 
249. In consideration of the above, addressing the impact of water stress on Jordan’s inhabitants, ecosystems and 
economy requires a climate change adaptation response based on an integrated landscape and water resources 
management (ILWRM) approach for land and water resources.  For Jordanians whose livelihoods are threatened by 
climate change, ILWRM offers a pathway towards more resilient livelihoods, based on sustainable resource use that 
takes into account the interconnections between different ecosystems, hydrological systems and various uses within 
 
235 WRI. 2019. Aqueduct baseline water stress rankings. Available at: https://www.wri.org/aqueduct/. 
236 Hammouri N, Adbulla F, Qdais HA & Freiwan M. 2015. Assessing the impacts of climate change on water resources of 
Jordan. World Water Congress XV. International Water Resources Association (IWRA), Edinburgh.

=== PAGE 82 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 78 of 144 
 
a landscape. However, while the climate change adaptation and mitigation potential of ILWRM is clear, its 
implementation in Jordan through the JILMI Project represents a major investment.   
 
250. To this end, the Government of Jordan (GoJ) is seeking US$44,904,203 of GCF grant financing to achieve this 
objective by reducing the negative impacts of climate change on water resources through a landscape-level 
approach. The funding will be used to implement climate-resilient interventions with a focus on, inter alia: i) 
ecosystem restoration; ii) managed aquifer recharge; iii) rainwater harvesting; iv) evaporation reduction; v) diversified 
natural resource livelihoods; and vi) strengthened governance. There are multiple factors which compound to make 
a very strong rationale for investing in ILWRM, particularly with a strong ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) 
component. These are summarised below.  
• 
Market failures: markets tend to be skewed toward hard infrastructure as they often do no account for any 
negative impacts of hard infrastructure and the wider benefits of ecosystem restoration and associated 
ecosystem services are undervalued.  
• 
Barriers to entry for alternative technologies: buyers may not have the technical knowledge to understand and 
be able to compare ecosystem-based approaches with traditional man-made alternatives.    
• 
Transaction costs: economically viable options are prevented from coming forward at a landscape scale without 
coordination as a result of the costs of bringing stakeholders together and strengthening governance processes.  
 
251. These factors provide a clear rationale for investing in an enabling environment that advances ILWRM in Jordan as 
a climate change adaptation response and demonstrates the potential to further reduce water scarcity through 
approaches which remain novel or unexploited. This is particularly the case for approaches with high upfront costs 
and where there is difficultly with regards to accessing credit. Such activities have the potential to be scaled up once 
their potential or the ability of investment to generate returns is proven within the country, given expectations of 
increased renewable water deficits (resulting from climate change) and consequent increases in the demand for 
solutions.  
 
252. Although Jordan is classified as an upper middle-income country237, the country continues to face major economic 
burdens including the continued influx of refugees from unstable states in the region, conflicts in major trading 
partners of Iraq and Syria, and the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic. Such burdens will continue to compound the 
country’s growing public debt, which increased to ~94% of the GDP in 2019 from 65.4% in 2011238,  totalling US$40.2 
billion in January 2019239.  This debt has been attributed largely to: i) successive governments adopting an 
expansionist fiscal policy240; ii) the provision of considerable subsidies for staple goods; iii) a reduction in foreign aid, 
which has forced the country to borrow externally; iv) increased welfare spending for citizens and refugees; v) 
excessive spending by the National Electric Power Company (NEPCO) and the Water Authority of Jordan (WAJ); 
and vi) low tax collections — nearly 95% of the population is not subject to income tax.  Compounding Jordan’s 
public debt is a high trade deficit (~26% in 2015) — mainly owing to a reliance on energy and food imports — and 
domestic fiscal deficit (6.6% in 2015, although down from 12.7% in 2012)241. In terms of the refugee influx, it is 
estimated that up to 3 million refugees (predominantly from Syria) are currently hosted by Jordan (total population of 
10.2 million), placing increased strain on financial and natural resources in the country.  The direct cost of Syrian 
refugees for the Jordanian government from 2011 to 2016 was ~US$4.2 billion242. The international community has 
recognised the strain placed upon Jordan’s economy and infrastructure, directing resources to alleviate some of that 
pressure, although matching these resources with needs on-the-ground is increasingly difficult243. The conflicts in 
Syria and Iraq, that are fuelling the influx of refugees, have also resulted in a considerable increase in Jordan’s trade 
deficit244, by disrupting exports that had relied on Syria as a major intermediary route to other important destinations 
such as Lebanon, Europe, Turkey and Central Asia245. From 2011, border crossings with Syria and Iraq were 
severely limited and between 2015 and late 2018 were completely closed, causing a profoundly negative impact on 
 
237 Source: https://datahelpdesk.worldbank.org/knowledgebase/articles/906519-world-bank-country-and-lending-groups 
238 Source: https://tradingeconomics.com/jordan/government-debt-to-gdp

https://aawsat.com/english/home/article/1642336/jordan%E2%80%99s-national-debt-exceeds-40-bn.

services.

International Studies. 11: 154-179. 
243 Amjad R, Aslan J, Borgnäs E, Chandran D, Clark E, Ferreira dos Passos A, Joo J, Mohajer O (eds). 2017. Examining 
barriers to workforce inclusion of Syrian refugees in Jordan. International Labour Office. Geneva.  
244 Appendino M, Cerisola M, Martin E, Salman F, Tiffin A, Morra P, Sobrinho N, Wagner H (eds). 2017. Jordan. Selected 
issues. International Monetary Fund. Washington, DC.  
245 Ibid.

=== PAGE 83 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 79 of 144 
 
Jordanian exports reaching their final markets246. Exports to Iraq — once Jordan’s major export market, accounting 
for almost a fifth of domestic exports and up to ~US$1.2 billion a year — have declined by more than 50% since the 
closing of the Iraqi-Jordanian border247. Such economic stresses are likely to continue in the short to medium term, 
reducing the amount of public finance available for climate change adaptation. Consequently, the demonstration of 
the benefits of investments in adaptation approaches such as ILWRM to decision-makers in Jordan will remain 
limited, preventing a shift to climate-resilient sustainable development. 
 
253. Jordan has recently seen the approval of GCF FP155 — Building resilience to cope with climate change in Jordan 
through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector (BRCCJ) — which includes US$ 25 million in grant 
finance for the GCF. However, there is little overlap between the proposed project and BRCCJ and opportunity for 
great complementarity. While both projects have a strong focus on water, their approaches differ with BRCCJ 
focusing on water use efficiency in the agriculture sector, and the proposed project focusing on ILWRM. Furthermore, 
the projects are being implemented in different areas of the country, BRCCJ in the Dead Sea Basin and the proposed 
project in the northern Jordan Valley — a detailed analysis of similarities, differences and complementarities is 
presented in Annex 29. This increases the scale of impact that GCF resources will have in Jordan, with the two 
projects collectively contributing to water security and climate resilient sustainable development in Jordan. As both 
projects focus on water, this further highlights the need for a paradigm shift in how water resources in Jordan are 
managed under conditions of climate change, the multitude of different, yet complementary, approaches which are 
required to do so, and the major funding gap that stands in the way of achieving adaptation priorities in the country.  
 
254. Given the funding gap between Jordan’s climate priorities and its public finances, adaptation will require a shift in 
national planning and budgeting (alongside international funding)248. Without GCF support, Jordan will continue to 
operate under the existing paradigm of an uncoordinated and compartmentalised model of land and water resources 
management that is not climate resilient. Under the current scenario, Jordan’s dwindling water resources will become 
increasingly vulnerable to the projected impacts of climate change. This will result in: i) reduced quantity and quality 
of water resources and water insecurity; ii) food insecurity; iii) increased desertification and degradation of rangelands 
and impacts on pastoral and rural communities’ livelihoods; iv) decreased supply of ecosystem goods and services; 
and v) increased conflict over water and other natural resources. Through GCF funding, however, these negative 
impacts of climate change will be avoided and a paradigm shift in the country towards climate-resilient sustainable 
development will be achieved249. This includes transforming from a compartmentalised, sector-specific approach to 
landscape management to one that is integrated, participatory and adaptation focussed, contributing to climate-
resilient sustainable development in the country. As a result, there will be: i) increased budgets allocated to 
adaptation; ii) multiple landscapes under climate-resilient ILWRM; iii) improved protection supply and use of water 
for domestic purposes under climate change conditions; and iv) behavioural change. 
 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
255. A framework of the proposed project’s Exit Strategy is presented in the paragraphs that follow, focusing on the role 
of the Water Fund, sustainability through technical and institutional capacity building, social and environmental 
sustainability, and financial sustainability. This framework will be further developed by the PMU during the last two 
years of project implementation through: i) taking stock of all project interventions for which O&M will be required in 
the long-term; ii) identifying which stakeholders will take over O&M of relevant interventions and signing agreements 
with them (if not already done so during implementation); iii) developing long-term O&M plans for the identified 
ILWRM interventions (if not already part of implementation); developing a scaling up and replication strategy for the 
project’s interventions; and iv) identifying sources of finance to cover the costs of long-term O&M, as well as to scale 
up and replicate its ILWRM interventions.  
 
256. A key financial source for the long-term O&M and scaling up and replication of the project’s ILWRM interventions in 
the Water Fund (Activity 2.1.4). The Water Fund will be designed specifically to generate financial flow to contribute 
to the sustained management of project interventions, as well as to fund their scaling up and replication. This will 
ensure that the benefit flows (particularly those related to water resources) generated through the implementation of 
the project are sustained. Included in the Water Fund’s design will be a grant mechanism to support the 
implementation of subprojects within the project’s ILWRM approach by WMCs and their members. During its

establishment phase, the Water Fund will become a ‘legal entity’ and long-term legal mechanisms such as contracts 
and bylaws will be formalised via the WMCs, ensuring its sustainability. During the Water Fund’s maturation phase, 
options for scaling up to other areas of Jordan will also be assessed through the development of a scaling up strategy. 
The scaling up strategy will link closely with a long-term financing strategy that will detail how financial resources for 
the Fund’s ongoing operation post GCF involvement will be leveraged from public and private sources. The long-
term financing strategy will include a detailed model describing how the Water Fund’s PES scheme will channel 
finance into the grant mechanism (which will include grant and results-based payments mechanisms). In addition, 
the development of the long-term financing strategy will consider how the Water Fund, once established and 
matured, could link to other financial instruments, such as EbA funds250, and development banks (e.g., the Islamic 
Development Bank), as well as leverage and unlock additional domestic finance and development banks which would 
contribute to ensuring long-term financial support for the fund — this will draw from experiences in other parts of the 
world. The leveraging of commitments from other financial instruments and partners will be fostered through proving 
the concept of the Water Fund through the project and developing the necessary enabling conditions for its long-
term success through the activities of Component 1. As the Water Fund is designed, established and matures, the 
project’s Exit Strategy for continued investment in and management of its ILWRM interventions will be built around 
it, resulting in the development of a rigid plan by project closure. It should be noted that the realisation of the scaling-
up scenarios presented in Section D.2. are not reliant on the Water Fund. The contribution of the project to the 
creation of an enabling environment (Component 1) that supports adaptation at scale is critical as it provides the 
necessary conditions. This includes strengthening the mandate and capacity of main government institutions and 
non-state actors for ILWRM in Jordan (Activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.4); mainstreaming ILWRM as an adaption approach 
into regulatory frameworks for land, water, agriculture and natural resources (Activity 1.1.2); and generating evidence 
of economic benefits of ILWRM (as an adaptation approach) for use by policy makers and planners (Activity 1.1.3). 
Through its policy-related activities in Component 1, the project will support budget reforms, increasing the allocation 
of public budgets to adaptation (ILWRM in particular), which would support upscaling of the interventions in the 
medium to long term. Additional details on the Water Fund’s contribution to financial sustainability are provided 
below. 
 
257. The financial, technical and social sustainability of interventions has been accounted for during the design process 
to deliver optimal climate change adaptation benefits to vulnerable communities in Jordan beyond the lifetime of the 
proposed project. Details of the specific steps taken to ensure long-term sustainability are presented below.   
 
Sustainability through technical and institutional capacity building 
258. The project will support the development of an enabling environment for the implementation of a climate-resilient 
ILWRM approach in Jordan, by strengthening governance and building institutional capacity at the national and local 
levels in a gender-responsive and -inclusive manner, focusing government attention, catalysing opportunities for 
mainstreaming, and addressing policies, plans and investment patterns that are currently barriers to adaptation in 
the sector. Institutional strengthening includes extending the mandate of the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA) to facilitate 
the uptake of ILWRM principles in the decision-making process for sustainable socio-economic development.  
Specifically, interventions under Activity 1.1.2 will include training of: i) policy- and decision-makers (at least 50% 
women) on the integration of climate resilient ILWRM into policies, plans and strategies; ii) national and sub-national 
planners on climate-resilient ILWRM planning; and iii) staff from relevant national and sub-national institutions (at 
least 50% women) on the use of climate data, the results of climate analyses, and risk assessment in policy- and 
decision-making as well as disaster risk management. These interventions will strengthen institutional capacity for 
adaptation actions and enhance the technical capacity of stakeholders for the planning and implementation of 
climate-resilient ILWRM.  Furthermore, through building the capacity of local partners such as MoEnv, MWI, MoA, 
and NGOs (Activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.4) for climate-resilient ILWRM, in-country capacity for accessing climate finance, 
including developing adaptation proposals for GCF support, will be enhanced. Technical capacity of stakeholders 
will also be strengthened as part of Activity 2.1.3, which involves training small-scale farmers in the three project 
sites on the use and maintenance of water conservation and harvesting measures (including at least 50% female-
headed households).  
 
259. To ensure that Jordan’s regulatory system is aligned with the overall objective of enhancing the resilience of its 
population to climate change, the country’s policy framework will be strengthened under Activity 1.1.2 — specifically 
through the incorporation of climate-resilient ILWRM. This will be achieved through the drafting of amendments to 
the country’s relevant legislation (such as Jordan’s Decentralisation Programme and National Development Plan), 
which will ensure that the ILWRM approach is sustained as part of the government’s development strategy under 
future climate conditions. The above activities will contribute to the sustainability of the project, particularly in 
supporting the development of an enabling environment for climate-resilient ILWRM in Jordan, by addressing 
 
250 Such a fund may include the Global EbA Fund, which is administered by UNEP and executed by IUCN: 
https://globalebafund.org

=== PAGE 85 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 81 of 144 
 
priorities identified in national policies and strategies (see Section 10 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study for specific details). 
This will focus government attention, catalysing opportunities for mainstreaming, and addressing policies, plans and 
investment patterns that are currently barriers to adaptation in the water sector. Targeting national and local 
processes in important national planning documents and policies, will also enhance project scalability and 
replicability. In addition, the GoJ will include the monitoring and analysis of the project’s on-the-ground interventions 
in their ongoing operations to contribute to the capacity building at the policy- and decision-making level and policy 
reform process under Activity 1.1.2 to integrate climate-resilient ILWRM into Jordan’s planning, policy-making and 
budgeting process. This will facilitate the embedding of coordination and operation of these mechanisms and 
structures in budgets and mandates beyond the life of the project. These plans will also include long-term funding 
commitments for enhancing the ecological infrastructure and maintaining the physical infrastructure associated with 
livelihood interventions.  Improved knowledge-management — such as the storage and dissemination of lessons 
learned and gender-responsive best practices on adaptation — under Activity 4.1.2 will complement policy reform 
through benefitting national and sub-national decision-making for climate change adaptation.  The abovementioned 
interventions will also ensure that best practices and lessons learned are incorporated into future adaptation actions.  
 
Social and environmental sustainability  
260. The proposed project has been developed and will be executed through a gender-inclusive participatory and 
consultative process, which will enable all relevant stakeholders to contribute to the design and implementation of 
Jordan’s climate change adaptation interventions. This approach will allow the project to be country-driven by 
enabling stakeholders at all levels to take ownership of project interventions (including vunerable groups such as 
women and youth), negating the need for a top-down approach to management and ensuring the long-term 
sustainability of interventions. The gender-responsive outreach programme undertaken as part of Activity 4.1.1 will 
secure the buy-in of stakeholders at the community level, which will ensure the continued maintenance of on-the-
ground interventions.  The outreach programme will also include facilitating the integration of climate change 
adaptation into school syllabi. This will promote the autonomous spread of adaptation awareness across the country, 
encourage the long-term maintenance and ownership of the project’s interventions, and provide a platform for the 
youth to enrol for climate changed-related studies at tertiary institutions in the future, and thereby contribute to 
climate-resilient sustainable development in Jordan. 
 
261. Under Activity 1.1.4, gender-inclusive WMCs will be established at each target watershed to facilitate dialogue 
between stakeholders in upstream and downstream areas, and further strengthen project ownership at the 
community level. This will ensure that local communities take ownership of interventions and that all stakeholders’ 
(including women) considerations are accounted for in decision-making that affects water resources they rely on in 
the long term, which is a paradigm shift in community participation in development projects. Once established, the 
project will work closely with the MoEnv (project Executing Entity) to formally recognize and include WMCs in relevant 
legislation — including defining their mandate related to working with communities. This will not only reinforce their 
contribution to the project and strengthen the participation of local stakeholders (communities in particular) but 
contribute to their sustainability and a paradigm shift in community participation in development projects. 
 
262. The passive restoration of ecosystems (Activity 2.1.2) will also contribute to both social and environmental 
sustainability as it is a traditional form of community-based natural resource management that places rangeland 
management into the purview of local pastoralist communities. This will similarly be the case for women-led 
community-managed nurseries established under Activity 2.1.2 that will afford local participants a greater sense of 
ownership in restoration interventions, increasing long-term care and maintenance of restored areas and improving 
survival rates of seedlings. The condition of ecological infrastructure to be established under active restoration 
interventions will be maintained by protecting the ecosystems, minimising human pressures and promoting natural 
ecological processes that facilitate recovery of ecosystems from a degraded state. Physical infrastructure, including 
rockfill dams and floating solar installations, will be maintained by ensuring that regular inspections take place, and 
that required repair work is undertaken timeously.  
 
263. The full socioeconomic and environmental benefits investments into ecosystem restoration as an adaptation 
approach are often only realised a decade or more after implementation. For this reason, the long-term data sets 
from the project will be archived within Jordanian research institutions and the long-term monitoring of the GCF 
project’s investments will also be institutionalised. Similarly, detailed long-term O&M plans will be developed for all 
ecological and physical infrastructure constructed by the project and embedded in the budgets of relevant 
government institutions. Government funding for O&M will include: i) financing of ongoing restoration, conservation 
and management of those ecosystems restored by the project; and ii) maintaining the mechanisms and tools 
established by the project.

=== PAGE 86 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 82 of 144 
 
264. An extensive gender-responsive stakeholder engagement plan, detailed in Annex 7, has also been developed to 
ensure that the proposed participatory approach is continued during implementation, fostering stakeholder buy-in 
and the long-term ownership of project interventions. Continued stakeholder engagements — including 
recommended participants from, inter alia, government ministries, the private sector, NGOs and representatives of 
local communities (including women’s groups); periods for engagements and a description of engagements required 
such as training, consultation and validation workshops — have been integrated into each relevant activity. This will 
ensure that all stakeholders are included in the implementation of relevant project activities which will contribute to 
the long-term sustainability of interventions. Gender-responsive community engagements during the project’s 
inception phase will focus on introducing and sensitising target communities to the project’s scope, objectives and 
expected results. The focus of these early engagements will be to: i) encourage buy-in; ii) ensure that there is a 
common understanding that the focus of the project is on climate change adaptation, avoiding any misconceptions 
that it is a poverty-alleviation or environmental protection initiative; and iii) manage community expectations. This will 
include making all relevant project documentation available — including translation into Arabic.  
 
265. Engagement of the private sector will centre on the Water Fund, focusing on fostering private sector buy-in for PES 
(improved water resources) allowing for the continued funding of climate-resilient ILWRM. This will include raising 
awareness on investing in climate-resilient ILWRM, via the Water Fund, in the long-term to climate-proof private 
sector enterprises and how PES will facilitate this. In addition, the private sector will be engaged via the proposed 
project climate change adaptation outreach programme under Output 3.1, to highlight the impacts of climate change 
on water resources and their operations, as well as the adaptation approaches being promoted to overcome current 
and future challenges.  
 
266. The impact and sustainability of adaptation interventions in Jordan will be further enhanced through the proposed 
project’s focus on ensuring that gender-equal benefits are experienced by beneficiaries. Gender-responsive targets 
and objectives for all project activities have therefore been set in the project’s Gender Action Plan (GAP; Annex 8) 
to ensure that project interventions are not gender-biased. This will contribute to the ownership of and involvement 
in project activities by both women and men which, in turn, will result in gender-responsive adaptation benefits being 
conferred beyond the project lifetime. 
 
Financial sustainability 
267. The project’s recommendations on amendments to Jordan’s policy framework under Activity 1.1.2 will promote the 
allocation of sufficient funds to climate change adaptation via government budgets and ensure domestic sources of 
finance are used for future adaptation initiatives.  Where project activities have ongoing costs, either related to 
operation or maintenance these have been examined through the economic analysis (Annex 3) to ensure that 
the beneficiaries of the interventions are sufficiently concentrated and benefit enough on an ongoing basis to have 
the incentive to ensure these costs are covered. In most cases this is both obvious and clear. For example, the 
expected value of electricity generated by solar installations far outweighs the anticipated ongoing costs, and the 
funds to pay for this should be generated directly; likewise, the alternative natural resource-based livelihoods 
examined are anticipated to be profitable at a household, over and above ongoing cost and hence at a household 
level maintenance of equipment etc will be incentive compatible. Similarly given anticipated levels of water scarcity, 
the value of rainwater harvested from public building and household is likely to exceed the costs of maintenance by 
a factor of five, implying a direct incentive for households / public authorities to dedicate the time / money to ensure 
the potential to harvest rainwater continues. 
 
268. Where there are more stakeholders involved both in delivering and receiving benefits, the key for financial stability 
will be having a mechanism in place to transfer resources from the beneficiaries to those bearing the costs of 
operation and maintenance. This is the case for the continued restoration and maintenance of ecosystems and 
rockfill dams for managed aquifer recharge in targeted watersheds. The value of additional ground water is expected 
to significantly exceed the ongoing costs of ecosystem restoration and dam maintenance, and the Water Fund is 
designed to channel resources from those who are willing and able to pay for increased ground water supplies to 
those whose actions within the landscape can deliver them. The operation of the Water Fund will be sustained 
through its operation and maturation phases that will be implemented under the project (Activity 2.1.4). The operation 
phase includes developing and implementing a work plan that guides: i) the systematic execution of activities; ii) 
M&E of activities; iii) communication of progress; and iv) improvement of the Water Fund through adaptive 
management. Results expected under this phase include an annual planning cycle that is aligned with the strategic 
plan, the ongoing implementation of interventions, development of a long-term strategy, routine review and 
communication of results as well as the application of corrective actions. Maturity of the fund will ensure its long-term 
viability. Safeguarding a sustainable and considerable impact on water security, the project will: i) secure long-term 
financing through fostering the support of the private sector for PES under the Water Fund; ii) integrate the Water 
Fund into the country’s legal framework; iii) monitor and evaluate progress systematically and apply adaptive

=== PAGE 87 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 83 of 144 
 
management where required; and iv) secure support from the public to maximise the positive impacts of the Water 
Fund. At the heart of the long-term financing strategy will be the Water Fund’s PES model, which will be used to 
foster medium to long-term investment in the Fund from major downstream water users. This will include securing 
agreements with major water users who are willing to pay for ongoing ecosystem management in return for the water 
resources generated. Payers for ongoing ecosystem management in upstream watersheds would include major 
water users in downstream areas of the Jordan Valley who would benefit from and rely on increased water resources 
generated via the Fund’s interventions in upstream ecosystems. Under the maturation phase, long-term financing 
will be committed, routine reports released to document the Water Fund’s ongoing impacts, the influence of the 
Water Fund demonstrated, and positive public opinion generated. As a result, financial sustainability beyond project 
finance is designed into the activities covered.

C.3. Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 pages) 
C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities?

C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer?

Technology transfer    
269. Under Outputs 2.1 and 3.1 of the proposed project, technology transfer will be facilitated through the: i) climate-
resilient restoration and management of ecosystems in target watersheds in the Jordan Valley as part of an EbA 
approach (Activity 2.1.2);  ii) introduction of gender-responsive climate-resilient natural resource-based livelihoods 
(Activity 2.1.3); iv) introduction of small-scale water-harvesting interventions (Activity 3.1.3); and v) implementation of  
floating solar panels (Activity 3.1.2).  
  
Capacity-building interventions   
270. Technical and institutional capacity of governmental and non-governmental actors involved in landscape management, 
as well as local communities (at least 50% female participation) for climate-resilient Integrated Landscape and Water 
Resources Management (ILWRM) will be strengthened under all three of the project’s outputs. In addition, Output 
1.1 will include interventions with the objective of strengthening the policy and legal framework for adaptation — with 
a focus on ILWRM — in Jordan. These interventions will involve enhancing the mandate and capacity of 
key stakeholders for gender-responsive participatory climate-resilient ILWRM in the Jordan Valley (Activity 1.1.1 and 
1.1.3). Under this activity, training of stakeholders (including at least 50% women) on climate-resilient 
watershed management (IWM) will be undertaken, and guidelines, standards and protocols for IWM developed. The 
output will also include drafting of amendments to Jordan’s land and water-management policy, planning and legal 
frameworks for 
the promotion 
of 
climate-resilient 
ILWRM as an EbA 
solution (Activity 
1.1.2). Under 
this 
activity training will be provided to policy- and decision-makers (at least 50% women) on integration of climate-resilient 
ILWRM practices into policies, plans and strategies. Training of relevant national and sub-national planners (at least 
50% women) on climate-resilient ILWRM planning will also be undertaken. In addition, staff from relevant national and

=== PAGE 90 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 86 of 144 
 
sub-national institutions (at least 50% women) will be trained to use climate data, risk assessments, and the results of 
climate analyses, in policy-and decision-making processes as well as in planning and disaster risk management.  
 
271. Capacity-building under Output 2.1 will include training of local communities and small-scale farmers (up to 75% 
women) on the climate-resilient additional livelihoods and improved farming methods. Under Output 3.1, local 
communities will be trained to use and maintain water harvesting technologies for domestic use (up to 50% female-
headed households). In addition, community members (at least 50% women) will be trained in additional 
livelihoods related to the monitoring and management of water.  
 
D. EXPECTED PREFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA 
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s 
Initial Investment Framework. 
 
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1page) 
 
272. The proposed project will contribute to the GCF’s fund-level adaptation objective of increased climate-resilient 
sustainable development by facilitating a shift towards conserving and augmenting water resources in Jordan. This 
shift will be realised via climate-resilient ILWRM. Along with improving the sustainability of water resources, ILWRM 
will climate proof agricultural and natural resource-based livelihoods, improving the climate resilience of local 
communities. This will be accomplished through the GCF’s fund-level impacts and outcomes presented below251. 
 
GCF Fund-level Impacts252 
• 
ARA 1 — Most vulnerable peoples and communities;  
• 
ARA 2 — Health, well-being, food and water-security; and 
• 
ARA 4 — Ecosystems and ecosystem services. 
 
273. The project will strengthen the mandates and capacities of relevant Jordanian institutions to implement climate-resilient 
ILWRM. This will be achieved through several elements of project design. First, proposed amendments to land-
management policy, planning and legal frameworks will be drafted to promote climate-resilient ILWRM. The drafting 
of these amendments will be preceded by the training of planners, as well as policy- and decision-makers on using 
ILWRM for planning and decision-making processes in the water and land sectors. The training will lay the foundation 
for the preparation of policy briefs with recommendations for the integration of ILWRM into relevant policies and plans. 
Second, Watershed Management Committees (WMCs) will be established in target watersheds to foster participatory 
ILWRM and dialogue between stakeholders253. Third, training on ILWRM, will be delivered to relevant stakeholders to 
strengthen capacity for the participatory implementation of the approach as a climate change adaptation measure. 
Following this training, participatory ILWRM plans will be developed for the project’s three target watersheds. Finally, 
ILWRM will be facilitated through the development of land-use guidelines, standards and protocols.   
 
274. Moreover, under the proposed project, a Water Fund will be established to facilitate investment in ongoing climate-
resilient management and restoration of upper watershed areas. This will not only promote the continued maintenance 
of restoration activities and improvement of water resources in these areas but also foster a participatory approach to 
watershed management. Water use and supply will be improved at the domestic and agricultural levels by: i) 
introducing small-scale water harvesting infrastructure at the community- and household-level; ii) empowering 
communities through education and training to monitor and manage water supply and demand; and iii) introducing in-
field water conservation and harvesting measures on smallholder farms. 
 
275. The project will strengthen the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities (including groups such as women and 
youth) in the Jordan Valley through the: i) climate-resilient restoration of degraded watershed areas, as well as 
diversification and strengthening of livelihoods in a gender-responsive manner; and ii) improved protection and 
provision of water resources under climate change conditions. The ecosystem restoration interventions will result in 
an improved supply of ecosystem services — including the regulation of water resources and reduction of soil erosion. 
In addition, ecosystem restoration combined with managed aquifer recharge (MAR) will result in improved rates of 
aquifer recharge. These interventions will be complemented by the installation of 1 MW of floating solar panels over 
the King Abdullah Canal, reducing evaporative water loss (see Section 8.7.3 of Annex 2). Additionally, rainwater-
 
251 Detailed information on the assumptions and estimates of the impact potential of the proposed project are provided in the 
project’s results framework and economic analysis (Section E of this document and Annex 3, respectively). 
252 A detailed description of the project activities that will be implemented under each output to achieve the GCF project 
outcomes is presented in Section E.6 of the Funding Proposal. 
253 Framework ToR describing the role and functions of WMCs are provided in Annex 28 and will be further developed by 
project Technical Partners during project inception.

=== PAGE 91 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 87 of 144 
 
harvesting interventions will enhance the provision of clean water resources to communities with a focus on female-
headed households (see Section 8.6 of Annex 2). As a result, reduced water stress will buffer local communities both 
upstream and downstream of project sites to the exposure of climate risks such as droughts. The project will also 
increase adaptive capacity by climate-proofing agricultural livelihoods and identifying additional gender-responsive 
climate-resilient natural resource-based livelihoods. Moreover, additional gender-responsive livelihoods related to the 
monitoring and management of water in target communities will be adopted — including plumbing and installation of 
solar pumps.  
 
276. Climate change adaptation awareness will be increased through the implementation of a gender-responsive outreach 
programme targeting local institutions and communities. In addition, access for decision-makers to relevant information 
related to effective adaptation will also be improved. The outreach programme will include: i) equipping community 
centres and local institutions to serve as climate vulnerability and information hubs; ii) identifying a public figure to 
champion the programme nationally; and iii) enhancing awareness of appropriate adaptation responses. At the 
institutional level, knowledge-management tools will be developed to facilitate the mainstreaming of gender-responsive 
adaptation best practices and lessons learned into decision-making processes. This will be complemented by the 
establishment of an adaptation-knowledge repository to increase access to best practices and lessons learned. 
 
Adaptation impact 
277. Project interventions will directly benefit a total of 246,942 people (~2.2% of the Jordanian population) — 135,736 men 
(55%) and 111,206 women (45%) — in the Jordan Valley by reducing their climate vulnerability. Of this total: i) 62,446 
(30,607 women and 31,839 men) are from the Yarmouk site — Khaled Bin Al Waleed and Al Seru municipalities; ii) 
105,841 (45,179 women and 60,662 men) are from the King Talal Dam site — New Ardah, Deir Alla and M’addi 
municipalities; and iii) 78,655 (35,420 women and 43,235 men) are from the Kafrain site — Shouna Alwasta and Mahes 
municipalities. Direct beneficiaries include all people who will benefit directly from a project activity or the result of an 
activity. For example, under Output 3.2, 1,200 smallholder farming households (~6,000 people — an average of 5 
people per smallholder household) across the three sites will benefit from on-farm interventions, while 2,800 
households across the sites (~14,000 people) will benefit directly from rainwater harvesting infrastructure implemented 
on houses. Direct beneficiaries also include all vulnerable peoples in the project’s three target areas, as they are all 
expected to benefit from the augmentation of water resources under Outputs 2.1, 2.2 and 3.1, either via improvements 
in water supplies for domestic and/or agricultural usage. Under Outcome 3, all 172,179 people living in the 
municipalities of the three target sites (and related municipalities) listed above will benefit from the improved protection, 
supply and use of water for domestic purposes under climate change conditions. Specifically, they will directly benefit 
from enhancements in the quality and quantity of ground and surface water through Activity 3.1.1. This will be achieved 
through the construction of rockfill dams to support managed aquifer recharge and the rehabilitation of active 
freshwater springs. As integrated land and water resources management interventions (ILWRM) are scaled up via the 
Water Fund (Activity 2.1.4), it is expected that an additional 4,000 ha of land (bringing the total to 12,000 ha) will be 
placed under improved management for water resources benefits, with at least one additional (adjacent) municipality 
per watershed site benefitting directly. Consequently, an additional 74,763 people (41,604 men and 33,159 women)254 
are expected to benefit directly from the project. See Annex 33 for additional beneficiary calculation details. 
 
278. 756,000 (48% women) people across the broader Jordan Valley will benefit indirectly from: i) the attenuation of water 
resources in the northern Jordan Valley; ii) strengthened local governance to implement climate-resilient ILWRM; iii) 
reduced water loss through evaporation from the King Abdullah Canal; and iv) improved awareness of the impacts of 
climate change and the adaptation options to address them. Indirect beneficiaries include people in the northern Jordan 
Valley who will not directly benefit from a project activity, or result of an activity. This includes all people located outside 
of the project’s three target sites in the northern Jordan Valley. 
 
279. Interventions at the regulatory and institutional level will indirectly benefit the Jordanian population as a whole, 
amounting to ~10 million people. Project interventions, such as the establishment of a Water Fund in the Jordan Valley, 
will also facilitate public and private sector investment in climate-resilient ILWRM. The Water Fund will serve as a 
mechanism to sustain the climate-resilient management and restoration of the Jordan Valley’s watershed areas in the 
long term by maintaining and attenuating water resources under conditions of climate change. Public and private sector 
beneficiaries of improved water resources will contribute to their maintenance through financial contribution to the 
Water Fund. This will contribute to creating an enabling environment for future upscaling and replication of climate 
change adaptation interventions locally, nationally and regionally.

Potential water resource benefits  
280. Under the proposed project, water supplies in the Jordan Valley will be improved through the implementation of several 
interventions across the project area. These interventions will include: i) the restoration of watershed ecosystems; ii) 
managed aquifer recharge; iii) rooftop and in-field rainwater harvesting; and iv) the installation of floating solar panels. 
The benefits of each of these interventions to Jordan’s water supply are summarised in Table 10 and the narrative 
below. Further details are provided in Section 8 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

Climate-resilient restoration in watersheds 
281. Increasing vegetative cover across the project’s intervention sites will reduce runoff and the subsequent loss of topsoil 
from watersheds caused by soil erosion. This will decrease sedimentation levels in downstream rivers or dams and 
lower the rate at which their storage capacity is reduced. For example, the Hima system has the potential to increase 
the storage capacity of the King Talal Dam by ~7,687,300 CM over a period of 25 years through a reduction in sediment 
loading of 5,490,931 tonnes256. Using similar interventions across the project’s 12,000 ha (Activity 2.1.2) could similarly 
increase dam storage capacity by 2.2 MCM over 25 years. Upscaling this intervention over 500,000 ha is predicted to 
increase the storage capacity of Jordan’s dams by 157.5 MCM over the same period. Improved vegetative cover will 
also result in greater surface roughness, reducing runoff and the speed of overland water flow, improving water 
infiltration, and consequently, soil moisture content and aquifer recharge rates. Total aquifer recharge is expected to 
increase by up to 35.3 CM/ha/yr257. This extrapolates to an additional average of 0.18 MCM of water infiltrating into 
aquifers annually258. Additional benefits of the increased infiltration will include the reduced rates of evaporation, 
reduced potential of flooding in downstream areas, and particularly, sustained basal flow of rivers, which will reduce 
the need of farmers downstream to extract water for irrigation from underground sources during dry periods.  
 
Managed aquifer recharge (MAR) 
282. The project’s ILWRM approach will also include the construction of small-scale water harvesting infrastructure for MAR 
in wadi areas to collect and store water from runoff and flash floods (Activity 3.1.1.1). Interventions will include rockfill 
dams of up to 20 m wide with potential catchments of up to 0.9 MCM/yr. With potential infiltration rates of ~0.02 mm/s, 
up to ~0.63 MCM of this amount will contribute to annual aquifer recharge259. Four rockfill dams will be constructed 
across each of the project’s three target areas (12 dams in total), resulting in a combined annual potential aquifer 
recharge of ~7.6 MCM/yr. Additional information on MAR is available in Section 11.2 of Annex 2. 
 
Rainwater harvesting 
283. Under Activity 2.1.3, in-field water conservation and harvesting measures will be implemented on smallholder farms. 
These interventions are not intended to improve aquifer recharge, but rather to increase soil moisture levels to support 
improved agricultural production and reduce pressure on underground water sources. Rooftop harvesting will be 
implemented across the project target area in the Jordan Valley (Activity 3.1.3). Potential for rainwater harvesting in 
the Balqa and Irbid governates is estimated to be 1.7 and 3.3 MCM/year, respectively260,261. Under the proposed 
project, 3,000 rainwater harvesting tanks (with a capacity of 32 CM per tank) will be installed, resulting in a minimum 
annual yield of 0.28 MCM/year. More information relating to in-field and rooftop rainwater-harvesting interventions is 
presented in Section 8.6 of Annex 2. 
 
Construction of floating solar panels (evaporation reduction) 
284. Evaporation reduction interventions will include the establishment of at least a 1 MW floating solar panel installation 
over the King Abdullah Canal within the project intervention area (Activity 3.1.2). This will prevent the evaporation of 
800,000 CM of water annually from the canal and provide ~1 MW of power to the MWI’s pump stations on the canal262.  
 
285. Overall, the project’s interventions are expected to improve water supply in the project implementation area in the 
Jordan Valley by up to 9.3 MCM/year by the end of project implementation (eight years) and potentially up to 83.8 
MCM/year after 25 years. While this increase is relatively small in comparison to the current and projected deficits, 
there is considerable scope for upscaling these interventions across the country through the enabling environment for 
climate-resilient ILWRM that will be created by the project. 
 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

286. Transformative change to climate-resilient integrated land and water resources management (ILWRM) in Jordan is 
currently inhibited by several barriers (as detailed in Section B.2.: Theory of Change), including: i) Insufficient 
institutional and technical coordination capacity, as well as limited mandates of state and non-state actors for climate 
change adaptation, including climate-resilient ILWRM; ii) Limited access to climate change adaptation information and 
data; iii) Insufficient awareness by politicians, community leaders, decision-makers and the public of the impacts of 
climate change and appropriate adaptation responses; iv) Insufficient financial capacity for climate change adaptation; 
and v) Limited awareness amongst decision-makers and farmers of the financial and economic effectiveness of 
dryland-adapted water management, including the management of green water, in Jordan as opposed to unsustainable 
abstraction of groundwater. The project intended approach to removing these barriers is summarised in Figure 15 
below — additional detail is provided in the project’s Theory of Change in Section B.2.  
 
287. Through the removal of the barriers presented above, the project will result in: i) strengthened governance and capacity 
for climate-resilient ILWRM; ii) climate-resilient ILWRM practices and ecosystem restoration implemented in three 
watersheds in the Jordan Valley; iii) improved protection, supply and use of water for domestic purposes under climate 
change conditions; iv) improved knowledge and awareness of climate change adaptation for land and water resources 
management; and v) enhanced availability of financial resources for long-term operations and maintenance of 
interventions, and for upscaling EbA across other parts of Jordan. The outcomes of these results will be increased 
public budgets for adaptation, 8,000 ha of land under climate-resilient ILWRM, improved protection, supply and use of 
water for domestic purposes under climate change conditions and household behavioural change through improved 
information outreach. This will catalyse an institutional paradigm shift within Jordan’s water sector from a 
compartmentalised, sector-specific approach to land and water resources management to one that is integrated, 
participatory and adaptation focussed, contributing to climate-resilient sustainable development in the country. For 
example, networks of non-state actors, including NGOs, will be leveraged as constituencies of change through their 
participation in, inter alia, WMCs — to facilitate dialogue and participatory watershed management between upstream 
and downstream stakeholders (Activity 1.1.4) — and outreach programmes that will serve as awareness-raising 
campaigns (Activity 4.1.1). A key transformative aspect of the project will be the establishment of a Water Fund (Activity 
2.1.4), which will leverage GCF resources to crowd-in private and public sector investment into EbA, thereby 
generating financial flows for the maintenance and upscaling of climate-resilient ILWRM interventions implemented by 
the project. The Water Fund will also serve as model that can be replicated in other areas of the country, transforming 
the water sector’s approach to ILWRM. Importantly, the project will work closely with the recently approved GCF-
financed “Building resilience to cope with climate change through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture 
sector (BRCCJ)” or FP155263 (see Section B.1., Table 1 and Annex 29). The project’s goal is to increase the climate 
resilience of water management systems and the farming community in four governates located in the Dead Sea Basin. 
The different, yet complementary, scope of BRCCJ allows the two projects to collectively contribute to the long-term 
climate-resilient transformation of Jordan’s water sector to one that promotes ILWRM and improves agricultural water 
use efficiency, supported by innovative financing models such as the Water Fund. 
 
288. In terms of the addressing of gender issues, the project will support transformational change both in the lives of female 
beneficiaries and in prompting gender mainstreaming in national climate change adaptation policy-making and 
implementation. The project seeks to promote gender equality by including actions and targets which will increase 
women’s participation, representation and awareness, as well as ensuring that training programmes are gender-
responsive and -inclusive. A primary objective of the project with regards to gender is increased resilience to climate 
change of at least 77,080 women in the project area. This will be achieved through direct improvement of women’s 
capacities for improved water supply and demand management though climate-resilient ILWRM practices. 
 
 
 
263 GCF. 2021. Building resilience to cope with climate change in Jordan through improving water use efficiency in the 
agriculture sector (BRCCJ). Available at: https://www.greenclimate.fund/project/fp155
