Upscaling potential of proposed water resource augmentation interventions 
289. Several scenarios, ranging from conservative to optimistic, have been presented in the table below for the upscaling 
potential of each of the identified interventions. Potential impact (in MCM) under each of the scenarios has been 
estimated after 8-, 20- and 25-year periods. Ecosystem restoration (12,000 ha under the with-project scenario) is the 
only intervention with an increase in cumulative impact over time, while the other interventions have fixed impact 
potentials, which do not change over time. Should the proposed project’s ecosystem restoration interventions be 
upscaled across the entire northern Jordan Valley (1,147,600 ha), it is predicted that annual streamflow/water yield will 
increase by ~16.8% (~186.5 MCM/year) compared with values under the BAU scenario in the area. Similarly, annual 
percolation changes are projected to increase by ~10.6% (~49.4 MCM/year) under an upscaling scenario. This 
indicates the considerable contribution that ecosystem restoration could have of the water resources of the northern 
Jordan Valley in the long term — more information on the upscaling potential of the project’s ecosystem restoration 
interventions is presented in Section 10.7 of Annex 2. Jordan's current water deficit is 224 MCM/yr. By 2040, it is 
expected to reach 687 MCM/yr. With regards to the extrapolations: the potential cumulative increase in water resources 
over 25 years by upscaling the project interventions is 34 MCM under a conservative scenario, and 344 MCM under 
an optimistic scenario (Table 11). The scaling up scenarios presented in Table 11, are indicative of what is possible 
should an enabling environment for climate-resilient ILWRM in Jordan be established and the required conditions 
created for the proposed interventions to be implemented at scale (see the description of Component 1 in Section B.3. 
for more detail on the project’s approach to strengthening the enabling environment for climate-resilient ILWRM). 
 
290. In terms of the managed aquifer recharge via rockfill dams, there may be potential limitations related to their scalability. 
These are related to the suitability of sites and potential social and environmental risks related to this type of 
intervention. Details on the selection of sites for rockfill dams, including criteria and potential risks to consider are 
presented in Annex 2: Section 8.12. Site feasibility assessments would therefore be required to confirm the scaling up 
potential as presented below.

Potential for upscaling, replication and strengthened regulatory frameworks 
291. The proposed project has been designed to maximise the potential for upscaling and replication of interventions to 
enhance the impact of the project across Jordan and the surrounding region. This potential will be reached through 
the establishment of standards and protocols for the adoption of climate-resilient integrated landscape and water 
resources management (ILWRM) interventions to be implemented under this project that can be readily scaled up and 
replicated at the national and sub-national level. Potential for upscaling, replication and strengthened regulatory 
frameworks through the proposed project will be realised primarily through its innovative approach to ILWRM. This 
approach focuses on the integration of landscape and water resources management into a single approach for climate 
change adaption in the Jordan Valley, ensuring complementarity between interventions that were historically 
implemented in isolation. Upscaling, replication and the strengthening of regulatory frameworks will be supported by 
inter alia: i) introducing a Water Fund as a long-term financing mechanism (an approach not yet introduced in Jordan); 
ii) developing land use guidelines, standards and protocols for climate-resilient integrated watershed management in 
the Jordan Valley addressing of current and future climate risks through improved use of the results of climate risk 
analyses; iii) training staff from relevant national and sub-national institutions to use climate data, and the results of 
climate analyses and risk assessments in policy- and decision-making processes, as well as planning and disaster risk 
management; iv) training policy- and decision-makers, and planners on the integration of climate-resilient ILWRM 
practices into policies, plans and strategies; v) reviewing Jordan’s national land-management policies, plans and 
legislation to identify gaps and opportunities for the consideration of climate-resilient ILWRM as an adaptation 
approach; vi) implementing a climate change adaptation outreach programme across the Jordan Valley; vii) improving 
knowledge management for climate change adaptation in Jordan to support national and sub-national decision-making, 
and build communities of practice; and viii) generating evidence of the economic benefits of ILWRM to project 
beneficiaries for use by policymakers and planners. 
 
292. The widescale adoption of an innovative ILWRM approach to be delivered under the proposed project will be supported 
by: i) amendments to Jordan’s water and land management policy, planning and legal frameworks; ii) strengthening 
the mandate and capacity of major government agencies and non-state actors for ILWRM; iii) developing land-use 
guidelines, standards, protocols and participatory climate-resilient watershed management plans for the Jordan Valley 
that will maximise the social, environmental and economic benefits of ILWRM and can be replicated in other areas; iv) 
establishing an outreach programme championed by several public figures, to promote climate change adaptation in 
Jordan amongst all demographics; and v) improving the creation or strengthening of knowledge generation and 
collective learning in the areas of ILWRM and EbA across local communities in the Jordan Valley. Specifically, 
knowledge can be spread autonomously to surrounding areas and communities through the mobility of migrant workers 
and WMC members, thereby creating opportunities for scaling up and replication. Moreover, the project will develop a 
training programme for all relevant state, non-state, municipal, NGO and community stakeholders on climate-resilient 
ILWRM, thereby building technical and institutional capacity for the application of ILWRM principles. This will facilitate 
the wide-scale replication of climate-resilient ILWRM at the national and sub-national level. Long-term sustainability of 
the upscaling strategy will be ensured through the establishment of a Water Fund under the project, which will serve 
as a mechanism for leveraging financial resources for the restoration of watersheds and for sustaining the supply of 
ecosystem services from these areas. The Water Fund will allow for downstream stakeholders who are benefitting 
from the increased water supply to channel finances to upstream stakeholders, facilitating the ongoing maintenance 
and upscaling of EbA interventions such as ecosystem restoration and other interventions as detailed in Section E.6. 
Please see the description of Activity 2.1.4 under Section B.3 for a discussion of implications for downstream and

=== PAGE 97 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 93 of 144 
 
upstream beneficiaries of the Water Fund. During the Water Fund’s maturation phase, options for scaling up and 
replication in other areas of Jordan will also be assessed through the development of a scaling up strategy. The scaling 
up strategy will link closely with a long-term financing strategy that will detail how financial resources for the Fund’s 
ongoing operation post GCF involvement will be leveraged from public and private sources. The long-term financing 
strategy will include a detailed model describing how the Water Fund’s PES scheme will continue to channel finance 
into the grant mechanism (which will include grant and results-based payments mechanism) once GCF finance is no 
longer available post project. The establishment and demonstration of a Water Fund through the proposed project can 
catalyze the establishment of similar funds, not only in Jordan, but also in the Middle East and North Africa (MENA) 
region as a whole, where very few Water Funds have been establishment. This will potentially change the approach 
to water resources management under climate change conditions across the MENA region. 
 
Potential for knowledge sharing and learning 
293. Jordan’s National Capacity Self-Assessment (NCSA) report published in 2007, identified low capacity for knowledge 
management as a significant constraint to climate change adaptation. Moreover, several other capacity gaps exist in 
areas related to knowledge sharing and awareness, including inter alia: i) the absence of a national database and 
monitoring system of desertification; ii) the lack of a national system for knowledge management and data processing 
for monitoring and reporting on biodiversity; and iii) insufficient long-term awareness and education programmes for 
biodiversity management269. The proposed project will improve current knowledge-management systems for climate 
change adaptation in Jordan by developing several knowledge-management tools that will increase policymakers, 
planners, and local communities’ (including women and youth) access to information on climate-resilient ILWRM 
approaches and their benefits. This will include the establishment of a national knowledge repository of lessons learned 
and gender-responsive best practices for climate change adaptation, focussing on the LIWRM approach proposed 
under this project. These knowledge-management tools will enhance the technical capacity of decision-makers to 
integrate ILWRM into national and sub-national plans and strategies. This technical capacity which will be further 
strengthened through the training of user groups in the use of these knowledge management tools. Moreover, the 
centralised nature of these tools will build communities of practice, thereby improving the generation and dissemination 
of knowledge under the project and other adaptation initiatives through the social nature of learning between groups 
of people with common concerns (such as women’s groups), who can learn from each other as they interact regularly. 
The accessibility and reach of the knowledge systems implemented through this project will be enhanced through the 
implementation of a gender-responsive outreach programme within the Jordan Valley on climate-resilient ILWRM. The 
outreach programme will include the establishment of local knowledge hubs to improve awareness of the impacts of 
climate change, both generally and more specifically on water resources, and appropriate adaptation responses among 
vulnerable communities (including women and youth). To support the outreach programme, local staff and public 
figures will be trained to provide information through the community centres and to champion the case for climate 
change adaptation, respectively. Additionally, relevant educational practitioners (including 50% women) will be trained 
on integrating climate change adaptation into the syllabi and curricula of Jordanian universities. The training of local 
staff, public figures and educational practitioners to support the outreach programme assist in the scaling up and 
sustainability of knowledge sharing beyond the proposed project’s scope.  
 
Potential for the creation of an enabling environment 
294. The creation of an enabling environment for IWRM in Jordan is essential for the sustainability of the abovementioned 
upscaling and replication strategies. The enabling environment will encompass strengthened governance and 
institutional capacity for climate change adaptation within which interventions implemented under the proposed project 
are sustained in the long term, with support from all levels of government. To achieve this, the project will establish 
WMCs within each of the three target watersheds which will facilitate dialogue and cooperation between upstream and 
downstream stakeholders and ensure the continuation of an integrated approach to land management at the project 
sites in the long-term. The effectiveness of the WMCs will be further enhanced by strengthening the mandate and 
capacity of major government agencies — including the Secretary Generals of the MoEnV, MoA and Ministry of Water 
and Irrigation (MWI) — and non-state actors to coordinate an integrated approach to watershed management. The 
continued funding of climate-resilient IWRM in Jordan will be facilitated by the mainstreaming of this adaptation 
approach into the national budget through the recommendations made during project implementation for amendments 
to relevant land-management legislation. In addition, the establishment of a Water Fund will generate sustainable 
financial resources to support the continued funding of project interventions at the local level. 
 
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
295. The proposed project will contribute towards achieving nine of the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), namely: 
SDG 2 — Zero hunger; SDG 3 — Good health and well-being; SDG 5 — Gender equality; SDG 6 — Clean water and 
 
269 Ministry of Environment. 2007. National Capacity Self Assessment for Global Environmental Management (NCSA) – Jordan.

sanitation; SDG 8 — Decent work and economic growth; SDG 10 — Reducing inequality; SDG 12 — Responsible 
consumption and production; SDG 15 — Life on land; and SDG 16 — Peace, justice, and strong institutions. In addition, 
project interventions will produce numerous environmental, social and economic co-benefits through a gender-
responsive and -responsive development approach, as described below. 
 
Environmental, social and economic co-benefits 
296. The climate-resilient restoration of watershed ecosystems along with the implementation of integrated landscape and 
water resources management (ILWRM) in project intervention areas will yield numerous environmental, social and 
economic benefits, which are presented below and summarised in Table 12.  
 
• 
Strengthened flood attenuation. Jordan’s current sporadic rainfall and intense storm events result in flash flooding 
and widespread soil erosion270. This causes multiple problems for local communities, such as deaths, damage to 
infrastructure, and destruction of croplands, both as a direct and indirect result of flooding. These disasters lead to the 
disruption of water purification systems and the contamination of sewage disposal systems, which in turn increases 
the likelihood of epidemics caused by vector-, water- and food-borne diseases271. Frequent flooding of downstream 
areas is also caused by increased rates of runoff and overland flow during heavy rainfall events, which is partly 
attributable to degradation of ecosystems. This is expected to be intensified by the impacts of climate change. By 
improving vegetative cover in restoration areas across the project’s intervention sites, there will be greater rainfall 
interception, reduced volume of runoff and speed of overland water flow compared with a BAU scenario. This will lead 
to a significant reduction in the flooding of downstream areas and the associated impacts.  
• 
Enhanced food security, livelihood resilience and biodiversity through the restoration of degraded habitats. 
Despite its high land- and water-usage, the agricultural sector in Jordan supplies around only 19% of the country’s 
food requirements, with the average Jordanian family spending ~40% of its income on food, much of which is imported. 
Additionally, with a baseline demand for fodder of ~790,000 tonnes/yr across the country, production of fodder within 
Jordan is unable to match demand. National biodiversity is being negatively impacted by the expansion of agricultural 
and pastoral land because of low yields of major crops and decreasing livestock and agricultural productivity, as well 
as, inter alia, unsustainable hunting, tourism and deforestation. The ILWRM approach will enhance the conservation 
of biodiversity and ecosystems, which include pastoral rangelands and areas surrounding agricultural land, thereby 
increasing the availability of fodder for livestock272. For example, after two years of passive Hima restoration in the 
Zarqa River basin, the dry yield of cereals for fodder in a rainfed systems increased from an average of 40 
kg/ha/yr to 113 kg/ha/yr. The overall avoided costs for supplementary fodder purchases using this system across 
109,000 ha of the Zarqa River Basin is likely to amount to US$23 million after 25 years. 
• 
Decreased pollution of water resources which will filter runoff, prevent erosion and slow sedimentation. Not only 
will decreased sedimentation of dams through reduced runoff and erosion increase the storage capacity of dams, 
but it will also reduce the associated maintenance costs and increase the lifespan of these structures. Additionally, 
the promotion of climate-resilient agricultural practices, such as permaculture, will reduce the need for fertilisers 
that negatively contribute to pollution levels. By improving infiltration through active and passive restoration, 
freshwater and riparian habitats will be less affected by pollutants such as heavy metals or fertilisers which cause 
eutrophication. Moreover, soil quality will improve, and health risks posed by nitrates, which may lead to 
numerous gastrointestinal illnesses, diarrhoea, cancer and methaemoglobinemia273,274, will be reduced. Increasing 
the water quality of existing water sources will be complemented by improving access to better health and 
sanitation through the enhanced supply of clean water as a result of the introduction of small-scale rainwater-
harvesting interventions. Moreover, by providing alternative clean water sources in the form of these rainwater 
harvesting interventions, conflict over resources between host and refugee communities is expected to decrease. 
• 
Energy generation and reduced evaporation of water from floating solar panels. Without protective coverings, 
water storage facilities are affected by incoming solar radiation and wind action, resulting in evaporation losses. 
Floating covers will be used to substantially negate these processes and trap water vapour below the cover, increasing 
humidity and further preventing evaporation. This method of solar energy generation has not previously been used in 
Jordan, but has considerable potential for contributing to the country’s aim of increasing its production of renewable 
energy. This will contribute to reducing MWI’s reliance on fossil-fuel generated energy to supply it with pumping 
stations, contributing to a transformative change in energy supply across the water sector in Jordan to one that is low 
emission. The floating solar system to be installed over the King Abdullah Canal can produce 1 MW of power and 
 
270 Weather Online. 1999. Jordan. Available at: https://www.weatheronline.co.uk/reports/climate/Jordan.htm. 
271 Ibid. 
272 NWRM. No date. Afforestation of reservoir catchments. Available at: http://nwrm.eu/sites/default/files/nwrm_ressources/f3_-
_afforestation_of_reservoir_catchments_0.pdf  
273 Methaemoglobinemia or ‘blue baby syndrome’ mainly affects infants and affects the blood’s ability to carry oxygen to various 
part of the body.

intensive agricultural area. American Journal of Applied Sciences 10: 442–448.

Gender-responsive development impacts 
297. The proposed project will support a transformation in the water sector to one in which women are included in the 
decision-making for and management of water resources at the community level, including being involved in monitoring 
of water resource us and maintenance of community-level water infrastructure. This will allow for their specific water-
related climate-change adaptation needs to be addressed. The project’s community-level gender-responsive approach 
also includes promoting gender-inclusive climate-resilient livelihoods that will benefit female-headed households (can 
be implemented at home and allow for the strengthening of adaptive capacity). Tailored awareness-raising and 
outreach programmes will also allow women to become leaders in the promotion of climate change adaptation in their 
communities. 
• 
Given the prevailing gender and social norms in Jordan, as well as challenges related to gendered vulnerability to 
climate impacts (see Section 1.2 in Annex 2), the proposed project’s objective of climate-resilient ILWRM will result in 
gender-responsive 
sustainable 
development. The

and

implementation of the proposed project is a high priority for the Jordanian government. The provision of gender-equal 
benefits to men and women, as outlined in the gender action plan (GAP), will contribute to equal ownership of project 
activities and will result in gender-responsive adaptation benefits being conferred beyond the project lifetime. The GAP 
additionally outlines gender-disaggregated impacts, outcomes and output statements, gender-responsive activities, 
and gender-disaggregated performance indicators and targets. As a result, the proposed project will contribute to the 
achievement of several of the sustainable development goals — most notably SDG 5 (Gender equality). 
• 
The project’s GAP outlines actions to reduce the potential to perpetuate or worsen gender inequality in Jordan, by inter 
alia, ensuring : i) women’s participation in training equal at least 50% of the total number of participants; ii) revisions 
to Jordan’s water and land-management policy, planning and legal frameworks  address women’s needs and interests; 
iii) at least 50% of the total population of women and youth in the targeted areas will participate in relevant project 
interventions and planning processes; iv) women and vulnerable groups participate in the consultation process for 
climate-resilient restoration of watersheds; v) 100% of female household beneficiaries are trained on the operation and 
maintenance of small-scale water-harvesting interventions; and vi) 20% of women farmers are represented by the 
women’s groups as water-wise ambassadors.  
• 
Disseminating climate change knowledge products to all user groups through a centralised platform will help to 
overcome gendered accessibility to information and resources, thereby ensuring gender-responsive climate change 
adaptation in Jordan.  
• 
Inclusion of gender considerations in all the policies reviewed as well as acknowledgement of gender differentials 
within government institutions in response to climate risks will ensure the continued and long-term impact of gender-
affirming actions and increased climate resilience in Jordan. 
 
D.4. Needs of the recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Vulnerability of the country 
298. Climate change will exacerbate the existing water stress in Jordan through decreases in annual rainfall, higher annual 
temperatures and more frequent, extreme and prolonged droughts. Because of the country’s existing vulnerability to 
critical water stress, even a minor decrease in water supply resulting from these climate change impacts is expected 
to have severe implications for the country’s water security. This water stress will particularly impact agricultural 
productivity, resulting in the need for increased food imports277 to alleviate food insecurity. This impact is placing an 
increasing financial burden on the Jordanian government and the country’s inhabitants. The financial impacts are 
compounded by the increasing cost of extracting usable water from declining water resources. For example, the over-
extraction of groundwater and reduced recharge rates of groundwater basins have resulted in lowering water tables in 
many areas, causing farmers to invest more money and resources into drilling deeper wells. Because of the high cost 
of drilling deeper wells to reach water that is decreasing in quality, many farmers abandon wells, and consequently 
also the livelihoods that depend on water from them.  
 
Vulnerability of beneficiary groups  
299. The project target area is located within the Jordan Valley — the primary agricultural region of the country. The majority 
of local communities in the region depend on agriculture for their livelihoods. As agricultural activities are vulnerable 
to temperature fluctuations, increased exposure to droughts and greater precipitation variability, crop productivity in 
the Jordan Valley is directly affected by climate change. Under future climate conditions, decreased agricultural yields 
will reduce the income potential for local communities who already face high poverty rates278 (see Section B.1). 
Moreover, women in Jordan are particularly vulnerable to the economic impacts of climate change because of high 
levels of gender inequality in the country, which disadvantages and underrepresents women in the labour force and 
decision-making roles279.  
 
300. Future climate change will additionally present multiple health risks to Jordan’s most vulnerable inhabitants, including 
children, women and refugees. Climate change impacts on health include an increase in the incidence of heat stress-
related illnesses and exposure to diseases from reduced water quality280. Also, the health of the population is adversely 
 
277 Currently, Jordan imports three times (~US$1.4 billion) more agricultural products than it exports (~US$563 million), a deficit 
that is compounded during drought years when production decreases. This deficit and its associated impact on the agricultural 
sector’s contribution to the economy is expected to be exacerbated under future climate scenarios (as described in Section 3 of 
the Feasibility Study), as water supplies will be further constrained. 
278 Poverty rate is the percentage of people whose income falls below the poverty line, which is taken as half the median 
household income of the total population. A higher poverty rate indicates a population with a higher proportion of individuals 
below the poverty line. 
279 OECD Development Centre. 2019. Social Institutions & Gender Index Country Report – Jordan. Available at: 
https://www.genderindex.org/wp-content/uploads/files/datasheets/2019/JO.pdf.  
280 USAID. 2016. Climate change risk profile: Jordan.

=== PAGE 101 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 97 of 144 
 
impacted by reduced food security related to lower agricultural yields281. As yields decline, the total quantity of food 
available in the country decreases, causing food prices to fluctuate. Consequently, higher prices lead to a reduction in 
the diversity of food that households can afford, causing malnutrition282. The capacity of vulnerable inhabitants to 
address the impacts of climate change described above is constrained by limited awareness of the public, politicians 
and decision-makers of the impact of climate change and potential adaptation options (Barrier 3, Section B.2). 
Additionally, there is limited awareness amongst decision-makers and farmers of the economic and financial benefits 
of dryland-adapted water management (Barrier 5, Section B.2).  
 
301. Project interventions will directly benefit a total of 246,942 people (~2.2% of the Jordanian population) — 135,736 men 
(55%) and 111,206 women (45%) — in the Jordan Valley by reducing their climate vulnerability. Of this total: i) 62,446 
(30,607 women and 31,839 men) are from the Yarmouk site — Khaled Bin Al Waleed and Al Seru municipalities; ii) 
105,841 (45,179 women and 60,662 men) are from the King Talal Dam site — New Ardah, Deir Alla and M’addi 
municipalities; and iii) 78,655 (35,420 women and 43,235 men) are from the Kafrain site — Shouna Alwasta and Mahes 
municipalities. Direct beneficiaries include all people who will benefit directly from a project activity or the result of an 
activity. For example, under Output 3.1, 1,200 smallholder farming households (~6,000 people — an average of 5 
people per smallholder household) across the three sites will benefit from on-farm interventions, while 2,800 
households across the sites (~14,000 people) will benefit directly from rainwater harvesting infrastructure implemented 
on houses. Direct beneficiaries also include all vulnerable peoples in the project’s three target areas, as they are all 
expected to benefit from the augmentation of water resources under Outputs 2.1, 2.2 and 3.1, either via improvements 
in water supplies for domestic and/or agricultural usage. Under Output 3.1, all 172,179 people living in the 
municipalities of the three target sites (and related municipalities) listed above will benefit from the improved protection, 
supply and use of water for domestic purposes under climate change conditions. As integrated land and water 
resources management interventions (ILWRM) are scaled up via the Water Fund (Activity 2.1.4), it is expected that an 
additional 4,000 ha of land will be placed under improved management for water resources benefits, with at least one 
additional (adjacent) municipality per watershed site benefitting directly. The 4,000 ha is related to total water 
attenuation potential for the three watersheds, in addition to the 8,000 ha to be restored directly by the project (see 
Table 27 and figures 116-118 in Annex 2).  The rainfall attenuation potential represents the slowing of overland water 
flows which reduces the amount of water running off the land and increases the amount of time available for water 
infiltration, and hence groundwater recharge.  The Water Fund will scale the set of restoration and land management 
interventions in Component 2 to target the entire 12,000 ha with potential for restoration to support water attenuation. 
Consequently, an additional 74,763 people (41,604 men and 33,159 women)283 are expected to benefit directly from 
the project.  
 
302. 756,000 (48% women) people across the broader Jordan Valley will benefit indirectly from: i) the attenuation of water 
resources in the northern Jordan Valley; ii) strengthened local governance to implement climate-resilient ILWRM; iii) 
reduced water loss through evaporation from the King Abdullah Canal; and iv) improved awareness of the impacts of 
climate change and the adaptation options to address them. Indirect beneficiaries include people in the northern Jordan 
Valley who will not directly benefit from a project activity, or result of an activity. This includes all people located outside 
of the project’s three target sites in the northern Jordan Valley. 
 
Socioeconomic status 
303. Jordan has a substantial public debt totalling US$40.2 billion in 2019 (96.7% of GDP284), which is attributed to: i) 
successive governments adopting an expansionist fiscal policy285; ii) the provision of considerable subsidies for staple 
goods such as bread in an attempt to improve the food security of vulnerable groups in the country286; iii) a reduction 
in foreign aid, which has resulted in the country borrowing externally; iv) increased welfare spending for citizens and 
refugees; v) subsidisation of the National Electric Power Company (NEPCO) and the Water Authority of Jordan (WAJ); 
 
281 Approximately 25% of those living below the poverty line in Jordan rely on agriculture for subsistence and as a source of 
income. Although food is currently still readily available in Jordan, the slowing economy and declining agricultural productivity 
are increasingly threatening food security for many households. The average Jordanian family already spends ~40% of its 
income on food.

and vi) low tax collections287. Moreover, limited opportunities exist for the public and private sector to access financial 
resources for addressing climate change impacts, resulting in a constrained capacity of Jordan’s national budget to 
prioritise adaptation. This is compounded by a high trade deficit resulting from a reliance on energy and food imports288 
as well as conflicts in neighbouring Syria and Iraq that disrupt exports from the country289. External investment is, 
therefore, critical to increase the resilience of Jordan’s population to climate change given the insufficient financial 
capacity of the country for climate change adaptation (Barrier 4, Section B.2).  
 
Institutional capacity needs 
304. Jordan’s Third National Communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change 
(UNFCCC)290, as well as its National Capacity Self-Assessment (NCSA)291, identified limited technical, institutional 
and financial capacity as major constraints inhibiting the implementation of adaptation interventions in the country 
(Barrier 1, Section B.2). In particular, the Jordanian government has limited capacity to mainstream climate change 
adaptation into policy- and decision-making, as well as into relevant financial mechanisms accessible by the public 
and private sector. This is largely as a result of, inter alia: i) insufficient access to knowledge on climate change 
adaptation — including on how to effectively implement, monitor and maintain climate change adaptation measures 
— and the resulting benefits (Barrier 2, Section B.2); ii) the absence of formalised processes to enforce such 
mainstreaming; and iii) limited expertise in relevant and effective adaptation technologies. 
 
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
305. 
The proposed project is well-aligned with Jordan’s national priorities, having been designed in collaboration with, 
and validated by, project stakeholders at all levels across inter alia the water, agriculture and environmental sectors. 
These priorities, and their complementarity with the proposed project, are outlined below.  
 
• 
The focus of the Aligned National Action Plan to Combat Desertification in Jordan 2015–2020 is to decrease the 
degradation of landscapes, thereby enhancing the resilience of the population by strengthening and diversifying their 
livelihoods. The plan is aimed at the facilitation of sustainable economic development in Jordan whilst mainstreaming 
climate change adaptation into landscape management. The proposed project will be aligned with the Plan’s overall 
focus on combating desertification, and in particular with its objective of promoting the mainstreaming of climate-resilient 
landscape management. 
• 
The proposed GCF project is well-aligned with the 2020 Strategic Goals of Jordan’s National Biodiversity Strategy 
and Action Plan 2015–2020 (NBSAP). Specifically, this alignment pertains to: i) enhancing the national governance of 
biodiversity by facilitating the participation of stakeholders at all levels as well as through mainstreaming and integration 
of biodiversity conservation into planning and policy frameworks; ii) reducing the impacts of pressures on biodiversity — 
including habitat destruction, political conflicts, and tourism; iii) maintaining protected areas, priority species and genetic 
resources; iv) enhancing the national understanding of dryland ecosystem benefits to national resilience, economic 
sustainability and local livelihoods; and v) developing biodiversity knowledge as the main tool for enabling behavioural 
change at the cultural level, to inform policy and decision-making support.  
• 
The National Climate Change Policy 2013–2020 advances strategic objectives, measures and instruments to facilitate 
Jordan’s adaptation to climate change in priority areas — including water, coastal zones, agriculture and food security, 
health, tourism, biodiversity and socioeconomic status and poverty. The proposed project aims to address relevant goals 
of the policy, including: i) achieving a climate-resilient Jordan that is well prepared for the future impacts of climate 
change; ii) maintaining healthy, sustainable and resilient communities; iii) maintaining sustainable water and agricultural 
resources; and iv) conserving thriving and productive ecosystems. The objective of the policy is to build the adaptive 
capacity of communities and institutions in Jordan — with a specific focus on gender equality and addressing the needs 
of vulnerable groups — through increasing the resilience of ecosystems, as well as water and agricultural resources, to 
climate change and optimising climate change mitigation opportunities. 
• 
The use of integrated approaches to natural resource management is a fundamental principle of the Climate Change 
Policy for a Resilient Water Sector (2016). The Policy describes the Integrated Water Resources Management 
(IWRM) approach upon which Jordan’s National Water Strategy (2023) is based (alignments follow). IWRM and 
Environmental protection: i) Goal 1 - Sustainably manage groundwater resources to restore safe yield levels and protect 
Jordan’s aquifers; ii) Goal 2 - Sustainably manage and protect surface water resources and infrastructure; iii) Goal 4 - 
Increase water use efficiency in households, tourism, industry, and other key business sectors. Energy efficiency and 
renewable energy in the water sector: i) Goal 2 - Expand the use of renewable energy across water sector operations

Gender-responsive management of watersheds, agriculture and rangelands is acknowledged as central to Jordan’s 
climate change adaptation strategy in the country’s updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) (2021). The 
project aligns with and directly responds to the updated NDC’s adaptation priority areas of: i) water resource 
management, by enhancing the enhancing water quantity, quality and use efficiency of water across the targeted 
regions; ii) agriculture and food security, by facilitating the introduction of climate-resilient agricultural practices; and iii) 
gender mainstreaming, by supporting gender-responsive livelihood diversification and climate change adaptation.

Plan (NAP) which builds upon the adaptation of the priorities of the NDC, but with gender added as a core component. 
• 
The project is aligned with the National Strategy for Women in Jordan (2020–2025), including supporting its goals: i) 
women and girls are able to exercise their economic, political and human rights and freely lead and participate in society 
free of gender-based discrimination; ii) women and girls enjoy a life free of all forms of gender-based violence; iii) positive 
gender norms, roles and attitudes support gender equality and women empowerment; and iv) institutions are executing 
and sustaining policies, structures and services that support girls and women empowerment in alignment with Jordan’s 
national and international commitments. 
 
306. 
The MoEnv (Component 1) which will be a co-EE with IUCN (Components 2 to 4), is well-suited to the NDA role 
for Jordan, as it leads the country’s environmental governance system — including other relevant governmental 
organisations — in initiating and driving projects, policies, legislation, strategies, and monitoring. Under MoEnv, the 
Climate Change Directorate (CCD) coordinates high-level policy formulation and implementation, facilitating the 
participation of relevant stakeholders at all levels to ensure uptake and buy-in amongst policy- and decision-makers. 
 
Use of domestic accountability mechanisms 
307. 
In 2015, following the recommendations of the National Climate Change Policy, the Climate Change Directorate 
(CCD) was established to serve as the institutional focal point for climate change activities in Jordan, including the 
country’s commitments as a party to the UNFCCC. Additionally, the CCD is the coordinating focal point to global and 
regional environmental governance systems292. The CCD coordinates high-level policy formulation and implementation, 
facilitating the participation of relevant stakeholders at all levels. It also oversees development of the internal capacity 
of MoEnv — as well as the government in general — in addressing the challenges and barriers of climate change293. 
Via its Adaptation to Climate Change Section, CCD, which will be a member of the PSC, will enforce accountability 
related to the project’s policy and institutional capacity-building activities (see Component 1’s description in Section 
B.3.). 
 
308. 
Outside of the project’s governance structure accountability during implementation will be supported by the 
National Committee on Climate Change (NCCC), which is convened and chaired by MoEnv, and includes the Ministries 
of Agriculture, Health, Water and Irrigation, Energy and Mineral Resources, as well as other ministries and participants 
from public institutions, civil society, and academia. The NCCC was established in 2001 and is the highest coordination 
and supervising body for climate policy in Jordan294. The NCCC’s responsibilities, which will contribute to maintaining 
accountability for the project, include: i) supervising and supporting the realisation of UNFCCC commitments in Jordan 
in accordance with national interests; ii) supervising and assuring the development and execution of legal, regulatory

and gender into national development strategies and plans; and v) providing feedback on, and overseeing, climate 
change programmes and projects, in terms of institutional and technical aspects295. 
 
309. 
An additional domestic mechanism that will support accountability during project implementation is the NAP’s 
Monitoring, Review and Learning (MRL) system296. The MRL system will be used to assess and report on progress 
made on addressing the impacts of climate change in alignment with the NAP’s goals and objectives (such as through 
the proposed project), to support learning, transparency and accountability. This includes assessments of progress in 
the implementation of: i) adaptation policies, plans and/or investments; ii) activities enabling the implementation of 
adaptation measures, or amplifying their impacts; and iii) adaptation measures, including their actual contribution to 
climate resilience. As the CCD will oversee the implementation of the NAP MRL, its linking with and use for accountability 
within the proposed project’s M&E process will be ensured. 
 
Stakeholder engagement  
310. 
The proposed project was designed following an extensive consultative process with MoEnv — which is 
responsible for the overall management of Jordan’s GCF portfolio — and the consortium of NGOs297, as well as all other 
relevant project stakeholders298. All stakeholder consultations were held in Arabic (where necessary, translation was 
provided) and gender sensitivities were considered. Stakeholders were mapped and consulted at the national level (24 
stakeholders) and local level across the three target areas (South Shounah – 38 stakeholders, Upper King Talal Dam – 
21 stakeholders, and Yarmouk – 33 stakeholders). Details of the stakeholder engagement process are summarised 
below, with further detail presented in Annex 7: Summary of Consultations and Stakeholder Engagement Plan. 
 
311. 
Stakeholder consultations took place during four in-country missions. In October 2018, an initial scoping mission 
was undertaken to discuss and agree on an approach to refining the proposed project’s rationale and design. This 
mission included a workshop and meetings with the consortium of NGOs and MoEnv, as well as an expedition across 
the Jordan Rift Valley to view potential project intervention sites and to gain an understanding of baseline conditions and 
challenges faced by local communities. Following the scoping mission, a second in-country mission was undertaken in 
April 2019 and included a two-day technical meeting. Attendees included representatives from the United Nations 
Environment Program (UNEP), NGOs that constitute the consortium and additional project consultants. The primary 
objectives of the meeting were to: i) discuss and agree on the refined scope and rationale of the project; ii) discuss and 
develop a common understanding on what is required to finalise the design of the project; iii) discuss the approach to 
the identification and selection of project intervention sites; and iv) discuss and agree on a plan for the remainder of the 
project preparation facility (PPF) phase. In addition, individual meetings were held with the members of the consortium 
of NGOs to identify how their expertise will best contribute to project design and implementation.  
 
312. 
A third in-country mission was undertaken in July 2019 to: i) hold an official project inception workshop with all 
relevant stakeholders; ii) discuss project design; iii) confirm the selection of project intervention areas; iv) discuss 
environmental and social safeguards (ESS) and gender requirements of the project design, including the development 
of required assessments and plans; v) discuss project financing — including the approach to the Financial and Economic 
Appraisal (FEA) and potential sources of co-financing; and vi) visit the Jordan Valley to view potential project intervention 
sites, collect additional baseline information (including on barriers to adaptation), consult with local stakeholders (state 
and non-state actors and local communities) and identify potential adaptation interventions. The inception mission was 
followed by a series of consultations and site visits by a local consultant team299 to gather additional information required 
to inform project design. An environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA), environmental and social management 
plan (ESMP), gender analysis and action plan, and an adaptation needs assessment were developed to assist design 
of the proposed project. In addition, the location and details of project intervention sites in the Jordan Valley were 
confirmed. Stakeholders consulted during this period included local communities, NGOs, sectoral experts300 and state 
and non-state institutions, as well as national-level stakeholders. Additionally, stakeholder needs have been accounted 
for by ensuring ministerial reports and reviews have been considered. For example, the MoA’s 2011 review of agriculture

313. 
An additional round of community consultations was held between October and November 2020 to explore and 
document local community perceptions, opinions, concerns and priorities on the scope of the proposed project, as part 
of the project’s validation process. The group of respondents interviewed by the research team was a mixed social group 
with stronger representation and focus on farmers from both upstream and downstream areas of the Yarmouk and King 
Talal Dam target areas, and only downstream areas from the Kafrain target area. The research sample was also almost 
equally distributed between women and women and a slight tilt towards men as they represent the majority of local 
community members involved in farming. A significant number of young people (i.e., youth groups with ages 18–35) 
represented almost half of the people interviewed with particular focus on unemployed men and women, and early 
retirees from both genders. Table 13 below summarizes the names of the settlements visited by the research team, their 
associated municipalities, the number of interviews conducted in each settlement, the number of respondents 
approaches in total and classified by gender. Recommendations and concerns that arose from these consultations have 
been used to update relevant aspects of project design across the Funding Proposal package. A report detailing the 
community consultations is presented in Annex 7: Stakeholder Consultations and Engagement Plan.

D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
315. 
The Government of Jordan is requesting US$44,904,203 in grant finance from the GCF to implement climate-
resilient integrated landscape and water resources management (ILWRM) as part of a national response to climate 
change. This will enable Jordan to balance its water demand with water supply in the long term despite predicted climate 
change impacts. The GCF investment will be supported by the Government of Jordan (GoJ) and Consortium of NGOs, 
which have committed approximately US$15,605,748 in co-financing and will be used to overcome the barriers to 
increasing the resilience of Jordan’s land and water resources management to climate change threats. While the co-
finance will be used to cover all baseline costs — such as business-as-usual repairs or maintenance to water supply 
infrastructure necessary to ensure the sustainability of climate-proofing interventions, GCF funding will solely be used 
to address the additional impacts of climate change. In addition to co-finance, parallel finance of US$13,133,000 has 
been identified for the project. The parallel finance consists of resources included under aligned initiatives and projects 
supported by the Consortium of NGOs (see Annex 30: Parallel Finance). With the support of GCF grant funding, the 
proposed project will deliver several adaptation benefits in Jordan that will contribute to achieving a paradigm shift in the 
country’s approach to land and water resources management towards the achievement of climate-resilient sustainable 
development301. The GCF and co-financing partners combined amount of US$60,515,256 in financing, supported by 
parallel finance of US$13,133,000, will be used efficiently and effectively to, inter alia: i) create an enabling environment 
for the use of  ILWRM to increase the resilience of communities in Jordan to the impacts of climate change by improving 
the management and augmentation of water resources; ii) strengthen the capacity of state and non-state actors — 
including local communities — to plan and implement climate-resilient  ILWRM; and iii) implement scalable and replicable 
on-the-ground adaption interventions including ecosystem restoration (the benefits of ecosystem restoration to 
Jordanian society far outweigh the costs302,303), managed aquifer recharge, evaporation reduction through the 
installation of floating solar panels, as well as rainwater harvesting and water-conservation measures, to ultimately 
improve Jordan’s water supply. The primary benefits of the ecosystem restoration interventions will be water related. 
These include: i) increased aquifer recharge, thereby increasing water supplies under climate change conditions; and ii) 
reduced sedimentation of surface water sources, resulting in an improvement in water volumes and quality. This is 
because decreased sedimentation of dams will positively impact both their lifespan and storage capacity. Similarly, water 
quality and quantity and the functioning of waterways will be enhanced because of increased filtration of runoff into soils, 
which prevents pollution of waterways. For example, restoration interventions implemented over a period of 25 years to 
reduce sedimentation of the King Talal Dam will reduce average annual sediment loading in the dam from a baseline of 
3,605,775 tonnes/ha to 3,386,137 tonnes/ha, which will result in an increase in the dam’s storage capacity from ~282,000 
MCM to ~307,500 MCM. The impacts of sedimentation on storage capacity of dams and reservoirs is discussed in more 
detail in Section 1.5.2 of the Feasibility Study.  
 
316. 
Project interventions will also target local communities (including smallholder farmers) who will benefit from the 
installation of rooftop rainwater harvesting systems and the introduction of climate-resilient livelihoods, as well as 
pastoralists who will see an increase in fodder productivity. These interventions have been designed to build upon and 
 
301 A detailed description of project activities is presented in Sections B.3 and E.6 of the Funding Proposal. 
302 Becker N & Katz DL. 2009. An economic assessment of Dead Sea preservation and restoration. In: The Jordan River and 
Dead Sea Basin. Springer, Dordrecht. 
303 Myint MM & Westerberg V. 2015. An economic valuation of a large-scale rangeland restoration project through the Hima 
system in Jordan. Report for the ELD Initiative by International Union for Conservation of Nature, Nairobi, Kenya.

=== PAGE 110 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 106 of 144 
 
scale up existing practices and technologies that have been shown to be effective in Jordan. For example, in-field 
rainwater harvesting structures have been used to improve soil structure and decrease soil erosion rates by increasing 
soil organic matter and intercepting runoff water at relatively short-distance intervals304,305. These structures have also 
successfully supported the re-establishment of drought-tolerant forage shrubs in degraded rangelands306 and an 
economic analysis, undertaken for the Badia Benchmark Project, demonstrated that planting of shrubs with harvested 
rainwater produced an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) estimated at ~17%, with the addition of the contribution 
of environmental benefits307. Rainwater harvesting and water conservation interventions will be introduced on local 
smallholder farms in the project target areas to increase the resilience of local farmers to increasing drought 
conditions308. Beneficiaries will subsequently be trained to use and maintain the rainwater harvesting and conservation 
measures to maximise their benefits and sustainability. The cost-effectiveness of the proposed project interventions 
as well as a summary of international adaptation best practices and lessons learned used to inform the project’s 
development are presented below.  
 
Cost-effectiveness of project interventions 
317. 
Jordan experiences interconnected climate phenomena that increase the scarcity of water resources in an already 
water-stressed country. The expected occurrence of more frequent and prolonged droughts — a function of reductions 
in Jordan’s average annual rainfall and increases in the average annual temperatures projected under climate change 
scenarios — will worsen water stress. Decision-making on the type of climate change adaptation options that the 
Jordanian government should employ to respond to increased water scarcity, should be informed by an understanding 
of the cost of adaptation measures, as well as the associated potential avoided costs of project interventions. In order 
to inform decision-making on adaptation interventions, a full economic analysis across all four project outputs has been 
undertaken using a cost benefits analysis (CBA) approach. This economic analysis, assuming a 25-year lifetime for 
the project and a discount rate of 5%, is presented in Annex 3: Economic Feasibility Assessment (EFA). The results 
for the main economic summary statistics for the proposed project are summarised below. 
 
Net Present Value (NPV)  
105,279,874 USD 
Benefit / Cost Ratio (BCR) 
3.00 
Economic Rate of Return (ERR) 
25% 
Payback period (years) 
9 
Total project cost (USD, undiscounted costs 
incurred in years 1-8)  
60,509,951  
 
 
318. 
The project is comparable to the cost of similar interventions within Jordan. For example, the GCF-funded project 
entitled ‘Building Resilience to Cope with Climate Change in Jordan Through Improving Water Use Efficiency in the 
Agriculture Sector (BRCCJ) (2021–2029) will cost US$33.3 million309 and the GEF-funded project entitled 
‘Mainstreaming Sustainable Land and Water Management Practices’ (2009–2015) cost ~US$30 million310,311. The 
overall BCR is 3, meaning the economic benefits of implementing the project are expected to exceed the GCF 
investment costs by a factor 3. On this basis, there is a clear economic case for implementing the JILMI project and 
for GCF grant funding to finance it. The cumulative economic benefits of implementing the JILMI project are expected 
to exceed the project costs shortly after project closure (i.e., the payback period of 9 years is equal to the project

311 GEF Independent Evaluation Office. 2016. Mainstreaming Sustainable Land and Water Management Practices: Terminal 
Evaluation Review form. Available at: https://www.thegef.org/project/menarid-mainstreaming-sustainable-land-and-water-
management-practices.

=== PAGE 111 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 107 of 144 
 
timeline of 8 years). This adds further support to the economic rationale for project funding, as the JILMI project is 
expected to deliver net economic benefits almost immediately after all project activities have been completed (and 
before the impact of any scaling up has started to deliver significant benefits).  
 
319. 
The ERR for the JILMI project is relatively high at 25%. This is considerably higher than the ERR hurdle rate of 
most international donor agencies.  For example, the Millennium Challenge Corporation (MCC) of the USA employs a 
hurdle rate of 10% ERR for projects312. The ERR for the MCC Jordan Compact project is estimated at 19%, indicating 
the high potential economic value of the proposed JILMI project in the context of climate change adaptation and water 
scarcity issues in Jordan313. According to the sensitivity analysis presented in the EFA, the economic summary 
statistics given above are robust to changes in input values. For example, the analysis is sensitive to reducing the 
value of groundwater to agriculture. However, when this is reduced by 30%, the ERR for the project is still favourable 
at 22%. Furthermore, the value of 0.5 USD/CM of water is well supported via other studies and literature. A second 
key parameter is the additional groundwater recharge from the rockfill dams. Given that there is more uncertainty 
around this quantity, it has been reduced by 50% for the sensitivity analysis. However, this still results in a favourable 
ERR for the project — with a rate of 20% overall. It is also shown that if no scale up of activities is achieved via Outputs 
1 and 4, the ERR remains favourable at 17%. Overall, this project therefore represents a highly cost-effective spend 
for the GCF. 
 
320. 
The proposed project will additionally reduce ecosystem pressures more widely than considered via the economic 
analysis.  For example, the avoided degradation of land that would accompany the project’s EbA interventions and be 
exacerbated via climate change, will have considerable co-benefits that are not captured in the economic statistics 
presented. Such co-benefits include: i) strengthened flood attenuation, reducing the related impacts — such as loss of 
life and damage to infrastructure — on downstream communities; ii) enhanced food security, livelihood resilience and 
biodiversity through the restoration of degraded ecosystems; and iii) decreased pollution of water resources which will 
filter runoff, prevent erosion and slow sedimentation. Additional details on the anticipated co-benefits of the proposed 
project are presented in Section D.3 of this Funding Proposal. 
 
321. 
Moreover, the proposed project will also ensure an enhanced capacity for cross-sectoral coordination and 
planning in the GoJ, through monitoring and analysis of the sustainable development benefits of the project’s ILWRM 
interventions during project implementation (Activity 1.1.3). The monitoring and analysis will consider: i) avoided costs 
— impacts or losses that would otherwise occur in the absence of ecosystem services, such as reduced water resource 
quantity and quality; and ii) replacement costs — the implication of implementing ‘grey’ artificial systems (as opposed 
to ‘green’ nature-based solutions) that would be required to deliver the same services as ecosystems. GoJ will include 
the monitoring and analysis in their ongoing operations. This will facilitate the embedding of coordination and operation 
of these mechanisms and structures in budgets and mandates beyond the life of the project. These plans will also 
include long-term funding commitments for enhancing the ecological infrastructure and maintaining the physical 
infrastructure associated with livelihoods interventions.  By recognising the positive environmental, economic and 
social co-benefits to communities of ILWRM, cross-sectoral cooperation and integrated planning to conserve and 
augment water resources will be fostered. 
 
322. 
The opportunity costs associated with the JIMLI are minimal and short term. They mainly relate to exclusion of 
illegal livestock grazing practices at the ecosystem restoration sites. Given that the fodder-production production 
potential at these sites will become increasingly reduced as a result of expected land degradation processes (including 
illegal grazing), there is an opportunity for the improved management of these areas as the project’s restoration 
interventions strengthen fodder production, allowing for legal sustainable grazing of livestock. 
 
Potential to leverage public and private finance 
323. 
Under Activity 2.1.4, a Water Fund will be designed to channel resources from those who are willing and able to 
pay for increased ground water supplies to those whose actions within the landscape can deliver them. The Water 
Fund will be designed to stimulate public and private investments into climate-resilient ILWRM as an adaptation 
approach, enhancing the local ownership of interventions targeted at water resource attenuation under climate change 
conditions, such as ecosystem restoration, MAR and floating solar installations. During the Water Fund’s maturation 
phase, a long-term financing strategy that will detail how financial resources for the Fund’s ongoing operation post 
GCF involvement will be leveraged from public and private sources. The long-term financing strategy will include a 
detailed model describing how the Water Fund’s PES scheme will continue to channel finance into grant mechanism 
(which will include grant and results-based payments mechanisms) once GCF finance is no longer available post

https://www.mcc.gov/resources/story/story-cdg-guidelines-for-economic-and-beneficiary-analysis

we-work/err/jordan-compact

project. Public and private finance will also be leveraged through the policy reforms to be supported by the project, 
including the integration of climate-resilient ILWRM into Jordan’s planning, policy-making and budgeting process. This 
will facilitate the embedding of coordination and operation of these mechanisms and structures in budgets and 
mandates beyond the life of the project. These plans will also include long-term funding commitments for enhancing 
the ecological infrastructure and maintaining the physical infrastructure associated with livelihood interventions. 
 
324. 
Implemented in parallel with the proposed project will be the recently approved GCF project; FP155 “Building 
resilience to cope with climate change through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector (BRCCJ)”314. 
BRCCJ’s approach of promoting water use efficiency in the agriculture sector in the Dead Sea Basin complements the 
proposed project’s climate-resilient ILWRM approach in the northern Jordan Valley, with both falling under the country’s 
GCF Programme. These parallel investments will collectively contribute to climate-resilient sustainable development 
in Jordan.  
 
Application of best practices in land and water resources management 
325. 
Best practices from similar projects as outlined in Section B.1 Climate context were used to inform the design of 
climate-proofing interventions to be implemented under the proposed project. Lessons learned from numerous 
baseline investments into climate change adaptation in Jordan were also used to guide the design of all project 
interventions. Such lessons include appropriate mechanisms to ensure that project activities are implemented in a 
participatory, gender-inclusive and sustainable way. The particular international best practices and lessons learned 
that were applied to the design of the proposed project and that will contribute to the efficiency and effectiveness of its 
interventions are summarised below. Specific details on all relevant best practices and lessons learned are presented 
in Section 8 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study. 
 
326. 
International best practices and lessons learned incorporated into the design of the project include: 
• 
ensuring that landscape planning, implementation of policies, strategies and monitoring processes are integrated and 
iterative to account for the dynamic nature of landscape processes; 
• 
acknowledging the need to engage stakeholders in an equitable manner to fairly distribute benefits and incentives of 
landscape management; 
• 
facilitating participatory monitoring and adaptive management by ensuring that information is widely accessible and 
not managed by any single stakeholder; 
• 
incorporating traditional knowledge into training for smallholders, pastoralists and local communities on climate-
resilient agricultural practices, water monitoring and the adoption of alternative livelihoods;  
• 
drawing on regional experiences and resources to increase institutional capacity to respond to the impacts of extreme 
events; 
• 
applying an integrated, systemic and coordinated approach to disaster response through the establishment of 
formalised communication channels; 
• 
developing appropriate strategies for securing financial resources for project development and implementation;  
• 
effectively managing the available financial and human resources to ensure the efficient and cost-effective 
implementation of project interventions; 
• 
undertaking regular monitoring and evaluation of climate-adaptive interventions to ensure that the most effective and 
appropriate solutions are being implemented under future conditions of climate change;  
• 
engaging and collaborating extensively with all relevant project stakeholders to encourage buy-in from national- and 
local-level decision-makers, therefore contributing to the sustainability of proposed adaptation interventions over the 
long term; and implementing effective financial and project management strategies to ensure the efficient use of 
financial resources and avoid delays during the implementation phase. 
 
314 A brief complementarity analysis of the two projects is presented in Section B.1., with more detail provided in Annex 29.

E.2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1-2 pages) 
 
Assessment 
Dimension 
Current state (baseline) 
Potential target scenario 
(Description) 
How the project/programme will contribute 
(Description) 
Description 
Rating 
Scale 
Currently (2023), landscape and 
water resources management in 
Jordan is compartmentalised and 
sector specific, lacking an integrated 
approach.  This has resulted in an 
unsustainable model of sectoralised 
water resource management where 
landscape-level ecosystem resilience 
is not prioritized, increasing the 
vulnerability of communities to 
climate change. 
Low 
The paradigm shift would involve a move away from 
a business-as-usual model of land and water 
resources that is uncoordinated and 
compartmentalised to one that is integrated across 
Jordan’s landscapes, economic sectors, 
government ministries and departments, 
municipalities, non-government organisations and 
civil society, as well as into water resources and 
land-management policy. This will result in 
increased water resource supplies at the landscape 
level under drying conditions. 
The project will result in strengthened governance 
and capacity for climate-resilient ILWRM (locally 
and nationally) and cumulatively improve water 
resource supplies by up to 154.5 million CM over 25 
years through ecosystem restoration, and improving 
the protection, supply and use of water at the 
community level, before any replication takes place, 
contributing significantly to water security.  
 
The scaling up strategy has various strands, as 
follows: 
i) a Water Fund as a long-term financing 
mechanism (an approach not yet introduced in 
Jordan); ii) developing land use guidelines, 
standards and protocols for climate-resilient 
integrated watershed management in the Jordan 
Valley; iii) trained staff in national and sub-national 
government institutions on climate-resilient ILWRM 
practices into policies, plans and strategies; iv) 
amendments to Jordan’s policy, planning and legal 
frameworks relevant to ILWRM; v) an outreach 
programme across the Jordan Valley; vi) improving 
knowledge management for climate change 
adaptation in Jordan to support national and sub-
national decision-making, and build communities of 
practice.

=== PAGE 114 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 110 of 144 
 
Replicability 
An ILWRM approach to climate 
change adaptation in the water has 
not yet been demonstrated in Jordan, 
so replication is not yet possible. This 
includes the use of a Water Fund 
serving as a mechanism for 
leveraging financial resources for the 
attenuation of water resources at a 
landscape level. 
Low 
If a case for the benefits (water resources and 
economic) of an ILWRM approach to climate 
change adaptation in the water sector can be 
demonstrated, including the contribution of a Water 
Fund to generating financial flows into the 
maintenance and scaling up of the approach, then it 
can be replicated in other areas of Jordan and the 
region. 
Climate-resilient ecosystem restoration along with 
investments in the protection supply and use of 
water at the community level as part of a climate-
resilient ILWRM to improve water resource supplies, 
along with a working model to increase private and 
public investments into adaptation (Water Fund), 
can inform efforts to replicate the intervention in 
other areas of Jordan and the region. 
Sustainability 
While the Kingdom of Jordan is 
committed to climate-resilient 
sustainable development, including 
water security, the mandate of state 
and non-state actors for adaptation is 
impeded by gaps in relevant policy, 
planning and legislature frameworks. 
In addition, the allocation of public 
budgets to adaptation is still 
insufficient due to ongoing economic 
strains on the country, landscape 
management does not yet consider 
an integrated approach and there is 
limited knowledge and awareness of 
landscape-level approached to 
adaptation in the water sector such 
as ILWRM.  
Low 
The potential target scenario would see improved 
governmental support for a landscape-level 
approaches to adaptation in the water sector, 
supported by policy, increased capacity and 
sufficient funding (from public and private sources), 
resulting in the attenuation of water resources under 
future climate scenarios. Knowledge availability and 
behaviour change at government and community 
levels, will support this, with the country as a whole 
supporting an ILWRM approach to adaptation. 
The mandates and capacity of state and non-state 
institutions to implement landscape-level adaptation 
approaches for enhancing water resource supplies, 
such as ILWRM, will be strengthened. This will be 
supplemented by stimulating increases in public 
budget allocations through providing evidence of the 
economic benefits of an ILWRM approach to 
adaptation, along with the establishment of a Water 
Fund to leverage finance for sustained financial 
flows into landscape-level approaches to water 
resources management. Additionally, gender-
inclusive behavioural change will be fostered 
through improved information outreach and 
knowledge management.

As integrated land and water resources 
management interventions (ILWRM) are 
scaled up via the Water Fund (Activity 2.4), it 
is expected that an additional 4,000 ha of 
land will be placed under improved 
management for water resources benefits, 
with at least one additional (adjacent)

attenuation potential for the three

restored directly by the project (see Table 27 
and figures 116-118 in Annex 2).  The rainfall 
attenuation potential represents the slowing 
of overland water flows which reduces the 
amount of water running off the land and 
increases the amount of time available for 
water infiltration, and hence groundwater 
recharge.  The Water Fund will scale the set 
of restoration and land management 
interventions in Component 2 to target the 
entire 12,000 ha with potential for restoration 
to support water attenuation.

How the project will contribute 
Coverage 
Core Indicator 5: Degree to 
which GCF investments 
contribute to strengthening 
institutional and regulatory 
frameworks for low emission 
climate-resilient development 
pathways in a country-driven 
manner 
Currently, an unsustainable model of sectoralised 
water resource management exists in Jordan that 
does not prioritise landscape-level ecosystem 
resilience. However, adaptation of the country’s 
population through conservation and adaptive 
measures in land and water resources 
management can be achieved through an 
integrated approach to conserving Jordan’s water 
resources under climate change conditions. 
Baseline gaps identified in the National Water 
Strategy (2016–2025) include that it does not 
reflect the initiated decline of available freshwater, 
presuming that alternative water supplies would 
increase in availability from 2025 (which is at odds 
with preceding and proceeding National 
Communications to the UNFCCC). Addressing this 
is part of the Jordan Water Management Initiative’s 
(2016–2021) scope, by increasing the 
accountability and sustainability of water sector 
management and governance. In addition, the 
National Water Strategy highlights gaps in how the 
GoJ manages water demand in the agricultural 
sector, as well as specific interventions proposed 
to address these gaps. The development of a 
legislative framework on climate change was 
identified as a gap through Vision 2025 and the 
EDP — which emphasised the need for a legal 
framework to regulate national climate adaptation 
activities, in addition to enhancing the capacities of 
national institutions and experts to address climate 
change impacts. In addition, the Climate Change 
Policy for a Resilient Water Sector aims to bridge 
the gap between priority areas to support policy 
coherence by building upon other policies and 
plans in the water sector.  
low 
Broadened mandate of state 
and non-state actors for climate 
change adaptation — including 
climate-resilient ILWRM. 
Integration of adaptation into 
relevant legislation and policy, 
and improved funding for 
adaptation. Enhanced cross-
sectoral coordination and 
dialogue related to adaptation 
and watershed management, 
particularly between the water

impacts and appropriate adaptation responses. 
Currently, there is a gap in Jordan between 
research and policy-making on climate change 
adaptation. The MoEnv, in seeking to achieve its 
mission to “maintain and improve the quality of 
Jordan’s environment, conserve natural resources, 
and contribute to sustainable development”, 
conducts activities concerned with, inter alia, public 
awareness and outreach and the support of 
decision-making through knowledge management. 
It leads the governance system — including other 
relevant governmental organisations — in initiating 
and driving policies, legislation, strategies and 
monitoring, as well as by mainstreaming 
environmental policies into all national 
development plans. To achieve this the MoEnv has 
set five objectives for the environment sector, 
notably the development of capacity and 
institutionalisation of a culture of excellence and 
raising public awareness and promoting 
behavioural change for environmental protection.

change impacts and appropriate 
adaptation responses. Removal

Component 1: Climate change adaptation governance in Jordan 
Output 1.1: Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient integrated landscape and water resources management (ILWRM) and  Output 1.2. Economic assessments developed 
quantifying net benefits of ILWRM as an adaptation approach 
Activity 1.1.1 Strengthen the mandate 
and capacity of main government 
institutions — including MoEnv, MWI 
and their subsidiaries332 — for climate-
resilient ILWRM in Jordan 
To improve the mainstreaming of climate-resilient 
ILWRM into Jordan’s relevant policy-making and 
planning processes, staff from relevant national and 
sub-national institutions (such as the Climate Change 
Directorate and National Committee on Climate 
Change) will be trained to interpret and apply climate 
data, as well as the results of climate analyses and 
risk assessments. The training, which will include two 
three-day workshops (for 20 people, with at least 50% 
female representation) will also focus on the use of 
this data for disaster risk management. Trained 
individuals will then work with experts procured by the 
project to review relevant policies and prepare policy 
briefs with recommendations for the integration of 
climate-related data into policies, plans and disaster-
risk management. This training will contribute to the 
1.1.1.1: Develop land use guidelines, standards 
and protocols for climate-resilient ILWRM in the 
Jordan Valley. 
 
1.1.1.2: Train staff from relevant national and 
subnational institutions to use climate data, and 
the results of climate analyses and risk 
assessments in policy- and decision-making 
processes, as well as planning and disaster risk 
management. 
 
1.1.1.1.a. One set of climate-resilient ILWRM 
guidelines and protocols 
 
1.1.1.2.a. 30 staff trained (30 national, 34 
sub-national; at least 50% women)

sustainability of this mainstreaming momentum, after 
project end. The policies will be presented and 
validated at the same workshop that will be hosted by 
the NCCC for the validation of policy briefs on 
climate-resilient ILWRM. 
Land use guidelines, standards and protocols will be 
developed for climate-resilient integrated watershed 
management in the Jordan Valley, including roles and 
responsibilities of all relevant actors.  
Activity 1.1.2. Draft amendments to 
Jordan’s policy, planning and legal 
frameworks for agriculture, land, water 
and natural resources, to mainstream 
climate-resilient ILWRM as a climate 
change adaptation approach 
Recommendations will be made via policy briefs for 
the integration of climate resilient ILWRM into relevant 
policies, plans and legislature, including Jordan’s 
Decentralisation Programme and National 
Development Plan. To ensure that this is done 
effectively, several capacity-building actions targeting 
national- and local-level decision-makers and 
planners will be implemented. These include training: 
i) policy and decision-makers on the mainstreaming of 
climate-resilient ILWRM; ii) planners on climate-
resilient ILWRM planning; and iii) staff from relevant 
national and sub-national institutions to use climate 
data, and the results of climate analyses and risk 
assessments in decision-making processes, as well 
as planning and disaster risk management. The 
capacity build through these trainings will facilitate the 
preparation of policy briefs with recommendations for 
the integration of climate-resilient ILWRM practices 
into relevant policies, plans, strategies and legislature. 
The policy briefs will be submitted to the National 
Committee of Climate Change (NCCC) for validation. 
1.1.2.1: Train policy- and decision-makers on the

into policies, plans and strategies. 
 
1.1.2.2: Train relevant national and sub-national 
planners on climate-resilient ILWRM planning. 
 
1.1.2.3: Review Jordan’s national land-
management policies, plans and legislation to 
identify gaps and opportunities for the 
consideration of climate-resilient ILWRM 
(including EbA, climate analyses and risk 
assessments) as an adaptation approach. 
 
1.1.2.4: Prepare policy briefs with 
recommendations for the integration of climate-
