Activity 1.1.3. Generate evidence of the 
economic benefits of ILWRM to project 
beneficiaries for use by policymakers 
and planners 
To prioritise and ILWRM approach, decision-makers 
need to understand and appreciate the relevant 
interlinked ecological processes and how they directly 
produce economic benefits. The relevant information 
and evidence will be generated by the monitoring and 
analysis of ILWRM interventions implemented under 
the project. The monitoring and analysis would 
consider: i) avoided costs — impacts or losses that 
would otherwise occur in the absence of ecosystem 
1.1.3.1: Gather primary and secondary data from 
project interventions, including a 1-day 
stakeholder engagement workshop at each site. 
 
1.1.3.2:  Analyse monitoring data from ILWRM 
interventions implemented under the project to 
identify benefit streams for valuing ILWRM 
benefits (including ecosystem services) under 
different climate scenarios.

benefit streams for valuing ILWRM benefits.

from local ecosystem services.

data streams and modelling methodology for 
valuing ILWRM benefits.

services, such as reduced water resource quantity 
and quality; and ii) replacement costs — the 
implication of implementing ‘grey’ artificial systems 
(as opposed to ‘green’ nature-based solutions) that 
would be required to deliver the same services as 
ecosystems. Where feasible, monitoring of the 
following sustainable development benefits will be 
included: flood attenuation, enhanced biodiversity, 
decreased pollution of water resources, improved 
access to clean water, strengthened food security, 
increased climate-resilience of pastoral livelihoods 
and energy generation from floating solar.

Activity 1.1.4. Strengthen the mandate 
and capacity of non-state actors for 
climate-resilient ILWRM in the Jordan 
Valley 
 
To ensure a participatory and cross-sectoral approach 
to landscape management, Watershed Management 
Committees (WMCs) will be established at each of 
the project’s three target areas. The WMCs will be 
chaired by the JVA and include representatives from 
all stakeholders involved in landscape management, 
including local communities. The technical capacity of 
WMC members to perform their roles (as defined in 
the climate-resilient integrated watershed 
management guidelines, standards and protocols) will 
be strengthened through a tailored training 
programme developed under the project by local 
experts. Once trained, the three WMCs will be 
assisted by land use planners (trained under Activity 
1.2) and adaptation experts to develop participatory 
climate-resilient integrated watershed management 
plans for their respective watersheds. 
1.1.4.1:  Establish Watershed Management 
Committees (WMCs), under the leadership of 
the JVA and local governors, at each of the 
three target watersheds to facilitate dialogue and 
participatory watershed management between 
upstream and downstream stakeholders. This 
will include working with MoEnv for the formal 
recognition of WMCs in relevant literature - 
including defining their responsibility towards 
working with communities. 
 
1.1.4.2: Develop a training programme on 
climate-resilient integrated watershed 
management, including EbA, targeting the WMC 
members, including JVA, LDCs, local leaders, 
decentralization committees, local councils and 
other relevant stakeholders. 
 
1.1.4.3: Train relevant watershed stakeholders 
(WMC members) on climate-resilient ILWRM 
and its economic benefits based on programme 
develop under Activity 1.1.4.2. 
 
1.1.4.4: Train staff from relevant sub-national 
institutions to use climate data, and the results of 
climate analyses and risk assessments in policy- 
and decision-making processes, as well as 
planning and disaster risk management. 
 
1.1.4.1.a. Three WMCs established and 
formally recognized in relevant legislature.

for 90 stakeholders.

Component 2: Integrated landscape and water resources management (ILWRM) under conditions of climate change 
Output 2.1: Climate-resilient ILWRM practices and ecosystem restoration implemented in three watersheds in the Jordan Valley  
Activity 2.1.1. Support the 
implementation of participatory climate-
resilient integrated watershed 
management plans (developed under 
Activity 1.4) in the Yarmouk, King Talal 
Dam and Kafrain areas of the Jordan 
Valley. 
The climate-resilient integrated watershed 
management plans developed for each of the project 
intervention sites (Activity 1.1.4) will be implemented 
under the support and guidance of project Technical 
Partners. These plans will be implemented by WMCs 
once they have completed their watershed 
management training under Activity 1.1.4. This will 
afford local communities management rights over 
their local ecosystems, incentivising the enforcement 
of land-management laws, resulting in reduced 
human-driven ecosystem degradation and increased 
support for restoration activities.  The implementation 
of the plans will include (as identified and detailed in 
the plans): i) implementing climate-resilient watershed 
management interventions in collaboration with local 
communities and NGOs; and ii) establishing the 
mechanisms via which climate-resilient natural 
resource-based additional livelihoods and climate-
resilient agricultural practices on smallholder farms 
will be implemented (Activity 2.1.4).  
2.1.1.1: Implement climate-resilient watershed 
management (WSM) measures at relevant 
project sites. 
 
2.1.1.2: Establish mechanisms for the 
implementation of gender-responsive climate-
resilient additional livelihoods and agricultural 
practices under Activity 2.4. 
 
2.1.1.3: Establish and implement community-
based participatory mechanisms for monitoring 
and evaluating change in community climate 
resilience and behavioural change, including 
community fora. 
2.1.1.1.a. 8,000 ha of watershed ecosystems 
in the northern Jordan Valley protected and 
strengthened in response to climate change 
and variability. 
 
 
2.1.1.2.a. Three (one per target site) gender-
responsive community climate-resilient 
additional livelihood and agricultural practice 
implementation models including marketing 
approaches. 
 
2.1.1.3.a. Three (one per target watershed) 
community groups established to monitor 
changes in community climate resilience. 
Activity 2.1.2. Restore ecosystems in 
the watersheds of the Yarmouk, King 
Talal Dam and Kafrain areas of the 
Jordan Valley. 
 
Restoration of forest, rangeland, riparian and wadi 
ecosystems in these watershed areas will be informed 
by the development of site- and ecosystem-specific 
restoration protocols. These will include 
recommended climate-resilient plant species, water 
harvesting and conservation measures and 
maintenance guidelines. The target ecosystems will 
then be restored to strengthen the supply of 
ecosystem services, such as water, fodder and 
NTFPs, to buffer the impacts of climate change on the 
lives and livelihoods of local communities. 
2.1.2.1: Develop protocols for the climate-
resilient restoration approaches identified for 
each project site, based on baselines 
assessments. 
 
2.1.2.2: Implement climate-resilient restoration of 
degraded watersheds at project sites, 3,178 ha 
within the Yarmouk site, 2,309 ha within the King 
Talal Site, and 2,512 ha within the Kafrain site. 
 
2.1.2.3: Establish central nursery at the RGB 
and community-managed nurseries (including 
the provision of seed and seedlings of species 
2.1.2.1.a. Three baseline assessments and

protocols (one for each site) 
 
2.1.2.2.a. 8,000 ha of restored ecosystems 
(~1,600 ha per year from year 3 of

2.1.2.3.a Central and community-managed 
nurseries 
 
2.2.4a Small-scale rainwater harvesting 
interventions at restoration sites

identified under 2.1.2.1) at each intervention site 
to grow the seedlings required for climate-

2.1.2.4: Implement small-scale rainwater 
harvesting interventions at restoration sites to 
enhance the success of restoration 
interventions. 
Activity 2.1.3.  Introduce gender-
responsive climate-resilient additional 
livelihoods and agricultural practices in 
the project’s three intervention areas. 
Climate-resilient additional livelihoods and agriculture 
practices will be introduced to reduce the dependency 
of beneficiary communities on livelihoods that are 
heavily reliant on water resources and responsible for 
ecosystem degradation. This approach will 
complement ecosystem restoration under Activity 
2.1.2 through both reducing unsustainable 
degradative practices and promoting livelihoods that 
are reliant on healthy ecosystems. As a result, the 
conservation as well as ongoing restoration of the 
ecosystems in the target watersheds will be 
promoted.  An important aspect of the climate-resilient 
agricultural practices will be the introduction of in-field 
water conservation and harvesting measures. 
2.1.3.1: Technical partner site visits to promote 
and train local communities on beekeeping as a 
climate-resilient additional livelihood to 
incentivize ecosystem restoration. 
 
2.1.3.2: Technical partner site visits to promote 
and train communities (at least 75% women) on 
vegetable gardening through hydroponics and 
permaculture (wicking beds) as an additional 
livelihood. 
 
2.1.3.3: Technical partner site visits to promote 
the uptake of climate-resilient agricultural 
practices on 1,200 smallholder farms across the 
three intervention areas to improve productivity 
under climate change conditions. 
 
2.1.3.4: Train selected small-scale farmers 
within the three project sites on the use and 
maintenance of climate-resilient agriculture 
techniques, including in-field water conservation 
and harvesting measures. 
 
2.1.3.5: Identify private sector champions in the 
agricultural sector to mentor the beneficiaries of 
climate-resilient additional livelihoods and

as a mechanism through which financial resources for

2.1.4.1: Design the Water Fund in a participatory

ecosystems in the three target watersheds and

water resources in particular. The objective of the 
fund is to leverage investment into the restoration of 
watersheds to reduce the need to build wastewater 
treatment plants for the attenuation of dwindling water 
supplies. The rationale behind this is to provide 
financial incentive to upstream communities to reduce 
practices that result in compromised water quality and 
quantity downstream, and to serve as custodians of 
the ecosystems in these areas. The Water Fund will 
be a model for the payment for ecosystem services 
(PES), leveraging financial from private (industry and

restoration of watershed ecosystems in the project

stakeholders and investors.

Activity 3.1.3. Introduce small-scale 
rainwater harvesting interventions in 
the project’s three target areas to 
improve access to clean water under 
climate change conditions in a gender-
responsive manner 
Under Activity 3.1.3. small-scale rooftop rainwater 
harvesting interventions will be installed at various 
buildings across the project’s three intervention sites. 
These buildings will include 2,800 private houses 
(~14,000 people — 8% of the target population), 50 
community/religious centres, 50 institutional buildings 
and 100 schools — 3,000 buildings in total. 
Beneficiary households will be selected based on the 
appropriateness of buildings for implementation, 
willingness of owners and water availability – i.e. 
those most vulnerable to water scarcity. A 32 CM 
cistern will be installed at each of these buildings to 
supplement and reduce the demand on domestic 
supplies during dry periods. This will result in a 
minimum increase in annual water supplies of 0.28 
MCM/year in the target area.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
327. 
The Project Coordinator (PC), under the oversight of the UNEP Task Manager, will be responsible for monitoring progress against the project’s results-based indicators 
during implementation. For on-the-ground monitoring of project interventions, a full time Monitoring Officer will be employed to conduct and coordinate monitoring and evaluation 
(M&E) activities for the project. At the start of project implementation, the Monitoring Officer will design and operationalise a performance monitoring framework to track the 
project’s progress towards achieving its targets. This will include: i) measuring performance against the project indicators (Sections E.3–5) to evaluate the progress of the 
project; ii) reporting the project’s performance to the PSC and PMU; and iii) providing technical support to the PC. Additionally, the Monitoring Officer will be responsible for 
overseeing and monitoring the application of gender-disaggregated indicators and ensuring that all gender targets are met. At particular milestones throughout the project — 
including annual performance reports, interim evaluations and at project conclusion — the project team will undertake evidence-gathering exercises to verify the progress. 
These internal reports will be further validated by reviews at the mid-term and end of the project implementation, as described below. UNEP will be responsible for managing 
the interim and the final evaluation of the project.  
 
Interim Evaluation 
328. 
The UNEP Task Manager will oversee the process of hiring an external independent consultant to carry out the interim evaluation, who will provide an assessment of 
project performance against its targets at the project’s mid-point. This will be a formative exercise and will include analysing whether the project is on track, what problems 
and challenges the project is encountering, and what corrective actions are required so that the project will achieve its intended outcomes by project completion in the most 
efficient and sustainable way. The PSC will participate in the interim evaluation and develop a management response to the evaluation recommendations along with an 
implementation plan. It is the responsibility of the UNEP Task Manager to monitor whether the agreed recommendations are being implemented during the remainder of the 
project’s operational life.  
 
Final Evaluation 
329. 
An independent consultant will be contracted by the UNEP Evaluation Office to conduct the final evaluation at the end of project implementation. UNEP‘s Evaluation 
Office (EO) will be responsible for overseeing the final evaluation at the end of project implementation — which is a summative evaluation – and will liaise with the UNEP Task 
Manager throughout the process. An independent assessment of project performance against standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, 
likelihood of impact and sustainability) will be made based on documentary evidence, stakeholder interviews and, in most cases, a field mission. The evaluation criteria will be 
aligned with the criteria contained in the GCF Evaluation Policy. Each evaluation criterion will be rated using a six-point rating scheme. and a weighted average will be 
determined to provide an overall performance rating for the project as a whole. Where there are any differences in ratings between the evaluation team and the Evaluation 
Office a final determination will be made by the Evaluation Office when the evaluation report is finalised. 
 
330. 
The draft final evaluation report will be sent to project stakeholders during a commenting process managed by the Evaluation Office. Formal comments on the report will 
be shared by the Evaluation Office in an open and transparent manner. This evaluation report will be publicly disclosed and will be followed by a recommendation on compliance 
processes. The costs for results monitoring and performance evaluation are included in the project budget (Annex 3) under budget lines 95, 96, 127, 128, 167 and 180. 
 
331. 
The EEs (MoEnv and IUCN) will submit semi-annual progress reports and quarterly financial statements to the PMU and the PMU will consolidate the reports and submit 
them to UNEP as the AE. In turn, UNEP will submit annual performance reports and semi-annual financial reports to GCF. The detailed reporting timelines are as follows: 
 
knowledge/information of climate-resilient 
ILWRM approaches.

F.  RISK ASSESMENT AND MANAGEMENT 
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages) 
 
Selected Risk Factor 1 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Technical and operational 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
High staff turnover could impede capacity building, the retention of skills and knowledge, and knowledge-sharing across 
the relevant institutions. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
4. 
To mitigate the potential impact of a high staff turnover, communities and district authorities at different levels 
will be involved in the implementation of the proposed project. As a result, knowledge regarding project planning and 
implementation processes will be spread across a range of individuals, thereby reducing the impact on the project if 
individuals leave their respective organisations. Another method of negating high staff turnover is to account for this 
factor in the selection criteria for the hiring of project staff. For example, individuals with diverse skill sets and a 
professional life expectancy of more than 10 years should be encouraged to apply — particularly young people with 
sufficient relevant experience. This will ensure that staff members remain in their positions for longer periods of time and 
that institutional knowledge is retained over the project’s lifespan.  
 
5. 
Under the proposed project, institutional stability will be provided by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) and 
technical working groups (TWG) established as part of the project management arrangements (see Section B.4 for more 
detail). In addition, a knowledge-sharing platform will be developed under Activity 4.1.2 to ensure that access to 
information on best practices and lessons learned related to climate change adaptation (including ILWRM) is available 
to all new staff. The training programme developed under Activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.3, will be made available to all relevant 
stakeholders to facilitate the appropriate training of all new staff, further contributing to the mitigating of this risk. 
Selected Risk Factor 2 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Technical and operational 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
The implementation of proposed project interventions will cease after the conclusion of the project. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
336. 
An Exit Strategy has been developed to ensure the proposed project’s long-term sustainability and to decrease 
the likelihood that this risk will occur (Section B.6). The primary aspects of the Exit Strategy include: i) the integration of 
climate-resilient  Integrated Landscape and Water Resources Management (ILWRM) into policies, strategies and plans, 
to ensure the maintenance, replication and upscaling of project interventions post project (Activity 1.1.2); ii) capacity 
building at national and local, governmental and non-governmental levels, to ensure that the maintenance of project 
activities is sustained (Activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.3); iii) the improvement of knowledge management for climate change 
adaptation in Jordan, facilitating the maintenance and upscaling of project interventions after the conclusion of the project 
(Activity 4.1.2); and iv) the establishment of a Water Fund at the project interventions sites, which will leverage funding 
for the maintenance and continued restoration of upstream watershed area in the Jordan Valley (Activity 2.1.4). 
Selected Risk Factor 3 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Technical and operational 
Low 
Medium 
Description 
337. 
Community trust in the project falters leading to conflict, lack of ownership and ineffectiveness of interventions.  
This may result from insufficient coordination between implementing agencies during project implementation, limited 
integration of local knowledge into project activities and/or lack of effective benefit sharing (for example, large farmers 
may gain control over certain project interventions, excluding others).

=== PAGE 136 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 132 of 144 
 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
338. 
The proposed project’s development has involved extensive engagement with local-level stakeholders to ensure 
their support for project activities. Extensive engagements with project partners and stakeholders will continue during 
project implementation, as detailed in the project’s Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 6).  
 
339. 
The development of standard operating procedures (SOPs) by the project’s Executing Entities (EEs) — MoEnv 
and IUCN — and the PMU that guide the project’s approach to working with target communities. The SOPs will be 
included in the contracts of all Technical Partners, Contractors, Consultants, etc., engaged under the project. Adherence 
to SOPs will be monitored by the project’s safeguards officer. 
 
340. 
The development and signing of agreements between target communities and project EEs related to 
implementation. Any Technical Partners, Contractors, Consultants, etc., will also be bound by these agreements. The 
agreements will outline commitments and obligations of the project to communities. 
 
341. 
Project implementation activities will build-on and/or draw from existing local committees/groups, systems and 
procedures to ensure local stakeholder participation. This will be complemented by WMCs established under 
Component 1 of the project, which will facilitate vertical as well as horizontal coordination and collaboration between 
local-level stakeholders and project partners. The presence of WMCs will ensure that target communities’ needs and 
concerns are considered in the development and implementation of water monitoring and management plans. 
Representatives of target communities will be members of the WMCs, providing them with direct inputs into decision-
making processes and ensuring that relevant information is always made available to them. These community 
representatives will be members of community-based committees already present on the ground in the three project 
areas, such as Development Advisory Committees, Stakeholders Advisory Groups, agricultural cooperatives, women’s 
cooperatives, youth groups and Water User Associations). 
 
342. 
A detailed stakeholder mapping analysis to ensure that all relevant stakeholders (including community groups 
are represented in WMCs). The representation of and engagement with communities will be built into the mandate/ToR 
of WMCs (a framework ToR for WMCs is presented in Annex 28). WMCs will ensure that on-the-ground activities (such 
as ecosystem restoration, Outputs 2.1 and 2.2) are only undertaken after resources users, who have traditional rights 
of access and use of the land, have been consulted and agreed to the interventions. Additionally, WMCs will monitor 
benefit sharing under the project’s activities, ensuring that no stakeholder group, such as large commercial farmers, 
gains control over certain project interventions, excluding others such as local communities. Fostering the formal 
recognition of WMCs in relevant environmental legislature (via MoEnv) will ensure their recognition and authority, 
contributing to the representation of communities and building their trust in the project. Moreover, the local experience 
and footprint of the consortium of NGOs involved in project execution will contribute to successful engagement with 
local-level stakeholders.  These NGOs will operate under the guidance of one of the project’s Executing Entities.  
 
343. 
Community support for interventions will also be fostered by undertaking of baseline assessments at each of the 
restoration sites, including consultations with local resource users to draw on their knowledge and foster their FPIC for 
the interventions. In addition, local resource users will be consulted during the development of restoration protocols to 
ensure that their traditional knowledge is considered. 
 
344. 
Should access by local resource users to restoration sites be restricted, then suitable — and agreed upon — 
alternatives or compensations arrangements needs to be developed, negotiated with local resource users, and formally 
adopted before the commencement of any field interventions.  During periods when resource users may have limited 
access to restoration sites, they will be identified as restoration workers so that they are able to continue earning an 
income. These individuals will also be targeted for the additional climate-resilient livelihoods introduced through the 
project. In the case of livestock owners, animal feed will be provided via the project, should restriction of access to 
grazing areas be encountered.  
 
345. 
The impact of awareness raining (Output 4.1) will be monitored under the outcome-level indicator for this output 
through the assessing of behavioural change of project beneficiaries. This will be facilitated by the establishment of 
community-based participatory mechanisms for monitoring and evaluating change in climate resilience, as well as 
behavioural change (Activity 2.1.1). 
Selected Risk Factor 4

Probability

Governance

Description 
Limited coordination between implementing partners reducing the efficiency of interventions. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
346. 
Although experience with multi-sectoral approaches is limited in Jordan, there are encouraging signs that their 
recognition is rising and that multiple players realise their synergetic value, particularly with regard to climate change 
(for example, through the Climate Change Policy, and NCCC). The project’s implementation strategy will build on this 
momentum and provide a catalytic impetus through hands-on implementation experience on the ground. This, in 
combination with the project’s multi-stakeholder implementation approach, will foster coordination. Relevant aspects of 
the project’s implementation strategy are presented below. 
• 
A project management unit and steering committee will be established to coordinate implementation (Further details 
on the implementation arrangements are presented in Section C.7). 
• 
A national-level TWG, as well as local-level TWG’s at each of the project intervention sites will be established to 
facilitate coordination during implementation. 
 
347. 
The relevant institutions were involved in the design of the project activities and have agreed on all roles and 
responsibilities. A monitoring and evaluation plan has been designed for the project, which will provide a platform for 
monitoring the performance of implementing partners and will ensure that these institutions and agencies are fulfilling 
their responsibilities during the proposed project’s implementation period. This will be supported by regular 
meetings/engagements between implementing partners to facilitate information sharing. 
Selected Risk Factor 5 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Other 
Low 
Medium 
Description 
348. 
Private sector stakeholders — for example profit-driven large-scale agriculture and industry — may not have the 
same goals or interests as other stakeholders such as small-scale farmers. This will lead to misalignment in priorities 
between stakeholders, inhibiting the realization of benefits and the effective implementation of project interventions. For 
these private sector stakeholders, short-term profits gained from continued business-as-usual practices may be a higher 
priority than the long-term benefits gained from improved quality and quantity of water resources enabled by restoration 
and water attenuation interventions implemented under Outputs 2.1, 2.2 and 3.1. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
349. 
Under the proposed project, due diligence of potential private sector partners will be undertaken; concrete and 
clear roles and responsibilities for each partner will be established and strict adherence to role and responsibilities will 
be ensured.

Conflicts in neighbouring countries and the potential influx of additional refugees could influence government priorities, 
resulting in delays in project implementation and reduce the likeliness of project sustainability. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
350. 
While the project will not prevent possible conflicts in neighbouring countries, nor a resulting influx of refugees, 
the inclusion of both state and non-state actors on the PSC, TWGs and as implementing partners, will contribute to the 
continued implementation of project activities should this risk materialize 
Selected Risk Factor 7 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Governance

Description

=== PAGE 138 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 134 of 144 
 
Changes in political leadership at local level, as well as within ministries and other participating governmental institutions 
could result in delays in implementation. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
351. 
As a constitutional monarchy, Jordan’s government is considered stable — particularly in comparison to other 
countries in the region. Even with more frequent changes in governing parties through elections and via personnel shifts 
in ministries, which are to be anticipated, the project will be fully anchored through the involvement of multiple ministries 
and institutions, as well as the leading role played by the consortium of NGOs during implementation, ensuring project 
continuity without major interruptions. The integrated approach to project implementation also aims for strong 
involvement of stakeholder groups and collaborative engagement of various administrative entities, facilitating continued 
implementation of interventions in the event of changes in the political situation in the country. Specific mitigation 
measures which have been built into the projects design are mentioned below. 
• 
Regular engagement with and capacity building of non-political actors — such as NGOs, government agency officials, 
staff and local community members — will be prioritised during project implementation. 
• 
Formulation of local ordinances (including TWGs and WMCs), mainstreaming of project interventions into local 
planning, policy-making and budgeting systems will ensure that political issues will have negligible impact on the 
sustainability of project interventions.

be enlisted to demonstrate their use and raise awareness on their benefit.

Description

plan. In addition, the project’s TPs include NGOs who work directly with women in the target areas. These TPs will directly 
support the implementation of gender-related activities, ensuring that they are implemented appropriately based on their 
experience and allowing for adaptive management as needed to ensure targets are met and benefits accrue.

=== PAGE 140 ===

Gender-responsive sensitisation to the participation and roles of women in relevant activities will take place both at project 
inception (Sub-activity 4.1.1.1) as well as prior to the commencement of relevant activities to ensure awareness and 
understanding across beneficiary groups regarding the project’s gender targets and gender-related benefits. The 
project’s gender officer (supported by TPs) will ensure that all relevant information is shared with beneficiary groups and 
that any concerns are addressed. All beneficiary groups will also be made aware of the project’s GRM, including how to 
access it.  
 
The implementation of relevant activities will be tailored to ensure that timing is suitable to female beneficiaries and that 
childcare is provided as needed. Where certain work may not be appropriate for women to undertake, this work will be 
allocated to men, with other roles being allocated to women in consultation with relevant stakeholders. The project’s 
gender officer, project staff, TPs and implementation partners will ensure that harassment is mitigated by providing 
oversight at all project intervention sites where beneficiaries are working. Access to the project’s GRM will also be made 
as convenient as possible. 
 
Please see additional details presented in the Gender Action Plan (GAP) in Annex 8.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
G.1. Environmental and social assessments (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages) 
355. 
The project is being proposed as a Category B or ‘moderate risk’ according to the GCF project classification. This 
classification is because any adverse environmental or social risks of project activities will have few impacts that are 
site-specific, irreversible or not readily addressed through mitigation measures. Justification for this is provided in the 
Environmental, Social and Economic Review Note (ESERN), Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), 
Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) and Social Safeguards Assessment in Annex 6. The main risks 
anticipated are summarised below in Table 15, along with mitigation measures to address them.  
 
Table 15. Summary of environmental and social risks and mitigation measures. 
Summary of risks 
Mitigation factors or measures and management strategies 
Environmental 
The risk of changes to the 
landscape and natural drainage 
systems. 
Through the correct selection of intervention sites related to project activities and the adoption of 
appropriate implementation protocols providing details of the interventions before 
implementation, substantial changes to biophysical features of the landscape will be avoided or 
kept to an absolute minimum.  
 
International best practices for ecosystem restoration will be adopted. Project planning and 
implementation teams will be trained on guidelines and techniques to ensure that these best 
practices are adhered to.  
The risk of solid and liquid 
waste generation. 
Procedures and products will be used that reduce the production of liquid or solid waste — 
including the limited use of single-use plastics and the use of water efficient appliances. Proper 
liquid and solid waste containers will be installed and workers will be trained on correct disposal 
procedures. All construction machinery will receive periodic maintenance to ensure efficient 
performance. The maintenance will be undertaken offsite in specialised maintenance stations to 
avoid the release of liquid waste.  
 
All construction or domestic liquid and solid waste will be collected and disposed of following the 
environmental legislative requirements. This includes the recycling and reuse of as much solid 
waste as possible, the avoidance of solid waste burning, and waste removal by professional 
waste management companies. All temporary solid waste dumping sites will be rehabilitated after 
construction is complete.  
The risk of air and noise 
pollution. 
To reduce emissions, the transportation of excavated material over long distances will be 
avoided. This will be accomplished by the synchronization of excavating and filling processes, 
which will additionally reduce the accumulation of any excavated material near the intervention 
site. Excavation machinery will be maintained to ensure efficiency performance, which will reduce 
both air and noise pollution. Activities will be avoided at night to reduce noise generation during 
these times.  
The risk of natural ecosystem 
deterioration and fragmentation. 
A detailed baseline assessment of target watersheds has been undertaken and will be used to 
inform decisions related to the design and planning of interventions. In addition to this, 
government-approved procedures will be used to screen and assess potentially adverse project 
impacts. Any facilities required for project management and support will be located as far as 
possible from ecologically sensitive areas. Workers will be trained on ecosystem monitoring and 
assessments using a set of clear and specific ecological indicators.  
The risk of disturbance of 
ecological corridors and the 
removal of vegetation.  
Major ecological corridors have been mapped to inform project interventions. All workers will be 
informed on the locations and sensitivities of these ecological corridors. Signs will be placed 
around ecologically sensitive areas within the project implementation sites.  
 
The removal of vegetation will be prohibited or kept to an absolute minimum. Specific instructions 
and safeguards for natural vegetation will be included in all contracts and work orders.  
The risk of disturbance of 
wildlife, including breeding or 
migratory birds.  
Signs will be placed in project sites prohibiting the killing or disturbance of wildlife. Stakeholders 
will be trained on the importance of wildlife and the correct method of dealing with their presence. 
Cooperation with law enforcement agencies will be ensured for temporary protection schemes at 
project sites.  
The risk of the deterioration of 
genetic resources. 
Areas of importance for genetic resources have been identified as part of the baseline 
assessments. Signs will be posted in these areas highlighting their ecological importance and 
stakeholders will be trained on the relevance of genetic resources.  
The risk of the introduction of 
invasive species. 
The selection criteria for intervention sites will include special provision for exotic or invasive 
species. Interventions plans will include the eradication of all exotic and invasive species from 
project sites, after which no planting of exotic species will be allowed.  
Social 
The risk of damage to known 
and unknown archaeological 
sites.  
A cultural assessment of all new sites for which information is not currently available will be 
undertaken. Any construction or excavation activities will be halted if any antiquities or 
archaeological items are discovered and will be reported to the Director of the Department of 
Antiquities or the nearest General Security Center. No removal or change to cultural sites or

=== PAGE 142 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 138 of 144 
 
artefacts will be permitted in project intervention sites. A special protection scheme with strong 
law enforcement arrangements will be applied for all cultural sites.  
Risks to the disturbance of local 
traditions and customs.  
Special training will be provided to all contractors and workers on the respect of local tradition 
and customs. Local communities will be able to submit complaints or report any intrusion on their 
local customs and norms through the provision of a clear and easy reporting mechanism. 
The risk of involuntary 
resettlement 
Output two of the project will include a temporary restriction of the use of degraded forests and 
rangelands by local communities. This restriction will gradually be removed as restoration and 
rehabilitation activities progress.  
 
The project will not affect official and clearly recognized land-tenure arrangements. The proposed 
project Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM; see Annex 6) will ensure local users have the 
ability to express their concerns with regards to any violation of their access and user rights 
which have not been explicitly recognized in the project design.  
Risks to labour and working 
conditions. 
The project will involve small-scale work activities and temporary employment. To prevent the 
potential of forced or child labour, the project’s procurement plan will strictly adhere to UNEP and 
the GCF’s procurement principles, as well as national laws.  
Risks to gender equality 
The project incorporates a Gender Analysis and Action Plan with specific gender-targeted 
activities built into the project design, to ensure that the different interests, roles and 
responsibilities of both women and men are assessed during the planning and implementation 
phases.  
 
356. 
The risks considered in the ESERN risk screening checklist have been further elaborated in the Environmental 
and Social Management Plan (ESMP) in Annex 6. This demonstrates how these risks have been considered in the 
fundamental design of the project and any additional mitigation measures that may be required. In addition, given the 
structure of the project, whereby the exact siting and detailed design of the project interventions are being determined 
as part of the project itself (through the integrated landscape and water resources management processes), the 
specific nature of any residual environmental and social risks that remain will be assessed as part of project 
implementation.  
 
357. 
Risks will be managed as described in the list below. 
• 
Undertaking planning, design and implementation processes with close involvement of an experienced environmental 
and social safeguards specialist. 
• 
Ensuring the project officers (forestry, livelihoods, agriculture and water) recruited onto the project are sufficiently 
experienced in safeguards relevant to their specialist technical areas. 
• 
Establishing methodologies for the environmental and social assessment of each of the activities under the project. 
• 
Ensuring the M&E process includes adequate review of environmental and social risks (this will be done by an M&E 
specialist with specific experience in reviewing environmental and social considerations). 
• 
Assigning formal roles and responsibilities for environmental and social management across the project team. 
• 
Establishing a reporting procedure for environmental and social concerns related to the interventions. 
• 
Incorporating environmental and social safeguard considerations in the training, capacity building and technical 
assistance delivered through the project. 
 
358. 
The environmental and social management processes will draw upon the following documents: 
• 
GCF Environmental and Social Management System; 
• 
UNEP safeguards systems; 
• 
summaries of stakeholder consultations; 
• 
baselines of environmental and social conditions; 
• 
project ESMP, including specific management frameworks and baselines (Annex 6); 
• 
project-level GRM (see Annex 6); and 
• 
project Gender Assessment and Action Plan (Annex 8). 
 
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
359. 
During project preparation, a gender assessment was undertaken to determine the differential vulnerability to, 
and impact of, climate change on women and men in Jordan, focusing on the Jordan Valley in particular. From the 
findings of this assessment and an analysis of the proposed project solution, a gender action plan (GAP) was 
developed. To design this gender analysis as part of preparation for a GAP, a rapid review was undertaken of 
supporting documents related to gender mainstreaming in climate change, as well as previous gender analyses 
conducted by other donors in Jordan — in addition to data available from the Department of Statistics (DoS). The 
gender assessment also identifies gender considerations that are relevant to the project and examines potential gender 
mainstreaming opportunities. Stakeholder consultations were undertaken and focus group discussions (FGDs) were

=== PAGE 143 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 139 of 144 
 
carried out in three target areas selected to benefit from the project with a total of 60 participants, of which more than 
50% were women. This was done to highlight gender and social norms and to identify key entry points for women’s 
participation in project activities within the study area.  
 
360. 
The Government of Jordan has established that gender-responsive planning, resource-allocation and 
implementation in the context of the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILMI) will be a priority. 
Therefore, supporting transformational change both in the lives of female beneficiaries as well as in prompting gender 
mainstreaming in national climate change policy-making and implementation was put forward as a priority in 
stakeholder consultations. In addition to describing men and women’s relative vulnerability and adaptation strategies 
to climate change, the gender assessment and problem analysis identified risks and opportunities relating to gender. 
Specific findings of the analysis are described below. 
 
• 
Only 30% of the FGD women participants knew about climate change and its disproportionate impacts on women and 
children.  
• 
The majority of FGD male participants (80%) said that the climate change conditions will affect both men and women 
in the same way.  
• 
Men control access to resources in which they own the key productive assets such as land, livestock and medium to 
large businesses. For climate change interventions particularly in drylands, this means that activities which include the 
use of productive resources like land or livestock should include both men and women in decision-making. Secondly, 
any proposed land use change relating to dryland conservation should include both local men and women as key 
stakeholders. 
• 
Access to credit is a key constraint in improving women’s economic productivity in the selected areas. Therefore, the 
project will focus on improving women’s access to credit through the Water Fund — which will be established under 
Activity 2.4 — to pay for ongoing watershed restoration in the upper-catchment areas of the project's three target sites.  
• 
In the targeted areas, rates of women’s illiteracy, poverty and unemployment are high, which make them more 
vulnerable to climate change impacts than men. Gender-responsive trainings provide opportunities to support 
vulnerable groups in the targeted areas, particularly women-headed households. 
• 
More investment is required to improve women’s participation in alternative livelihoods, to ensure that their rights and 
interests are recognised.  
• 
The proposed project will provide support to the Ministry of Environment to activate gender mainstreaming actions 
stated in the climate change policy and involve women in the decision-making process. 
• 
Increased collaboration and partnership with CSOs, NGOs and the entities working on climate change issues will 
increase awareness of gender and environmental sustainability challenges in the targeted areas. The objective of this 
awareness-raising should be to increase women’s knowledge and equal rights, in particular. In addition, CSOs are 
expected to support gender mainstreaming by involving whole communities — including men and local leaders — to 
overcome sensitivity to the traditional divisions of labour that may help to achieve better social acceptability and 
inclusion for project activities. 
 
361. 
To mitigate against introducing gender biases amongst project participants and beneficiaries during 
implementation and after project completion, the GAP outlines several interventions to ensure that the project does 
not unintentionally contribute to perpetuating or worsening gender equality and gender-based climate vulnerability in 
the project areas. The GAP seeks to promote gender equality by including actions and targets which will increase 
women’s participation, representation and awareness, as well as ensuring that training programmes are gender-
responsive and -inclusive. It includes clear targets, sex-disaggregated data collection, gender-responsive design 
features and quantifiable performance indicators to promote women’s participation and benefits from the project. The 
project will also: 
• 
ensure gender inclusion in all the policies reviewed;  
• 
ensure sustainability and long-term impact of gender-affirming actions (please see the actions listed below); 
• 
provide capacity building for government institutions in acknowledging gender differentials in responding to climate 
risks; and 
• 
increase social accountability as described int the list of actions below.  
 
362. 
To achieve the objectives, several actions have been identified which will be part of the GAP. These are listed 
below. 
• 
Ensure women’s participation in trainings of at least 30% of total number of participants. 
• 
Ensure that revisions to Jordan’s land-management policy, planning and legal framework are gender-responsive by 
including sections addressing women’s needs and interests. 
• 
Ensure women and youth participation of 50% of the total population in the targeted areas. 
• 
Ensure participation of women CSOs in each targeted area.

=== PAGE 144 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 140 of 144 
 
• 
Introduce success stories highlighting the impact of women and other vulnerable groups participation in the climate 
change adaptation. 
• 
Include at least two women’s CSOs in each of the targeted areas. 
• 
Ensure women and vulnerable groups participate in the consultation process for climate-resilient restoration of 
watersheds. 
• 
Ensure 100% of female household beneficiaries are trained on the operation and maintenance of small-scale water-
harvesting interventions. 
• 
Create women’s groups as water-wise ambassadors via the community-based water use management and monitoring 
system. 
• 
Ensure 20% of women farmers are represented by the women’s groups as water-wise ambassadors.  
• 
Create funding mechanisms for women. 
  
363. 
A primary objective of the project with regards to gender is increased resilience to climate change of at least 
77,080 women in the project area. This will be achieved through direct improvement of women’s capacities for 
improved water supply and demand management though climate-resilient ILWRM practices. The project will employ a 
full-time Gender Officer, who will be situated within the Project Management Unit (PMU), to oversee the implementation 
of the GAP, as well as provide inputs and conduct gender mainstreaming during project implementation. The full 
Gender Assessment and project-level gender action plan are included as Annex 8. 
 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
364. 
The financial management and procurement within the project will be guided by UN financial regulations, rules 
and procedures, as well as UNEP’s Programme Manual. The financial rules of UNEP, which follow International Public 
Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS), are promulgated pursuant to the Financial Regulations and Rules of the UN. 
Within this context, funding-allocation mechanisms are managed as per the UN framework, including eligibility criteria, 
proposal evaluation processes, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is audited 
annually by the UN Board of Auditors and has established dedicated trust funds for GCF resources. 
 
365. 
The funding of a project will be established through a distinct grant within the Trust Fund, with the project itself 
being set up in the UNEP Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) as a “Work Breakdown Structure” (WBS), which is itself 
further broken down into “Work Breakdown Structure Elements” (WBSEs), organised by output and outcome. The 
grant of the project will be linked to the WBSEs to fund the activities contributing to the delivery of specific outputs (as 
per the disbursement schedule detailed in the term sheet). The fee income will be managed through a dedicated grant 
independently and separately of the GCF project grant funds. In line with UNEP procedures, the project will appoint a 
Financial Officer within the PMU who will be responsible for monitoring, reporting on and approving requests for funds 
on a quarterly basis for the activities executed by the Ministry of Environment (MoEnv). Reports to summarise the 
disbursement and projected demands for project funding will be prepared and submitted to a UNEP Task Manager 
who will conduct project supervision, in line with reporting standards and methodologies applied in past projects, such 
as those implemented using GEF modalities. The UN financial regulations and rules require the segregation of duties, 
and safeguards to ensure compliance with UN financial rules and regulations. In addition, a Fund Management Officer 
will be appointed to assist UNEP’s Task Manager with all financial monitoring and supervision functions. All 
procurement will be undertaken in line with UN procurement regulations, rules and policies. UNEP’s modalities for 
project implementation, results in funds being transferred in tranches to the EEs (MoEnv and IUCN) once the EEs 
have satisfied the conditions that are defined under the legal/cooperation instrument — including the Project 
Cooperation Agreement (PCA) and Internal Cooperation Agreement (ICA), to be signed between UNEP’s Ecosystems 
Division and MoEnv. The PCA will include specific obligations for the EEs on financial management and reporting and 
will require periodic financial reporting to follow international financial and auditing standards. The PCA specifically 
requires the audit be undertaken by a recognised firm of certified public accountants or, for governments, by a 
government auditor. This auditor should state whether the GCF proceeds were covered by the scope of the audit.  
 
366. 
As a GoJ authority, MoEnv follows the government’s financial and procurement rules and standards. During the 
proposed project’s inception phase, UNEP will undertake a thorough assessment of MoEnv’s capacity to undertake 
procurement in line with UN regulations, rules and processes. This assessment will guide the procurement monitoring 
plan which will be agreed upon between UNEP and MoEnv. The assessment will be undertaken following project 
approval but prior to project implementation. It will include assessments of elements of governance and public 
accountability such as, inter alia, review of the existence and quality of policies, legal and institutional framework, and 
systems supporting transparency, accountability and controls, especially in the use of donor funds. The outcome of 
the assessment will determine the threshold for procurement that is undertaken by MoEnv, which will be reflected in 
the procurement monitoring plan. This plan is an annex to the PCA and will be shared with GCF upon signature, as 
required by the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). The procurement monitoring plan will also be reviewed periodically. 
The project’s investments in equipment will be undertaken in accordance with UN procurement procedures. Finally, in

=== PAGE 145 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | Page 141 of 144 
 
line with the UNEP Programme Manual, MoEnv will be requested to provide an annual compliance audit covering all 
aspects of the project execution including review of all expenditures incurred during the financial year. 
 
G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal 
 
☒ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be 
disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence. 
☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not 
be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited 
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes: 
• 
full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an 
explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and 
• 
redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website. 
 
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.
