I. 
Overall assessment of the Secretariat  
1. 
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks: 
Strengths 
Points of reflection 
Comprehensive approach: Jordan 
Integrated Landscape Management Initiative 
employs an integrated land and water 
resources management approach, aiming to 
reduce the negative impacts of climate 
change on water resources through improved 
ecosystem management, managed aquifer 
recharge, rainwater harvesting, evaporation 
reduction, improved agricultural practices 
and strengthened governance. 
The structure of the water fund will be 
designed during the first two years of the 
implementation. This offers a unique 
opportunity to factor in further consultations 
and ensure a very robust design of the fund.  
Wide-reaching impact: the project targets 
significant environmental and social 
outcomes, including the enhancement of 
water security for households, increased 
resilience of ecosystems and direct benefits 
to 246,942 people in the Jordan Valley, 
reducing their climate vulnerability. 
 
Collaborative funding and execution: the 
project involves collaboration between the 
Government of Jordan, various non-
governmental organizations and GCF, with 
substantial co-financing. This collective effort 
enhances project credibility and resource 
pooling. 
 
Alignment with Sustainable Development 
Goals: the projects aligns with multiple 
Sustainable Development Goals, addressing 
urgent adaptation needs, ecosystem 
restoration and food security, all of which are 
crucial for Jordan's resilience to climate 
change impacts.

=== PAGE 154 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 154 
 
 
Scalability and replicability: the project is 
designed with the potential for replication 
and scaling across different regions, which 
could amplify its impact and serve as a model 
for similar arid regions facing water scarcity 
and climate change challenges. 
 
2. 
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the 
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity, and, if considered 
appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/B.39/02. 
II. 
Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment 
2.1 
Project background 
3. 
Jordan is considered one of the most drought-prone countries in the world, with 
agricultural consumption accounting for the majority of the water resources available in the 
country. Jordan’s current food systems face significant challenges, with climate change having 
an irreversible impact on current and future food production as well as a direct negative impact 
on the ecosystems and their ability to maintain and support the hydrological cycle in the 
country.  
4. 
Farmers and communities face challenges in rapidly transitioning to the less drought-
prone, diversified and more resilient local food systems necessary to secure future food 
production, while simultaneously managing, supporting and restoring the ecosystems in their 
crucial role in driving the hydrological cycle.  
5. 
The proposed project seeks to overcome these climate challenges through focusing on 
ecosystem restoration, improving local food systems and, through improved governance and 
water management strategies, facilitating a sustainable landscape-led approach with long-term 
benefits. The project presents strong adaptation impacts with significant mitigation co-benefits. 
6. 
The targeted interventions are estimated to reach 246,942 direct and 756,000 indirect 
beneficiaries across three target watersheds in the Jordan Valley – Yarmouk, King Talal Dam 
and Kafrain – achieving ecosystem restoration aimed at water resources benefits across 12,000 
ha.  
7. 
The total project financing is USD 60,509,951, of which a GCF grant of USD 44,904,203 is 
requested. Co-financing of USD 15,605,748 has also been secured to support the project. The co-
financing will be provided by the Government of Jordan and a consortium of non-governmental 
organizations (NGOs) in the form of grants and in-kind contributions. In addition to the co-
financing, the project brings parallel finance worth USD 13,133,000. The project is submitted 
under environmental and social safeguards category B, and the total duration of the project is 
eight years. 
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis 
Component 1: Climate change adaptation governance in Jordan 
Outcome 1: Increased public budgets for adaptation (total costs: USD 2,338,394; GCF costs USD 
1,616,864, or 69 per cent)

=== PAGE 155 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 155 
 
 
8. 
Component 1 will play a strategic role in creating the enabling policy and governance 
environment to facilitate future investments in the food and ecosystems sectors for the project 
lifetime and beyond. The component will implement interventions seeking to mainstream 
integrated landscape water resources management (ILWRM) into key regulatory frameworks in 
Jordan in the land, water, agriculture and natural resources sectors. This will support the 
creation of sound and practical evidence for the economic benefits of ILWRM investments.  
9. 
The component will establish key protocols, standards and guidelines on ILWRM for the 
site-specific conditions of the Jordan Valley across the three targeted watersheds to ensure full 
coordination and strategic approaches across these sites. Currently there are insufficient 
practices, capacity and knowledge on the ground to systematically embed ILWRM approaches, 
and hence component 1 plays an important role in ensuring a sustainable roll-out of the 
proposed ILWRM interventions. These protocols, standards and guidelines will target both State 
and non-State actors in the country and is based on a highly participatory approach in securing 
the most practical and sustainable use and implementation of the tools and guidelines.   
10. 
These key materials will be the basis for a number of capacity-building interventions 
across the three watersheds and further help to form the basis for draft amendments to the 
current policy landscape on planning and legal frameworks for agriculture, land, water and 
natural resources to ensure that ILWRM innovations are well represented and embedded. This 
will help to ensure that all future investments and innovations in the sectors are built on the 
solid ground of ILWRM strategic ideas and evidence.  
11. 
The component further seeks to establish water management committees, which will be 
a core part of the proposed four components and their successful implementation as well as the 
long-term transition of the food and ecosystems sectors envisaged by the project. The water 
management committees will be composed of a solid representation of all key stakeholders in 
the three watersheds and will ensure a sound and practical solution platform where key 
solutions and practical advice will be conveyed and scaled up. The component will be financed 
by GCF resources with co-financing from the Government of Jordan.  
 
Component 2: Integrated landscape and water resources management under conditions 
of climate change 
 
Outcome 2. Climate-resilient integrated landscape and water resources management practised in 
three Jordan Valley watersheds (total costs: USD 32,925,452; GCF costs USD 22,395,865, or 68 per 
cent) 
12. 
 Component 2 of the project emphasizes the implementation of climate-resilient ILWRM 
across three watersheds in the Jordan Valley, aiming to enhance adaptation to climate change 
through ecosystem restoration. A key strength of this component is its holistic approach to 
managing environmental degradation by integrating various restoration practices that focus on 
both mitigating current damage and preventing future vulnerabilities. This approach is 
operationalized through outcome 2, which promotes climate-resilient ILWRM practices, and 
output 2.1, where specific techniques and ecosystem restorations are implemented.  
13. 
In particular the component will establish and design a water fund to operate as a key 
mechanism for financial resources to support restoration activities, ecosystem-based 
adaptation. The architecture of the fund will be developed during the first two years of the 
project enabling a solid and robust design building on a model for payment for ecosystem 
services.  
14. 
The component also addresses critical issues such as vegetation loss and soil erosion 
exacerbated by climate change, employing both passive and active restoration methods. Passive

=== PAGE 156 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 156 
 
 
restoration includes measures such as removing sources of degradation, while active 
restoration involves re-establishing vegetation and afforestation.  
15. 
Community involvement is central to the project, with local communities participating 
actively in restoration activities. This not only fosters community ownership of the initiatives 
but also helps in building local capacity through training and employment opportunities, 
particularly for women and other marginalized groups. Gender-responsive climate-resilient 
agricultural techniques will be implemented on smallholder farms to increase productivity 
under climate change conditions, providing immediate benefits for local communities, while at 
the same time reducing the pressure on degraded ecosystems undergoing restoration. 
 
Component 3: Improved protection, supply and use of water resources under conditions  
of climate change 
 
Outcome 3: Enhanced water security for households in target areas (total costs: USD 15,891,345; 
GCF costs 12,758,414 or 80 per cent) 
16. 
This component will aim to achieve enhanced water security for households in target 
areas. The main output will be improved protection, supply and use of water under climate 
change conditions. Key interventions under this component will include (i) rehabilitation of 
active freshwater springs and building of natural check dams; (ii) establishment of rockfill dams 
to capture rainwater run-off and contribute to aquifer recharge; and (iii) introduction of rooftop 
rainwater harvesting systems, specifically in communities that are vulnerable to water scarcity, 
primarily focusing on female-headed households. These interventions aim to improve both the 
quality and quantity of surface water and groundwater, subsequently enhancing water security 
for households. The implementation of rooftop rainwater harvesting is projected to save a 
significant amount of the total domestic demand if applied across Jordan. 
17. 
Furthermore, the installation of floating solar panels over the King Abdullah Canal 
should reduce water loss due to evaporation by minimizing exposure to direct sunlight and 
wind, thus increasing water storage efficiency, improving water availability and reducing 
overall water demand. Gender-responsive community-based water monitoring and 
management plans will also be developed for target areas to ensure better management and 
sustainable use of water resources. This will involve the wider community, ensuring that needs 
and concerns are considered and that information is made available to them. Training and 
livelihood support related to water management will also be promoted, including skills such as 
plumbing, rainwater harvesting infrastructure installation and maintenance, and installation of 
solar pumps. 
18. 
Overall, this component targets improving communities' resilience to droughts, 
contributing to increased adaptive capacity, and enhancing awareness of climate threats and 
risk reduction processes. 
 
Component 4: Knowledge management and awareness on climate change adaptation 
 
Outcome 4: Gender-responsive household behavioural change achieved through improved 
information and outreach (total costs USD 3,879,500; GCF costs USD 3,229,500, or 83 per cent) 
19. 
The strategic goal of this component is the consolidation and enhancement of Jordan's 
climate change knowledge as well as the creation of knowledge management tools to facilitate 
cross-sectoral learning. This would be achieved through the establishment of a national 
repository of climate change adaptation knowledge with a view to strengthening awareness of 
climate threats and risk-reduction processes.

=== PAGE 157 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 157 
 
 
20. 
Ultimately, the successful execution of the component will contribute to the adoption of 
an integrated land and water resource management approach, incorporating gender-responsive 
elements and promoting local, national and regional awareness of the importance and benefits 
of biodiversity. Therefore, the performance of this component crucially depends on effective 
communication strategies, community engagement and the development of evidence-based 
knowledge management systems. 
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria 
3.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
  
           Scale: Medium to high 
21. 
The adaptation impact potential of the project is significant, focusing on enhancing the 
resilience of Jordan’s water resources and agricultural systems to climate change. The project 
aims to significantly enhance the capacity of Jordan to adapt to climate change, particularly in 
terms of water resources management and agriculture. The project is expected to directly 
benefit 246,942 individuals in the Jordan Valley by reducing their vulnerability to climate-
related impacts. Additionally, it will indirectly benefit another 756,000 people across the 
northern Jordan Valley through improved water quality and quantity, stronger governance for 
integrated land and water resources management, and enhanced resilience to the impacts of 
climate change. It is expected that 12,000 ha land will be placed under improved management 
for water resources benefits, with at least one additional (adjacent) municipality per watershed 
site benefiting directly. Interventions at the regulatory and institutional level will indirectly 
benefit the Jordanian population as a whole, amounting to approximately10 million people. 
3.2 
Paradigm shift potential  
  
  
 
 
        Scale: High 
22. 
The proposed project's vision for paradigm shift in Jordan’s management of land and 
water resources under climate change conditions is centred on transforming a fragmented 
system into an integrated, participatory and climate-resilient framework. The initiative aims to 
catalyse transformative change by addressing the institutional, technical and financial barriers 
that have historically hindered ILWRM in Jordan. The project’s theory of change is robust, 
detailing mechanisms through which the identified barriers (e.g. insufficient institutional 
coordination, limited access to climate change adaptation information and financial constraints) 
will be addressed. By strengthening governance, enhancing technical capacity and improving 
financial mechanisms through the creation of a water fund, the project seeks to enable a 
systemic shift. This shift is expected to improve resilience and adaptability across various 
sectors and communities, focusing on inclusive approaches that engage women and other 
marginalized groups. The project outlines clear pathways for replication and scaling, 
emphasizing the upscaling potential of interventions such as ecosystem restoration and 
managed aquifer recharge. The project addresses sustainability through the establishment of 
the water fund, which is designed to secure ongoing financial resources for the maintenance and 
expansion of climate-resilient practices. This financial mechanism, coupled with the training of 
stakeholders and the integration of climate-resilient ILWRM into national policies, underscores 
a commitment to sustaining outcomes over the long term. The project effectively identifies and 
proposes solutions to the most binding constraints to systemic change in Jordan’s water sector. 
By targeting institutional and awareness barriers and leveraging a participatory approach to 
engage a broad range of stakeholders, the project is well positioned to foster a supportive 
environment for sustained change. 
3.3 
Sustainable development potential  
  
 
        Scale: High

=== PAGE 158 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 158 
 
 
23. 
The proposed project holds substantial potential for sustainable development across 
several domains. It has several economic, social and environmental co-benefits. Regarding 
economic co-benefits, the project aims to offer community members training in areas such as 
plumbing, installation and maintenance of rainwater harvesting infrastructure and setting up 
solar pumps. This capacity-building could significantly contribute to job creation and boost 
income levels, particularly among the women trained. In terms of social co-benefits, improved 
access to water is likely to have a ripple effect on health and safety. It could reduce incidences of 
waterborne diseases and cut the time spent on water collection, especially for women and girls. 
These saved hours could then be channelled towards educational pursuits, thus potentially 
enhancing school attendance rates. Furthermore, the upgrades in sanitation facilities that the 
project envisions could result in additional social co-benefits. As far as environmental co-
benefits are concerned, the use of rainwater harvesting systems and solar energy should 
contribute towards improved air, water and soil quality, not to mention conservation and 
biodiversity. The project's focus on using renewable water sources and minimizing water loss 
due to evaporation presents clear environmental benefits.  
24. 
A vital aspect of the project is its emphasis on gender empowerment. It seeks to bridge 
gender inequalities by building capacities, involving women in water management committees, 
and targeting a significant percentage of interventions – such as rainwater harvesting 
technology – to benefit female-headed households. This focus on empowerment can lead to 
greater decision-making power for women, equitable access to resources and enhancement of 
livelihood opportunities.  
25. 
In addition to these benefits, the project aligns well with the following Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 4 
(Quality education), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 
(Affordable and clean energy), SDG 13 (Climate action) and SDG 15 (Life on land). 
3.4 
Needs of the recipient  
    
 
  
                    Scale: High 
26. 
The project proposal addresses the financial, economic, social and institutional needs of 
Jordan, particularly in the context of barriers to accessing climate-related finance. It outlines the 
country's challenges in securing domestic and international funds for climate adaptation and 
mitigation. By focusing on strategic interventions such as improving water management and 
enhancing local governance, the project directly tackles these barriers, ensuring a clear pathway 
to alleviating the financial and knowledge constraints identified. 
27. 
The proposal highlights Jordan’s pronounced vulnerability to climate change due to its 
critical water stress conditions, specifying the impact of reduced rainfall, increased 
temperatures and frequent droughts on the nation's water supply and agricultural productivity. 
The project’s targeted approach in the Jordan Valley aims to protect and improve water 
resources, which is crucial for the local agricultural communities who are at the front line of 
climate impacts. This is aligned with addressing the direct needs of these vulnerable groups, 
including women and children, enhancing their resilience to climate-induced challenges. 
28. 
Institutional capacity constraints are also a significant focus of the proposal, 
acknowledging Jordan’s limited ability to integrate climate change adaptation into policy-
making and sectoral financial planning. The project aims to fill these gaps by fostering better 
access to knowledge and technical expertise, thus empowering policymakers and practitioners 
to implement effective climate responses. This comprehensive consideration of Jordan's 
socioeconomic and institutional landscape ensures that the project not only mitigates 
immediate climate vulnerabilities but also builds long-term resilience. 
3.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
  
 
        Scale: High

=== PAGE 159 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 159 
 
 
29. 
The proposed project demonstrates strong alignment with Jordan's nationally 
determined contributions and various national strategies, demonstrating robust country 
ownership. It directly corresponds with national plans such as the Aligned National Action Plan 
to Combat Desertification 2015–2020 and the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan 
2015–2020, aiming to enhance biodiversity, mitigate desertification and integrate climate 
resilience into agricultural and landscape management. Furthermore, it supports the goals of 
the National Climate Change Policy 2013–2020 by focusing on increasing the adaptive capacity 
of water and agricultural resources and conserving ecosystems, which are critical areas under 
Jordan’s climate adaptation strategy. This alignment ensures that the project is not only relevant 
but also enhances the likelihood of successful implementation and sustainability. 
30. 
The institutional framework for the project involves key national authorities such as the 
Ministry of Environment (MoEnv) and the Climate Change Directorate, which coordinate policy 
implementation and stakeholder engagement. This structure underpins the project’s foundation 
in Jordan’s environmental governance, enhancing its integration into national policies and 
strategies. The establishment of the Climate Change Directorate as a focal point for climate 
change activities and its role in coordinating this project ensures high-level policy support and 
alignment with Jordan’s commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on 
Climate Change. Such integration is crucial for the project's success and its ability to influence 
broader policy and institutional changes. 
31. 
Extensive stakeholder engagement has been a core component of the project’s 
development, ensuring that the project reflects the needs and inputs of a broad range of 
stakeholders, including government bodies, NGOs and local communities. Detailed 
consultations, including in-country missions and workshops, have been conducted to refine the 
project’s design and objectives, ensuring that they are well informed by local conditions and 
challenges. This thorough engagement process supports the project’s relevance and feasibility, 
reflecting a well-rounded approach to understanding and addressing the specific climate 
vulnerabilities and adaptation needs of Jordan. 
3.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness   
                       Scale: Medium to high 
32. 
The total investment from GCF is USD 44,904,203, supplemented by USD 15,605,748 in 
co-financing from the Government of Jordan and consortium of NGOs, with an additional USD 
13,133,000 in parallel finance. This financing structure indicates a substantial leverage of 
additional funding relative to the GCF contribution, emphasizing the project’s efficiency in 
mobilizing financial resources for large-scale climate adaptation initiatives. 
33. 
In terms of cost-effectiveness, the project presents a favourable economic profile. The 
economic analysis predicts a net present value of USD 105,279,874 and an economic rate of 
return of 25 per cent, which significantly exceeds typical thresholds for such projects, indicating 
a high level of economic justification for the investment. The benefit–cost ratio of 3.00 further 
underscores the project's effectiveness, suggesting that the economic benefits substantially 
outweigh the costs. These figures are grounded in well-established practices and reflect a 
thoughtful application of adaptation measures tailored to Jordan's specific climate challenges, 
particularly in water resources management. 
34. 
Furthermore, the proposal details the application of best practices in ILWRM, which are 
essential for addressing Jordan’s critical water scarcity issues exacerbated by climate change. 
The project leverages lessons learned from similar initiatives and incorporates adaptive 
management strategies, participatory monitoring and stakeholder engagement, ensuring that 
interventions are both sustainable and community-focused. This approach is expected to 
enhance resilience across vulnerable communities and ecosystems, demonstrating a 
comprehensive application of international best practices tailored to local conditions.

=== PAGE 160 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 160 
 
 
IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
35. 
Project overview. The project aims to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on 
water resources in Jordan through an ILWRM approach. It has four component covering 
enhanced governance, resource management practices such as restoration, protection and use 
of water resources coupled with knowledge management and climate change awareness and 
outreach activities. The environmental and social co-benefits of the project include reduced 
sedimentation as a result of flood attenuation, enhanced biodiversity due to improved habitats, 
decreased water pollution due to reduced eutrophication, sedimentation and improved soil 
quality. Social co-benefits include improved health and sanitation due to better access to clean 
water and improved conflict management on water resources. 
36. 
Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instrument. The project 
falls under environmental and social safeguards category B classification. The Secretariat 
confirms the risk category, which is also within the environmental and social risk accreditation 
level of the accredited entity (AE). Its site-specific interventions are small scale, and while 
potential negative impacts may vary, they are expected to be minor, dispersed over an area and 
reversible. Additionally, the development and execution of integrated watershed management 
plans, which can potentially negatively affect specific groups or sectors, will be conducted 
through a consultative and participatory approach. This ensures that any potential adverse 
impacts are carefully considered and addressed during the planning phase. Consequently, the 
environmental and social risks associated with this project are assessed as low to moderate. 
Since the final details of proposed activities (e.g. plans, protocols, specific locations of 
interventions) will still be determined through further engagement with stakeholders during 
the implementation of the project, an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) 
has been prepared.  
37. 
Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards. The 
following discussion describes the environmental and social risks and/or impacts of the project 
and the proposed mitigation measures. 
38. 
ESS1 (Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and 
Impacts). The ESMF provides an assessment of the potential social and environmental impacts 
and risks of the project activities. It provides background information about the physical, 
biological and socioeconomic profile of the project area, including information about water 
resources and waste management. The ESMF has assessed that the project will have mainly 
positive impacts on the biophysical and human environment. The negative impacts have been 
identified and their corresponding mitigation measures provided. 
39. 
The ESMF enumerates the country laws and regulations and international conventions 
that are of relevance to certain aspects of the project. It also discusses the biophysical and 
socioeconomic profile of the target areas, as well as the procedures and requirements for 
assessing and managing the environmental and social impacts and risks of individual 
subprojects/activities under the project. An environmental and social management system will 
also be established during the operation of the water fund. 
40. 
ESS2 (Labour and Working Conditions). The project will involve small-scale work 
activities which would create temporary employment. The ESMF has identified the issues of 
child labour and migrant workers (e.g. trafficking in persons) as potential sources of risks which 
will be managed by adhering to the procurement principles and national law requirements of 
the AE. Measures include maintaining regular inspection of occurrence and cases of trafficking 
in persons, including the recording and reporting procedures for documenting and reporting

=== PAGE 161 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 161 
 
 
such cases. Constant monitoring of the labour status and compliance of contractors with 
national labour law regulations will also be implemented. 
41. 
ESS3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention). The project is expected to 
significantly contribute to resource efficiency in terms of fuel and water use as addressing these 
issues is a major aim of the project. In terms of pollution, the project is not expected to generate 
significant amounts of pollutants apart from minor noise, dust, air emissions from vehicles, 
sedimentation and small amounts of solid and liquid wastes during construction. These can be 
addressed through standard mitigation measures that can be implemented by the contractors. 
Pollution from pesticides and other agrochemicals are also expected to be reduced in the long 
run as a result of the adoption of integrated pest management principles and sustainable 
practices in the agricultural production to be supported under the project.  
42. 
ESS4 (Community Health, Safety and Security). The project poses construction-
related safety hazards to nearby residents, visitors and passers-by. However, these are expected 
to pose only minor risks to community health and safety; the construction of rockfill dams and 
installation of solar panels are the only major works under this project and are not large-scale 
operations and will not be concentrated in one area. The risk of accidents at construction sites 
and routes will be addressed through ensuring that safety measures rules and regulations are 
applied on site and in transport routes.  
43. 
Changes in the water regime of the area could also alter the ecological balance and result 
in negative impacts such as resurgence of vector-borne diseases. The increased availability of 
water due to the construction of rockfill dams could create an environment where vector-borne 
diseases could proliferate. This aspect will be further explored in the conduct of the 
environmental and social impact assessment and, if necessary, integrated vector management 
approaches will be implemented. Potential stakeholder conflicts will be addressed within the 
project’s consultative mechanisms such as those of the watershed management committees and 
participatory monitoring processes. 
44. 
ESS5 (Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement). The project is not expected 
to involve displacement or relocation of people and the infrastructure development activities 
are likely to be undertaken in government-owned facilities (e.g. King Abdullah Canal for the 1 
MW floating solar panels) or in public lands (e.g. wadi areas for rockfill dams). However, there is 
a possibility of local communities experiencing temporary restriction of use or access to the 
forests and rangelands being subjected to rehabilitation. The temporary restrictions are 
expected to be relaxed as restoration and rehabilitation activities progress. The project will aim 
for a maximum level of local user engagement and participation the development of land-use 
restrictions/plans/schemes based on mutually acceptable agreements. Moreover, the project 
will provide alternative or additional livelihood options. The project’s grievance redress 
mechanism (GRM) will also ensure that local users can express their concerns with regard to 
any restriction of access and/or failure to respect their user rights. 
45. 
ESS6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural 
Resources). In the long run, the project is deemed to have mostly positive impacts on the 
biological environment, particularly in terms of conservation of fragile habitats, enhancing 
biodiversity and conservation of genetic resources. These positive impacts are expected as a 
result of the adoption of ecosystem-based approaches in restoration efforts; effective 
management of existing protected areas and hotspots; promotion of nature-based alternative 
livelihoods; adoption of sustainable agricultural practices; and effective enforcement of 
appropriate policies and regulations, among others. Increased activities in the project area, 
particularly around the site of the facilities during construction and later during operation will 
have some negative impacts on natural habitats, including vegetation loss; alteration of surface 
morphology and natural water run-off schemes; and wildlife disturbance. The construction 
activities and subsequent operation and maintenance of facilities may also result in vegetation 
loss, littering and disturbance to wildlife around the facilities. It is therefore recommended that

=== PAGE 162 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 162 
 
 
the formulation of the integrated landscape and watershed management plans be based on a 
thorough ecological study and robust ecological monitoring for each watershed and that an 
adaptive management approach can also be employed, when needed. 
46. 
GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS7 (Indigenous Peoples). The presence of 
Indigenous Peoples in the area of the project is probable, and it has been confirmed in the 
vicinity areas, which and an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework has been provided. 
Potential impacts on Indigenous Peoples include temporary restrictions on access to land and 
natural resources and changes in the current use patterns of their territories. Restrictions on 
land and resource access will only proceed once the free, prior and informed consent is secured, 
and the modalities of implementation are consulted and agreed upon with Indigenous Peoples. 
Documentation and diffusion of Indigenous knowledge in a platform is subject to securing the 
free, prior and informed consent, and the attribution of intellectual property rights to the 
knowledge holders. It is recommended that the AE engage with the GCF Indigenous Peoples 
Advisory Group on the relevant Indigenous Peoples' issues that may be encountered. 
47. 
ESS8 (Cultural Heritage). Jordan is renowned for its abundant historical and cultural 
heritage sites. Hence there is a risk that project activities could inadvertently damage artifacts 
or historical sites. The safeguards screening conducted by the AE has confirmed this risk. To 
address this, the ESMF required the conduct of comprehensive cultural assessments for each 
project development site where information about the presence of cultural heritage resource is 
unavailable. It also required that construction contract documents incorporate provisions 
allowing for the suspension of construction and excavation activities in the event of any 
discoveries of antiquities or archaeological items. Chance finds will be promptly reported to the 
Director of the Department of Antiquities or the nearest general security centre. 
48. 
Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH). The ESMF identified the 
contextual risks related to gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH in Jordan, underpinned by 
the Syrian refugee crisis and increasing vulnerability of the displaced populations. The AE also 
identified that insufficient legal frameworks and enforcement contribute to the persistence of 
GBV and SEAH in the target country. Against such contextual factors, the project may exacerbate 
SEAH risks owing to various factors, such as the project’s close interaction with highly 
vulnerable groups, construction activities to be undertaken and the use of security forces. The 
project will integrate SEAH consideration across all project activities to identify and mitigate the 
project-driven SEAH risks, including to assessed and addressed specific SEAH risks at the 
subproject level in line with the SEAH due diligence process outlined in the ESMF and as part of 
the environmental and social management system for the water fund. The AE will develop a 
methodology for assessing potential GBV and SEAH risks during the project implementation and 
during the project operation. The ESMF requires a zero-tolerance policy for all employees or 
implementing partners. The AE will provide training and capacity-building support at the 
community level and operate a parallel process in the GRM for SEAH-specific complaints in a 
survivor-centred manner. The AE has also identified and will leverage SEAH service provisions 
based on a network of GBV and SEAH survivor support organizations that are active in the 
Jordan Valley. 
49. 
Implementation arrangements. The project will be overseen by a project steering 
committee and implemented by a national-level project management unit (PMU). Hosted by 
MoEnv, the PMU will consist of specialists including an Environmental and Social Safeguards 
Officer and a Gender Officer. The Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer will be engaged 
as the lead technical advisor on environmental and social management matters. The role 
includes overall implementation of the ESMF and oversight of safeguards across the project 
components. This includes the implementation and oversight of specific mitigation actions as 
described in the ESMF; coordination and implementation of additional safeguards assessments 
and safeguards instruments; monitoring and reporting of the environmental and social 
performance of the project; assessment and capacity-building towards executing entities and/or

=== PAGE 163 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 163 
 
 
implementing partners; and coordination with other members of the PMU, such as the 
Monitoring and Evaluation Officer and the Gender Officer where required. 
50. 
Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure. The project was crafted 
through an extensive consultation process involving various stakeholders encompassing 
workshops, technical meetings, interviews and field visits and is detailed in annex 7 to the 
funding proposal (Stakeholder Consultations and Engagement Plan). The interactions involved a 
wide spectrum of participants, including NGOs, government bodies, local communities and 
stakeholders at both the national and the local level, including farmers from the three 
designated project areas. 
51. 
The ESMF and the Stakeholder Consultations and Engagement Plan also provided a 
structured plan for engaging stakeholders throughout the project's execution. Prior to initiating 
any on-the-ground activities, a comprehensive Stakeholder Engagement Plan will be prepared 
by the PMU. A framework outlining key components and requirements for this implementation 
phase Stakeholder Engagement Plan has been provided, including an outline of activity-level 
stakeholder engagements which aligns with the participatory planning requirements that have 
been integrated into the design of project activities such as the development and 
implementation of the ILWMP.  
52. 
Grievance redress mechanism. A project-level GRM, which will be made accessible to 
all stakeholders, has been prepared and presented as part of the ESMF. The mechanism will 
utilize various communication channels (email, telephone, contact person, personal address) 
and would consists of four levels. Concerns can be lodged at the lowest appropriate levels, 
starting at the PMU, followed by the Representative of the International Union for Conservation 
of Nature (IUCN), the IUCN regional office level and the IUCN Head of Oversight. The Safeguard 
Specialist at the PMU will act as the GRM focal person. The Project Manager of the PMU will be 
responsible for documenting and reporting on any grievances received and how they were 
addressed. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF indigenous peoples focal 
point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made. 
4.2 
Gender policy  
53. 
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan that complies with the GCF 
gender policy requirements. The project acknowledges the critical role that women play as 
agents of change in climate resilience, while also noting that women are disproportionately 
affected by climate change with unequal access to finance, resources and assets, barriers to 
decision-making and limited mobility, to name but a few disadvantages. The gender action plan 
provides interventions that address the challenges faced by women in ILWRM sectors and 
covers baseline indicators, targets, timelines, gender and social expertise and budgets. The 
interventions included in the gender action plan reflect the findings of desk reviews, 
stakeholder consultations and recommended actions by the Gender Unit of the Ministry of 
Water and Irrigation.   
54. 
The project will focus on addressing the practical needs of women by including 
interventions through the water fund for the Jordan Valley, which focuses on improving local 
women’s access and benefits from credit and grant financing to support ILWRM initiatives in 
their own communities. Further, it will work on enhancing women’s agency in dealing with 
climate change risks by including activities targeted exclusively at women. The Water Wise 
Women initiative is central to the gender action plan and includes measures to support 
women’s empowerment and decision-making capabilities, including strengthening rural 
women’s voices and leadership capacities to advocate for gender-sensitive strategies and 
policies related to adaptation to climate change.

=== PAGE 164 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 164 
 
 
55. 
The AE will hire a full-time social inclusion and gender expert to mainstream gender 
throughout the project along with gender consultants to support the implementation of specific 
gender action plan activities. The gender action plan is inclusive of women, female-headed 
households, youth and the elderly. It includes measures to mitigate GBV and provides women 
with access to grievance mechanisms.   
56. 
The main risks associated with the success of the gender action plan are the entrenched 
sociocultural norms that have led to Jordan being consistently ranked as one of the countries 
with the lowest gender equity; and lack of land and resource tenure for women that threatens 
the sustainability of the impact of the project’s investments on women’s empowerment and 
well-being. 
4.3 
Risks  
4.3.1. 
Overall programme assessment (medium risk)  
57. 
The Secretariat assesses overall programme risk as medium. 
4.3.2. 
Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme 
(low risk) 
58. 
Project execution will be by the national executing entity and national designated 
authority, MoEnv, and the lead executing entity IUCN, which is also an AE to GCF. Both executing 
entities have experience of operating in the country.    
4.3.3. 
Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk) 
59. 
Political risk: political unrest due to ongoing conflict in the region may spill over into 
Jordan with subsequent safety risks and may lead to political instability and loss of project 
assets. As mitigants, the AE adequately identified this risk and will inform GCF of any adverse 
change in the risk assessment during implementation on a regular basis. Furthermore, the AE 
has privileges and immunities in the Host Country. 
60. 
Operationalization of the water fund: a weak appetite to co-invest in the water fund may 
result in insufficient liquidity of the revolving resources that are required and intended, on a 
revolving resources basis, for delivering replicability and scalability to target communities and 
water conservation efforts in other parts of the country. However, the Secretariat takes comfort 
from the inclusion in the term sheet of the delivery of a detailed structure and strategy for the 
water fund subject to a condition prior to the third disbursement to be reviewed and approved 
by the Secretariat.   
4.3.4. 
Compliance risk (medium risk)   
61. 
The project addresses water programs in Jordan.  Generally, the proposed activities 
themselves do not pose an unusually high level of risks for money laundering.  The Government 
of Jordan has made great strides in its efforts to combat money laundering and terrorist 
financing, resulting in the removal of Jordan from the FATF “great list” in recent months.  
Nevertheless, there are as noted above, a number of conflicts and instances of political unrest in 
the region that would merit continued scrutiny by the AE in monitoring for, and addressing, 
potential instances and other vulnerabilities.  To that extent, the AE has indicated its ability to 
undertake those precautions. 
62. 
Based on a review of the proposed activities of the project, and taking into account, the 
potential risks that regional conflicts may have with regard to money laundering, terrorist 
financing, and related prohibited practices, and taking into account the proposed measures of

=== PAGE 165 ===

Page 165 
 
 
the AE to monitor and take action to prevent such instances, the Compliance risk for this project 
is rated as “MEDIUM”. 
4.3.5.

63. 
In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in 
terms of results area and single proposal is not material.

4.4 
Fiduciary 
65. 
The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) will serve as the AE overseeing 
the project, with MoEnv and IUCN acting as executing entities. MoEnv will manage component 1, 
while IUCN will handle components 2 to 4. Responsibilities for the AE include overseeing 
implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and project closure in 
coordination with a national-level project steering committee and PMU. UNEP will ensure 
compliance with the Accreditation Master Agreement and the funded activity agreement, 
monitoring financial management through established procedures, including annual audits and 
internal controls. 
66. 
The project will adhere to United Nations regulations, with UNEP managing fiduciary 
aspects. Financial management and procurement will follow the UNEP Programme Manual, 
guided by International Public Sector Accounting Standards and United Nations financial rules. 
Each executing entity will sign subsidiary agreements, clarifying roles and ensuring compliance 
with United Nations anti-fraud frameworks and GCF policies. The legal capacities of MoEnv and 
IUCN allow them to independently contract and manage financial resources for their respective 
components.  
67. 
A Financial Officer within the PMU will oversee financial operations, ensuring 
compliance with United Nations financial regulations. This includes quarterly fund requests and 
reporting, supervised by a UNEP Task Manager. Project funding will be disbursed in tranches to 
executing entities upon meeting specified conditions in the project cooperation agreement and 
the internal cooperation agreement. 
68. 
MoEnv, as a government authority, follows national financial rules, and its capacity for 
procurement will be assessed by UNEP to ensure alignment with United Nations standards. This 
assessment will influence the procurement monitoring plan, a critical part of the project 
cooperation agreement shared with GCF as mandated. The project's equipment investments will 
also adhere to United Nations procurement procedures, with MoEnv conducting an annual 
compliance audit of all project expenditures.

Results monitoring and reporting

69. 
The project aims to deliver benefits under Adaptation Results Areas 1, 2 and 4. It seeks 
to reach 246,942 direct beneficiaries and 756,000 indirect beneficiaries (accounting for 2.2 and 
7.2 per cent respectively of the national population). The project has an implementation period 
of 8 years and a lifespan of 25 years. The project was reviewed over five rounds, where detailed 
comments were provided to the AE for improving results management under the intervention.  
70. 
The project theory of change adequately captures the results that are expected to be 
generated at different levels, including outcomes, co-benefits, outputs and activities. These are 
linked to underlying barriers and risks, and the theory of change also identifies assumptions 
that are being made for the project results to materialize.  
71. 
The logical framework provides a clear account of the results, associated indicators and 
targets under the project. This includes relevant GCF results areas and associated core and 
supplementary indicators. Importantly, project-level outcome and co-benefit indicators are also 
identified along with clear mid-term and final targets. 
72. 
The supporting annexes related to monitoring and evaluation were found to be clear 
and detailed. Relatively minor observations relating to annex 5 (Implementation Timeline) have 
been noted and shared with the AE during the latest round of review.  
4.6 
Legal assessment  
73. 
The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 
December 2016, and became effective on 20 February 2017, which was amended and restated 
pursuant to a first amendment and restatement agreement dated 13 July 2023, and which 
became effective on 2 August 2023 (the “AMA”). 
74. 
The Accredited Entity has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all 
internal approvals, and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project. 
75. 
The proposed project will be implemented in the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan, a 
country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst 
other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks 
need to be further assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and 
immunities and a background note to the Government of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan 
most recently on 19 November 2022. 
76. 
The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity 
Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress 
activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with 
relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the 
GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and 
expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities. 
77. 
To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by 
the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and 
(b) 
Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP232 
Proposal name: 
Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILMI) 
Accredited entity: 
United Nations Environment Programme  
Country: 
Jordan  
Project/programme size: 
Medium 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Overview 
1. 
This funding proposal (FP)1 for a medium-sized adaptation project in environmental 
and social safeguards category B is submitted by the Government of Jordan through the United 
Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) functioning as the proposed project’s accredited 
entity (AE). The executing entities (EEs) are the Government of Jordan, represented by the 
Ministry of Environment (MOENV), and the International Union for Conservation of Nature 
(IUCN) through its Regional Office for West Asia (IUCN-ROWA) based in Amman, with a 
consortium of local non-governmental organizations (NGOs) providing co-finance and technical 
support. 
2. 
The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 60.5 million, of which USD 44.9 million is 
proposed as GCF finance and USD 15.6 million as co-finance from the Government of Jordan 
(GOJ) and the consortium of NGOs – see section 1.6 below for more detail. The project 
implementation period is eight years, while benefits are calculated over a lifespan of 25 years.  
3. 
The proposed project’s objective is to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on 
water resources in Jordan by improving aquifer recharge using a landscape-level approach. The 
project focuses on three target watersheds of the northern Jordan Valley – the Yarmouk, 
Amman Zarqa and Jordan Rift Valley Basins – where agriculture and human well-being are 
threatened by water scarcity worsened through climate change. The proposed integrated land 
and water resources management (ILWRM) approach includes: i) improved ecosystem 
management through land restoration, improved agricultural practices and managed aquifer 
recharge; ii) rainwater harvesting; iii) evaporation reduction from the King Talal Dam; and iv) 
strengthened governance, with long-term finance through a Water Fund for upscaling ILWRM 
across the Jordan Valley. 
4. 
The project involves implementation of four interlinked technical components (as well 
as monitoring and evaluation, and project management):

1.2 
Impact potential  
 
                                                  Scale: High 
5. 
The project targets three of the GCF results areas for adaptation, aiming to enhance the 
resilience of i) the most vulnerable people and communities; ii) health and well-being, and food 
and water security; and iii) ecosystems and ecosystem services.  
6. 
Adaptation impact. The funding proposal highlights the hot and arid baseline climate 
of Jordan, in which over 90 per cent of its limited annual rainfall is lost through evaporation, and 
shows how water stress2 is worsening with climate change. Severe droughts have become more 
common in the past 50 years, with the worst recorded drought in 900 years occurring between 
1998 and 2012, when rainfall decreased by up to 70 per cent compared with long-term 
averages, resulting in severe agricultural, socioeconomic and environmental impacts. Climate 
projections under Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5 and RCP 8.5 scenarios for 
the period 2070–2100 anticipate an increase in average annual temperature by 2–4°C, and a 
reduction in annual average rainfall, with extended dry periods, a shorter rainfall season, and 
increased frequency of high-intensity rainfall events that can also cause flash flooding. Staple 
barley production is expected to decline by up to 50 per cent under future climate conditions, 
with pressure also on other crops and animal fodder, severely affecting local food supplies. 
7. 
Natural ecosystems and agroecosystems in the country are already being degraded by 
non-climatic drivers such as overgrazing, unregulated fuelwood harvesting, and cultivation on 
steep slopes, accelerating water run-off and preventing infiltration. With climate change, it is 
anticipated that shifts in rainfall will reduce river baseflow and aquifer recharge, and higher 
temperatures will increase evaporation rates, reducing available water supplies. At the same 
time, rising temperatures will increase demand for fresh water for irrigation and domestic 
consumption, encouraging further extraction of groundwater. These combined effects are 
expected to worsen the existing water deficit of Jordan (i.e. the gap between supply (from all 
water resources, including non-renewable) and demand in the country), which was assessed at 
265 million m3 per year in 2022, and is expected to climb steeply to 835 million m3 per year by 
2050.3  
8. 
Project work on the ground in the Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Kafrain areas of the 
Jordan Valley in components 2 and 3 (under IUCN), will engage communities in implementing 
participatory climate-resilient integrated watershed management plans, developed in 
 
2 2023 data from the Aqueduct Water Risk Atlas of the World Resources Institute shows that Jordan is one of 25 
countries facing extremely high water stress each year. 
3 Figures from Jordan's fourth national communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate 
Change (2022). (Note: the FP quotes the third national communication submitted in 2014.) The water deficit when 
calculated as the gap between supply from renewable water resources and demand is significantly higher at 422 
million m3 per year.

=== PAGE 169 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 169 
 
 
component 1 (under the MOENV). Implementation will involve restoring 8,000 ha of forest, 
rangeland, riparian and wadi ecosystems across the three sites. Protocols will be developed for 
both passive restoration, via the traditional Hima system,4 and active restoration through 
revegetating degraded areas and afforestation with saplings raised in community nurseries. 
Active restoration will be complemented by small-scale, in-field rainwater harvesting 
interventions (e.g. bunds, planting pits, Negarim and Vallerani basins, and run-off strips) and 
irrigation (e.g. porous hose and deep pipe), improving soil moisture and plant survival in the 
sites. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) welcomes section 8 of the feasibility 
study on “Best practices and lessons learnt”, which illustrates and details best practice 
restoration techniques relevant to Jordan, and encourages the AE to consider producing this 
excellent resource in Arabic as a technical manual. 
9. 
Component 2 also includes support to women and men on new climate-resilient natural 
resource-based livelihoods – including vegetable gardening (hydroponics and permaculture) 
and beekeeping – and introduction of water-efficient practices, including selection of climate-
resilient crop varieties and permaculture. Learning exchanges will be promoted between 
communities; training will be provided on equipment maintenance; and private-sector 
champions, including women, will be supported to mentor farmers to develop microenterprises 
in post-harvest processing, and to access markets and finance. In component 3, water resources 
will be enhanced through installing 3,000 rooftop rainwater harvesting tanks on community 
buildings and homes, with community monitoring and management plans through multi-
stakeholder Water Management Committees (established under component 1), including 
women. Floating solar panels will be installed on the King Abdullah Canal, which will reduce 
water loss through evaporation, while supplying 1 MW of renewable energy into the local grid 
for sustainable water pumping.5  
10. 
In addition to restoring ecosystem productivity and enhancing soil moisture, ecosystem-
based adaptation (EbA) techniques will also be used to replenish shallow aquifers to help adapt 
to water scarcity. Permeable rockfill dams will be established in wadis6 at 12 sites, allowing for 
managed aquifer recharge, with small check dams established in upstream gullies to trap 
sediment and further enhance infiltration. As groundwater is replenished, 10 freshwater 
springs will be rehabilitated, with refurbishment of irrigation canals. The project’s EbA 
approach represents one element of a multi-pronged strategy by government and partners that 
includes increased agricultural water efficiency,7 desalination, water treatment for reuse, as 
well as more controversial mega-projects that pump up irreplaceable fossil water from deep 
aquifers.8 It is hoped that the project’s combined interventions in aquifer recharge, ecosystem 
restoration, rainwater harvesting and evaporation prevention will lead to an increase in the 
country’s annual water supply of approximately 8.9 million m3 by project end, with a potential 
cumulative increase of approximately 154.5 million m3 by the end of the project’s 25-year 
lifespan.  
11. 
The project is expected to directly benefit9 a total of 246,942 people (45 per cent of 
them female) in the Jordan Valley by reducing their climate vulnerability. The FP shows the 
spread of beneficiaries across i) the Yarmouk site – Khaled Bin Al Waleed and Al Seru 
 
4 Originating from an ancient system of sacred sites, a “hima” is a reserved pasture, where a community’s trees and 
grazing lands are protected from indiscriminate harvesting on a seasonal basis, allowing for natural regeneration of 
productivity. 
5 The AE has confirmed that no new boreholes will be drilled, and no new solar photovoltaic pumps will be installed, 
with GCF funding (in written responses to iTAP questions, received from UNEP on 22 May 2024). 
6 A “wadi” refers to a river channel or valley that is dry except in the rainy season. 
7 Tackled in the GCF project, Building resilience to cope with climate change in Jordan through improving water use 
efficiency in the agriculture sector project (FP155). 
8 https://doi.org/10.3390/w15213729 
9 Direct beneficiaries are all those who will benefit directly from participating in a project activity or the result of an 
activity.

=== PAGE 170 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 170 
 
 
municipalities; ii) the King Talal Dam site – New Ardah, Deir Alla and M’addi municipalities; and 
iii) the Kafrain site – Shouna Alwasta and Mahes municipalities. Annex 33 to the FP shows the 
detailed calculations for anticipated beneficiary numbers. For example, under output 3.2, 1,200 
smallholder farming households (approximately 6,000 people, i.e. an average of 5 people per 
household) across the three sites will benefit from on-farm interventions, while 2,800 
households across the sites (approximately 14,000 people) will benefit directly from rainwater 
harvesting infrastructure installed on rooftops. Also included are direct benefits from improved 
protection, supply and use of water supplies for domestic and/or agricultural usage, and 
benefits from enhancements in the quality and quantity of ground and surface water through 
managed aquifer recharge and spring rehabilitation. 
12. 
As ILWRM interventions are scaled up via the Water Fund, to be created during 
implementation, it is anticipated that an additional 4,000 ha of land could be placed under 
improved management for water-resource benefits, with at least one additional (adjacent) 
municipality per watershed site benefiting directly. Water storage potential would thus be 
increased across these 4,000 ha in addition to the 8,000 ha to be restored directly by the project. 
Water storage potential10 is derived from modelling the effects of slowing overland water flows 
on water infiltration into the groundwater. As the flow slows, so the water spreads out over a 
larger area and the time available for the water to seep into the ground increases (see section 
11.6 of the feasibility study). If the Water Fund is successful in leveraging resources, it could 
scale up the set of restoration and land management interventions in component 2 to target the 
entire 12,000 ha, and the additional 74,763 beneficiaries in this area are thus included in the 
number of direct beneficiaries of the project.  
13. 
A total of 756,000 people (48 per cent of them female) across the broader Jordan Valley 
are expected to benefit indirectly from: i) enhanced water resources in the northern Jordan 
Valley; ii) strengthened local governance to implement climate-resilient ILWRM; iii) reduced 
water loss through evaporation from the King Abdullah Canal; and iv) improved awareness of 
the impacts of climate change and the adaptation options to address them. Although not 
included in the indirect beneficiary calculations, the project proponents note that interventions 
at the regulatory and institutional levels, and the example set by the establishment of a Water 
Fund, could indirectly benefit the Jordanian population as a whole, amounting to an estimated 
10 million people.  
14. 
Monitoring of the project results in terms of success in groundwater replenishment is 
crucial, both for reporting to GCF, and for generating evidence for the effectiveness and cost-
effectiveness of EbA measures. This evidence will be crucial in making the case for corporate 
and utility water users downstream to contribute to the Water Fund for ongoing catchment 
management to secure quality and quantity of water supply. The iTAP therefore welcomes the 
inclusion of explicit monitoring of groundwater levels by water management committees 
(WMCs), who will take regular piezometer readings at 12 sites downstream of the rockfill dams. 
The iTAP notes that readings should be undertaken at the start and end of the dry season each 
year, and combined with data on the amount of water extracted over the last period (which 
must be continuously monitored by WMCs or other water-user associations), in order to 
determine the level of the groundwater and the extent to which upstream landscape based 
interventions have been able to replenish it. 
15. 
The iTAP assesses the impact potential of the project as high. 
1.3 
Paradigm shift potential 
                                               Scale: Medium  
16. 
The proposed project intends to catalyse an institutional paradigm shift within the 
water sector in Jordan from a compartmentalized, sector-specific approach to land and water 
 
10 Referred to as “attenuation potential” in the funding proposal.

=== PAGE 171 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 171 
 
 
resources management, to one that is integrated, participatory and adaptation focused, 
contributing to climate-resilient sustainable development in the country.  
17. 
Enabling environment. The project includes legal work to support the Ministry of 
Water and Irrigation (MWI) to expand the remit of the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA) to include 
ILWRM matters explicitly, and to establish multisectoral WMCs in the three target watersheds. 
The project will support the establishment and capacity development of the new WMC 
structures, aiming to achieve dialogue and cooperation between upstream and downstream 
stakeholders and undertake collective management actions. In response to a question from 
iTAP about the government’s openness to these new structures and willingness to provide 
budget support beyond the project, the AE indicated in general terms that “Government buy-in 
has been shown in multiple consultations, as well as the letters of approval received for the 
project from MWI and other ministries, through the climate change national committee”.11  
18. 
At national scale, the project design includes activities to strengthen the mandate and 
capacity of major government agencies, including the ministries of environment, agriculture, 
and water and irrigation. An analysis of relevant national policy and legislation will be 
undertaken, to develop policy briefs, and propose amendments to relevant legislation, though 
there is no guarantee that these will be made. Other interventions to remove barriers to 
integrated climate-risk informed resource management will include the development of land-
use guidelines, standards and protocols for climate-resilient watershed management; training of 
officials to use the results of climate analyses in planning and decision-making, including 
disaster risk management; and generating evidence of the economic benefits of ILWRM to 
project beneficiaries for use by policymakers and planners. 
19. 
Knowledge-sharing and learning. The proposed project includes specific 
interventions to improve the current knowledge management system for climate-change 
adaptation in Jordan, extending the project’s influence. This will include the establishment of a 
national knowledge repository of lessons learned, likely based in one of the country’s 
universities, and gender-responsive best practices for climate-change adaptation, focusing on 
the ILWRM approach; the training of WMCs and other user groups in the use of climate data and 
knowledge tools; and the implementation of an outreach programme, with local knowledge 
hubs supported by trained local officials and community members. Additionally, relevant 
educational practitioners (50 per cent of whom will be women), will be trained on integrating 
climate-change adaptation into the syllabuses and curricula of Jordanian universities.  
20. 
Scaling up of results. At a technical level, the project interventions are shown to have 
strong potential for upscaling. The FP outlines four possible scenarios for upscaling the key 
project interventions (i.e. ecosystem restoration, managed aquifer recharge (rockfill dams), 
rooftop rainwater harvesting, and evaporation reduction (floating solar panels)). Table 11 in the 
FP shows the potential impact on water supply under each of the scenarios – conservative, 
mildly conservative, optimistic and best case scenarios. The AE calculates that, if the ecosystem 
restoration interventions alone were to be upscaled across the entire northern Jordan Valley 
(1,147,600 ha), the potential cumulative increase in water resources over 25 years could range 
from 34 million m3 under a conservative scenario, to 344 million m3 under the best-case 
scenario. The combined effect of all four interventions at maximum potential over 25 years 
could make a cumulative total available of between 297 and 3,059 million m3 (or an average of 
11.88 to 122.36 million m3 per year12). 
21. 
Sustainability of results. The project’s main strategy for resource mobilization is to 
sustain the gains of the interventions in the three target sites, replicate these in other sites in the 
target basins, and scale them up across the northern Jordan Valley (and potentially beyond), is 
 
11 Written responses to iTAP questions, received from UNEP on 22 May 2024. 
12 By the calculations of the iTAP, this maximum potential contribution would represent a significant 29 per cent of 
Jordan’s current annual water deficit (when defined as the gap between supply and demand based on renewable 
water resources – see footnote 3).

the establishment and capitalization of a Water Fund (activity 2.1.4). The fund is planned as a 
mechanism for leveraging financial resources for watershed management in the long term, from 
water-dependent stakeholders in the private sector (industry and commercial agriculture) and 
public sector (municipalities/utilities). Such a fund could ensure the ongoing availability of 
funding for the maintenance and restoration of ecosystems in the three target watersheds and 
beyond. The project includes activities to engage multiple stakeholders in the design of the fund, 
and to establish the necessary legal mechanisms and operational capacity. Anchored in the JVA, 
the fund will be governed by a multi-stakeholder public–private steering committee that 
includes representatives of WMCs from the target watersheds, NGO partners, the three key 
ministries, water utilities, and private-sector partners.

=== PAGE 173 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 173 
 
 
Model for a water fund (fig. 2 from the FP) 
 
22. 
Activity 2.1.4 also makes USD 7.5 million available for community-level ILWRM 
subprojects through a GCF project on-granting facility, which “will function alongside the Water 
Fund’s grant mechanism”. The iTAP asked the AE if the option of using the GCF on-granting 
funds to kick-start the actual Water Fund had been considered, thereby leveraging immediate 
private-sector contributions by using the momentum created by GCF investment. The AE 
explained that establishing the Water Fund will be a lengthy and complex process, requiring 
that legal mechanisms be put in place in the project’s third year, with the fund unlikely to 
become operational before the fourth year. The project’s on-granting facility would need to start 
earlier than this, but can nevertheless help to “establish and embed the on-granting practice 
that will be upscaled by the Water Fund”.13  
23. 
Key potential investors in the Water Fund are listed in the FP – large agri-companies, the 
Alfaqih Group and Haddadin Farms; large industry, including the Manaseer Group, Arab Potash 
Company and Jordan Phosphate Mines Company; and water utilities such as the Yarmouk Water 
Company. As confirmed by the AE,14 no discussions have yet been held with any of these 
private-sector players, who will be engaged only during implementation. Given the centrality of 
the proposed Water Fund to the project’s exit strategy, and the historical significance of 
powerful vested interests in skewed water delivery in Jordan, the failure to engage these key 
players during the design phase represents a serious risk to the project’s sustainability. 
24. 
On one small aspect of sustainability, it is noted that the project will contribute the full 
input costs, worth USD 4.4 million in GCF funds and co-finance, for the community-run tree 
nurseries that will produce plant material for the restoration interventions (see annex 4 to the 
FP, budget note B13–B17). The iTAP asked how the shift would be made to a commercial set-up 
post-project, in which nurseries would cover their own costs and find their own paying 
customers, and avoid the frequent fate of such enterprises folding after project completion. In 
response, the AE stated that the community-based nurseries will “be run as income generating 
activities and sell the produced seedlings to the market – to farmers, restoration projects, etc., 
ensuring sustainability”.15 It is unclear, however, what model is envisaged to achieve a 
successful transition from fully subsidized project nursery to self-sustaining business. 
25. 
Based on all the above, the paradigm shift potential of the project is assessed as medium.

1.4 
Sustainable development potential  
                               Scale: High 
26. 
The proposed project is aligned with the United Nations 2030 Agenda for Sustainable 
Development, including contributions towards the efforts of Jordan to meet the Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs), namely: SDG 2 (Zero hunger); SDG 3 (Good health and well-being); 
SDG 5 (Gender equality); SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation); SDG 8 (Decent work and economic 
growth); SDG 10 (Reducing inequalities); SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production); 
SDG 15 (Life on land); and SDG 16 (Peace, justice and strong institutions). Specific co-benefits 
anticipated from project interventions are discussed below. 
27. 
Socioeconomic benefits: Improved access to clean water will improve farming 
households’ health and sanitation, and reduce healthcare costs accordingly. Social cohesion 
should also improve due to decrease in conflict over scarce water resources. Project 
interventions through activity 2.1.3, which will introduce new climate-resilient additional 
livelihoods and agriculture practices that reduce dependency on water-intensive crops, as well 
as support for microenterprises for post-harvest processing, should lead to diversified and 
improved incomes and better nutrition. The re-establishment of drought-tolerant forage shrubs 
in degraded rangelands through the Hima passive restoration approach should improve 
livestock condition and productivity. This was demonstrated in a project in the Zarqa River 
basin, where after two years of practising the Hima system, the dry yield of cereals for fodder in 
a rain-fed system increased from an average of 40 kg/ha/yr to 113 kg/ha/yr.16 The floating 
solar power installation will create employment and income-generating opportunities in solar-
panel installation and maintenance. 
28. 
Environmental benefits: The project’s interventions in the landscape will have 
numerous environmental co-benefits, beyond the core adaptation results in relation to reduced 
vulnerability to climate change related water scarcity. The restoration and recharge measures 
should also lead to decreased pollution of water resources, decreased soil erosion and 
decreased sedimentation of dams. Promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices will reduce 
the need for fertilizers that contribute to pollution levels. By improving infiltration through 
restoration, freshwater and riparian habitats will be less affected by pollutants such as 
fertilizers which cause eutrophication.17 Soil quality will improve, and health risks posed to 
humans by nitrates will be reduced.  
29. 
Some flood reduction co-benefits are expected from the project as well. Improving 
vegetative cover will allow greater rainfall interception, and reduce the volume of run-off and 
speed of overland water flow. This will decrease the potential for flash flooding of downstream 
areas and the associated loss of life and destruction of infrastructure and crops. In response to 
a question from the iTAP about why the project is primarily focused on drought and not 
flood, the AE confirmed that the topography is unsuitable for large-scale landscape engineering 
to capture and divert floodwaters to prevent downstream flooding and maximize water for 
recharge, and that the limited anticipated flood reduction from revegetation and some capturing 
of floodwaters in the rockfill dams was more accurately reflected as a project co-benefit.18 
30. 
Mitigation co-benefit. The floating solar panel energy system to be installed over the 
King Abdullah Canal, with the adaptation benefit of saving up to 0.8 million m3 of water annually 
through reduced evaporation, will also produce 1 MW of clean energy, contributing to the 
country’s aim of increasing its production of renewable energy, and reducing the reliance of the 
MWI on fossil-fuel generated energy to supply pumping stations in the area. 
 
16 http://nwrm.eu/sites/default/files/nwrm_ressources/f3_-_afforestation_of_reservoir_catchments_0.pdf  
17 Eutrophication is over-enrichment of water with nutrients, causing excessive growth of algae that may deplete the 
water of oxygen.
