31. 
Gender and social inclusion: The proposed project helps to take forward the national 
strategy for women in Jordan (2020–2025), with the following goals: i) women and girls are 
able to exercise their economic, political and human rights and freely lead and participate in 
society free of gender-based discrimination; ii) women and girls enjoy a life free of all forms of 
gender-based violence; iii) positive gender norms, roles and attitudes support gender equality 
and women empowerment; and iv) institutions are executing and sustaining policies, structures 
and services that support the empowerment of girls and women in alignment with the national 
and international commitments of Jordan.  
32. 
The project will include women in the decision-making for and management of water 
resources at the community level, including monitoring of water-resource use and maintenance 
of community-level water infrastructure. This will allow for the specific water-related climate-
change adaptation needs of women and girls to be addressed. The project will promote gender-
inclusive climate-resilient livelihoods that will benefit female-headed households, and can be 
implemented at home, and tailored awareness-raising and outreach programmes that will allow 
women to become leaders in the promotion of climate-change adaptation in their communities. 
The project’s gender action plan (annex 8 to the FP) outlines gender-disaggregated impacts, 
outcomes and output statements, as well as performance indicators and targets, and includes 
gender-responsive activities. In response to a question from the iTAP about the use in the FP of 
both “gender-transformative” and “gender-responsive” to characterize the project’s approach, 
the AE agreed to standardize to “gender-responsive” which was felt to be most accurate term.19 
33. 
The iTAP assesses the sustainable development potential of the project as high. 
1.5 
Needs of the recipient                
 
                             Scale: High 
34. 
The climate of Jordan has historically been arid, with a high degree of rainfall variability 
and temperatures in summer that often exceed 40°C. Approximately 94 per cent of the annual 
rainfall of Jordan is lost through evaporation, limiting freshwater availability. These dry 
conditions resulted in the country’s ranking in 2019 as the fifth most water-stressed country in 
the world, with only 125 m3 of water available per capita per year.20 This baseline water stress 
is being exacerbated by climate change, with observed trends and future projections indicating 
decreases in annual rainfall, higher annual temperatures, and more frequent, extreme and 
prolonged droughts. This water stress will particularly impact upon agricultural productivity, 
resulting in the need for increased food imports21 to ensure food availability. 
35. 
Rising costs of food imports are compounded by the increasing cost of extracting usable 
water from declining water resources. In providing motivation for the need for the proposed 
project and the 100 per cent concessionality of GCF funding, the FP includes figures on the 
country’s indebtedness that are now five years out of date. The iTAP notes that Jordan was 
reported by the International Monetary Fund as having a debt-to-gross domestic product ratio 
 
19 The following definitions from the United Nations Fund for Population Activities may be useful:  
• 
Gender sensitive: “programming that recognizes different needs of women, men, boys and girls and 
acknowledges gender power dynamics but does not necessarily address these”; 
• 
Gender responsive: “programming which includes specific action to try to reduce gender inequalities within 
communities”; and 
• 
Gender transformative: “programming which is designed around a fundamental aim of addressing root causes 
of gender inequality within society”. 
https://www.unfpa.org/sites/default/files/admin-resource/thematic%20note%201_gender_final.pdf 
20 World Resources Institute. 2019. Aqueduct baseline water stress rankings. Available at: 
https://www.wri.org/aqueduct/  
21 Jordan imports three times more agricultural products than it exports – a deficit that is compounded in times of 
drought.

=== PAGE 176 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 176 
 
 
of 89.5 per cent, in December 2023, with total sovereign debt standing at USD 45.5 billion.22 
This level of indebtedness mitigates against the country meeting its climate adaptation needs 
through debt finance for a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient integrated land and water-
resource management. 
36. 
The project targets small-scale farming communities in the three target sites (Yarmouk, 
King Talal Dam and Kafrain) in the northern Jordan Valley that are shown to be highly 
vulnerable to climate-change impacts. This is compounded by overextraction of groundwater 
and poor land-management practices that reduce the recharge rates of groundwater basins. 
With lowering water tables in many areas, farmers abandon existing wells and seek to invest 
more money and resources into drilling deeper wells, which may further exacerbate 
overextraction and inequality in access to water. The Jordan Valley is the primary agricultural 
region of the country, where most local communities depend on agriculture for their livelihoods, 
and are directly affected by the intensifying water shortage. Under future climate conditions, 
decreased agricultural yields will reduce the income potential for local communities which 
already face high poverty rates. Future climate change will additionally present multiple health 
risks to Jordan’s most vulnerable inhabitants, including children, women and refugees, including 
an increase in the incidence of heat stress related illnesses, exposure to diseases from reduced 
water quality, and a reduction in the diversity of food that households can afford, causing 
malnutrition.  
37. 
Overall, based on the above, the needs of the recipients are rated as high. 
1.6 
Country ownership 
  
 
                                           Scale: High           
38. 
The proposed project has been designed following extensive consultation with 
government, and a range of stakeholders, and is aligned with the country’s policies. The roles of 
the AE and EE, as well as technical partners to support the project upon implementation, are 
clearly set out in the FP package.  
39. 
Policy and strategy alignment: The proposed project is well aligned with the national 
priorities of Jordan as outlined in various policy and strategy documents, including the country’s 
updated nationally determined contribution (2021) which acknowledges gender-responsive 
management of watersheds, agriculture and rangelands as central to the country’s adaptation 
strategy, and highlights the need for interventions in managed aquifer recharge, rainwater 
harvesting, and watershed restoration for surface and groundwater provision. The project is 
aligned with the priority sectors for climate-change adaptation that were identified in the 
country’s national adaptation plan, the national climate change policy 2013–2020, the aligned 
national action plan to combat desertification in Jordan 2015–2020, and the national strategy 
for women in Jordan (2020–2025). 
40. 
In the water resources sector, the project aligns well with the climate change Policy for a 
resilient water sector (2016) which sets out the integrated water resources management  
approach upon which the national water strategy (2023) for Jordan is based – including the 
following four goals:  
(a) 
“Sustainably manage groundwater resources to restore safe yield levels and protect 
Jordan’s aquifers”;  
(b) 
“Sustainably manage and protect surface water resources and infrastructure”;

41. 
Roles of key entities. The two main EEs are the Jordanian Ministry of Environment for 
component 1, and IUCN for components 2–4. The suitability of these organizations for these 
roles is demonstrated in the FP and its annex 23, and can be summarized as follows: 
42. 
International Union for Conservation of Nature Regional Office for West Asia: Through 
its member organizations, IUCN supports scientific research and national conservation 
legislation, policies and practices; and funds, operates or manages thousands of field projects 
worldwide. The organization has a presence and footprint in Jordan via its regional office 
(IUCN-ROWA) which is based in Amman, with locally based Jordanian staff who are familiar 
with the national context and have experience in working with stakeholders in government, 
communities and the consortium of NGOs which will be technical partners (see below). 
According to the AE, “the selection of IUCN as EE, as opposed to UNEP, for example, is strategic 
in that it provides a strong operational foundation for the implementation of the project” (FP, 
para. 236).  
43. 
Ministry of Environment: The MOENV leads the country’s environmental governance 
system – including other relevant governmental organizations – in initiating and driving 
projects, policies, legislation, strategies, and monitoring, and is the national designated 
authority for GCF. The Climate Change Directorate coordinates high-level climate-change policy 
formulation and implementation, facilitating the participation of all relevant stakeholders. 
MOENV is well equipped to fulfil the role of EE for component 1, possessing the necessary 
technical and financial management capacity. The ministry leads the development of the 
country’s nationally determined contribution, national communications to the United Nations 
Framework Convention on Climate Change and national adaptation plan process, and has been 
involved in a number of related projects in recent years, focusing on, inter alia, climate-change 
adaptation, landscape and water-sector management, and sustainable development.  
44. 
Technical partners for implementation: The required expertise to implement project 
technical activities (components 2 to 4) will be sourced primarily from members of the 
consortium of NGOs, whose track record and capacity are set out in detail in annex 23 to the FP. 
In addition to IUCN-ROWA, these organizations include the Jordanian Royal Society for the 
Conservation of Nature, Royal Marine Conservation Society of Jordan, Princess Alia Foundation, 
Horizons for Green Development, Jordan Hashemite Fund for Human Development, Royal 
Botanical Garden, and Future Pioneers Empowering Communities. The selected technical 
partners will be contractually bound through legal agreements signed with IUCN, and will 
receive cash advances to carry out activities, dependent on cleared expenditure and progress 
reports.  
45. 
Engagement with stakeholders, including civil society. The proposed project was 
designed following an extensive consultative process with MOENV, which is responsible for the 
overall management of the country’s GCF portfolio, and the consortium of NGOs, as well as all 
other relevant project stakeholders,. Key government stakeholders involved were 
representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture, MWI, JVA, Water Authority of Jordan, Ministry 
of Planning and International Cooperation, and National Agricultural Research Centre, as well as 
participating NGOs and local communities. All stakeholder consultations were held in Arabic 
(with translation provided where necessary) and gender sensitivities were considered. 
Stakeholders were mapped and consulted at the national level (24 stakeholder organizations)

46. 
Details of the stakeholder engagement process are summarized in annex 7, covering

the scope of the proposed project, as part of the validation process. The group of respondents 
interviewed had strong representation of upstream and downstream farmers, near equal 
distribution of men and women, and nearly half were young people (aged 18–35 years). The

1.7 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
                                 Scale: Medium to High 
48. 
The GOJ is requesting USD 44,904,203 in grant finance from GCF to implement climate-
resilient ILWRM as part of a national response to climate change. This will make a contribution 
towards the ability of Jordan to balance its water demand with its water supply over the long 
term, despite evolving climate-change impacts. The GCF investment will be supported by co-
finance of USD 1,000,000 in-kind from the GOJ and USD 14,605,748 as a combination of grant 
and in-kind co-finance from a consortium of NGOs. The combined co-finance forms 26 per cent 
of the total project costs, while the GCF funds form 74 per cent.  
49. 
The co-finance is described as covering “all baseline costs – such as business-as-usual 
repairs or maintenance to water supply infrastructure necessary to ensure the sustainability of 
climate-proofing interventions, [and] GCF funding will solely be used to address the additional 
impacts of climate change”. In addition to official co-finance reflected in letters of commitment, 
parallel finance of USD 13,133,000 has been identified for the project, consisting of resources 
included under aligned initiatives and projects supported by the consortium of NGOs (detailed 
in annex 30 to the FP). 
50. 
Cost-effectiveness. Jordan experiences interconnected climate-change impacts that 
increase the scarcity of water resources in an already water-stressed country, requiring 
effective and cost-effective responses. In order to inform decision-making on adaptation 
interventions, a full economic analysis across all four project outputs was undertaken using a 
cost–benefit analysis approach – see the economic feasibility analysis in annex 3a and 3b to the 
FP. This builds on previous studies indicating that the benefits of ecosystem restoration to 
Jordanian society far outweigh the costs,24,25 including improvement of the country’s water 
supply. 
51. 
The results of this analysis, assuming a 25-year lifespan for the project and a discount 
rate of 5 per cent, (a social discount rate, on the grounds of the project’s multiple social and 
environmental public benefits, and not intending to provide a benchmark for competing with 
 
24 Becker N & Katz DL. 2009. An economic assessment of Dead Sea preservation and restoration. In: The Jordan River 
and Dead Sea Basin. Dordrecht: Springer. 
25 Myint MM & Westerberg V. 2014. An Economic Valuation of a Large-scale Rangeland Restoration Project through the 
Hima System in Jordan. Report for the ELD Initiative by International Union for Conservation of Nature, Nairobi, Kenya. 
Available at www.eld-initiative.org

=== PAGE 179 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 179 
 
 
market-based rates of return26) are summarized below. The results shows that the total 
investment of USD 60,509,951 across all four project outputs will generate a net present value 
of USD 105 million. The overall benefit to cost ratio is 3.00, meaning that the economic benefits 
of implementing the project are expected to be three times greater than the costs (to all parties).

52. 
According to the sensitivity analysis, the economic summary statistics above are robust 
to changes in input values. For example, when the value of groundwater to agriculture is 
reduced by 30 per cent, the economic rate of return (ERR) for the project is still favourable at 22 
per cent (and the value of USD 0.5/m3 of water is supported by other studies). A second key 
parameter is the additional groundwater recharge from the rockfill dams. Given that there is 
more uncertainty around this quantity, it has been reduced by 50 per cent for the sensitivity 
analysis. However, this still results in a favourable ERR for the project — with a rate of 20 per 
cent overall. It is also shown that if no scale-up of activities is achieved via components 1 and 4, 
the ERR remains favourable at 17 per cent. Overall, this project therefore represents a cost-
effective spend for GCF. 
53. 
The economic feasibility analysis also makes an assessment of financial sustainability 
assessment post-project (in section 6 of annex 3a). For project interventions to be sustainable, 
there is a need for an “ongoing financial flow to support the operations and maintenance of key 
infrastructure, equipment, and livelihood and household practices established via project 
activities”. These operations and maintenance costs are further elaborated in the operations 
manual framework (annex 21 to the FP), and are assessed against the results of the benefits 
analysis in order to demonstrate that the project’s exit strategy is financially viable. The floating 
solar panels will generate revenue through selling electricity, easily covering costs. The value of 
rainwater harvesting benefits exceed maintenance costs by a factor of five, which means that 
building owners and households can be expected to cover the maintenance costs using savings 
achieved from decreased purchase of water from other suppliers, or income generated using the 
water. The water fund is intended to deliver the financial resources required for ongoing 
maintenance of the rockfill dams and the ecosystem restoration sites. The analysis shows that 
the annual benefit in additional groundwater recharge from ecosystem restoration exceeds the 
associated annual maintenance costs, indicating that sufficient funds could, at least in theory, be 
leveraged via the fund to cover these costs.   
54. 
The extent to which the Water Fund will be successful in practice is unknown, and the 
absence of engagement of the key potential private-sector funding partners increases the risk 
that large-scale capitalization or ongoing replenishment via a payments for ecosystem services 
model will turn out not to be realistic. There is also no discussion in the FP of specific long-term 
public revenue sources which might be utilized to support the fund. Section 8.5 of the feasibility 
study explores lessons learned from water funds globally, and there is a section on previous 
studies on Jordanian farming communities’ willingness to pay for the preservation and 
provisioning of environmental resources, but this is not extended to larger corporate water 
users. A detailed application of global models and lessons to Jordan’s circumstances is not 
attempted, and the FP indicates that only during the Water Fund’s maturation phase (Years 7 
and 8), will a long-term financing strategy be developed, detailing how financial resources for 
the ongoing operation of the Water Fund post-GCF involvement will be leveraged from public 
and private sources (see subactivity 2.1.4.9).

The iTAP believes that the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative project 
applies a best practice EbA approach to enhance the resilience of small-scale farmers in the 
northern Jordan Valley, whose livelihoods, and the ecosystems on which these depend, are 
highly vulnerable to the negative impacts of climate change. The project also develops national

contribution to the efforts of Jordan to adapt to climate change intensified water scarcity.

iTAP’s recommendation to translate the best practice restoration techniques presented in

the information and make requirements for the selection of the partner contingent on

Groundwater monitoring at rockfill dams will be prioritised at both the start and end of the 
dry season each year. This will be combined with data on the amount of water extracted to 
determine overall impact on groundwater. 
Paradigm shift potential 
The AE confirms government’s openness to the establishment of WMCs and will be fostered 
further as the relevant activities under Component 1 (executed by the Ministry of 
Environment) are implemented (including consultations with relevant partners). 
Discussions with potential private sector investors into the Water Fund will be prioritised 
from the design phase (FP, Activity 2.4.1.1) to ensure both their involvement and support 
(including investment) are encouraged from an early stage. 
Shifting community-based nurseries to commercial units post-project will be informed by 
similar successful initiatives led by project partners such as RBC.  
Sustainable development potential 
We agree with iTAP that the potential flood reduction co-benefits of the project are 
important, despite the project focus being on water resource attenuation (to address a drying 
trend — drought).  While the primary focus is on strengthening water security, there will be 
flood attenuation co-benefits as report in the FP as water flow across the landscape is slowed 
and run-off is reduced. Section 11.6 of the FS provides an analysis of the Impacts of 
restoration interventions one the extent of coverage and speed of flow of rainwater over 
project target areas to support water harvesting and aquifer recharge. Managed Aquifer 
Recharge under Activity 3.1.1, will contribute to flood attenuation through the capturing of 
flood waters in rockfill dams, which will then slowly recharge aquifers with this water.  
Mitigation co-benefit: in addition to the mitigation benefit of the suspended solar installation, 
mitigation co-benefits will also accrue via the project’s landscape management interventions 
(restoration and improved ecosystem management). It is expected that the project will result 
in 537,390 tCO2eq GHG emissions (tCO2eq) reduced as a result of improved land use

management practices (by project end). This is presented as a measurable co-benefit with

Efficiency and effectiveness 
iTAP’s feedback is well noted. During the design phase of the Water Fund (Activity 2.4.1.1) in 
the first two years of the project, the options for the long-term financing of the fund will be 
identified. Relevant options will continue to be identified and assessed, with partnerships 
being built throughout the project period, contributing to the development of the long-term 
financing strategy during years 7 and 8. Much exploratory work would have already taken 
place by the time the development of the strategy commences. Additionally, the project 
design phase will also include the detailed application of global models and lessons to 
Jordan’s circumstances, in addition to work already carried out during project design. During 
Feasibility Study development (Annex 2), best practices and lessons related to the design, 
establishment and operationalisation of Water Funds around the world were assessed and 
captured (see Section 8.5 of Annex 2). Very few examples of Water Funds in a similar context 
to Jordan (none in the MENA region) were found. Hence, details on global models and lessons 
relevant to Jordan’s context are sparse. However, were possible, relevant lessons and 
modelled were applied to the Jordanian context. 
iTAP confirms the robustness of the economic feasibility.  We would also like to note here that 
there are many benefits values associated with ecosystem restoration that were not captured 
in the economic appraisal, as follows: 
 
Ecosystem restoration will reduce surface water runoff, thereby attenuating downstream 
flood risks. However, whilst the supply of this service is acknowledged as a co-benefit of 
ecosystem restoration it has not been modelled due to the uncertainties and complexities 
surrounding the associated hydrological modelling. Similarly, additional benefits such as an 
increase in surface albedo, reductions in methane emissions from livestock, capture of methane 
in the soil and improved biodiversity are not quantified and monetized meaning that the 
ecosystem restoration benefits can only be seen as partial at best. 
 
With regards to albedo, the restoration is likely to lead to an increase in the cover of 
indigenous grasses. This is in turn likely to increase albedo, which mitigates global warming1, 
because grasses, particularly in the dry season, have a lighter coloration than the shrubs that 
prevail in degraded ecosystems in Jordan. In terms of methane emissions, it is well 
documented that restoration of ecosystems increases the quality of fodder available for 
 
1 Haslter et al. 2023. Accounting for albedo change to identify climate-positive tree cover restoration. Nature 
Communications. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-024-46577-1

=== PAGE 183 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.07 
Page 183 
 
 
 
livestock, which in turn greatly reduces the methane production from livestock2. The amount 
of methane produced per head of cattle can, for example, potentially be reduced by tens of 
kilograms per year3. This effect will contribute to climate change mitigation as methane is a 
powerful greenhouse gas. In addition to reducing methane emissions, recent studies show 
that it is likely that restoration of rangelands will also capture methane in topsoils (in the 
order of 10 kgC/ha/year)4. Lastly, with regards to biodiversity, the restoration will in time 
increase the diversity of indigenous plants, which in turn improves the quality of habitat for a 
wide range of indigenous animals, including insects, birds, reptiles and mammals.  
 
The details can be found in Annex 4:  Financial and Economic appraisal.

1 Background 
 
The overall objective of the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILMI) is to 
contribute to enhanced resilience of representative Jordanian ecosystems and vulnerable 
communities in the Jordan Rift Valley, Zarqa River Basin and the Gulf of Aqaba through integrated 
landscape and water resources management (ILWRM). This will include the promotion of climate-
smart land- and sea-use planning, small-scale sustainable energy opportunities and the 
diversification of local livelihoods and economy options.  
 
Experience and evidence indicate that women, youth and children are among the most vulnerable 
to the impacts of climate change, and that a large proportion of vulnerable people who are highly 
dependent on local natural resources for their survival are women and children. The Government 
of Jordan has therefore established that gender-sensitive planning, resource-allocation and 
implementation in the context of the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative will be a 
priority. Supporting transformational change both in the lives of female beneficiaries and in 
prompting gender mainstreaming in national climate change policymaking and implementation was 
therefore considered a priority in stakeholder consultations. 
 
To fulfill the project’s design requirements, this assessment was undertaken in accordance with 
GCF guidelines to inform the project design on gender barriers, constrains and opportunities for 
women to benefit from the projects activates. In addition, the Gender Action Plan will ensure 
women and other marginalised groups are included in the project’s activities. The Gender Action 
Plan is based on the proposed project logical framework and focused on avoiding and minimising 
the negative impacts of the project activities on the local communities, and maximising the positive 
impacts and social benefits focusing on women and marginalised groups. 
 
 Desk review and Field Research 
 
To design this gender analysis as part of preparation for a Gender Action Plan, an accelerated 
review was undertaken of supporting documents related to gender mainstreaming in climate 
change, as well as previous gender analysis conducted by other donors in Jordan — in addition to 
DOS available data. 
 
This gender assessment identifies gender considerations that are relevant to the project and 
examines potential gender mainstreaming opportunities. Therefore, stakeholder consultations were 
undertaken and focus group discussions (FGDs) were carried out in three sample provinces 
selected to benefit from the project (Al yarmouk, Al kafreen and tal al Romman) with 60 total 
participants, of which more than 50% were women. This was done to highlight gender and social 
norms and to identify key entry points for women’s participation in the project activity within the 
study area.  
 
The consultations helped to identify constraints/barriers to women’s participation, their 
opportunities and prioritize the areas for capacity building to mainstream gender in the future 
activities of the project. 
 
2 
Jordan context on gender equality 
 
Jordan is ranked 138th out of 153 countries in the World Economic Forum 2020 Global Gender 
Gap Report — it is part of the five countries with the highest absolute decrease in its overall score 
in recent years, with the main sub-index that led the decrease being economic participation. 
Challenges facing women’s economic participation include: i) low wage levels and low minimum 
wages; ii) high unemployment rates; iii) failure to regulate foreign (migrant) labor; iv) a large

=== PAGE 188 ===
5 
 
unregulated sector; and v) non-alignment between (academic and vocational) education outputs 
and the labor market needs1. 
 
Inequalities remain in participation in economic and political spheres, as well as in legal rights 
related to marriage, inheritance, divorce and employment. There are indications that, within 
society, boys are preferred to girls and decision-making within the home is largely in the hands of 
husbands and male family members. Structural and systemic gender inequality is a major obstacle 
impeding women’s empowerment in Jordan, preventing women from exercising their right to lead 
a full life in both public and private spheres. Gender inequality affects the level to which women 
have control over resources such as wealth, land, property, water, fertile soil and transportation, 
which in turn reduces their ability to enter public life, establish a business, find employment, travel 
to markets, stand for office and speak out in public. 
 
 
Women's economic empowerment   
 
The socio-economic situation in Jordan is categorized by high unemployment rates, economic 
inequality, political marginalization of youth and women, and a mismatch between educational 
skills and skills sought by the labor market2. 
 
In the second quarter of 2019, the Labor Force Participation Rate in Jordan decreased to 34.60%, 
from 35.10% in the first quarter of 2019. From 2017 until 2019, the Labor Force Participation Rate  
averaged 37.13%, reaching an all-time high of 40.60% in the first quarter of 2017 and a record low 
of 34.60% in the second quarter of 20193. The Jordanian government has notably succeeded in 
closing the gender gap in education. However, it has been slow to recognize how this translates 
into opportunities for women’s contribution to the economy.

Figure 1. The number of economically active Jordanians (aged 15+ years) by sex and sector. 
 
The Jordanian labor market is characterized by large gender differences. Labor force participation 
among women is below 14%, compared to ~64% for men4. As a result, unemployment rates among 
women are almost twice as high as for men. These differences are strongly related to the traditional 
social structures that place a higher value on women’s reproductive roles. Women’s roles and 
responsibilities have traditionally been defined within the household context. As a result, any 
economic benefits women can gain tend to be considered for family and household, rather than 
individual, causing invisibility of women’s economic contributions. 
 
Figure 2.  Population Age 15+ Years by Economic Activity Status, Sex, Educational Level & Nationality (% 
age Distribution) 2019 
 
Women in Jordan are expected to take on a disproportionate amount of family-related 
responsibilities, which creates pressure on them because it is challenging to balance professional 
and family responsibilities. Therefore, women’s participation decreases significantly after marriage 
because of the perceived difficulties in balancing professional and household responsibilities. The 
working environment, whether there are many men or not, influence women’s decisions. Generally, 
workplaces that do not require long commutes and which are “female friendly” are considered 
desirable5.

Unemployment in Jordan increased to 19.2% in 2019, although this was not completely out of line 
with other countries in the region. DOS statistics for 2019 place the rate for men at an average of 
17.1% and women’s unemployment at 27.2% (Figure 2)6. 
 
 
Figure 3.  Unemployment disaggregated by gender 
 
Although educational opportunities for young women and men in Jordan are equal, their ease of 
entry into the labor market is not. This occurs because of several reasons, including the limited 
range of jobs considered to be appropriate and acceptable for women and employers. There are 
also preferences for hiring men, who are considered to be more in need of jobs as they must 
provide for families. For university graduates (holding a bachelor’s degree), male unemployment 
is 18.5% in 2018 and for women 73.7% in 2018, underlining the difficulties faced even by educated 
women in entering the Jordanian job market. 
 
In Jordan, women’s participation in the labour market is limited by gender-specific social 
constraints, such as cultural and class-based expectations regarding women’s household roles 
and unofficial ‘occupational segregation’, where various jobs are seen as appropriate for men or 
for women. Some legal regulations reinforce notions of a women’s work as being secondary to that 
of her husband and diminish incentives for married women to work. For example, women 
employees in the public sector are not entitled to receive family allowance if the fathers of their 
children are alive and fit for work. Moreover, a deceased wife’s pension cannot be transferred to 
her husband unless he is unfit for work and does not have any other source of income7. 
 
There are extraordinary differences for women in the work force by sector, with the public sector 
employing half of all employed women in Jordan. There are many social taboos around women 
working in male-dominated environment, such as construction or engineering. Many poor women 
work out of economic necessity, although a substantial number of working women come from 
middleclass families. Jordanian women are more likely to work as professionals (48.8% of all 
employed women), and technicians and associate professionals (24.0%). Women constitute 54.6% 
of all employees in the education sector, 45.5% in health and social work, and 28.9% in banking8. 
 
The 1996 Labor Code applies to all employees in the formal sector, with the exception of domestic, 
agricultural and unpaid workers. It stipulates normal working hours, allows for temporary contracts 
and outlines terms of leave and dismissal as well as giving the Minister of Labor the authority to 
either temporarily or permanently shut down any enterprise in which an incident of sexual

8 National Center for Human Resources Development. Available at: 
http://www.almanar.jo/en/documents/Mayssa%20Gender%20report.pdf

harassment (as defined in the Penal Code) has taken place. The employment of civil servants is 
governed by the Civil Service Statute amended in 20079. 
 
 
Figure 4. Jobs created for persons aged 15+ (2009–2017). 
 
Cultural constraints also clearly militate against women working in areas of low prestige, in jobs 
requiring travel and unsocial working hours or contact/proximity with strangers, these often in 
areas with the most critical labor shortages, such as tourism10. 
 
 
Women's political participation 
 
A gender quota system for political participation in the Parliament was adopted in 2003 and revised 
in 2015 to increase the number of seats reserved for women parliamentary candidates from 6 to 
15 (of 130 total available seats for elected representatives). However, women’s political 
participation in Jordan remains low. The 2020 Global Gender Gap Index ranks Jordan at 113h 
under the political empowerment section11. Moreover, the 2020 Women in Parliament Index ranks 
Jordan 113th out of 153 countries. Traditional gender stereotypes are used to justify low levels of 
women participation in the political sphere. Women are poorly represented in the political life, which 
hinders them from effectively contributing in the decision-making process by not conveying the 
actual priorities, policies and practices for women. Therefore, politics in Jordan is considered a 
male domain and decisions are taken by men and therefore generally serve male interests.

Peace, and Security 
 
Jordan is one of 80 countries that has developed a National Action Plan for the implementation of 
Security Council resolution 1325 on Women, Peace and Security. Resolution 1325 is a landmark 
achievement of the international community coming together to address the importance of women’s 
participation in sustaining peace and building security. The Jordanian National Action Plan (JONAP) 
included actions to drive women’s engagement in local, regional, and national processes of 
peacebuilding and social inclusion. 
 
 
Rule of law 
 
Jordan ratified the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against 
Women (CEDAW) in 1992. However the country maintains reservations on some articles, such as 
giving equal rights to women with regard to nationality of children, same rights and responsibilities 
during marriage and divorce12, rights and responsibilities as parents, as well as personal rights — 
including the right to choose a family name, a profession and an occupation. However, in 2009, a 
Cabinet decision lifted its reservations on giving women freedom of mobility and choice of residence 
without consent of their husbands or other male family members. Some progress has also been 
achieved with regards to policy, legislation and procedures adopted to promote gender equality and 
protect women’s rights in general, but improvements are still needed. As the Arab Women 
Organization concludes "The steps did not address the core of the problem; the patriarchal system 
itself as there is no political will to genuinely promote equality in the public and political sphere.13  
 
Legislations in Jordan is civil, with the exception of the Personal Status Law that is based on religion. 
Therefore, it is highly dependent on the interpretations of the religious precipices. Despite some 
progressive amendments, significant legal Inequalities persist in issues related to personal status 
(marriage, child custody, inheritance, divorce), Penal Code, labor laws, and, violence against women 
is not sufficiently addressed14. This situation contributes towards the legal perpetuation of the inferior 
status of women in society, which plays an essential role in the continuation of gender stereotypes.15 
 
 
Health 
 
The health situation in Jordan is amongst the best in the Middle East because of the secure and 
stabile conditions in the Kingdom. It is also a result of a set of effective development plans and 
projects which included health as an important element and essential part of sustainable 
development. The health sector in Jordan as such has witnessed remarkable development and 
reflected positively on the wellbeing of citizens. The general health 2018 indicators have reflected 
the quality and efficiency of the delivered health services, putting Jordan in an advanced rank 
globally. 
 
12 JIF, 2019. Available at: https://jordaningoforum.org  
13 Women’s Rights in Jordan, Arab Women Organization, 2013 
14Gender Profile JORDAN, Institute for women studies in the Arab word,2018  
15 AWO, 2013

=== PAGE 193 ===

Figure 5.  Life expectancy and fertility rates. 
There was a comparatively rapid decline in fertility between the 1990 and 2002 — according to 
JPFHS surveys — from 5.6 children per woman to 3.7 children per woman (Figure 5). The TFR 
remained relatively stable between 2007 and 2012, ranging from 3.5 to 3.8 children per woman, 
before declining to 2.7 children per woman in 2017-1816. 
Among women age 30–49, the median age at first birth is approximately 26 years. This is related to 
educational level, as the most educated women tend to marry later (by approximately five years) 
than women with middle education levels17. 
Approximately half of married women (49%) want no more children or have been sterilized. However, 
~7% of married women are undecided about whether they want more children, but men are much 
less likely than women to express a desire to limit childbearing where ~31% are undecided about 
future childbearing. The mean ideal family size among currently married women (age 15–49) is 2.9 
children; compared with men, who have a mean ideal family size of 3.8 children. 
The proportion of married women age 15–49 currently using any contraceptive method decreased 
from 61% in 2012 to 52% in 2017-18. In this regard, it was found that ~ 26% men in some 
governorates tend to decide about use of family planning for their wives which can impact women 
health both physically and psychologically. 
Mortality rates are generally higher among children born to Syrian women than among children born 
to Jordanian women or women of other nationalities. Overall, the under-five mortality rate is 25 
deaths per 1,000 live births among children whose mothers are Syrian and 16 deaths per 1,000 live 
births among children whose mothers are Jordanian or of other nationalities. However, women face 
some constraints in receiving medical and health care services. Many factors can prevent women 
from getting medical advice or treatment for themselves when they are sick, particularly during 
pregnancy and at the time of delivery. 42% of married women age 15–49 in Jordan report having at 
least one of the specified problems in accessing health care. The most frequently reported problems 
were: i) having to take transport (25%) and not wanting to go alone (24%); ii) distance to a health 
facility and Maternal Health Care; and iii) difficulty in getting money for treatment (both 22%). The 
problem reported least often was getting permission to go for treatment (9% of respondents). 
The total fertility rate in rural areas is higher (3.1 children) compared to urban areas (2.7). This result 
is in line with the average size of the Jordanian family of 4.8 person, as determined by the General 
16 Jordan Population and Family Health Survey, 20017-18. Available at: 
https://dhsprogram.com/pubs/pdf/FR346/FR346.pdf  
17 Jordan Population and Family Health Survey, 20017-18. Available at: 
https://dhsprogram.com/pubs/pdf/FR346/FR346.pdf

=== PAGE 194 ===
11 
Population and Housing Census – 2015. The largest disparity in total fertility rate is among Syrian 
women (at 4.7), compared with 2.6 children per Jordanian woman and 1.9 per woman from other 
nationalities.18 Among women overall, mean ideal number of children is slightly higher in rural (4.0 
children) than urban (3.8 children) areas, this number is higher among women of Syrian nationality 
(4.1 children) than among women of Jordanian nationality. 
Education 
Jordan has made great progress in increasing access to education, with nearly universal primary 
enrollment, gender parity in Jordanian schools, and a 98% adult literacy rate (Figure 6).  Despite 
widespread access to education, challenges remain. 
Figure 6. Indicators of Education (2009–2018). 
Schools are overcrowded and in varying states of disrepair, teachers receive limited training and 
support, and learning outcomes remain low, with only a fifth of second and third graders reading at 
grade level. The growing population of Jordanians and the influx of refugees have placed additional 
pressure on Jordan’s public education system, and children and youth who fall out of the system 
lack opportunities to get back on track. Ensuring that all children and youth have the opportunity to 
gain skills and knowledge to succeed is essential to their economic productivity. 
Jordan has almost achieved its target of universal access to primary education — with 97% of 
children in school — and has maintained gender parity in education since 1999. However, progress 
in school enrolment has not benefited all children equally, including children with disabilities and 
refugee children, and improving the quality of education requires greater focus and investment. 
Children from poor socio-economic backgrounds, children involved in child labor and those with 
disabilities are at a higher risk of being out of school. Children with multiple and overlapping 
vulnerabilities are at greater risk of either dropping out or being out of education altogether. Early 
childhood development opportunities occur primarily in homes, with only 13% of children aged 3 to 
4 years old accessing Kindergarten 1 (KG1); and 59% of those aged 5 to 6 years old attending 
Kindergarten 2 (KG2).  
Sending a girl to school allows her to build confidence and contribute to the country’s economic, 
social and political development. Although education in Jordan has reached gender parity in 1999,

social norms and traditions, along with other factors, block Jordanian girls from fully utilizing their 
education in the job market19. More female students are enrolled in secondary school than male 
students. Boys are more likely to drop out of school due to poor academic achievements, violence, 
bullying and the need to find employment. Lagging boys’ performance may be tied to teacher quality, 
availability of male teachers, violence in schools, availability of quality school environments and 
unfavourable student-teacher ratio20. 
 
Meanwhile, girls are more likely to be kept at home because of protection concerns and household 
responsibilities such as caring for younger siblings or ailing parents. Girls are also far more likely to 
be married at an early age. There is no specific gender disparity in Jordanian primary schools. Over 
94 % of girls attend school compared to 95 % of boys. Girls in rural areas are just as likely as girls 
in urban areas to attend school. Approximately 10% of girls who are of secondary school age (12–
17 years old) are not participating in the education system, compared to 15% of male youth of the 
same age. Although the percentage is in favor of girls, it is not satisfactory and is primarily the result 
of child marriage or low school performance followed by dropout. 
 
Since 14% of the country lives below the poverty line, child marriage occurs often among Jordanian 
girls in order for parents to be relieved of their financial responsibility. Around 13% of girls in Jordan 
marry before turning 18 years of age. Among men age 20-49, by contrast, only 1% were married 
before their 18th birthday. Over 86% of girls who marry under the age of 18 have only finished their 
elementary education. The median age at first marriage among women aged 25–49 is 22.7 years. 
However, the median age at first marriage cannot be calculated for men for the same age because 
less than 50% of men had married before age 25. The situation is not better for the Syrian refugee 
girls coming to Jordan since 33% of them are already married. This significantly decreases their 
chances of school enrollment21. 
 
The Syrian refugee crisis has strongly impacted on education in Jordan. It has created overcrowded 
classes and increased educational costs for the government. The government strives to improve its 
educational standards for girls and boys alike, despite the setback caused by the humanitarian crisis. 
UNICEF is partnering with the Ministry of Education to educate refugees and supply classroom 
furniture and learning materials. Plan International Jordan creates safe child-friendly spaces for 
Jordanian and Syrian refugee children under the age of 5, which increases their chances of attend 
primary school22.

Figure 7. Higher education in Jordan. 
Jordan has one of the highest literacy rates for girls in the Middle East, which is estimated to be as 
high as 97%. However, this educational advancement does not transfer over to the workforce. 
Jordan has one of the world’s lowest rates of women participating in the workforce, at 13.2%. If a 
gender gap in Jordan’s workforce continues to exist, the country will experience a reduction in 
potential GDP growth of 0.5 to 0.9% per year. 
A good education is no guarantee that the girl will find employment. Approximately 30% of Jordanian 
women with a university degree or above are unemployed. The percentage of woman that believe 
there are obstacles to women’s employment is estimated at 76%. They consider these obstacles to 
have a cultural and religious background that pressures women to stay at home, as well as a lack 
of women’s job opportunities. There is a large socio-economic gap that exists in Jordan. In 2009, 
only 16% of girls from underprivileged households excelled above level 2 math, compared to 57% 
of girls from wealthier households. Costly private schools that usually offer better education are 
reserved for the upper class of society23. 
Learning outcomes in Jordan are poor throughout basic and secondary education. 70% of students 
in Grades 2 and 3 are reading without comprehension24. Students consistently perform poorly in 
international standardized tests, including in mathematics and science25. 
Education for women and men as well as vocational training programs in the growing sectors of the 
country’s economy will contribute to macroeconomic growth and also to gender equity26. According 
to the theory of demographic transition, fertility begins to decline when at least half of the female 
population is educated. In Jordan, education has progressed much faster than changes in behaviour 
concerning motherhood. This is due to the low employment rate of women because of social 
constrains related to the reproductive role for females. Women tend to have one child more than 
desired. This is especially the case for the poorest women, while the gap narrows in the wealthiest 
categories who manage to obtain their desired fertility. The recent report of the Population and 
Family Health Survey 2017–2018, showed a decline in the Total Fertility Rate from 5.6% in 1990 to 
3.7% in 2002 and 3.5 % in 2012 to reach 2.7% in 2017-2018, which means that a woman in Jordan 
has an average of 2.7 children throughout her life.

 Gender norms  
 
Jordan has witnessed changes over the past decades that included the social construction and the 
prevailing values as a result of major changes in the educational, economic and technological 
aspects. These changes appeared as family patterns began to shift from the extended family to the 
nuclear family, as a result of urbanization, education and economic situation. As a result, the 
perception of women's education and work, reproduction and social relations — particularly in urban 
and civilized areas — and the change of the standards that control behavior. 
 
However, the tribe still represents the primary social structure (paternal/masculine), and its 
orientations and representation are masculine as well. Therefore, this system of values, customs 
and traditions define an individual's awareness and concepts, and form their culture and behaviour 
especially for women. As a result, gender role allocation focuses on the man as head of household 
and the main source of income for the family and the woman as wife and mother. According to 
prevailing cultural norms, interactions between the sexes and mobility for women are limited so that 
family honour and dignity can be maintained. The norms and values that underscore this pattern 
place numerous obstacles in the way of women who hope to fulfil and balance their productive and 
reproductive roles. 
 
Decision-making in the household depends on the issue at hand and is influenced by level of 
education, residency, age, status in the family and control of assets. With the exception of decision 
on women's own health care most decisions are made jointly by the couple. Regarding decision-
making in the household most decisions regarding health care, major household purchases, and 
visits to their family or relatives are made jointly. The lowest decision-making ability of women pertain 
to major household purchases27. 
 
 Women-headed households in Jordan 
 
Women-headed households constitute about 17.5% of Jordan's households. By region of residence, 
religion, and household size they differ little from households in the country at large. 392,300 families 
out of 2.242 million families are woman-headed. 94.7 percent of the women who head their families 
are economically inactive. 105,642 households received monthly assistance from the National Aid 
Fund in 202028. Nearly half of women-headed households (48 percent) are living below the poverty 
line, compared with about 13 percent in the population at large. Their annual spending is less than 
one-third of the nationwide average. Very few of the women household heads work outside the 
home: 78 percent of them are not and have never been employed. Their education levels are low: 
about 54 percent are either illiterate or have only basic schooling, and about 27 percent have 
secondary education. The problems faced by women household heads include malnutrition (a 
problem in nearly one in five of these families); social stigma attached to themselves and their 
children; problems in rearing their sons, many of whom drop out of school and become socially 
withdrawn; and pressures from their own or their husband's family29. 
 
 
Women in the agricultural sector  
 
Although the contribution of the agriculture sector to Jordan’s Gross Domestic Product (GDP) and 
labour force has declined over the last decades, women’s participation in the sector remains a critical 
source of employment for the country’s poorest citizens, and also serves as a major source of 
subsistence and food security in the country30. While only 2% of the total working population in the 
country — and 0.9% of its total female labour force — was employed in agriculture as of 2014, 
 
27 Gender Analysis report, 2015 
28 https://jordantimes.com/news/local/392300-families-jordan-women-headed-—-sigi#:~:text=AMMAN%20—

29 https://www.worldscientific.com/doi/10.1142/9781783267347_0006 
30 UN women, 2018. Women's Participation in the Agricultural Sector, Rural Institutions and Community Life.

=== PAGE 198 ===
15 
 
approximately 25% of the total number of poor people who live in rural areas continue to depend on 
agriculture as a primary source of livelihood31.  
 
The agricultural sector in Jordan is also known to have the highest proportion of informal workers 
compared to other economic sectors. Approximately 16% of women who work in the agricultural 
sector are informally employed, which is higher than the proportion of men (~5%). According to FAO, 
women in Jordan are also actively involved in home-based agricultural activities, typically managing 
small homestead gardens and looking after the family plot31F32.   
 
Despite women’s seemingly active role in the agriculture sector, a review of existing literature and 
secondary data suggests that limited up-to-date information is available on the subject of women’s 
involvement in the sector, possibly due to its informal nature33. While some research exists on the 
challenges facing the agricultural sector in general, very limited up-to-date gender-disaggregated 
data is available on the challenges specific to women working in the sector. 
 
The majority of the rural population depend either directly or indirectly on agriculture for their 
livelihoods. The rural poor are expected to face the most severe consequences of climate change 
because of their greater dependence on agriculture, their relatively lower ability to adapt and the 
high share of income they spend on food. Climate change could undermine the progress that has 
been made in poverty reduction and negatively impact on food security and economic opportunities 
for vulnerable rural populations. The negative impacts of climate change will be strongly by women.  
 
The role of women in the rural economy is substantial. Women are traditionally responsible for the 
household economy and are active in agriculture as well. Women make crucial contributions in 
agriculture and rural enterprises in drylands as farmers, animal caretakers, workers and 
entrepreneurs. For rural women and men, land is the most important asset to support production 
and provide for food, nutrition and income security. However, while having the legal right to own 
land, social norms diminish women’s access to land by favouring male inheritance and providing 
disincentives for women to purchase land34. Legal and social restrictions on land inheritance have 
particularly adverse effects on poor women, since often in poorer, rural areas the primary way in 
which wealth is transferred is through land inheritance. Moreover, the acquisition of land by single 
or married women is viewed as outside the usual social norms, which is not the case for single or 
married men, as is the registration of wives as joint owners of property of their husbands. Likewise, 
given the customary patrilineal inheritance systems, where property is transferred to and through 
male members of the family, women often have to perform unpaid work on family farms. For 
instance, women account for only 5% of agriculture holders in Jordan, though the number of large 
(10 ha and above) agricultural area owned by men is twice as large as that of female holders35. In a 
recent study, it was found that the majority of female respondents were workers on and not owners 
of the farms36. Only 4.8% of the respondents were owners of the farm, while only 1.6% of them 
reported that her farm ownership is of private partnership nature37. 
 
Women empowerment programs may be another source of support for farms as these farms already 
utilize women labour in agricultural operations. In fact, 59.8% of the farms surveyed have women 
among their labour. The nature of work performed by women includes cutting and picking (38.2%),

and have asked on several occasions to be involved in the mentioned activities. 
 
 Women and land ownership 
 
Property ownership is a male domain in Jordan, where women are dependence on men for housing. 
A patriarchal pattern of power dominates both inheritance and property. While the inheritance rights 
of women are formally enshrined in the constitution, in Islamic law (Sharia), and in the customary 
law particularly common in the steppe regions, female heirs continue to face social pressure to 
renounce their rights in favor of male heirs. Most women either do not receive the share of 
inheritance that the law entitles them to or they are simply denied their right to housing and land. 
Jordanian Sharia Court data show that in 2014 a third of heirs fully relinquished their inheritance 
rights through the process of takha¯ruj, the voluntary “opting out” of their rights (exclusion). Records 
do not specify their gender, but survey evidence shows most of those who requested takha¯ruj were 
women. A survey conducted in the Irbid governorate in 2010 by the Jordanian National Forum for 
Women indicated that 20 percent of women had renounced their inheritance rights and that three-
quarters of them had done so willingly. Jordanian women still appear to abide by the belief that they 
will harm the economic and social status of their brothers if they take up their right to inheritance. 
These social norms and beliefs have led Jordanian women to internalize and condone the privileges 
of men in matters of inheritance. Besides social pressure, women are deprived of their inheritance 
in several other ways. One takes the form of a donation to male heirs before a person’s death. 
Another is when owners and their male heirs choose to leave a property undivided, which prevents 
female heirs from using or selling their share for years and even decades. This often happens with 
agricultural land and family-owned buildings. In most cases male heirs give symbolic gifts to women, 
called badal or takr¯ım, which are worth far less than the value of shares they are legally entitled to 
receive. In general, women are kept in the dark about the real value of assets such as land or an 
apartment39. 
 
As a consequence of such cultural norms, only 6.8 percent of ever married women owned their 
houses and only 7 percent owned land in 2012. A recent trend has seen women managing to register 
residential apartments in their name upon marriage, mainly in the major cities of Amman and Irbid. 
Women owned 19.5 percent of registered apartments in Jordan in 2014. But since apartments 
constitute 42 percent of housing units, mainly built as a form of investment, that translates into 
women owning a mere 10 percent of all apartments. In comparison, women owned 22.5 percent of 
securities, whether bonds, shares, or options in 2011. Husbands often register apartments and 
securities in the name of their wives to protect their assets from creditors, if their business goes 
bankrupt. Less than 3 percent of Jordanian people are Christian, but historically they have owned 
large tracts of land and are significant players in the nation. Sharia law has been applied to Christian 
property inheritance since the Ottoman period in Jordan, and although Christian tradition stipulates 
that women be granted the same shares as men, Christian deputies have never asked for the 
inheritance law to be revised40. 
 
Women’s rights activists have succeeded in amending the Jordanian Personal Status Law No. 36 
of 2010 by working with the Sharia Supreme Court (Qa¯dı¯al-Quda¯), the country’s highest religious 
and legal institution. In 2011 seven amendments were made and two articles added: articles 318 
and 319 on property and exclusion. The most important amendment introduced a mandatory three 
months after the death of the owner before either exclusion (takha¯ruj) or power of attorney 
(waka¯la) could be invoked. Another amendment stated that an attorney dealing with an estate was

under obligation to inform the heirs of all properties included in an inheritance. Another specified that 
a judge must explain to the heirs the legal consequences of the exclusion of inheritance; this has 
resulted in judges asking for better education for women so that they can better defend their rights. 
The improvement of women’s rights to inheritance has been proffered since the 1960s as a solution 
to poverty and a means of preventing women from falling into destitution. Property rights and security 
of tenure are basic not only to shelter but also to improvement of livelihoods, economic prosperity, 
and sustainable development. The United Nations’ human settlement program, UN-Habitat, and the 
Office of the High Commissioner for Human Rights support property rights and the security of tenure 
under the Right to Adequate Housing Framework. Jordan is working on incentives for better inclusion 
of women in the economy, and inheritance and property should be a major dimension of this policy41. 
 
 Women and Water  
 
Jordan suffers from water scarcity — a fact that places severe strain on its social and economic 
development prospects. Currently almost 60% of Jordan’s water is used for irrigated agriculture. 
Water allocation for domestic use comprises less than 30%. In terms of drinking water allocation, 
cuts below a certain threshold endanger the very survival of people. 
 
Women play an important role in water management. They are most often the collectors, users and 
managers of water in the household as well as farmers of irrigated and rainfed crops. Because of 
these roles, women have considerable knowledge about water resources, including quality and 
reliability, restrictions and acceptable storage methods, and are key to the success of water 
resources development42. Decisions about water rationing have a major impact on women, since 
they are responsible for household activities that require water, such as cooking and cleaning. The 
gender assessment study conducted by USAID in 2012 highlighted that, both women and men 
mentioned that in neighbourhoods where water is only available during certain days and times, 
women must be at home during those periods in order to complete their household chores.43 
 
Women are not only responsible for food preparation, but also for keeping the family healthy, which 
means taking care of sick family members. The combination of these family roles makes them 
potentially strong advocates for clean water. Therefore, it is important to educate women on the 
connection between safe drinking water, contamination of the water supply, and the impact on the 
health of the family (especially children). The environmental movement in Jordan already has strong 
women advocates who are showing interest in policymaking and political engagement, making them 
potential role models for other women. Research in Jordan has identified that programmes which 
involved women in their activities were very successful in achieving their objectives. They had a 
greater impact on conserving water resources and helping local communities to achieve acceptable 
living conditions and to adopt the sustainable use of water resources44.  
 
 Climate change 
 
In the fight against climate change, the role of women as agents of change is frequently overlooked. 
Women are often perceived as passive members of households or indirect victims of climate change. 
However, it is critical to recognise the actual role of women within their communities as educators, 
practitioners, and influential agents of change. Empowering women to harness their leadership 
capacities is therefore essential. Women can inform and implement actions and strategies related

to water management, energy, agriculture and food security, economic growth and livelihoods, 
waste management, and policymaking. 
 
In this regard, a study released in 2010 by the International Union for the Conservation of Nature 
(IUCN) demonstrated the strength of women’s ability to enact social change by utilising their 
knowledge, skills and capacities, therefore informing Jordan’s climate change policies. The resulting 
gender-sensitive Climate Action Plan (ccGAP) outlined a framework for integrating a gender 
perspective in climate change efforts, acknowledging the fundamental role of women in the sectors 
of water, agriculture, energy, and waste management and the vast contribution that women could 
make to climate change adaptation and mitigation45. 
 
3 
Demographic Information  
 
Jordan, is an Arab kingdom in the Middle East. It is located between the Mediterranean and Red 
Seas and contains the massive Jordan River. The land itself is mostly flat and covers 34,495 square 
miles (89,342 square kilometres) of surface area.  
 
95-97% of Jordanians are of Arab decent, but there are many other ethnic groups in the country. 
There are ~500,000 Iraqis, over 500,000 Syrians, ~30,000 Kurds, ~5,000 Armenians Assyrian 
Christians make up 0.8% of the population. In 2019, the population of Jordan was estimated at ~10 
million, which makes it the 89th most populous nation. Jordan is an extremely urban country, with 
nearly 90% of the people living there residing in cities or the surrounding metro areas. Jordan has 
become the destination of several waves of migrants from Palestine and recently from Iraq and 
Syria.  The total number of Syrian refugees in Jordan is an estimated at 1.4 million persons. UNHCR 
reports that of the refugees of Syrian nationality, % are living under the poverty line. Most refugees 
registered with UNHCR live in urban areas where competition for resources and services with Jordan 
communities is most intense.

The influx of Syrian refugees has increased the demand for energy in the Jordan and total residential 
energy consumption has risen significantly in recent years47. According to the results of the 
Population and Housing Census 2015 and the Migration streams, a significant increase in population 
growth occurred between the periods of the censuses 2004–2015, reaching ~5.3%. 
Figure 9. Age group share of total population48.  
 
The overwhelming majority of the population of Jordan is Arab, and mostly Sunni Muslim. Jordanian 
society also includes ethnic minorities, including Circassians, Kurds, Turkomans, Chechens, and 
Armenians. A substantial number of Jordanians are Christian, the second most numerous religious 
community, and there is a small Druze community. The Jordanian state as a whole is very supportive 
of all the cultural, ethnic, and religious components of its society49. Traditional social institutions such 
as the family and tribal system, as well as modern institutions — for example family associations 
and charitable organizations — are very influential in the preservation of the identity of the smaller 
groups. They also play an important role in supporting a collective Jordanian cultural identity. 
Political, social and religious leadership, as well as civil-society organizations, are also very 
influential in preserving diversity.  
 
The Jordanian Constitution stipulates freedom of religion to all Jordanians, regardless of ethnic or 
religious origin. Access to political, civil, and welfare rights is guaranteed to all Jordanians by the 
Constitution. Although in family matters the law is heavily influenced by Islamic jurisprudence, the 
Christian community has its own judicial councils to resolve disputes between followers of the 
various Christian denominations.

Jordan's economy is among the smallest in the Middle East, with insufficient supplies of water, oil, 
and other natural resources, underlying the government's strong reliance on foreign assistance. 
Other economic challenges for the government include chronic high rates of unemployment and 
underemployment, budget and current account deficits, and government debt50. Jordan has a very 
young population. In 2018, about 35% of Jordanians was under the age of 15 (male 1,835,094 and 
female 1,735,773), whereas, 20% are 15–24 years of age. The estimated Jordan population growth 
rate is ~2% for the year 2019.  
Figure 10. Population by age group. 
 
New research findings highlighted the relationship between demography and age structural factors, 
showing that demography is an important factor for development. Youth should be one of the main 
economic assets for a country or a region, therefore, benefiting from this age structure will be through 
sound policies, which ensure that, young people enter the labor market, they are adequately 
educated and jobs exist for them. Education, training and a well-functioning labor market are 
prerequisites to obtain the benefits of progress through this demographic transition51. 
  
Economic growth will have been downbeat in the third quarter, after slowing slightly in the second 
quarter. In the first two months of the quarter, bank lending growth in year-on-year terms averaged 
below Q2’s over four-year low, suggesting fixed investment growth was depressed in Q3. Moreover, 
industrial production decreased in July compared to the same month a year earlier for the third 
consecutive month. However, spending increased in July from the same month a year earlier, which 
should have supported the economy somewhat, although the government’s fiscal deficit widened in 
January–July in annual terms. In addition, inflationary pressure was lower in Q3, which should have 
supported private consumption52.

Jordan’s real GDP registered a growth of 2% in 2018, a bit lower than growth in 2017, constrained 
by structural weaknesses and a difficult regional setting. On the supply side, a strong services sector 
(contributing 1.5% to GDP growth), followed by the industrial sector (0.4% to GDP growth), and 
provided the dominant sector. On the demand side, growth from a significant improvement in net 
exports was countered by a major contraction in private demand during the first half of 2018. 
Investment growth was moderate due to a major reduction in public capital expenditures and low 
inflows of private investment.53 
 
4 
Legal Framework  
 
 
Jordan Constitution  
 
The Constitution of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan stipulates that all Jordanians shall be equal 
before the law. There shall be no discrimination between them as regards to their rights and duties 
on grounds of race, language, or religion. Reference in the Constitution to the rights of Jordanians 
includes both men and women. However, the Constitution does not specifically prohibit gender 
discrimination. National reports issued by Jordan arising from international human rights conventions 
recognize that the Constitution does not distinguish between women and men.

22 
 
While women have been appointed to the judiciary since 1995, they are poorly represented in the 
judicial system — only 19% of judges and 27% of all practising lawyers.  
 
Figure 12.  Women’s representation in the judiciary (as judges). 
 
In 2018, the first woman was appointed to the Court of Cassation, but no women serve in the Sharia 
Courts. Although qualified women are permitted to serve in this role, those who oppose the idea of 
having women in Sharia courts state that women are too emotional to occupy such positions. The 
presence of female lawyers and judges encourages women to claim their rights through the legal 
system and increases their knowledge about their legal rights54. 
 
 
Jordan’s International Commitments 
 
Jordan is signatory to all major international instruments pertaining to women’s rights and gender 
equality, including: i) the Universal Declaration of Human Rights; ii) the International Covenant on 
Economic, Social and Cultural Rights; iii) the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights; iv) 
the Convention on the Political Rights of Women; v) the Convention on the Rights of the Child; vi) 
the United Nations Convention against Transnational Organized Crime and its Protocol to Prevent, 
Suppress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children; vii) the Arab Charter 
of Human Rights; and the International Labor Organization (ILO) conventions on equal remuneration 
and discrimination in respect of employment and occupation, among others. Jordan is signatory to 
the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW, ratified 
in 1992)55. 
 
However the country maintains reservations on the article 9 (2) (which is supposed to give equal 
rights to women with regard to nationality of children), to article 16 paragraph 1 (c) (same rights and 
responsibilities during marriage and at its dissolution), (d) (same rights and responsibilities as 
parents), and (g) (same personal rights, including the right to choose a family name, a profession 
and an occupation). Jordan maintains its reservations on article 16 (1) (c) and (d) because these are 
not compatible with Shari’a provisions. In 2009, a Cabinet decision lifted its reservations on 
paragraph 4 of Article 15, and gave women freedom of mobility and choice of residence without 
consent of their husbands or other male family members.56 
 
Although the government made improvements in the legislations, the provision leaves a gap, Jordan 
still have to take actions in some of the discriminatory laws against women. For example, Jordan 
should address the Jordanian Nationality Law, which does not allow Jordanian women married to 
foreign-born spouses to pass on their nationality to their spouses and children, and stop what is 
 
54 https://www.undp.org/content/dam   
55 OECD. 2019 Social Institutions and Gender Index. Country profile: Jordan. Available: 
https://www.genderindex.org/wp-content/uploads/files/datasheets/2019/JO.pdf  
56 JIP, 2018. Archives for 2018. Available at: https://jordaningoforum.org/2018

=== PAGE 206 ===
23 
 
called “administrative detention”, which is used as a protective measure for women considered to 
be at risk of being killed by a family member, and allows her indefinite detention under the country’s 
1954 Crime Prevention Law. It is worth noting that, despite a minimum age of marriage set at 18, 
judges are allowed to approve marriage agreements for girls at 15 years old, which contributes to 
the rise of child marriage in Jordan, including in the Syrian refugee community.57 
 
 
National Women’s mechanisms  
 
4.4.1 The Jordanian National Commission for Women JNCW 
 
The Commission was established in 1992, it was the first national mechanism mandated to achieve 
gender equity and social justice by improving the status of women, enhancing their economic, social 
and political participation, safeguard their gains, and defend their rights. The responsibilities 
assigned to JNCW and translated in its mandate focused on policy, laws and legislations, networks, 
exchange of knowledge and expertise and the national women strategy. 
 
JNCW lack of government commitment to integrate gender within its various ministries which results 
in scattering JNCW's efforts here and there instead of systematic approach in the handling of women 
issues. This affects JNCW role and transforms their tasks to a more ad hoc basis. JNCW remains 
an active participant in any request from the Government to ensure gender integration and 
addressing women issues. JNCW works strategically with Ministries of Planning, Justice, Labour, 
Social Development and Public sector reform to integrate comprehensive programs on gender 
equality and women empowerment.58 
 
JNCW works strategically with Ministries of Planning, Justice, Labor, Social Development and Public 
sector reform to integrate comprehensive programs on gender equality and women empowerment. 
Also, JNCW built strategic partnerships with Working NGOs for effective results on the ground and 
youth groups to change the current cultural practices and spread new culture of tolerance and 
respect for women's rights.59 
 
JNCW drafted the first national strategy for women in 2003, it serves as road map and guiding 
document for government to promote the rights of women under each component. The latest national 
strategy for women 2013–2017 was endorsed by the cabinet in 2013, covering human security, 
political empowerment, economic empowerment, media and ICT, integration of gender in policies 
and legislations, Institutional improvement for organizations working on women issues and the 
popular culture on women empowerment. 
 
 
Governmental Mechanisms to promote Gender equality and women empowerment   
 
The governmental gender focal point network was established by Cabinet decree in 1996. The 
network has representation of most governmental organizations. They are gender units of four 
Ministries namely, Ministry of Labour, Planning, Social Development and Health.  
 
4.5.1 Directorate of Women, Ministry of Labour60 
 
In 2006 the Ministry of Labour had a division for women. It was renamed in 2008 to take on the 
responsibilities of a directorate according to the new organizational structure. The tasks of the 
women directorate include economic empowerment, as well as awareness and education. The
