24 
 
directorate worked on amending the labour law to include article on creating nurseries in the 
workplace, conducting training on pay equality and implementing in cooperation with ILO program 
on pay equity. However, the directorate needs to be empowered. There is a need to win the support 
and championship of the senior management to strategically mainstream gender within the 
departments/directorates of the Ministry and adequate funding to implement certain initiatives.  
 
4.5.2 Gender Unit, Ministry of Planning and International Cooperation 
 
The department develops economic policies through conducting research and studies. It contributes 
to determining objectives, policies and development indicators with full coordination with JNCW and 
the gender focal points in the ministries, government institutions and non-governmental 
organizations. In addition, one of the department’s mandates is to build a system to monitor and 
evaluate policies and programs and their impact on gender equity.  
 
As other gender units in other ministries, the gender unit does not have the proper implementation 
mechanisms to ensure gender is properly addressed at all levels of the ministry, besides the limited 
financial and human resources to provide technical assistance on gender mainstreaming. 
 
4.5.3 Gender Unit, Ministry of Social Development 
 
The gender unit falls under the supervision of the directorate of Studies and Policy Development. 
The directorate’s objective is to prepare strategic studies, as well as to follow up on progress and 
implementation. The directorate formulates policies, analyses data and social norms. However, the 
unit is operated by only one staff member and lacks sufficient financial and human resources. In 
addition, leadership in the area of gender analysis and mainstreaming is required. There is also a 
need to have a customized manual to give guidance to the gender units on how to mainstream 
gender in policies, plans, programmes and budgets. 
 
4.5.4 Ministry of Health 
 
There is a gender focal point that has received advanced trainings on gender and gender responsive 
budgeting through the trainings provided by UN Women and other EU programs. The gender focal 
point is in continuous cooperation with JNCW and UN Women on all matters related to gender within 
the Ministry. However, the position, authority and resources of the gender units should be 
strengthened, and the structure of the gender unit revised.61 
 
4.5.5 National Laws  
 
Since 2007, the Government of Jordan has taken significant steps to address issues raised by civil 
society regarding the status of women in national law. 
 
4.5.6 Labor Law of 2008 
  
Jordan has ratified the International Labour Organization (ILO) Equal Remuneration Convention and 
included its provisions in Jordanian law, legally discrimination in employment on the basis of gender 
or marital status is not permitted. Sexual harassment is an offence and abuse of women and children 
in the workplace is liable to punishment. However, no monitoring system is in place, which indicates 
that these laws are not activated applied. Implementation of laws is negatively affected by lack of 
capacities, prevalent patriarchal norms and gender roles, and the domination of men in the public 
sphere.  
 
 
61 UN Women, 2015. Assessment of National Women 
Machineries. Available at: https://www2.unwomen.org

=== PAGE 208 ===
25 
 
Therefore, a significant pay gap remains even for the same type of work, the wage gap between 
genders has reached 13.8 % in the public sector and 14.2% in the private sector. In the health sector, 
the gap was at 31.8% and in private education it had reached 30.2%62. In terms of social benefits, 
the Civil Service Code remains discriminatory in some of its articles. Family allowances are 
automatically paid to men, while it is granted to women only in certain circumstances. Within the 
social security system, pension payments to the family are only paid to a woman’s family under 
limited circumstances despite the fact that social security contributions are equal. 
 
Many firms discriminate against hiring women due to the perceived higher additional costs they 
occur for maternity leave. Several reforms have been introduced in recent years to address this 
discrimination, but they still do not go far enough to close the gender gap in labor legislation. 
 
The labor law amendment made in 2008 condemning sexual harassment in work place and the new 
Civil Services Statute 200763 that considers sexual harassment in public service was a positive step, 
however activation of these two provisions is limited and no procedures are put in place to raise 
women’s awareness on the importance of these laws. Also, cultural norms constrains women from 
reporting on sexual harassment incidents so not to be shamed and humiliated.   
 
4.5.7 Personal Status laws 
 
The Personal Status Law (No. 61 of 1976) which is also known as “Family Law”, organizes all 
matters related to the family including children. The Law is adapted from Islamic jurisprudence. 
 
The Personal Status Law is meant to guide decisions and regulation in matters of marriage, divorce, 
custody, and inheritance. In 2012, the Government of Jordan introduced some positive major 
revisions to the Personal Status Law, it address past Inequalities, as in the field of inheritance, in 
which the amended Law establishes a three-month waiting period before a women can waive her 
inheritance rights after the death of her husband or father, which prevent her male relatives to 
approach her to sign documents that would waive her inheritance rights without her knowledge64. 
Another reform that is very beneficial for divorced Jordanian men is the clause that divorced parents 
are able to see their children at their homes instead of police stations or local organisations.  
 
Under the Nationality Law, women cannot pass citizenship to their children or spouse in the same 
way as men. Jordanian women married to non-Jordanian men cannot pass their citizenship to their 
children or husbands, although they may retain their own Jordanian citizenship65. In November 
2014, the government approved granting Jordanian women married to foreigners and their children 
a set of privileges. These privileges did not amount to Jordanian women being allowed to pass on 
their citizenship to their children and spouses (a right Jordanian men enjoy) but substantially eased 
their lives66. 
 
4.5.8 Election Law 
 
Women are poorly represented in the political sphere, which prevents them from successfully 
contributing in the decision-making process that would shape the political priorities, policies and 
practices for women. The quota system was introduced in the 2003 elections through a legislative 
amendment, reserving 6 out of 110 seats for women candidates. The quota was doubled to 12 seats 
in the Election Law of 2010, resulting in a total number of 13 women in the Lower House of

Parliament; 12 filled by quota and one by direct competition. The new electoral law implemented in 
the 2013 parliamentary elections increased the number of parliamentary seats to 130 and women’s 
quota from 12 to 15 seats, it kept the level of representation of women to around 11%, far short of 
the Millennium Development Goals (MDG). 
 
On the other hand, some steps have been made in easing the atmosphere for organizing as part of 
the government’s recent efforts to promote democratic reform in the areas of freedom of assembly, 
freedom of association and freedom of expression. However, these changes in legislation have not 
been translated into practice in ways to produce the desired effect of real freedom and equality67. 
 
4.5.9 Protection against Domestic Violence 
 
In Jordan, domestic violence is widely regarded as a family matter that affects large numbers of 
women, children and adults across their lifespan. In cultural and religious matters, Jordanian society 
has conservative attitudes towards gender roles within society, and the behaviour of men and 
women is strictly defined and formed according to the social system. The structure of the Jordanian 
family and the distribution of power within it is one of the reasons behind violence in Jordan. In other 
words, men according to the cultural norms have the power and exercise complete authority over all 
the family’s affairs due to the financial power they have. On the other hand, women are expected to 
fulfil their childbearing and homemaking roles, including loyalty to their husbands and preserving the 
reputation of their families, otherwise they are condemned socially and may be exposed to physical 
punishment. To avoid such consequences, women have to comply with social and cultural rules and 
should obey all instructions and orders given by men to avoid punishment68. In this regard, women 
in Jordan still widely accept that violence against them by men could sometimes be justifiable. As a 
result, women do not seek help from governmental agencies. 
 
Article 98 of the Penal Code was applied to reduce penalties for men who perpetrated violent crimes 
against women, such as murder or assault, after the woman had committed a dishonourable act. 
Article 98 was amended in 2017 to prevent it from being used to reduce penalties for so-called 
‘honour’ crimes against women69. Jordan has undertaken a number of procedural, administrative, 
judicial and legislative measures to combat and eliminate domestic violence. It has created many 
public welfare institutions and has given them a general mandate to provide protection programmes, 
promoted in all regions of the kingdom. Departments for family protection have been established at 
both the Public Security Directorate and the Ministry of Social Development, and a National Council 
for Family Affairs has also been created. 
 
4.5.10 National government strategies and policies for climate change and gender  
 
The Ministry of Environment (MOE) is primarily responsible for overseeing the policy and legal 
frameworks that guide climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts of the country, including the 
development of the Third National Communication (TNC) to the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Intended Nationally Determined Contributions 
(INDC). The MOE works closely with the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Health and the 
Ministry of Water and Irrigation, which is responsible for managing water resources by implementing 
irrigation policy, determining water allocation, constricting water infrastructure and establishing water 
conservation programs.  
 
Jordan has prepared climate strategies, communications to the UNFCCC, and action plans 
including: 
• 
Initial National Communication (1999)

4.5.10.1 The National Climate Change Policy 
 
The National Climate Change Policy (2013–2020) provides an overview of Jordan’s strategies to 
combat climate change across various sectors and actions that the Jordan will implement in the 
coming years .Special attention is given to “vulnerable groups” that stand to be severely impacted 
by the negative effects of climate change, as well as strategies to address gender imbalances 
between men and women (Jordan was the first Middle Eastern country to include gender 
considerations in its national climate change policies).70 
 
4.5.10.2 Third National Communication 
 
Third National Communication (TNC) (2014) builds on the objectives and proposed actions of the 
Climate Policy. The TNC includes projected impacts of climate change on Jordan, as well as a 
comprehensive mitigation assessment and a detailed inventory of GHG emissions. Using 
downscaling projection models, it reports that Jordan will be highly vulnerable to climate change 
impacts, especially the projected decrease in precipitation and increase in temperature and dry 
spells. It notes that this is an extreme concern for a country that is among the poorest in availability 
of water resources in the world and facing a huge challenge of accommodating the massive influx 
of refugees from neighbouring countries. The TNC contains comprehensive vulnerability 
assessments for major sectors in Jordan, including assessments of four rural communities, and 
identifies opportunities for mitigation and adaptations that Jordan will pursue with support of the 
international community71. 
 
5 
Situational Analysis — Jordan Valley Targeted Areas 
 
 
Socio economic profile  
 
The Jordan Valley forms part of the larger Jordan Rift Valley. The internationally recognized World 
Heritage values of the Jordan Valley are strongly related to its unique historic, religious, cultural, 
economic, and environmental values, due to its typical rift valley topography. The lower part of the 
Jordan River (LJR) originates at the Sea of Galilee and meanders along 200 km down to the Dead 
Sea through the Jordan Valley.  
 
Climatically, the Jordan Valley is characterized by hot dry summers and mild wet winters, becoming 
progressively drier moving southward through the valley towards the Dead Sea. Climate change 
impacts are likely to intensify the water supply-related problems in the Jordan Valley72. Impacts of 
climate change includes a foreseen reduction in local annual water resources with a maximum of 20 
% by 2050 and increasing temperatures and related surface water evaporation rates based on 2008 
climate change impact assessment. 
 
 
70 The National Climate Change Policy of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan 2013–2020 
71 Third National Communication, 2014 
72 https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-319-30036-8_2

=== PAGE 211 ===

The northern part of the East Bank of the Jordan Valley in Jordan will be impacted most negatively 
by climate change, with a foreseen substantial reduction of annual and winter rainfall, although 
summer rainfall will increase slightly. 
 
The study area houses a population of about (561,510)73. The information with regard to the 
population numbers in the study area have been obtained through the Jordanian Department of 
Statistics (DOS). The population growth rate for the Jordanian section of the study is estimated at 
2.2 %74. 
 
The Jordan Valley houses large number of informal foreign workers originating mainly from Egypt 
and Iraq, and lately including some refugees from Syria. It is estimated that ~108,964 households 
live in the Jordan Valley today, some of them employed in the agricultural sector75.

13% of the total population lives in rural areas, where poverty is more prevalent than in urban areas 
and living conditions are difficult. As an arid country with less than 5% of land available for farming 
and little to no rainfall, prospects of self-sustainability through farming are limited. The most 
vulnerable groups include large rural households with low literacy and education levels, women-
headed households, households 12% with sick or elderly people, and households that do not own 
land or have very little land.  
Figure 14. Households by sex of household head in the target area (2008–2018). 
 
Local citizens of the Jordan Valley are involved in mixed farms that covered crop and livestock 
production systems. In addition, some Bedouins also live in the Jordan Valley and use the lands as 
grazing ground for their sheep and goats during the winter months because of its warm climate and 
available fodder for their animals. The livestock will be impacted by climate change, whereas self-
sufficiency degree (SSD) for most food items would decline by 2050. Of great concern, production

of red meat would be highly affected by climate change, as rangelands would not provide enough 
forage for sheep and goats.80 
 
In the Jordan Valley there is a small group of wealthy agricultural entrepreneurs, but also to a large 
group of labourers who live close to the poverty line of JD 32.6 per person per month. The negative 
impacts of climate change will be strongly felt by women. Women make crucial contributions in 
agriculture and rural enterprises as farmers, animal caretakers, workers and entrepreneurs. Land is 
the most important asset to support production and provide for food, nutrition and income security, 
however, while having the legal right to own land, social norms diminish women’s access to land by 
favoring male inheritance and providing disincentives for women to purchase land.81

=== PAGE 215 ===

Industries in the Jordan valley are mostly small industries for the construction sector and package 
industry. There are several quarries that produce materials for the construction of buildings and 
infrastructures in the north-eastern governorates of Jordan. Some quarries export marble. There are 
also several metal processing plants in the central and southern part of the Jordan valley that 
produce metal frameworks for greenhouses and install these for commercial farmers. Small plants 
are located across the region, that produce wooden and plastic crates and boxes for the commercial 
farmers, for packing their produce in accordance with the demands of the export markets.  
 
Figure 17. Percentage of people in the target area, aged 15+ employed in main economic sectors. 
 
In terms of women’s engagement in the agricultural sector specifically, FAO statistics show that as 
of 2014, 0.9% of the country’s total female population was employed in agriculture, and this could 
be related to the high prevalence of informal employment in this sector. It’s worth noting that home-
based agriculture was found to be the most common type of agricultural activity undertaken by 
women.  
 
Most of women in the targeted areas are in health and education sectors 56% and 62% respectively, 
while the majority of men are working in agriculture (89%) compared with only 11% of women82. 
 
Meanwhile, women were found to be engaging in home-based agriculture to produce food for 
household consumption. Women’s engagement in agricultural activities has economic importance 
for their household. The majority of FGD women participants confirmed that women engaged in both 
paid agricultural labour and home-based agriculture contribute to their household’s income that 
enhanced their ability to independently take decisions within the household.  
 
The FGD participants stated that, the majority of women who engaged in home-based agriculture in 
are Jordanian, compared to Syrian women which is linked to their or their family’s ability to own land, 
unlike Syrians. These motivating factors and variations within them are important to take into account 
when identifying avenues for programming.

Illiteracy rate in the targeted area for women is relatively high for women aged 15+ (9.7%), with 9.2% 
in Ain El Basha, comparing with 3.8% among men. However, the school enrolment rates are similar 
for both boys and girls, however the enrolment ratio is higher among girls enrolled in the secondary 
education 53% comparing with 46% for boys, where they enrol in the vocational education 98%.

However, the unemployment rate among women is approximately 27%, which is higher than for men 
(15%).  This rate is even higher for single (never married) women (41.5%) and widows (19.5%). 
Figure 19. Percentage of unemployed of the population by marital status. 
 
 
Access to finance and resources  
 
The majority of women are not engaging in any financial loans, FGD said that they used to get 
revolving funds. However, these funds were not well controlled by the donors and become a financial 
burden for women. Most of these funds were for the purpose of family issues such as schools and 
universities. On the other hand, women have no control of these funds, since husbands usually take 
these funds to be used for other purposes.  Men usually refuse to guarantee the fund for their wives, 
as a result, women will not be able to pay back the money which leads to social and legal distresses.  
 
Many research assessments on the revolving funds impact indicated that, women entrepreneurs in 
Jordan face considerable gender-specific barriers to accessing loans, such as having to provide a 
guarantee from their husbands and being subject to larger collateral amounts. Studies has indicated 
that men are usually interested in acting as guarantor if they can have control over the money; few 
women have the means to guarantee loans.

Women participation in the public sphere and the women CSOs involvement in the decision-
making process in their communities  
 
The Jordan Valley Solidarity Network is supporting community-based organizations in the valley that 
provide social services to poor and vulnerable individuals, households and Syrians.  Some of the 
CBOs in the targeted areas participated in the FGD and contribute to the discussion of social impact 
on proposed interventions. One of the key CBOs is the Jordan Hashemite Fund for Human 
Development (JOHUD). JOHUD supports national initiatives to improve the management of scarce 
water resources, through a wide range of water related projects. To build sustainable awareness 
and to spur behavioural change on the local level in water resource management.  
 
The other key CBO is the Jordan Environment Society (JES). The objective of JES includes, but is 
not limited to, protecting the environment and its basic elements of water, air, soil and wildlife, based 
the belief that the environment is both the endowment and responsibility of all people. In addition of 
the Royal Society for Conservation of Nature to create, manage and advocate for a national network 
of protected areas to conserve Jordan’s biodiversity and support local community development, 
while also promoting wider public support and action for the protection of the natural environment 
within Jordan and neighboring countries. 
 
Finally, IUCN, Jordan to influence, encourage and assist society to conserve the integrity and 
diversity of nature and to ensure that any use of natural resources is equitable and ecologically 
sustainable. A range of women CSOs operate in their areas, there is some focus on empowerment, 
such as training, they also targeting boys and men, especially in awareness activities and revolving 
funds. On the other hand, CBOs may enjoy a level of public trust and be considered to be safe or 
acceptable spaces for women to gain training and receive information. 
 
However, these organisations are not seen as a powerful tool to communicate with women and other 
vulnerable groups in the local communities, it was explained that they don’t have a network relation

with each other nor the other official or private entities in the community. Those CSOs are 
approached by donors, providing them with trainings such as food processing and handmade crafts, 
however these trainings are not reflected or support for women in enhancing their social situations 
because of poor marketing. In addition, some CSOs are active in delivering awareness sessions for 
the local communities such as JOUHD community centre in Al Yarmouk on water management, 
environment and drugs for youth. 
 
 
Social norms and cultural constrains 
 
There was much evidence to indicate a gendered division of labor within households in the three 
areas, with women (and to some extent girls) responsible for cooking, cleaning, childcare and 
‘indoor’ domestic work and men responsible for working outside of the home. The family size 
reported by focus group participants generally ranged from 6 to 10, though a family size of more was 
reported where the man had more than one wife. Women participants indicated that there is a high 
rate of divorce cases for girls aged 18–23 which lead them to be more vulnerable because they are 
unemployed and don’t have financial power. 
 
Where feasible, participants were asked to discuss the decision-making dynamic around child 
spacing (as a proxy indicator for women’s access to decision making within the family). Nearly all 
said that reproductive decisions are the domain of the husband, with the possibility of significant 
involvement by either mother in law. 
 
In terms of financial decision making, participants varied. There was a fair degree of consensus that 
women make decisions about small, daily expenditures such as food, though while women said they 
were given a spending allowance by their husbands, the latter mostly denied providing an allowance 
for their wife’s autonomous use. Some financial decisions, such as schooling and health, were said 
to be taken by both men and women together. The general picture that emerged was one where, 
though the wife may participate in and even control small spending decisions, financial decision 
making ultimately lies in the hands of the husband. 
 
Norms around mobility, women’s mobility was not entirely restricted by all husbands, it is dependent 
upon the husband’s preference. Most men and women reported that if the husband was not at home 
and, for example, shopping needed to be done, then the woman could go out for that purpose. 
Similarly, however, most participants reported that if the husband or an older son was available, it 
would be preferable that he would be the one to leave the home and go out. Participants mentioned 
that a significant percentage of youth are still unemployed and still actively looking for suitable jobs. 
Employed individuals mainly work in different unsustainable jobs, in the military sector, or in daily-
paid fluctuating jobs (per day jobs). 
 
 
Specific findings in line with Gender Analysis Framework 
 
1. It was observed that, only 30% of the FGD women participants know about the climate change 
and how it may affect women and children more, saying that women are responsible for taking 
care of children and the doctors’ visits. In addition they are more affected by risks associated 
with rain floods and poor sanitation facilities. While the majority of men (80%) participants said 
that the climate change conditions will affect both men and women the same way.  Given that 
there is a different understanding between men and women related to the impacts of climate 
change between genders there is an opportunity to address this through the project’s 
awareness-raising interventions under Output 4.1 — see Activity 4.1.1 in the GAP below. This 
will also be addressed through the livelihoods- (Output 2.1) and water supply and management-
based activities (Output 3.1), where the specific needs of female beneficiaries related to 
vulnerabilities and climate change adaptation are emphasised during implementation — see 
Activities 2.1.3 and 3.1.3 in the GAP.

=== PAGE 219 ===
36 
 
2. Men control access to resources in which men own the key productive assets such as land, 
livestock and medium to large businesses. For climate change interventions especially in dry 
lands, this means that activities which include the use of productive resources like land or 
livestock should include men and women in decision-making. Secondly, any proposed land-use 
change relating to dry land conservation should include both local men and women as key 
stakeholders. This provides an opportunity for the representation of women in decision-making 
bodies to be enhanced, as well as focusing on improved productivity and income generation 
that promote the participation of women and their access to resources. Under Activity 1.1.4, the 
establishment of Watershed Management Committees (WMCs) will be supported, local 
decision-making bodies focused on the climate-resilient management of watersheds and their 
resources. This is an opportunity to improve the representation and participation of women in 
the management of their local watersheds and associated resources to support climate change 
adaptation in their communities — see Activity 1.1.4 in the GAP.  
 
3. Access to credit is a key constraint in improving women’s economic productivity in the selected 
areas. Hence it is important that the project focuses on improving women’s access to credit 
through the Water Fund in the Jordan Valley to pay for ongoing watershed restoration in the 
upper-catchment areas of the project's three target sites. The on-granting facility and the Water 
Fund’s grant mechanism (Activity 2.1.4) provides an opportunity for women to access grant 
finance to the support the implementation of gender-responsive ILWRM initiatives, empowering 
them to contribute to climate change adaptation in their own communities that directly benefits 
local women. All proposals submitted for consideration for funding by the on-granting facility will 
need to be gender responsive, promoting the inclusion of women, considering their roles in 
society and have activities specifically tailored for women and align with the GAP (see below) 
— proposals will be reviewed by JILMI’s gender expert to confirm this. 
 
4. The project can provide support to the Ministry of Environment, to activate gender 
mainstreaming actions stated in the climate change policy and involve women in the decision-
making process. Output 1.1 includes the drafting of amendments to Jordan’s planning, legal and 
policy framework for agriculture, land, water and natural resources, to mainstream climate-
resilient ILWRM (Activity 1.1.2). This provides an opportunity to further mainstream gender into 
policy- and decision-making processes, contributing to a gender-responsive cross-sectoral 
response to the impacts of climate change in Jordan. Participants in this activity will include at 
least 50% female representation — see Activity 1.1.2 of the GAP. 
 
5. Women’s illiteracy, poverty and unemployment are high in the targeted areas, which make them 
more vulnerable to climate change impacts. In addition, more investment is needed to improve 
women’s participation in alternative livelihoods, to ensure that their rights and interests are 
recognized and encouraging home-based farming. Gender-sensitive schemes can support 
vulnerable groups in the targeted areas and particularly women-headed households. Under 
Activity 2.1.3 (Introduce gender-responsive climate-resilient additional livelihoods and 
agricultural practices in the project’s three intervention areas), up to 75% of beneficiary 
households will be female headed, depending on the livelihood. Livelihoods have been selected 
and will be further tailored during implementation to be gender-responsive — see Activity 2.1.3 
in the GAP. Activity 3.1.4 will see local communities, including women, empowered to monitor 
and manage local water supplies more effectively. This will include developing gender-
responsive community-based water use monitoring and managing plans for project beneficiary 
communities; implementing a gender-inclusive outreach campaign to train community members 
to monitor and manage the use of clean water (100% female representation); and promoting 
additional livelihoods related to the monitoring and management of clean water (including at 
least 50% female beneficiaries) — see Activity 3.1.4 in the GAP. 
 
 
6. Increased collaboration and partnership with CSOs, NGOs and the entities working on climate 
change issues can help increase awareness of gender, and environmental sustainability issues

=== PAGE 220 ===
37 
 
in the targeted areas. This awareness raising should seek to increase women’s knowledge and 
equal rights in particular. In addition, CSOs could support gender mainstreaming by involving 
whole communities, men, and local leaders, to overcome sensitivity to the traditional divisions 
of labor that may help to design the project better to be more socially acceptable. Activity 4.1.1 
will see the implementation of a gender-responsive climate change adaptation outreach 
programme across the Jordan Valley, bringing together local CSOS, NGOs and other entities. 
The outreach programme will create awareness around climate change issues, including those 
which are gender specific, through a specific model prioritizing gender and youth — see Activity 
4.1.1 in the GAP.

Gender Action Plan (GAP)

Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILMI) 
Impact Statement  
Research and anecdotal evidence indicate that women, children and youth are among the groups most vulnerable to the impacts of 
climate change. In addition, a large number of vulnerable people in communities that are highly dependent on local natural resources 
for their survival are women and children. Therefore, the Government of Jordan has established that gender-sensitive planning, 
resource-allocation and implementation in the context of the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILMI) will be a 
priority. Supporting transformational change both in the lives of female beneficiaries and in prompting gender mainstreaming in 
national climate change policy-making and implementation was therefore seen as a priority in stakeholder consultations.  
 
Expected total numbers of direct and indirect beneficiaries (reduced vulnerability or increased resilience), are: 
• 
246,942 people directly benefit from project interventions (135,736 men and 111,206 women) 
• 
756,000 people indirectly benefit from the project interventions (355,320 women and 400,680 men) 
The project will also: 
• 
ensure gender inclusion in all the policies reviewed to be more gender sensitive;  
• 
ensure sustainability and long-term impact of gender-affirmative actions; 
• 
build capacity for government institutions in acknowledging gender differentials in responding to climate risks; 
• 
ensure that women should take leadership with regards to project components related to them, however, without the 
marginalization of men with the intent to avoid their resistance and negative influence; and 
• 
increase social accountability.  
Outcome statement  
Increased resilience to climate change of at least 111,206 women in project area through direct improvement of their capacities to 
resources for agricultural/agroforestry practices and water management, climate smart agriculture and agroforestry practices 
and/or to grant facility.

Sub-total of dedicated costs Gender 
Officer and Gender mainstreaming costs 
The Gender Officer will oversee all activities related to gender mainstreaming, 
including the implementation of action plans. The officer will ensure that the project-
level grievance redress mechanism (GRM) (See Annex 6: ESMP) will be accessible 
to women and men, including to women who are subject to GBV, SEAH and 
sensitive to their special confidentiality and protection needs and refer cases to 
existing protection mechanisms accordingly.
